Quotient EM under Misspecification: Tight Local Rates and Finite-Sample Bounds in General Integral Probability Metrics
Koustav Mallik

Abstract
. We study the expectation-maximization (EM) algorithm for general latent-variable models under (i) distributional misspecification and (ii) nonidentifiability induced by a group action. We formulate EM on the quotient parameter space and measure error using an arbitrary integral probability metric (IPM). Our main results give (a) a sharp local linear convergence rate for population EM governed by the spectral radius of the linearization on a local slice, and (b) tight finite-sample bounds for sample EM obtained via perturbed contraction inequalities and generic chaining/entropy control of EM-induced empirical processes.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
62F10, 62F12, 62H12, 62G20

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Misspecification, symmetry, and distributional error criteria

Let
\mathcal{X}
denote an observation space and let
\{p_{\theta}(x,z):\theta\in\Theta\}
be a dominated family of latent-variable models on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}
, with observed marginal
p_{\theta}(x)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,dz
and induced distribution
P_{\theta}
on
\mathcal{X}
.
Given i.i.d. data
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P^{*}
from an
arbitrary
distribution
P^{*}
on
\mathcal{X}
, consider the population (cross-entropy) objective

(1)

\Phi(\theta)\;:=\;\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X)\big].

When
P^{*}\notin\{P_{\theta}\}
, the appropriate statistical target is the
KL projection set

(2)

\bar{\Theta}\;:=\;\argmax_{\theta\in\Theta}\Phi(\theta)\;=\;\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\KL(P^{*}\,\|\,P_{\theta}),

where the equivalence follows from the elementary identity

\KL(P^{*}\,\|\,P_{\theta})=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[\log p^{*}(X)]-\Phi(\theta)
.
In misspecified settings,
\bar{\Theta}
may be non-singleton and the curvature of
\Phi
at
\bar{\Theta}
need not correspond to any “true parameter”; nonetheless
\bar{\Theta}
is the canonical benchmark for both statistical and algorithmic analysis
[
5
,
6
]
.

A second structural feature is
nonidentifiability induced by symmetry
.
We model nonidentifiability by a group action
G\curvearrowright\Theta
satisfying observed invariance

(3)

P_{g\cdot\theta}\;=\;P_{\theta},\qquad\forall g\in G,\ \theta\in\Theta.

Invariance (
3
) arises in finite mixtures (label switching), symmetric mixtures (sign flips), and factor models (rotations), among many others.
Under (
3
), the map
\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}
factors through the quotient
\Theta/G:=\Theta/G
, and the statistically meaningful estimand is the orbit
\left[\theta\right]\in\Theta/G
rather than the ambient parameter
\theta
.
This elementary observation has a consequential implication: any error criterion that distinguishes points
within
a single orbit cannot be an appropriate target for consistent estimation (and is typically incompatible with any meaningful algorithmic rate statement).
Accordingly, our analysis is formulated on
\Theta/G
; see also recent symmetry-aware estimation frameworks in related contexts
[
7
]
.

A third feature, closely tied to misspecification and nonidentifiability, is the choice of
error metric
.
Rather than measuring error in an ambient parameter norm, we measure discrepancy between distributions using an
integral probability metric
(IPM) generated by an arbitrary function class
\mathcal{F}
,

(4)

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q)\;:=\;\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}f-\mathbb{E}_{Q}f\big|.

This framework encompasses total variation, bounded-Lipschitz and Wasserstein-type metrics, and kernel-based IPMs, among others
[
8
,
9
]
.
Because of (
3
),
d_{\mathcal{F}}
induces a canonical quotient metric

(5)

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\left[\theta^{\prime}\right])\;:=\;d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}}),

and the natural performance criterion relative to the misspecified target is the distance-to-set functional

(6)

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\bar{\Theta}/G)\;:=\;\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\left[\bar{\theta}\right])=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}}).

The aim of this work is to establish
sharp local algorithmic rates
and
finite-sample guarantees
for EM in the general regime described by
(
2
)–(
6
): arbitrary misspecification, group nonidentifiability, and an arbitrary IPM.

1.2.
EM maps and the quotient state space

The EM algorithm is classically derived as a minorization–maximization scheme for the observed log-likelihood
[
4
,
12
]
.
Given a current iterate
\theta
, define the population surrogate

(7)

Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,Z)\Big],\qquad M(\theta)\in\argmax_{\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta),

and define sample analogs
(\widehat{Q}_{n},\widehat{M}_{n})
by replacing the outer expectation in (
7
) by
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\cdot)
.
The fundamental ascent property—
\Phi(M(\theta))\geq\Phi(\theta)
and
\Phi_{n}(\widehat{M}_{n}(\theta))\geq\Phi_{n}(\theta)
for exact M-steps—is well known
[
4
,
12
]
.

Nonasymptotic analyses of EM typically proceed by (i) proving that the
population
EM operator is contractive on a basin around a target point, and (ii) showing that the
sample
operator is a uniform perturbation of the population operator on that basin
[
1
]
.
Under misspecification, the appropriate target is the projection set (
2
) and the relevant local geometry is governed by the curvature of the misspecified objective at that set
[
5
,
6
]
.
In the presence of symmetry (
3
), however, both
\Phi
and
\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}
are constant along orbits, and the natural state space for EM is the quotient
\Theta/G
.
A first step is therefore to show that EM descends to a well-defined quotient operator (possibly set-valued), and to formulate contraction and perturbation results directly in quotient-compatible coordinates.

We adopt a local “slice” strategy: in a neighborhood of interest in
\Theta/G
, select a measurable section
S\subset\Theta
that intersects each orbit exactly once.
This eliminates tangential directions along group orbits and yields locally identifiable coordinates in which linearization and sharp local rates are meaningful.
The quotient/IPM formulation then yields guarantees that are invariant by construction under nonidentifiable directions, and are directly interpretable as distributional convergence.

1.3.
Contributions and main results

We summarize the contributions in terms of the quantitative constants appearing in the theorems.

(1) Quotient IPM formalism and EM well-posedness.

Under (
3
), the quotient IPM (
5
) is well-defined and induces the canonical error criterion (
6
).
Under mild equivariance conditions, EM induces a quotient map
\bar{M}:\Theta/G\to\Theta/G
(possibly set-valued), and retains ascent for
\Phi
and
\Phi_{n}
(Section
5
).
These results formalize the basic requirement that any EM analysis in symmetric models must be orbit-invariant.

(2) Sharp local linear rate for population EM (spectral-radius governed).

Let
T
denote the slice-restricted population EM map near a fixed-point representative
\theta^{\star}\in S
.
Assuming differentiability of
T
at
\theta^{\star}
and
\rho(DT(\theta^{\star}))<1
, we prove that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a neighborhood in which

(8)

\|\theta_{t}-\theta^{\star}\|\;\leq\;C_{\varepsilon}\big(\rho(DT(\theta^{\star}))+\varepsilon\big)^{t}\|\theta_{0}-\theta^{\star}\|,

for all initializations
\theta_{0}
in that neighborhood.
The rate constant
\rho(DT(\theta^{\star}))
is the correct sharp linear factor for smooth fixed-point iterations (Section
6
).

(3) Convergence in an arbitrary IPM.

To translate (
8
) into a distributional statement, we assume a local regularity modulus for the model map
\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}
into
d_{\mathcal{F}}
on the slice basin (e.g., local Lipschitz continuity).
This yields geometric decay of
d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\theta^{\star}})
(and the corresponding quotient distance-to-set) with the same geometric factor as in slice coordinates.
We also provide a distance-to-set formulation that avoids choosing a representative when
\bar{\Theta}/G
is set-valued (Section
7
).

(4) Tight finite-sample bounds for sample EM via perturbed contractions.

On a contraction basin where
T
is
\gamma
-contractive, we treat sample EM as a perturbation
\widehat{T}_{n}
of
T
and show that a uniform operator deviation bound

\sup_{\theta\in B}\|\widehat{T}_{n}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|\leq\Delta_{n}(\delta)\quad\text{with probability at least }1-\delta

implies the
exact
perturbed-contraction bound

(9)

\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\theta^{\star}\|\;\leq\;\gamma^{t}\|\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta^{\star}\|+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\Delta_{n}(\delta),

and therefore an IPM bound of the same form after transfer.
The amplification factor
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
in (
9
) is unavoidable for bounded additive perturbations of contractions and is therefore the correct notion of tightness at this level of generality (Section
8
).

(5) Operator deviation bounds via EM-induced empirical processes (generic chaining).

The deviation term
\Delta_{n}(\delta)
is controlled not by
\mathcal{F}
itself, but by an
EM-induced class

\mathcal{H}
(typically involving gradients and/or Hessians of the EM surrogate over the basin).
We derive high-probability bounds of order

(10)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\mathcal{H}\end{subarray}}\big|(P_{n}-P^{*})h\big|\;\lesssim\;\frac{\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\|H\|_{L_{2}(P^{*})}\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}},

with corresponding bracketing-entropy variants; see
[
10
,
11
]
.
This form is essentially the sharpest available for general function classes, and it clarifies why complexity control is necessary when
\mathcal{F}
(and hence
\mathcal{H}
) is allowed to be arbitrary (Section
8
).

(6) Extensions and limits.

We discuss algorithmic variants (inexact EM, approximate E-steps, stochastic EM, regularization, annealing, multi-start) and show how the quotient contraction framework yields corresponding guarantees.
We also include an impossibility statement formalizing that, absent complexity control (e.g., bounded
\gamma_{2}
or entropy integrals), no uniform finite-sample guarantee in a general IPM can hold.
Where appropriate, we connect to algorithmic stability
[
3
]
and global convergence templates for nonconvex alternating schemes under KŁ regularity
[
2
]
.

1.4.
Organization and notation

Section
3
introduces the model, misspecification target, and IPMs.
Section
4
develops quotient geometry, the quotient IPM, and local slices.
Section
5
establishes quotient well-posedness of EM and ascent properties.
Section
6
proves sharp local linear rates for population EM via linearization on a slice, and Section
7
transfers these rates to general IPMs, including distance-to-set formulations.
Section
8
provides finite-sample guarantees for sample EM via perturbed contractions and generic chaining/entropy bounds for EM-induced classes.
Section
9
treats extensions, and Section
1.3
records lower bounds and asymptotic refinements.

Notation.

We write
\left[\theta\right]
for the orbit of
\theta
,
\Theta/G=\Theta/G
for the quotient, and
S
for a local section.
Constants
c,C
may change from line to line.
All probability statements are with respect to
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P^{*}
unless stated otherwise.
“Sharp” refers to spectral-radius governed local linear rates for smooth fixed-point maps, and “tight” refers to the exact perturbation amplification
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
for bounded additive perturbations of contractions.

2.
Related work

The results in this paper draw on several well-developed literatures: classical EM theory (ascent and convergence),
nonasymptotic analyses that relate sample EM to population EM, misspecification and singularity effects on EM rates,
symmetry/nonidentifiability and orbit-space viewpoints, distributional metrics (in particular IPMs) and their empirical estimation,
and empirical process tools (generic chaining/entropy) that yield sharp uniform deviation bounds.
We review only what is needed to (i) position our contributions precisely and (ii) justify the technical choices made in
Sections
4
–
8
.

2.1.
Classical EM: ascent, fixed points, and convergence

The EM algorithm was introduced by Dempster, Laird, and Rubin as a general procedure for maximum likelihood estimation
with latent or incomplete data, with the fundamental monotonicity property that the observed-data likelihood
does not decrease under exact M-steps
[
4
]
.
Wu established broad convergence guarantees for EM, including conditions under which the likelihood sequence converges
and limit points of the iterates are stationary points of the observed likelihood
[
12
]
.
These classical foundations motivate our use of EM as a majorization–maximization scheme and underwrite the ascent statements
formalized in Section
5
.

2.2.
Modern nonasymptotic EM: population-to-sample perturbation theory

A central theme in contemporary analyses is to treat sample EM as a perturbation of the population EM map:
(i) establish contraction (or more generally stability) of the population operator on a basin, and
(ii) control the deviation between the sample operator and the population operator uniformly on that basin.
Balakrishnan, Wainwright, and Yu developed a systematic version of this program and derived finite-sample guarantees
for EM in canonical latent-variable settings
[
1
]
.
Our finite-sample results in Section
8
adopt this perturbation viewpoint; however, we formulate it on the
quotient state space and in an IPM, which requires quotient-compatible stability notions and distributional transfer moduli.

2.3.
Misspecification and singularity effects on EM rates

Under misspecification, EM ascends the cross-entropy objective (
1
) and converges (when it converges)
to the KL projection set (
2
) rather than to a ground-truth parameter.
Dwivedi et al. established sharp guarantees for EM under misspecified Gaussian mixture models and analyzed how
model mismatch alters contraction behavior and basin geometry
[
5
]
.
Subsequent work studied the impact of singularities and misspecification on EM convergence rates, including regimes
with nonstandard rates driven by local degeneracy of curvature
[
6
]
.
These results motivate two design principles in the present paper:
we state targets relative to the projection set and keep local contraction constants explicit, because both may depend
sensitively on misspecification and local geometry.

2.4.
Symmetry, nonidentifiability, and orbit-space viewpoints

Nonidentifiability induced by group symmetries is ubiquitous in latent-variable models (e.g., label switching in mixtures),
and it implies that the statistically meaningful estimand is an orbit in the quotient
\Theta/G
.
While classical EM convergence statements are often expressed in ambient parameters, symmetry implies that ambient distances
can be ill-posed or uninterpretable: points within an orbit are observationally indistinguishable.
Recent symmetry-aware estimation perspectives emphasize orbit-space formulations and normal-direction geometry;
see, for example,
[
7
]
for a modern orbit-space viewpoint in folded mixture models and related settings.
Our contribution is to incorporate this orbit-space perspective into a general EM perturbation theory:
we formulate EM on the quotient, implement analysis on local slices, and report convergence in quotient/IPM distances
(Sections
4
–
7
).

2.5.
Integral probability metrics and empirical estimation

Integral probability metrics provide a unified framework for distributional discrepancies, generalizing classical metrics
and encompassing many modern choices (e.g., bounded-Lipschitz, Wasserstein-type, and kernel IPMs)
[
8
]
.
The empirical estimation of IPMs and associated concentration phenomena have been characterized in generality,
with rates determined by the complexity of the generating class (and related entropy/chaining functionals)

[
9
]
.
In our setting, IPMs enter in two distinct roles:
(i) as the final error metric for the discrepancy between
P_{\hat{\theta}}
and the projection set, and
(ii) indirectly, through the complexity of the EM-induced function classes that govern sample-to-population deviations
in the EM operator (Section
8
).

2.6.
Empirical process tools: generic chaining and entropy

To obtain sharp uniform deviation bounds over general function classes, we rely on the empirical process machinery
based on generic chaining and entropy.
Talagrand’s monograph provides the canonical development of
\gamma_{2}
-functionals and sharp bounds for suprema
of stochastic processes
[
10
]
.
The monograph of van der Vaart and Wellner develops entropy and bracketing methods and provides the foundational tools
for uniform laws of large numbers and Donsker theory
[
11
]
.
Because we allow an arbitrary IPM and a model-dependent induced class, it is neither possible nor appropriate to assume
a priori VC/RKHS/Lipschitz structure in the core theorems.
Accordingly, we state the operator deviation term in intrinsic complexity form (generic chaining / bracketing entropy),
and provide specialization pathways in later sections.

2.7.
Algorithmic stability as an alternative generalization route

Uniform stability yields generalization guarantees for learning algorithms without explicit entropy control in some regimes.
Bousquet and Elisseeff introduced a general stability framework that bounds the generalization gap by the sensitivity
of the algorithm to the replacement of a single sample
[
3
]
.
In Section
8
we include an optional stability-based complement to the empirical-process route, formulated in
slice/quotient coordinates and transferred to IPM error through the model regularity modulus.

2.8.
Nonconvex alternating schemes and global convergence templates

EM may be viewed as an alternating maximization/minorization method.
General global convergence results for nonconvex and nonsmooth alternating schemes are often obtained under
Kurdyka–Łojasiewicz (KŁ) regularity combined with sufficient-decrease and relative-error conditions.
Bolte, Sabach, and Teboulle developed a widely used template in this direction
[
2
]
.
While our primary contributions are local and quantitative (sharp local linear rates and tight finite-sample perturbation bounds),
Section
9
discusses how quotient formulations interface with KŁ-type global convergence arguments.

2.9.
Summary of distinctions

Relative to
[
4
,
12
,
1
,
5
,
6
]
,
the present work makes three principled changes that are necessary for correctness and interpretability in the regimes we target:

(1)

Orbit-invariant state space:
we formulate EM on
\Theta/G
and analyze it via local slices, ensuring that
all convergence statements are invariant under nonidentifiable directions
[
7
]
.

(2)

Misspecification-first targets:
we state convergence relative to the projection set
\bar{\Theta}
and keep local constants explicit,
in line with the misspecification-sensitive analyses in
[
5
,
6
]
.

(3)

Distributional metrics with sharp complexity control:
we evaluate error in a general IPM
[
8
,
9
]

and express finite-sample operator deviations through intrinsic empirical process complexity
[
10
,
11
]
.

These choices yield a framework in which EM guarantees are orbit-invariant, misspecification-aware, and metrically meaningful at the level of distributions.

3.
Setup and notation

3.1.
Observed and latent spaces, dominating measures

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{X})
be a measurable space for observations and
(\mathcal{Z},\mathscr{Z})
a measurable space for latent variables.
Fix
\sigma
-finite dominating measures
\mu
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{X})
and
\nu
on
(\mathcal{Z},\mathscr{Z})
.
All densities below are understood with respect to
\mu
on
\mathcal{X}
and
\mu\otimes\nu
on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}
.

3.2.
Latent-variable model family

Let
\Theta
be a parameter space (a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or a smooth manifold; we only assume enough structure to state local differentiability and to define a group action in Section
4
).
For each
\theta\in\Theta
, let
p_{\theta}(x,z)
be a nonnegative measurable function on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}
satisfying

\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,(\mu\otimes\nu)(d(x,z))=1.

Define the observed (marginal) density

(11)

p_{\theta}(x):=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz),

and the corresponding probability measure
P_{\theta}
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{X})
by

P_{\theta}(A):=\int_{A}p_{\theta}(x)\,\mu(dx),\qquad A\in\mathscr{X}.

Whenever
p_{\theta}(x)>0
, define the posterior (E-step) kernel

(12)

p_{\theta}(dz\mid x):=\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}\,\nu(dz).

3.3.
Data-generating distribution and misspecification

We observe i.i.d. samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P^{*}
, where
P^{*}
is an arbitrary probability measure on
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{X})
.
No assumption is made that
P^{*}\in\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
(misspecification is allowed).
We assume the following integrability condition to ensure the objective is well-defined.

Assumption 3.1

(Log-likelihood integrability)
.

There exists at least one
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
such that
\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}|\log p_{\theta_{0}}(X)|<\infty
.
Moreover, for all
\theta
in the neighborhoods considered later,
\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}|\log p_{\theta}(X)|<\infty
.

Define the population (cross-entropy) objective

(13)

\Phi(\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X)\big],

and the empirical objective

(14)

\Phi_{n}(\theta):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log p_{\theta}(X_{i}).

The
KL projection set
(population maximizers) is

(15)

\bar{\Theta}:=\argmax_{\theta\in\Theta}\Phi(\theta).

By the identity
\KL(P^{*}\,\|\,P_{\theta})=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[\log p^{*}(X)]-\Phi(\theta)
, we equivalently have

(16)

\bar{\Theta}=\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\KL(P^{*}\,\|\,P_{\theta}).

We do not assume
\bar{\Theta}
is singleton.

3.4.
Integral probability metrics (IPMs)

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a class of measurable functions
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{P}|f|<\infty
for all
P
under consideration (in particular
P^{*}
and the
P_{\theta}
in neighborhoods of interest).
Define the integral probability metric

(17)

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q):=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}f-\mathbb{E}_{Q}f\big|.

No structure is imposed on
\mathcal{F}
beyond measurability and integrability; all rates will therefore be expressed through intrinsic complexity quantities associated with
\mathcal{F}
or with induced classes (Section
8
).

Remark 3.2

(Examples)
.

The definition (
17
) includes, as special cases:
(i) total variation (bounded
\mathcal{F}
), (ii) bounded-Lipschitz metrics (Lipschitz and bounded
\mathcal{F}
),
(iii) Wasserstein-type metrics (Lipschitz
\mathcal{F}
on metric
\mathcal{X}
under suitable moment conditions),
and (iv) maximum mean discrepancy (RKHS unit balls).
For background and empirical estimation theory, see
[
8
,
9
]
.

3.5.
Population EM operator

Define the population EM surrogate (expected complete-data log-likelihood under the current posterior)

(18)

Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,Z)\Big],

whenever the expectation is well-defined.
The (possibly set-valued) population EM map is

(19)

M(\theta)\in\argmax_{\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta).

We will use the term
exact EM
when (
19
) is computed exactly (global maximizer of the surrogate) and otherwise refer to inexact/approximate EM variants in Section
9
.

3.6.
Sample EM operator

Define the sample surrogate

(20)

\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta^{\prime};\theta):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X_{i})}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X_{i},Z),

and the (possibly set-valued) sample EM map

(21)

\widehat{M}_{n}(\theta)\in\argmax_{\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta}\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta^{\prime};\theta).

3.7.
Regularity conventions

The main results are local, and we will explicitly state the regularity assumptions needed in each section.
To avoid repetition, we adopt the following conventions.

•

“Neighborhood” always means a subset of
\Theta
(or of a local slice in Section
4
) on which all involved expectations are finite and the operators are well-defined.

•

Differentiability assumptions are imposed only when needed (e.g., for linearization in Section
6
); otherwise we work with variational and contraction inequalities.

•

When
\argmax
is set-valued, fixed-point and convergence statements are interpreted in the standard set-valued sense (e.g., distance-to-set formulations in Section
7
).

3.8.
Preview: symmetry and quotient formulation

The group action and quotient geometry induced by observational invariance (
3
) are introduced in the next section.
In particular, we will define the quotient parameter space
\Theta/G=\Theta/G
, the orbit notation
\left[\theta\right]
, and the quotient IPM

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\left[\theta^{\prime}\right])=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})
.
All subsequent convergence statements will be formulated either in terms of
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
or in terms of
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot,\bar{\Theta}/G)
.

4.
Quotient geometry and IPM metrics

This section isolates the minimal geometric structure forced by observational nonidentifiability and records
the basic metric objects used throughout the paper.
The key point is that under symmetry-induced nonidentifiability, the parameter
\theta
is not the estimand;
the estimand is the
orbit

\left[\theta\right]
in the quotient
\Theta/G:=\Theta/G
.
Accordingly, all discrepancies are defined either between orbits or between an orbit and an orbit-invariant target set.
We work with an arbitrary IPM
d_{\mathcal{F}}
on distributions (
17
)
[
8
,
9
]
and
transfer it canonically to the quotient.

4.1.
Group actions and the induced equivalence relation

Definition 4.1

(Measurable group action)
.

Let
G
be a group.
A (left) action
G\curvearrowright\Theta
is a map
(g,\theta)\mapsto g\cdot\theta
from
G\times\Theta
to
\Theta

satisfying
e\cdot\theta=\theta
and
(g_{1}g_{2})\cdot\theta=g_{1}\cdot(g_{2}\cdot\theta)
.
When
\Theta
is equipped with a
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{T}
, we say the action is
measurable
if the map

G\times\Theta\to\Theta
is measurable with respect to the product
\sigma
-field.

Definition 4.2

(Orbit equivalence and quotient)
.

Define an equivalence relation
\sim
on
\Theta
by

\theta\sim\theta^{\prime}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists g\in G:\ \theta^{\prime}=g\cdot\theta.

For
\theta\in\Theta
, the equivalence class (orbit) is

\left[\theta\right]:=\{\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta:\theta^{\prime}\sim\theta\}=\{g\cdot\theta:\ g\in G\}.

The quotient set
\Theta/G
is the set of all orbits, and the quotient map is
q:\Theta\to\Theta/G
,
q(\theta)=\left[\theta\right]
.

4.2.
Observed invariance and factorization through the quotient

Let
\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}
denote the model map from parameters to distributions on
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{X})
,
as defined in Section
3
.

Assumption 4.3

(Observed invariance)
.

For all
\theta\in\Theta
and
g\in G
,

(22)

P_{g\cdot\theta}=P_{\theta}.

Remark 4.4

(Equivalent formulation)
.

Assumption
4.3
is equivalent to the statement that the model map
\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}
is constant on
equivalence classes of
\sim
. Hence it factors through the quotient: there exists a well-defined map

\bar{P}:\Theta/G\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
such that
\bar{P}(\left[\theta\right])=P_{\theta}
.

4.3.
Quotient IPM: definition and line-by-line proof of well-definedness

Recall the IPM
d_{\mathcal{F}}
on distributions (
17
).
We now define its quotient analogue.

Proposition 4.5

(Quotient IPM is well-defined and is a pseudometric)
.

Assume Assumption
4.3
.
Define for
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\Theta/G
,

(23)

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime}):=d_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\bar{P}(\xi),\bar{P}(\xi^{\prime})\big),

equivalently
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\left[\theta^{\prime}\right]):=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})
.
Then:

(i)

(
23
) is well-defined (independent of representatives).

(ii)

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
is a pseudometric on
\Theta/G
.

(iii)

If
\mathcal{F}
is
determining
for the range
\{\bar{P}(\xi):\xi\in\Theta/G\}
(i.e.
d_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q)=0\Rightarrow P=Q
for
P,Q
in this range),
then
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})=0
implies
\bar{P}(\xi)=\bar{P}(\xi^{\prime})
; in particular,
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
separates orbits up to equality of induced observed distributions.

Proof.

We give a fully explicit argument.

Step 1 (Representative-independence).

Let
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\Theta/G
and pick arbitrary representatives
\theta\in q^{-1}(\xi)
and
\theta^{\prime}\in q^{-1}(\xi^{\prime})
.
If
\tilde{\theta}\in q^{-1}(\xi)
is another representative, then by Definition
4.2
there exists
g\in G
with

\tilde{\theta}=g\cdot\theta
. By Assumption
4.3
,

P_{\tilde{\theta}}=P_{g\cdot\theta}=P_{\theta}.

Thus for any
\theta^{\prime}\in q^{-1}(\xi^{\prime})
,

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\tilde{\theta}},P_{\theta^{\prime}})=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}}).

The same argument applies if
\theta^{\prime}
is replaced by another representative of
\xi^{\prime}
.
Therefore
d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})
depends only on
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
, proving well-definedness.

Step 2 (Pseudometric properties).

Nonnegativity and symmetry hold because
d_{\mathcal{F}}
is nonnegative and symmetric by definition (
17
).
Also
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi)=d_{\mathcal{F}}(\bar{P}(\xi),\bar{P}(\xi))=0
.
For the triangle inequality, let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\Theta/G
.
Then, using the triangle inequality of
d_{\mathcal{F}}
on distributions,

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{3})=d_{\mathcal{F}}(\bar{P}(\xi_{1}),\bar{P}(\xi_{3}))\leq d_{\mathcal{F}}(\bar{P}(\xi_{1}),\bar{P}(\xi_{2}))+d_{\mathcal{F}}(\bar{P}(\xi_{2}),\bar{P}(\xi_{3}))=\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})+\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{2},\xi_{3}).

Hence
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
is a pseudometric.

Step 3 (Separation on the model range).

If
\mathcal{F}
is determining for the range of
\bar{P}
, then
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})=0
implies

d_{\mathcal{F}}(\bar{P}(\xi),\bar{P}(\xi^{\prime}))=0
, hence
\bar{P}(\xi)=\bar{P}(\xi^{\prime})
by the determining property. ∎

Remark 4.6

(When is
d_{\mathcal{F}}
determining?)
.

Whether
d_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q)=0
implies
P=Q
depends on
\mathcal{F}
.
For example, if
\mathcal{F}
is the unit ball of bounded measurable functions, then
d_{\mathcal{F}}
is total variation and is determining.
If
\mathcal{F}
is too small, distinct distributions may be indistinguishable under
d_{\mathcal{F}}

[
8
,
9
]
.
Our results remain valid in the pseudometric setting; where orbit-identification is required, we state additional
assumptions explicitly (Section
4.7
).

4.4.
Distance to orbit-invariant sets: definition and proof

The misspecified target
\bar{\Theta}
is set-valued in general; thus distance-to-set formulations are necessary.

Definition 4.7

(Orbit-invariant set)
.

A set
A\subseteq\Theta
is
G
-invariant
if
g\cdot A=A
for all
g\in G
.
Equivalently,
A
is a union of orbits: if
\theta\in A
then
\left[\theta\right]\subseteq A
.

Proposition 4.8

(Distance-to-set is well-defined on the quotient)
.

Assume Assumption
4.3
.
Let
A\subseteq\Theta
be
G
-invariant.
Define for
\xi\in\Theta/G
,

(24)

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,A/G):=\inf_{\alpha\in A}\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\left[\alpha\right])=\inf_{\alpha\in A}d_{\mathcal{F}}(\bar{P}(\xi),P_{\alpha}).

Then (
24
) is well-defined (independent of all representatives), and the function

\xi\mapsto\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,A/G)
depends only on the orbit class
\xi
.

Proof.

Fix
\xi\in\Theta/G
and choose any representative
\theta\in q^{-1}(\xi)
.

Step 1 (Representative-independence in the first argument).

If
\tilde{\theta}\in q^{-1}(\xi)
is another representative, then
\tilde{\theta}=g\cdot\theta
for some
g\in G
and

P_{\tilde{\theta}}=P_{\theta}
by Assumption
4.3
.
Hence for any
\alpha\in A
,

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\tilde{\theta}},P_{\alpha})=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\alpha}).

Taking the infimum over
\alpha\in A
shows the value of (
24
) does not depend on the chosen representative of
\xi
.

Step 2 (Representative-independence inside
A
).

If
\alpha\in A
and
\tilde{\alpha}\in\left[\alpha\right]
, then
P_{\tilde{\alpha}}=P_{\alpha}
by Assumption
4.3
,
so
d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\tilde{\alpha}})=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\alpha})
.
Thus taking
\inf_{\alpha\in A}
is the same as taking
\inf_{\left[\alpha\right]\in A/G}
, and the result depends only on
A/G
. ∎

4.5.
The KL projection set is orbit-invariant: detailed proof

Lemma 4.9

(Orbit-invariance of the KL projection set)
.

Assume Assumption
4.3
and Assumption
3.1
.
Let
\bar{\Theta}
be defined by (
15
).
Then
\bar{\Theta}
is
G
-invariant. Consequently,
\bar{\Theta}/G
is well-defined and the quantity

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\bar{\Theta}/G)
is well-defined via Proposition
4.8
.

Proof.

Fix
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
and
g\in G
.

Step 1 (Invariance of the observed density a.e.).

Assumption
4.3
states
P_{g\cdot\theta}=P_{\theta}
as measures on
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{X})
.
Since both are dominated by
\mu
, their Radon–Nikodym derivatives satisfy

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=p_{\theta}(x)\quad\text{for $\mu$-a.e.\ $x$.}

Step 2 (Invariance of the population objective).

By (
13
) and Step 1,

\Phi(g\cdot\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\big[\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X)\big]=\Phi(\theta),

where the expectations are finite by Assumption
3.1
in the neighborhoods of interest.

Step 3 (Invariance of maximizers).

Because
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
, for all
\theta\in\Theta
,

\Phi(\bar{\theta})\geq\Phi(\theta).

By Step 2,
\Phi(g\cdot\bar{\theta})=\Phi(\bar{\theta})
, hence for all
\theta\in\Theta
,

\Phi(g\cdot\bar{\theta})=\Phi(\bar{\theta})\geq\Phi(\theta),

which implies
g\cdot\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
by definition (
15
). ∎

4.6.
Local sections (slices): measurable representatives

To state sharp local rates (Section
6
) we work in identifiable coordinates.
We therefore assume the existence of a local cross-section.

Assumption 4.10

(Local measurable section)
.

There exist a set
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\Theta/G
and a set
S\subseteq\Theta
such that:

(i)

(
Uniqueness
) For every
\xi\in\mathcal{U}
, the intersection
q^{-1}(\xi)\cap S
consists of exactly one point.

(ii)

(
Measurability
) The induced map
\pi:\mathcal{U}\to S
, defined by selecting this unique point, is measurable.

For
\xi\in\mathcal{U}
, write
\theta_{S}:=\pi(\xi)
.

Lemma 4.11

(Slice representative map: uniqueness and consistency)
.

Under Assumption
4.10
, for every
\theta
with
\left[\theta\right]\in\mathcal{U}
there exists a unique

\theta_{S}\in S
such that
\left[\theta_{S}\right]=\left[\theta\right]
, namely
\theta_{S}=\pi(\left[\theta\right])
.
Moreover, if
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
satisfies
\left[\theta^{\prime}\right]=\left[\theta\right]
and
\left[\theta\right]\in\mathcal{U}
, then

\pi(\left[\theta^{\prime}\right])=\pi(\left[\theta\right])
.

Proof.

Fix
\theta
with
\left[\theta\right]\in\mathcal{U}
.

Step 1 (Existence and uniqueness).

By Assumption
4.10
(i), the set
q^{-1}(\left[\theta\right])\cap S
contains exactly one element.
Define
\theta_{S}
to be this element.
Then
\theta_{S}\in S
and
q(\theta_{S})=\left[\theta\right]
, i.e.
\left[\theta_{S}\right]=\left[\theta\right]
.

Step 2 (Consistency across representatives).

If
\left[\theta^{\prime}\right]=\left[\theta\right]
, then
\left[\theta^{\prime}\right]
and
\left[\theta\right]
are identical elements of
\Theta/G
.
Since
\pi
is a function on
\mathcal{U}
,
\pi(\left[\theta^{\prime}\right])=\pi(\left[\theta\right])
. ∎

Remark 4.12

(When do local sections exist?)
.

Assumption
4.10
is purely local and is the minimal structure needed to define identifiable coordinates.
In many smooth settings (e.g. a Lie group acting properly on a smooth manifold), local slices exist by classical slice theorems;
see, e.g., the Palais slice theorem (not required for our arguments, which are conditional on the existence of a section).
We keep the section assumption explicit to avoid committing to topological/differential hypotheses that are unnecessary for the core results.

4.7.
Metric transfer: orbit distances and moduli

To transfer contraction bounds from slice coordinates to distributional discrepancy, we use a local modulus controlling

\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}
into the IPM. The following formulation is designed to be both minimal and explicit.

Definition 4.13

(Orbit distance in parameter space)
.

Assume
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is equipped with a norm
\|\cdot\|
.
Define an orbit pseudometric on
\Theta/G
by

d_{\Theta}(\left[\theta\right],\left[\theta^{\prime}\right]):=\inf_{g\in G}\|\theta-g\cdot\theta^{\prime}\|.

Assumption 4.14

(Two-sided modulus comparison on a local basin)
.

There exist a subset
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\Theta/G
and nondecreasing functions

\underline{\omega},\overline{\omega}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\underline{\omega}(0)=\overline{\omega}(0)=0
such that
for all
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
,

(25)

\underline{\omega}\!\big(d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\big)\;\leq\;\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\;\leq\;\overline{\omega}\!\big(d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\big).

Assume additionally that
\underline{\omega}
is strictly increasing on
[0,r_{0}]
for some
r_{0}>0
(so it admits a well-defined inverse on its range).

Theorem 4.15

(Local topological equivalence and quantitative contraction transfer)
.

Assume Assumption
4.14
. Then:

(i)

(
Local topological equivalence
) The pseudometrics
d_{\Theta}
and
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
generate the same local topology on
\mathcal{U}
.
Concretely, for any
\xi\in\mathcal{U}
and any
r>0
such that
r\leq\underline{\omega}(r_{0})
, the inclusions

B_{d_{\Theta}}\big(\xi,\overline{\omega}^{-1}(r)\big)\subseteq B_{\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\xi,r)\subseteq B_{d_{\Theta}}\big(\xi,\underline{\omega}^{-1}(r)\big)

hold (for any chosen right-inverse
\overline{\omega}^{-1}
and the inverse
\underline{\omega}^{-1}
on
[0,\underline{\omega}(r_{0})]
).

(ii)

(
Contraction transfer
) Let
\Psi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
satisfy

d_{\Theta}(\Psi(\xi),\Psi(\xi^{\prime}))\leq\gamma\,d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\qquad\forall\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}

for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
Then for all
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
with
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq\underline{\omega}(r_{0})
,

(26)

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Psi(\xi),\Psi(\xi^{\prime}))\leq\overline{\omega}\!\Big(\gamma\,\underline{\omega}^{-1}(\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime}))\Big).

In particular, if
\underline{\omega}(r)\geq cr
and
\overline{\omega}(r)\leq Lr
for
r\in[0,r_{0}]
, then

(27)

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Psi(\xi),\Psi(\xi^{\prime}))\leq\frac{L}{c}\,\gamma\,\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\qquad\text{whenever }\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq cr_{0}.

Proof.

(i)
Fix
\xi\in\mathcal{U}
and
r\leq\underline{\omega}(r_{0})
.

Step 1 (Inner inclusion).

If
d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})<\overline{\omega}^{-1}(r)
then by the upper bound in (
25
),

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq\overline{\omega}(d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime}))<\overline{\omega}(\overline{\omega}^{-1}(r))\leq r,

so
\xi^{\prime}\in B_{\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}}(\xi,r)
.

Step 2 (Outer inclusion).

If
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})<r
, then by the lower bound in (
25
),

\underline{\omega}(d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime}))\leq\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})<r.

Since
r\leq\underline{\omega}(r_{0})
and
\underline{\omega}
is strictly increasing on
[0,r_{0}]
, the inverse is well-defined and yields

d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})<\underline{\omega}^{-1}(r)
, so
\xi^{\prime}\in B_{d_{\Theta}}(\xi,\underline{\omega}^{-1}(r))
.

(ii)
Fix
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
with
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq\underline{\omega}(r_{0})
.

Step 1 (Convert
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
to
d_{\Theta}
).

From the lower bound in (
25
),

\underline{\omega}(d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime}))\leq\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime}).

Applying
\underline{\omega}^{-1}
(valid by the assumed range restriction) gives

d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq\underline{\omega}^{-1}(\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})).

Step 2 (Apply contraction in
d_{\Theta}
).

By the hypothesis on
\Psi
,

d_{\Theta}(\Psi(\xi),\Psi(\xi^{\prime}))\leq\gamma\,d_{\Theta}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq\gamma\,\underline{\omega}^{-1}(\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})).

Step 3 (Convert back to
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
).

Using the upper bound in (
25
) for
(\Psi(\xi),\Psi(\xi^{\prime}))
yields (
26
).
If, moreover,
\underline{\omega}(r)\geq cr
and
\overline{\omega}(r)\leq Lr
on
[0,r_{0}]
, then for
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\leq cr_{0}
we have

\underline{\omega}^{-1}(t)\leq t/c
and hence

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Psi(\xi),\Psi(\xi^{\prime}))\leq\overline{\omega}\!\left(\gamma\,\underline{\omega}^{-1}(\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime}))\right)\leq L\gamma\,\frac{\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})}{c},

which is (
27
). ∎

Remark 4.16

(Minimality of the modulus framework)
.

Assumption
4.14
isolates the exact regularity required to translate between orbit distances in parameter space and distributional discrepancies in a general IPM.
A one-sided modulus (upper bound only) suffices for upper bounds on
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
along iterates.
A two-sided modulus is needed only when one wishes to infer orbit-identification or to transfer contraction
backward

from
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
to
d_{\Theta}
.
We keep these requirements explicit to avoid conflating statistical identifiability assumptions with algorithmic stability assumptions.

5.
EM on the quotient: operators, equivariance, and ascent

This section formalizes EM as an operator on the quotient
\Theta/G=\Theta/G
and proves the ascent properties
(population and sample) in a form that is compatible with misspecification and set-valued maximizers.
While ascent is classical
[
4
,
12
]
, we present a formulation that (i) is explicitly orbit-invariant,
(ii) cleanly separates the surrogate improvement from the posterior-KL term, and (iii) supports subsequent contraction
arguments on slices.

5.1.
Well-posedness of the EM surrogate

Recall the population surrogate
Q
and the population objective
\Phi
from
(
18
) and (
13
).
To avoid repeating integrability conditions in every statement, we impose the following local standing assumption.

Assumption 5.1

(Local well-posedness of
Q
and conditional log terms)
.

Let
B\subseteq\Theta
be a neighborhood (later, typically a slice-basin).
Assume:

(i)

For all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
,

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big|\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,Z)\Big|<\infty,

so
Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
is finite.

(ii)

For all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
,

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big|\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z\mid X)\Big|<\infty,

where
p_{\theta^{\prime}}(z\mid x)
is defined by (
12
) whenever
p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)>0
.

Analogous assumptions are imposed on the empirical neighborhood for
\widehat{Q}_{n}
(with
P^{*}
replaced by
P_{n}
).

Remark 5.2
.

Assumption
5.1
is purely local and is standard in EM analyses; it ensures that the
decompositions used below are legitimate (finite expectations and permissible conditioning).
All subsequent results are stated on neighborhoods where Assumption
5.1
holds.

5.2.
Equivariance at the complete-data level

Observed invariance (
22
) alone is sufficient to define quotient IPM errors
(Section
4
), but it does
not
by itself guarantee that the EM surrogate
Q(\cdot;\cdot)

transforms equivariantly under the group action.
To ensure that EM descends to the quotient as an operator, we impose a standard compatibility condition that
captures the usual symmetries in latent-variable models (e.g. label permutations).

Assumption 5.3

(Complete-data equivariance via a latent transport)
.

There exists a family of measurable bijections
\{\tau_{g}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}\}_{g\in G}
such that:

(i)

(
Group property
)
\tau_{e}=\mathrm{id}
and
\tau_{g_{1}g_{2}}=\tau_{g_{1}}\circ\tau_{g_{2}}
.

(ii)

(
Reference-measure invariance
)
\nu\circ\tau_{g}^{-1}=\nu
for all
g\in G
.

(iii)

(
Complete-data equivariance
) For all
g\in G
and
\theta\in\Theta
,

(28)

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)=p_{\theta}\big(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(z)\big)\quad\text{for $(\mu\otimes\nu)$-a.e.\ $(x,z)$.}

Lemma 5.4

(Observed invariance and posterior transport)
.

Assume Assumption
5.3
. Then:

(i)

(
Observed invariance
)
P_{g\cdot\theta}=P_{\theta}
for all
g,\theta
.

(ii)

(
Posterior transport
) For
\mu
-a.e.
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)>0
,

(29)

p_{g\cdot\theta}(\,\cdot\mid x)=(\,\tau_{g}\,)_{\#}\,p_{\theta}(\,\cdot\mid x),

i.e. for any measurable
A\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
,

p_{g\cdot\theta}(A\mid x)=p_{\theta}(\tau_{g}^{-1}(A)\mid x)
.

Proof.

(i)
Fix
g\in G
and
\theta\in\Theta
. By definition (
11
) and (
28
),

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(z))\,\nu(dz).

By the change-of-variables formula for the measure-preserving bijection
\tau_{g}
(Assumption
5.3
(ii)),

\int_{\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(z))\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=p_{\theta}(x),

hence
p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=p_{\theta}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
and thus
P_{g\cdot\theta}=P_{\theta}
.

(ii)
Fix
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)>0
.
For any measurable
A\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
, using (
12
) and (
28
),

p_{g\cdot\theta}(A\mid x)=\int_{A}\frac{p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)}{p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)}\,\nu(dz)=\int_{A}\frac{p_{\theta}(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(z))}{p_{\theta}(x)}\,\nu(dz).

Apply the same measure-preserving change of variables
z=\tau_{g}(z^{\prime})
:

\int_{A}\frac{p_{\theta}(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(z))}{p_{\theta}(x)}\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\tau_{g}^{-1}(A)}\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime})}{p_{\theta}(x)}\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=p_{\theta}(\tau_{g}^{-1}(A)\mid x),

which is exactly (
29
). ∎

5.3.
Equivariance of the EM surrogate and induced quotient maps

We now show that EM descends to an orbit map (generally set-valued) on
\Theta/G
.

Lemma 5.5

(Equivariance of the EM surrogate)
.

Assume Assumption
5.3
and Assumption
5.1
on a neighborhood
B
.
Then for all
g\in G
and all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
,

(30)

Q(g\cdot\theta^{\prime};\,g\cdot\theta)=Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta).

The same statement holds for the sample surrogate
\widehat{Q}_{n}
(with
P^{*}
replaced by
P_{n}
).

Proof.

Fix
g\in G
and
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
. By definition (
18
) and Lemma
5.4
(ii),

Q(g\cdot\theta^{\prime};\,g\cdot\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{g\cdot\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log p_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(X,Z)\Big].

Condition on
X=x
and apply the posterior transport (
29
):
if
Z\sim p_{g\cdot\theta}(\cdot\mid x)
then
Z=\tau_{g}(Z^{\prime})
for
Z^{\prime}\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)
.
Therefore

\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{g\cdot\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\log p_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(x,Z)=\mathbb{E}_{Z^{\prime}\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\log p_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(x,\tau_{g}(Z^{\prime})).

By complete-data equivariance (
28
) applied to
\theta^{\prime}
,

p_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(x,\tau_{g}(z^{\prime}))=p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,z^{\prime})\quad\text{for $(\mu\otimes\nu)$-a.e.\ $(x,z^{\prime})$}.

Hence, for
\mu
-a.e.
x
,

\mathbb{E}_{Z^{\prime}\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\log p_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(x,\tau_{g}(Z^{\prime}))=\mathbb{E}_{Z^{\prime}\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,Z^{\prime}).

Taking expectation over
X\sim P^{*}
yields (
30
). The sample version is identical with
P^{*}
replaced by
P_{n}
. ∎

Theorem 5.6

(Equivariance of EM and induced quotient operator)
.

Assume Assumption
5.3
and Assumption
5.1
on a neighborhood
B
.
Let the population EM correspondence be

M(\theta):=\argmax_{\theta^{\prime}\in B}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta).

Then for all
g\in G
and
\theta\in B
,

(31)

M(g\cdot\theta)=g\cdot M(\theta):=\{g\cdot\eta:\ \eta\in M(\theta)\}.

Consequently, the
quotient EM correspondence

\bar{M}:\ q(B)\rightrightarrows\Theta/G,\qquad\bar{M}(\left[\theta\right]):=\{\,\left[\eta\right]:\ \eta\in M(\theta)\,\},

is well-defined (independent of the representative
\theta
of the orbit).
The same conclusions hold for the sample EM correspondence
\widehat{M}_{n}
defined via
\widehat{Q}_{n}
.

Proof.

We proceed in explicit steps.

Step 1 (Argmax invariance under equivariant reparametrization).

Fix
g\in G
and
\theta\in B
.
By Lemma
5.5
, for every
\eta\in B
,

Q(\eta;\theta)=Q(g\cdot\eta;\,g\cdot\theta).

Thus the ordering of surrogate values over
\eta\in B
is preserved under
\eta\mapsto g\cdot\eta
when
\theta
is replaced by
g\cdot\theta
.

Step 2 (Show
g\cdot M(\theta)\subseteq M(g\cdot\theta)
).

Let
\eta^{\star}\in M(\theta)
.
Then for all
\eta\in B
,

Q(\eta^{\star};\theta)\geq Q(\eta;\theta).

Using Lemma
5.5
on both sides,

Q(g\cdot\eta^{\star};\,g\cdot\theta)\geq Q(g\cdot\eta;\,g\cdot\theta)\quad\forall\eta\in B.

Since
g
acts bijectively on
B
(as a restriction of a group action),
\{g\cdot\eta:\eta\in B\}=B
.
Therefore
g\cdot\eta^{\star}
maximizes
\eta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\eta^{\prime};\,g\cdot\theta)
over
B
, i.e.
g\cdot\eta^{\star}\in M(g\cdot\theta)
.
This proves
g\cdot M(\theta)\subseteq M(g\cdot\theta)
.

Step 3 (Reverse inclusion).

Apply Step 2 with
g^{-1}
in place of
g
:

g^{-1}\cdot M(g\cdot\theta)\subseteq M(\theta).

Applying
g
to both sides yields
M(g\cdot\theta)\subseteq g\cdot M(\theta)
.

Step 4 (Conclude (
31
) and well-definedness on the quotient).

Steps 2–3 give equality (
31
).
Now let
\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in B
satisfy
\left[\theta\right]=\left[\tilde{\theta}\right]
, so
\tilde{\theta}=g\cdot\theta
for some
g
.
Then (
31
) gives
M(\tilde{\theta})=g\cdot M(\theta)
, so the set of orbits

\{\left[\eta\right]:\eta\in M(\tilde{\theta})\}
equals
\{\left[\eta\right]:\eta\in M(\theta)\}
.
Hence
\bar{M}
is independent of the representative and is well-defined.
The sample statement is identical with
Q
replaced by
\widehat{Q}_{n}
. ∎

Remark 5.7

(Why set-valued on the quotient?)
.

Even when
Q(\cdot;\theta)
admits multiple maximizers, Theorem
5.6
ensures that the maximizer set is transported by the group action.
Thus the induced quotient map is naturally a correspondence.
Later, when proving contraction and sharp linear rates, we work on a slice where a single-valued representative map can be imposed.

5.4.
Ascent and the posterior-KL decomposition (population)

We now present the canonical EM ascent inequality in a form that is especially convenient under misspecification:
it expresses the improvement in
\Phi
as the surrogate improvement
minus
a nonnegative posterior KL term.
This is the standard EM mechanism
[
4
,
12
]
, stated here with explicit conditioning and integrability.

Lemma 5.8

(Exact identity underlying EM ascent)
.

Assume Assumption
5.1
on a neighborhood
B
.
Fix
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
. Then

(32)

\Phi(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi(\theta)=\Big(Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-Q(\theta;\theta)\Big)-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X)\big)\Big].

In particular,

(33)

\Phi(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi(\theta)\ \geq\ Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-Q(\theta;\theta).

Proof.

Fix
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
.

Step 1 (A conditional decomposition of
\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)
).

For
\mu
-a.e.
x
with
p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)>0
and
p_{\theta}(x)>0
, Bayes’ rule gives

\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)=\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,z)-\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(z\mid x)\quad\text{for $\nu$-a.e.\ $z$}.

Taking expectation in
z
under the conditional law
p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)
yields

(34)

\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,Z)\big]-\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z\mid x)\big],

since
\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)
is constant in
z
.
Assumption
5.1
ensures both conditional expectations are finite and hence (
34
) is legitimate.

Step 2 (Take expectation over
X\sim P^{*}
).

Taking
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}
of (
34
) and using the definition of
Q
gives

(35)

\Phi(\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z\mid X)\Big].

Setting
\theta^{\prime}=\theta
yields similarly

(36)

\Phi(\theta)=Q(\theta;\theta)-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log p_{\theta}(Z\mid X)\Big].

Step 3 (Subtract and identify the posterior KL term).

Subtract (
36
) from (
35
):

\Phi(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi(\theta)=\big(Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-Q(\theta;\theta)\big)-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log\frac{p_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z\mid X)}{p_{\theta}(Z\mid X)}\Big].

By the definition of conditional KL divergence,

\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X)\big)=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log\frac{p_{\theta}(Z\mid X)}{p_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z\mid X)}.

Thus the last term equals
-\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[\KL(\,p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X)\,)]
,
which gives (
32
).

Step 4 (Conclude the ascent inequality).

Since KL divergence is nonnegative, the second term on the right-hand side of (
32
) is
\leq 0
,
yielding (
33
). ∎

Lemma 5.9

(Population EM identity (fully expanded))
.

Assume Assumption
5.1
on a neighborhood
B
.
In particular, assume the following hold for every
\theta\in B
:

(i)

Dominated model and measurability:
there exist
\sigma
-finite measures
\mu
on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})

and
\nu
on
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
such that
P_{\theta}^{XZ}\ll\mu\otimes\nu
with a jointly measurable density

(x,z)\mapsto p_{\theta}(x,z)
.
Define the marginal density
p_{\theta}(x):=\int p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)
, assumed
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

(ii)

Posterior existence (a version):
for
P^{*}
-a.e.
x
, if
p_{\theta}(x)>0
then the conditional density

p_{\theta}(z\mid x):=\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}\quad\text{(as a density w.r.t.\ $\nu$)}

is well-defined
\nu
-a.e. in
z
and the mapping
(x,z)\mapsto p_{\theta}(z\mid x)
is jointly measurable on

\{(x,z):p_{\theta}(x)>0\}
.

(iii)

Support compatibility on
B
:
for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
,

p_{\theta}(x)>0\ \text{and}\ x\ \text{in the $P^{*}$-support}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)>0\quad(P^{*}\text{-a.s.}),

so that all log terms below are
P^{*}
-a.s. finite (no
\log 0
on sets of positive
P^{*}
-mass).

(iv)

Integrability/Fubini:
for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
,

\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\!\big[\,|\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,Z)|+|\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)|\,\big]<\infty,\qquad\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\big[\,|\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X)|+|\log p_{\theta}(X)|\,\big]<\infty.

Then for any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
,

(37)

\Phi(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi(\theta)=\big(Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-Q(\theta;\theta)\big)+\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\Big[\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X)\big)\Big].

In particular,

\Phi(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi(\theta)\ \geq\ Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-Q(\theta;\theta),

since the KL term is nonnegative.

Proof.

Fix
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
.

Step 0: Define the full-measure set on which all objects are finite and defined.

By the support-compatibility and posterior-existence parts of Assumption
5.1
,
there exists a measurable set
A\in\mathcal{F}
with
P^{*}(A)=1
such that for every
x\in A
:

•

p_{\theta}(x)>0
and
p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)>0
;

•

z\mapsto p_{\theta}(z\mid x)
and
z\mapsto p_{\theta^{\prime}}(z\mid x)
are well-defined
\nu
-a.e.;

•

all logarithms below are finite
p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)
-a.s. (in particular, the Radon–Nikodym
ratio
p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)/p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid x)
is defined
p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)
-a.s.).

Step 1: Expand the conditional KL at a fixed
x\in A
.

Fix
x\in A
.
By definition of conditional KL (with respect to the base measure
\nu
),

\displaystyle\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid x)\big)

\displaystyle:=\int\log\!\Big(\frac{p_{\theta}(z\mid x)}{p_{\theta^{\prime}}(z\mid x)}\Big)\,p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\,\nu(dz)

(38)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\!\Big[\log p_{\theta}(Z\mid x)-\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(Z\mid x)\Big].

Step 2: Substitute Bayes’ rule into the integrand.

For
x\in A
and
\nu
-a.e.
z
,

\log p_{\theta}(z\mid x)=\log p_{\theta}(x,z)-\log p_{\theta}(x),\qquad\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(z\mid x)=\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,z)-\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x).

Substitute these identities into (
38
):

\displaystyle\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid x)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\Big[\big(\log p_{\theta}(x,Z)-\log p_{\theta}(x)\big)-\big(\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,Z)-\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\big)\Big]

(39)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\log p_{\theta}(x,Z)-\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,Z)\big]+\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)-\log p_{\theta}(x).

The last equality uses that
\log p_{\theta}(x)
and
\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)
are constants in
z
.

Step 3: Rearrange to obtain the pointwise decomposition.

Rearrange (
39
) to isolate the observed-data log-likelihood difference:

(40)

\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)-\log p_{\theta}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,Z)-\log p_{\theta}(x,Z)\big]+\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid x)\big),\qquad x\in A.

Step 4: Integrate (
40
) over
X\sim P^{*}
.

Take expectation of both sides of (
40
) under
X\sim P^{*}
.
Since
P^{*}(A)=1
, the equality remains valid after taking expectation.
By the integrability assumption in Assumption
5.1
, we may apply Fubini/Tonelli to exchange

\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}
and the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\big[\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X)-\log p_{\theta}(X)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\big[\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,Z)-\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\big]

(41)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\Big[\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X)\big)\Big].

Step 5: Identify each term with
\Phi
and
Q
.

By definition,

\Phi(\vartheta)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[\log p_{\vartheta}(X)]\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\big[\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X)-\log p_{\theta}(X)\big]=\Phi(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi(\theta).

Also, by definition of
Q
,

Q(\vartheta;\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}[\log p_{\vartheta}(X,Z)].

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\big[\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,Z)-\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\big]

\displaystyle=Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-Q(\theta;\theta).

Substituting these identifications into (
41
) yields (
37
).

Step 6: Inequality.

Since
\KL(\cdot\|\cdot)\geq 0
pointwise, its expectation is
\geq 0
, giving

\Phi(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi(\theta)\geq Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-Q(\theta;\theta)
.
∎

Remark 5.10

(Equivalent rearrangement)
.

Equation (
37
) is equivalent to

\Phi(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi(\theta)-\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\!\Big[\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X)\big)\Big]=Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-Q(\theta;\theta).

The two forms differ only by moving the KL term across the equality.

Theorem 5.11

(Population EM ascent)
.

Assume Assumption
5.1
on a neighborhood
B
, and define the EM correspondence

M(\theta)=\argmax_{\vartheta\in B}Q(\vartheta;\theta).

Then for every
\theta\in B
and every selection
\theta^{+}\in M(\theta)
,

(42)

\Phi(\theta^{+})\ \geq\ \Phi(\theta).

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in B
and pick any
\theta^{+}\in M(\theta)
.

Step 1: M-step improvement in
Q
.

Since
\theta^{+}
maximizes
\vartheta\mapsto Q(\vartheta;\theta)
over
\vartheta\in B
,
we have

(43)

Q(\theta^{+};\theta)\ \geq\ Q(\theta;\theta).

Step 2: Convert
Q
-improvement into
\Phi
-improvement via the EM identity.

Apply Lemma
5.9
with
\theta^{\prime}=\theta^{+}
:

(44)

\displaystyle\Phi(\theta^{+})-\Phi(\theta)

\displaystyle=\big(Q(\theta^{+};\theta)-Q(\theta;\theta)\big)+\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\Big[\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{+}}(\cdot\mid X)\big)\Big].

Step 3: Nonnegativity of both terms.

By (
43
), the first term on the right-hand side of (
44
) is
\geq 0
.
The second term is
\geq 0
because KL divergence is nonnegative pointwise.
Therefore
\Phi(\theta^{+})-\Phi(\theta)\geq 0
, i.e. (
42
).
∎

Lemma 5.12

(Sample analogue of the EM identity (fully expanded))
.

Assume the empirical analogue of Assumption
5.1
on a neighborhood
B
.
Then for
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
,

(45)

\Phi_{n}(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi_{n}(\theta)=\big(\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta;\theta)\big)+\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}\Big[\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X)\big)\Big],

and in particular

\Phi_{n}(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi_{n}(\theta)\ \geq\ \widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta;\theta).

Proof.

Write the empirical measure as
P_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
.

Step 1: Express
\Phi_{n}
and
\widehat{Q}_{n}
as finite averages.

By definition,

\Phi_{n}(\vartheta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}[\log p_{\vartheta}(X)]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log p_{\vartheta}(X_{i}),

and

\widehat{Q}_{n}(\vartheta;\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}[\log p_{\vartheta}(X,Z)]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X_{i})}[\log p_{\vartheta}(X_{i},Z)].

Step 2: Apply the pointwise identity at each datum
x=X_{i}
.

Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
By the empirical well-posedness assumptions, the pointwise identity (
40
) holds at
x=X_{i}
:

(46)

\displaystyle\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X_{i})-\log p_{\theta}(X_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X_{i})}\big[\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X_{i},Z)-\log p_{\theta}(X_{i},Z)\big]+\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X_{i})\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X_{i})\big).

Step 3: Average (
46
) over
i=1,\dots,n
.

Sum (
46
) over
i
and divide by
n
:

(47)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X_{i})-\log p_{\theta}(X_{i})\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X_{i})}\big[\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X_{i},Z)-\log p_{\theta}(X_{i},Z)\big]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X_{i})\,\|\,p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X_{i})\big).

Step 4: Identify the averages with
\Phi_{n}
,
\widehat{Q}_{n}
, and
\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}[\mathrm{KL}]
.

The left-hand side of (
47
) is
\Phi_{n}(\theta^{\prime})-\Phi_{n}(\theta)
.
The first average on the right-hand side equals
\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta^{\prime};\theta)-\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta;\theta)
by Step 1.
The last average is exactly
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}\big[\KL(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\|p_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid X))\big]
.
Substituting these identifications yields (
45
).
The inequality follows from nonnegativity of KL.
∎

Theorem 5.13

(Sample EM ascent)
.

Let
\widehat{M}_{n}(\theta)=\argmax_{\vartheta\in B}\widehat{Q}_{n}(\vartheta;\theta)
.
Under the empirical well-posedness conditions, for every
\theta\in B
and every selection
\theta^{+}\in\widehat{M}_{n}(\theta)
,

\Phi_{n}(\theta^{+})\ \geq\ \Phi_{n}(\theta).

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in B
and choose
\theta^{+}\in\widehat{M}_{n}(\theta)
.

Step 1: M-step improvement in the empirical
Q
-function.

By definition of
\argmax
,

\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta^{+};\theta)\ \geq\ \widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta;\theta).

Step 2: Apply the sample EM identity.

Apply Lemma
5.12
with
\theta^{\prime}=\theta^{+}
:

\Phi_{n}(\theta^{+})-\Phi_{n}(\theta)=\big(\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta^{+};\theta)-\widehat{Q}_{n}(\theta;\theta)\big)+\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}\Big[\KL\!\big(p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{+}}(\cdot\mid X)\big)\Big].

Step 3: Conclude by nonnegativity.

Both terms on the right-hand side are nonnegative, hence
\Phi_{n}(\theta^{+})\geq\Phi_{n}(\theta)
.
∎

Corollary 5.14

(Ascent is orbit-invariant)
.

Assume Assumption
4.3
and the hypotheses of Theorem
5.11
.
If
\theta^{+}\in M(\theta)
, then for every
g\in G
,

\Phi(g\cdot\theta^{+})\geq\Phi(g\cdot\theta)\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi(\theta^{+})\geq\Phi(\theta).

The analogous statement holds for sample EM and
\Phi_{n}
.

Proof.

Fix
g\in G
.

Step 1:
\Phi
depends only on the observed-data marginal.

By definition,

\Phi(\vartheta)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[\log p_{\vartheta}(X)],

where
p_{\vartheta}
is a version of the
\mu
-density of the observed marginal
P_{\vartheta}^{X}
.
Thus if two parameters
\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}
induce the same observed marginal, then

p_{\vartheta_{1}}(x)=p_{\vartheta_{2}}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
, and therefore

\log p_{\vartheta_{1}}(X)=\log p_{\vartheta_{2}}(X)

P^{*}
-a.s. (under the positivity conditions),
implying
\Phi(\vartheta_{1})=\Phi(\vartheta_{2})
.

Step 2: Apply observed-model invariance along the orbit.

Assumption
4.3
states that
P_{g\cdot\vartheta}^{X}=P_{\vartheta}^{X}
for all
\vartheta
and
g
.
Applying Step 1 gives

\Phi(g\cdot\vartheta)=\Phi(\vartheta)\qquad\text{for all }\vartheta\in B.

Step 3: Combine with population ascent.

By Theorem
5.11
,
\Phi(\theta^{+})\geq\Phi(\theta)
.
Using Step 2 with
\vartheta=\theta^{+}
and
\vartheta=\theta
,

\Phi(g\cdot\theta^{+})=\Phi(\theta^{+})\ \geq\ \Phi(\theta)=\Phi(g\cdot\theta).

This proves the corollary for the population objective. The sample statement follows identically since

\Phi_{n}(\vartheta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log p_{\vartheta}(X_{i})
depends only on the observed marginal as well.
∎

6.
Population theory I: fixed points, stationarity, and linearization

This section develops the population EM dynamics in a neighborhood of the misspecified target set.
There are three technical goals:

(1)

to relate the
misspecified projection set

\bar{\Theta}
to the
fixed points
of EM (set-valued in general);

(2)

to record a rigorous
stationarity principle
(fixed points are stationary for the population objective) under minimal differentiation/interchange conditions, in the spirit of
[
12
]
;

(3)

to obtain an explicit
linearization
of the (slice-restricted) EM map via the implicit function theorem,
leading to sharp local linear rates governed by the spectral radius, aligning with the fixed-point viewpoint in modern nonasymptotic EM analyses
[
1
]
.

Throughout, we work on a neighborhood where the basic well-posedness conditions of Section
5
hold.

6.1.
Projection points are fixed points (set-valued formulation)

Recall
\bar{\Theta}=\argmax_{\theta}\Phi(\theta)
and the EM correspondence

M(\theta)=\argmax_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
(restricted to a neighborhood when needed).
Because
M(\theta)
may be set-valued, we define fixed points in the natural correspondence sense.

Definition 6.1

(Fixed points of a correspondence)
.

Let
\Gamma:\Theta\rightrightarrows\Theta
be a correspondence.
A point
\theta^{\star}\in\Theta
is a
fixed point
of
\Gamma
if
\theta^{\star}\in\Gamma(\theta^{\star})
.
We write
\mathrm{Fix}(\Gamma):=\{\theta:\theta\in\Gamma(\theta)\}
.

Theorem 6.2

(Projection points are EM fixed points)
.

Assume Assumption
5.1
holds on a neighborhood
B\subseteq\Theta
containing
\bar{\Theta}\cap B
,
and define
M(\theta):=\argmax_{\theta^{\prime}\in B}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
.
Then

\bar{\Theta}\cap B\ \subseteq\ \mathrm{Fix}(M).

That is, every maximizer of
\Phi
in
B
is a fixed point of the population EM correspondence restricted to
B
.

Proof.

Fix any
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}\cap B
.

Step 1 (Choose an EM update at
\bar{\theta}
).

Let
\theta^{+}\in M(\bar{\theta})
be an arbitrary selection, so by definition

(48)

Q(\theta^{+};\bar{\theta})\ \geq\ Q(\eta;\bar{\theta})\qquad\forall\eta\in B.

Step 2 (Use population ascent at
\bar{\theta}
).

By Theorem
5.11
(applied on
B
),

(49)

\Phi(\theta^{+})\ \geq\ \Phi(\bar{\theta}).

Step 3 (Maximality of
\bar{\theta}
forces equality).

Since
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
is a maximizer of
\Phi
over
\Theta
, in particular over
B
,
we also have
\Phi(\theta^{+})\leq\Phi(\bar{\theta})
.
Combined with (
49
), this yields

(50)

\Phi(\theta^{+})=\Phi(\bar{\theta}).

Step 4 (Invoke the EM identity to force surrogate equality).

Apply the exact EM identity of Lemma
5.8
with
\theta=\bar{\theta}
and
\theta^{\prime}=\theta^{+}
:

\Phi(\theta^{+})-\Phi(\bar{\theta})=\big(Q(\theta^{+};\bar{\theta})-Q(\bar{\theta};\bar{\theta})\big)-\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}\Big[\KL\big(p_{\bar{\theta}}(\cdot\mid X)\,\|\,p_{\theta^{+}}(\cdot\mid X)\big)\Big].

By (
50
), the left-hand side is
0
.
The KL term is nonnegative. Therefore we must have

(51)

Q(\theta^{+};\bar{\theta})-Q(\bar{\theta};\bar{\theta})\ \leq\ 0.

Step 5 (But
\theta^{+}
is a surrogate maximizer, so the surrogate gap is also nonnegative).

From (
48
) with
\eta=\bar{\theta}
,

(52)

Q(\theta^{+};\bar{\theta})-Q(\bar{\theta};\bar{\theta})\ \geq\ 0.

Combining (
51
) and (
52
) yields

(53)

Q(\theta^{+};\bar{\theta})=Q(\bar{\theta};\bar{\theta}).

Step 6 (Conclude
\bar{\theta}
is itself a surrogate maximizer).

Since
\theta^{+}
is a maximizer of
\eta\mapsto Q(\eta;\bar{\theta})
over
B
and achieves value

Q(\theta^{+};\bar{\theta})
, equality (
53
) shows that
\bar{\theta}
attains the same maximal value.
Hence
\bar{\theta}\in\argmax_{\eta\in B}Q(\eta;\bar{\theta})=M(\bar{\theta})
, i.e.
\bar{\theta}\in\mathrm{Fix}(M)
.

Because
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}\cap B
was arbitrary, the inclusion follows. ∎

Remark 6.3

(Quotient interpretation)
.

Under observed invariance,
\bar{\Theta}
is
G
-invariant (Lemma
4.9
) and
M
descends to a quotient correspondence (Theorem
5.6
).
Thus Theorem
6.2
implies
\bar{\Theta}/G\subseteq\mathrm{Fix}(\bar{M})
in the natural quotient sense.

6.2.
Stationarity: Fisher identity and fixed points

The next results make precise the relationship between the gradients of the observed objective
\Phi
and the surrogate
Q
.
This is classical (see
[
12
]
) but we record it with explicit differentiability/interchange assumptions.

Assumption 6.4

(Differentiation under the integral (local))
.

Let
B\subseteq\Theta
be a neighborhood.
Assume:

(i)

For
\mu
-a.e.
x
, the map
\theta\mapsto p_{\theta}(x)
is differentiable on
B
and
\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)
is measurable in
x
.

(ii)

There exists an integrable envelope
G(x)
such that for all
\theta\in B
,

\left\|\frac{\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)}{p_{\theta}(x)}\right\|\mathbf{1}\{p_{\theta}(x)>0\}\ \leq\ G(x)\quad\text{for $\mu$-a.e.\ }x,

and
\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[G(X)]<\infty
.

(iii)

For
\mu
-a.e.
x
, the map
\theta\mapsto p_{\theta}(x,z)
is differentiable for
\nu
-a.e.
z
and the derivative is dominated so that
differentiation may be interchanged with
\int_{\mathcal{Z}}(\cdot)\nu(dz)
in (
11
).

Lemma 6.5

(Fisher identity (score as posterior expectation))
.

Assume Assumption
6.4
holds on
B
.
Then for every
\theta\in B
and for
\mu
-a.e.
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)>0
,

(54)

\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,Z)\big].

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in B
and
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)>0
.

Step 1 (Differentiate the marginal density).

By (
11
) and Assumption
6.4
(iii), we may differentiate under the integral:

\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)=\nabla_{\theta}\int_{\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz).

Step 2 (Rewrite in terms of complete-data score).

For
\nu
-a.e.
z
with
p_{\theta}(x,z)>0
,

\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x,z)=p_{\theta}(x,z)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)
.
Hence

\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz).

Step 3 (Normalize by
p_{\theta}(x)
to obtain a posterior expectation).

Divide both sides by
p_{\theta}(x)>0
:

\frac{\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)}{p_{\theta}(x)}=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}\,\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\,p_{\theta}(dz\mid x),

using the definition of the posterior kernel (
12
).
Finally,
\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x)=\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)/p_{\theta}(x)
, giving (
54
). ∎

Lemma 6.6

(Gradient of
\Phi
equals surrogate gradient on the diagonal)
.

Assume Assumptions
5.1
and
6.4
on
B
.
Assume moreover that
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
is differentiable and the derivative can be interchanged with the outer expectation.
Then for every
\theta\in B
,

(55)

\nabla\Phi(\theta)=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)\big|_{\theta^{\prime}=\theta}.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in B
.

Step 1 (Differentiate
\Phi
).

By definition (
13
) and Assumption
6.4
(ii), differentiation under
\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}
is justified:

\nabla\Phi(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(X)\big].

Step 2 (Apply Fisher identity inside the expectation).

By Lemma
6.5
, for
P^{*}
-a.e.
X
with
p_{\theta}(X)>0
,

\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(X)=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\big].

Insert into Step 1 and use Fubini/Tonelli justified by the well-posedness assumptions:

(56)

\nabla\Phi(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\big]\Big].

Step 3 (Differentiate the surrogate with respect to
\theta^{\prime}
and evaluate at
\theta^{\prime}=\theta
).

By definition (
18
),

Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,Z)\Big].

Differentiate with respect to
\theta^{\prime}
and then set
\theta^{\prime}=\theta
(per the stated interchange assumption):

\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)\big|_{\theta^{\prime}=\theta}=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,Z)\big|_{\theta^{\prime}=\theta}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X)}\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\Big].

Comparing with (
56
) yields (
55
). ∎

Theorem 6.7

(Fixed points are stationary for
\Phi
)
.

Assume Assumptions
5.1
and
6.4
on a neighborhood
B
,
and assume
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
is differentiable for
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B
with the interchange conditions
in Lemma
6.6
.
If
\theta^{\star}\in B
is a fixed point of EM in the sense that
\theta^{\star}\in M(\theta^{\star})

and the maximization is unconstrained (interior point of
B
), then

\nabla\Phi(\theta^{\star})=0.

Proof.

Fix
\theta^{\star}\in B
with
\theta^{\star}\in M(\theta^{\star})
.

Step 1 (First-order optimality for the surrogate).

Because
\theta^{\star}
maximizes
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta^{\star})
over a neighborhood and is an interior maximizer,
the first-order necessary condition gives

\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta^{\star})\big|_{\theta^{\prime}=\theta^{\star}}=0.

Step 2 (Identify this gradient with
\nabla\Phi(\theta^{\star})
).

By Lemma
6.6
applied at
\theta=\theta^{\star}
,

\nabla\Phi(\theta^{\star})=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta^{\star})\big|_{\theta^{\prime}=\theta^{\star}}.

Combining with Step 1 yields
\nabla\Phi(\theta^{\star})=0
. ∎

Remark 6.8

(Constrained/slice stationarity)
.

When the M-step is performed on a slice
S
(or under constraints), the corresponding conclusion is stationarity
with respect to feasible directions (i.e. vanishing of the projected gradient on the tangent space).
We state and use the unconstrained form for clarity; the slice-restricted form follows by replacing ordinary gradients
with gradients restricted to local coordinates on the slice.

6.3.
Slice-restricted EM map and differentiability

To obtain sharp local rates, we work with a single-valued slice map.
Fix a local section
S
as in Assumption
4.10
and let
B_{S}\subseteq S
be a neighborhood
containing a representative
\theta^{\star}\in S
of an orbit in
\bar{\Theta}/G
.

Assumption 6.9

(Strong concavity in the M-step variable)
.

On
B_{S}
, for each fixed
\theta\in B_{S}
the function

\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
is twice continuously differentiable and
strictly
(equivalently, strongly)
concave on
B_{S}
, with Hessian
\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
negative definite for all

\theta^{\prime},\theta\in B_{S}
.

Under Assumption
6.9
, the M-step on the slice is unique and defines a map.

Definition 6.10

(Slice EM map)
.

Assume Assumption
6.9
.
Define the slice EM map
T:B_{S}\to B_{S}
by

(57)

T(\theta):=\argmax_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta),

which is single-valued by strict concavity.

Lemma 6.11

(First-order characterization of
T
)
.

Assume Assumption
6.9
.
Then
\theta^{\prime}=T(\theta)
if and only if

(58)

\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in B_{S}
.

Step 1 (Necessity).

If
\theta^{\prime}=T(\theta)
is the unique maximizer of the concave
C^{1}
function
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)

on an open neighborhood, then
\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)=0
at the maximizer (first-order condition).

Step 2 (Sufficiency).

Conversely, if
\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)=0
and the Hessian is negative definite, then
\theta^{\prime}
is a strict local maximizer.
By strict concavity on
B_{S}
, there can be only one maximizer, hence
\theta^{\prime}=T(\theta)
. ∎

We now linearize
T
.

Theorem 6.12

(Differentiability and Jacobian formula for
T
)
.

Assume Assumption
6.9
and additionally that
Q
is
C^{2}
jointly in
(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
on
B_{S}\times B_{S}
.
Fix
\theta^{\star}\in B_{S}
and suppose
\theta^{\star}
is a fixed point:
T(\theta^{\star})=\theta^{\star}
.
Then
T
is continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
\theta^{\star}
and its Jacobian satisfies

(59)

DT(\theta)=-\Big(\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta);\theta)\Big)^{-1}\Big(\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q(T(\theta);\theta)\Big),

for
\theta
near
\theta^{\star}
.
In particular,

(60)

DT(\theta^{\star})=-\Big(\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\star};\theta^{\star})\Big)^{-1}\Big(\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q(\theta^{\star};\theta^{\star})\Big).

Proof.

Standing conventions.

We work in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Gradients are column vectors. For a
C^{2}
scalar function
g(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
,

\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}g
denotes the gradient w.r.t.
\theta^{\prime}
and
\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}g
the Hessian w.r.t.
\theta^{\prime}
.
All Jacobians are taken in the usual Fréchet sense, and since we are in finite dimension, this coincides with entrywise differentiation.

Assumption
6.9
unpacked.

There exists
\mu>0
such that for every fixed
\theta\in B_{S}
, the map

\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)

is
\mu
-
strongly concave
on
B_{S}
, meaning that for all
\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{2}\in B_{S}
,

(61)

Q(\theta^{\prime}_{2};\theta)\leq Q(\theta^{\prime}_{1};\theta)+\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}_{1};\theta),\,\theta^{\prime}_{2}-\theta^{\prime}_{1}\rangle-\frac{\mu}{2}\|\theta^{\prime}_{2}-\theta^{\prime}_{1}\|^{2}.

Since
Q
is
C^{2}
in
\theta^{\prime}
(indeed jointly
C^{2}
), (
61
) is equivalent to the pointwise Hessian bound

(62)
