\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)\ \preceq\ -\mu I_{d}\qquad\text{for all }(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in B_{S}\times B_{S},

i.e. the Hessian is negative definite with eigenvalues bounded above by
-\mu
.

A calculus lemma we will use (stationary point of strongly concave function).

Lemma 6.13
.

Fix
\theta\in B_{S}
and define
g(\theta^{\prime}):=Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
.
Assume
g
is differentiable and
\mu
-strongly concave on
B_{S}
.
If
\bar{\theta}^{\prime}\in B_{S}
satisfies
\nabla g(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})=0
, then
\bar{\theta}^{\prime}
is the unique global maximizer of
g
on
B_{S}
.

Proof.

Take any
\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}
and apply (
61
) with
\theta^{\prime}_{1}=\bar{\theta}^{\prime}
and
\theta^{\prime}_{2}=\theta^{\prime}
:

g(\theta^{\prime})\leq g(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})+\langle\nabla g(\bar{\theta}^{\prime}),\,\theta^{\prime}-\bar{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle-\frac{\mu}{2}\|\theta^{\prime}-\bar{\theta}^{\prime}\|^{2}=g(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})-\frac{\mu}{2}\|\theta^{\prime}-\bar{\theta}^{\prime}\|^{2}\leq g(\bar{\theta}^{\prime}).

If
\theta^{\prime}\neq\bar{\theta}^{\prime}
, then
\|\theta^{\prime}-\bar{\theta}^{\prime}\|^{2}>0
so the inequality is strict, hence
\bar{\theta}^{\prime}
is the unique maximizer.
∎

Define the implicit map.

Define
F:B_{S}\times B_{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

F(\theta^{\prime},\theta):=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta).

Since
Q
is
C^{2}
jointly in
(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
, each component of
F
is
C^{1}
jointly, hence
F
is
C^{1}
as a map

B_{S}\times B_{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Step 1 (Implicit equation defining
T
).

By definition, for each
\theta\in B_{S}
,
T(\theta)
is the (unique) maximizer of
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
over
B_{S}
.
By Lemma
6.11
(first-order optimality for the slice maximizer, valid under our hypotheses and the fact that we will work locally around

\theta^{\star}
in the interior of
B_{S}
), we have the stationarity condition

\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta);\theta)=0,

i.e.

(63)

F(T(\theta),\theta)=0\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in B_{S}.

In particular, since
T(\theta^{\star})=\theta^{\star}
,

F(\theta^{\star},\theta^{\star})=F(T(\theta^{\star}),\theta^{\star})=0.

Step 2 (Nonsingularity of the partial Jacobian in
\theta^{\prime}
).

We compute the Jacobian of
F
with respect to its first argument.
Because
F(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
and
Q
is
C^{2}
jointly,
the derivative of
F
in the
\theta^{\prime}
direction is the Hessian:

(64)

D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta).

By (
62
), for every
(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in B_{S}\times B_{S}
the symmetric matrix

\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
is negative definite. In particular it is invertible.
(Indeed, if
Hv=0
for a negative definite symmetric matrix
H
, then
0=v^{\top}Hv<0
for
v\neq 0
, a contradiction; hence
\ker(H)=\{0\}
.)
Thus
D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta^{\star},\theta^{\star})
is invertible.

Step 3 (Apply the implicit function theorem, and identify the resulting solution with
T
).

(a) Implicit function theorem (statement).

Since
F
is
C^{1}
and
F(\theta^{\star},\theta^{\star})=0
with
D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta^{\star},\theta^{\star})
invertible,
the (classical) implicit function theorem yields:
there exist neighborhoods
U
and
V
of
\theta^{\star}
(with
U\times V\subseteq B_{S}\times B_{S}
) and a unique
C^{1}
map

\widetilde{T}:V\to U

such that

(65)

F(\widetilde{T}(\theta),\theta)=0\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in V,

and moreover any
(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in U\times V
solving
F(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=0
must satisfy
\theta^{\prime}=\widetilde{T}(\theta)
.

(b)
\widetilde{T}
coincides with
T
on
V
.

Fix
\theta\in V
. By (
65
),
\widetilde{T}(\theta)
is a stationary point of
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
.
By Lemma
6.13
(applied to
g(\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
), this stationary point is the unique global maximizer.
But
T(\theta)
is
defined
to be that unique maximizer. Hence

\widetilde{T}(\theta)=T(\theta)\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in V.

Therefore
T
is
C^{1}
on
V
.

(c) Continuous differentiability.

The IFT already gives that
\widetilde{T}
is
C^{1}
, hence
DT
is continuous on
V
(since
T=\widetilde{T}
on
V
).

Step 4 (Differentiate the implicit equation and derive the Jacobian formula).

On the neighborhood
V
we have the identity
F(T(\theta),\theta)\equiv 0
.
Differentiate this identity with respect to
\theta
.

To make the chain rule completely explicit, write
F=(F_{1},\dots,F_{d})^{\top}
and
T=(T_{1},\dots,T_{d})^{\top}
.
For each
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
define the scalar function

G_{i}(\theta):=F_{i}(T(\theta),\theta).

Then
G_{i}(\theta)\equiv 0
on
V
. Fix
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
and differentiate
G_{i}
with respect to
\theta_{j}
:

0=\frac{\partial G_{i}}{\partial\theta_{j}}(\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial\theta^{\prime}_{k}}(T(\theta),\theta)\,\frac{\partial T_{k}}{\partial\theta_{j}}(\theta)+\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial\theta_{j}}(T(\theta),\theta).

In matrix form, letting
D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(T(\theta),\theta)
denote the
d\times d
Jacobian of
F
in its first argument and

D_{\theta}F(T(\theta),\theta)
the
d\times d
Jacobian in its second argument, the above identities for all
i,j
are exactly

(66)

D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(T(\theta),\theta)\,DT(\theta)+D_{\theta}F(T(\theta),\theta)=0.

By Step 2,
D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(T(\theta),\theta)
is invertible for
\theta
near
\theta^{\star}
(in fact on
V
),
so we can solve (
66
) for
DT(\theta)
:

(67)

DT(\theta)=-\Big(D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(T(\theta),\theta)\Big)^{-1}\,D_{\theta}F(T(\theta),\theta).

Finally, identify the Jacobians of
F
with Hessians of
Q
.
From
F(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
and joint
C^{2}
regularity,

D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta),\qquad D_{\theta}F(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta),

where
\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q
denotes the matrix of mixed second derivatives

\big[\partial^{2}Q/\partial\theta^{\prime}_{i}\,\partial\theta_{j}\big]_{i,j}
.
Substituting into (
67
) yields (
59
). Evaluating at
\theta=\theta^{\star}
and using
T(\theta^{\star})=\theta^{\star}

gives (
60
). ∎

6.4.
Sharp local linear rate via spectral radius

The Jacobian formula (
60
) yields an exact linearization of the EM dynamics on the slice.
A general fact in smooth fixed-point theory is that the best achievable local linear rate is governed by the spectral radius
of the Jacobian at the fixed point.

Theorem 6.14

(Sharp local linear rate (spectral-radius governed))
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
6.12
.
Let
A:=DT(\theta^{\star})
and assume
\rho(A)<1
.
Then for every
\varepsilon>0
there exist a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a neighborhood
U_{\varepsilon}\subseteq B_{S}
of
\theta^{\star}
,
and a constant
C_{\varepsilon}<\infty
such that for all
\theta_{0}\in U_{\varepsilon}
, the iterates
\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
satisfy

(68)

\|\theta_{t}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\big(\rho(A)+\varepsilon\big)^{t}\|\theta_{0}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon},\qquad t=0,1,2,\dots.

Consequently, by norm equivalence, the same statement holds in the Euclidean norm with a different constant.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
.

Preliminaries (induced operator norms).

Given any norm
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define the induced operator norm

\|M\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}:=\sup_{v\neq 0}\frac{\|Mv\|_{\varepsilon}}{\|v\|_{\varepsilon}}.

Then for all matrices
M,N
and vectors
v
:

(i)

\|Mv\|_{\varepsilon}\leq\|M\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\,\|v\|_{\varepsilon}
(by definition of the supremum),

(ii)

\|MN\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq\|M\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\|N\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}
(submultiplicativity: apply (i) twice and take sup).

Step 1 (Construct an adapted norm with
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
close to
\rho(A)
).

We prove the following lemma and then apply it with
\eta=\varepsilon/2
.

Lemma 6.15

(Adapted norm lemma)
.

For any
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and any
\eta>0
, there exists a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\eta}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that its induced operator norm satisfies

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op},\eta}\leq\rho(A)+\eta.

Proof.

It is convenient to work over
\mathbb{C}^{d}
and then restrict to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
at the end.
By Schur triangularization, there exists a unitary
U\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
such that

U^{\ast}AU=T,

where
T
is upper triangular and its diagonal entries are the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d}
of
A
(counted with algebraic multiplicity).
Write
T=\Lambda+N
where
\Lambda=\diag(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d})
and
N
is strictly upper triangular (i.e.
N_{ii}=0
and
N_{ij}=0
for
i\geq j
).

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
and define the diagonal scaling

D_{\delta}:=\diag(1,\delta,\delta^{2},\dots,\delta^{d-1}).

Consider the similar matrix

T_{\delta}:=D_{\delta}^{-1}TD_{\delta}=D_{\delta}^{-1}\Lambda D_{\delta}+D_{\delta}^{-1}ND_{\delta}=\Lambda+N_{\delta},

since
\Lambda
commutes with
D_{\delta}
. For
i<j
,

(N_{\delta})_{ij}=(D_{\delta}^{-1}ND_{\delta})_{ij}=\delta^{\,j-i}N_{ij},

so every strictly upper-triangular entry is multiplied by a factor
\delta^{j-i}
.

Now use the
\ell_{\infty}
norm on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
:
\|x\|_{\infty}=\max_{i}|x_{i}|
, whose induced operator norm is

\|M\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}|M_{ij}|.

Then

\|T_{\delta}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\Lambda\|_{\infty}+\|N_{\delta}\|_{\infty}=\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}|+\|N_{\delta}\|_{\infty}=\rho(A)+\|N_{\delta}\|_{\infty}.

Moreover,

\|N_{\delta}\|_{\infty}=\max_{i}\sum_{j>i}|(N_{\delta})_{ij}|=\max_{i}\sum_{j>i}\delta^{\,j-i}|N_{ij}|\;\xrightarrow[\delta\downarrow 0]{}\;0,

by dominated convergence for a finite sum. Hence choose
\delta
small enough that
\|N_{\delta}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
.
Then
\|T_{\delta}\|_{\infty}\leq\rho(A)+\eta
.

Define a norm on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
by

\|v\|_{\eta}:=\|D_{\delta}^{-1}U^{\ast}v\|_{\infty}.

This is a norm because
v\mapsto D_{\delta}^{-1}U^{\ast}v
is a linear isomorphism and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
is a norm.
Its induced operator norm satisfies, for
v\neq 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\|Av\|_{\eta}}{\|v\|_{\eta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|D_{\delta}^{-1}U^{\ast}Av\|_{\infty}}{\|D_{\delta}^{-1}U^{\ast}v\|_{\infty}}=\frac{\|D_{\delta}^{-1}U^{\ast}AUD_{\delta}w\|_{\infty}}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\quad\text{where }w:=D_{\delta}^{-1}U^{\ast}v\neq 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\|T_{\delta}w\|_{\infty}}{\|w\|_{\infty}}\leq\|T_{\delta}\|_{\infty}.

Taking the supremum over
v\neq 0
yields
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op},\eta}\leq\|T_{\delta}\|_{\infty}\leq\rho(A)+\eta
.
Finally, restrict
\|\cdot\|_{\eta}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}
to obtain a real norm with the same bound. ∎

Apply Lemma
6.15
with
\eta=\varepsilon/2
. This yields a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
such that

(69)

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq\rho(A)+\varepsilon/2.

Step 2 (Continuity of
DT
gives a uniform derivative bound on a convex neighborhood).

By Theorem
6.12
,
T
is
C^{1}
on some open neighborhood
V
of
\theta^{\star}
(hence
DT:V\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is continuous).
By continuity of
DT
at
\theta^{\star}
, there exists an open neighborhood
U_{0}\subseteq V
of
\theta^{\star}
such that

(70)

\|DT(\theta)-A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq\varepsilon/2\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in U_{0}.

Since
U_{0}\cap B_{S}
is a neighborhood of
\theta^{\star}
(in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
), choose
r_{\varepsilon}>0
such that the
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
-ball

U_{\varepsilon}:=\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ \|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}<r_{\varepsilon}\}

satisfies
U_{\varepsilon}\subseteq U_{0}\cap B_{S}
. In particular,
U_{\varepsilon}
is open and convex.

For any
\theta\in U_{\varepsilon}
, the triangle inequality and (
69
)–(
70
) yield

\|DT(\theta)\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}+\|DT(\theta)-A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq(\rho(A)+\varepsilon/2)+\varepsilon/2=:\gamma_{\varepsilon},

so

(71)

\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\rho(A)+\varepsilon.

Since
\rho(A)<1
, taking
\varepsilon>0
small ensures
\gamma_{\varepsilon}<1
.

Step 3 (Integral mean-value identity and contraction on
U_{\varepsilon}
).

We use the following standard calculus lemma, proved for completeness.

Lemma 6.16

(Integral mean-value identity)
.

Let
f:W\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be
C^{1}
on an open convex set
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then for any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in W
,

f(\theta)-f(\theta^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{1}Df\big(\theta^{\prime}+t(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\big)\,(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\,dt.

Proof.

Define
g:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
g(t)=f(\theta^{\prime}+t(\theta-\theta^{\prime}))
.
Because
W
is convex,
\theta^{\prime}+t(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\in W
for all
t\in[0,1]
, and since
f
is
C^{1}
,
g
is
C^{1}
.
By the chain rule,

g^{\prime}(t)=Df\big(\theta^{\prime}+t(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\big)\,(\theta-\theta^{\prime}).

By the fundamental theorem of calculus (applied componentwise),

f(\theta)-f(\theta^{\prime})=g(1)-g(0)=\int_{0}^{1}g^{\prime}(t)\,dt=\int_{0}^{1}Df\big(\theta^{\prime}+t(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\big)\,(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\,dt.\qed

Apply Lemma
6.16
with
f=T
and
W=U_{\varepsilon}
(convex). For
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in U_{\varepsilon}
,

T(\theta)-T(\theta^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{1}DT\big(\theta^{\prime}+t(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\big)(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\,dt.

Taking
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
norms and using the induced norm bound,

\displaystyle\|T(\theta)-T(\theta^{\prime})\|_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\Big\|DT\big(\theta^{\prime}+t(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\big)(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\Big\|_{\varepsilon}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\Big\|DT\big(\theta^{\prime}+t(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\big)\Big\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\,dt\ \|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\gamma_{\varepsilon}\,dt\ \|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\varepsilon}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\varepsilon},

where we used (
71
) and that
\theta^{\prime}+t(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\in U_{\varepsilon}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Thus
T
is a contraction on
U_{\varepsilon}
in
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
.

Step 4 (Forward invariance and geometric decay to the fixed point).

Since
T(\theta^{\star})=\theta^{\star}
, the contraction bound with
\theta^{\prime}=\theta^{\star}
gives, for any
\theta\in U_{\varepsilon}
,

(72)

\|T(\theta)-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}=\|T(\theta)-T(\theta^{\star})\|_{\varepsilon}\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}.

In particular, if
\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}<r_{\varepsilon}
, then

\|T(\theta)-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}r_{\varepsilon}<r_{\varepsilon}\qquad(\text{since }\gamma_{\varepsilon}<1),

so
T(\theta)\in U_{\varepsilon}
. Hence
U_{\varepsilon}
is forward-invariant:
T(U_{\varepsilon})\subseteq U_{\varepsilon}
.

Now let
\theta_{0}\in U_{\varepsilon}
and define
\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
. By invariance,
\theta_{t}\in U_{\varepsilon}
for all
t
,
so applying (
72
) at
\theta=\theta_{t}
yields

\|\theta_{t+1}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}\|\theta_{t}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}.

By induction on
t
(using repeatedly the previous inequality),

\|\theta_{t}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{t}\,\|\theta_{0}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}=(\rho(A)+\varepsilon)^{t}\,\|\theta_{0}-\theta^{\star}\|_{\varepsilon}.

This is (
68
) with
C_{\varepsilon}=1
.

Step 5 (Norm equivalence to return to Euclidean norm).

We record the equivalence-of-norms lemma with proof.

Lemma 6.17

(Equivalence of norms)
.

Let
\|\cdot\|_{a}
and
\|\cdot\|_{b}
be norms on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then there exist constants
0<m\leq M<\infty
such that

m\|v\|_{b}\leq\|v\|_{a}\leq M\|v\|_{b}\qquad\text{for all }v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Proof.

Let
S_{b}:=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ \|v\|_{b}=1\}
. In finite dimensions,
S_{b}
is compact.
The map
v\mapsto\|v\|_{a}
is continuous, hence attains its minimum and maximum on
S_{b}
:

m:=\min_{v\in S_{b}}\|v\|_{a},\qquad M:=\max_{v\in S_{b}}\|v\|_{a}.

We have
M<\infty
by compactness/continuity. Also
m>0
because
\|v\|_{a}>0
for
v\neq 0
and
0\notin S_{b}
.
For any
v\neq 0
, write
v=\|v\|_{b}\cdot(v/\|v\|_{b})
with
v/\|v\|_{b}\in S_{b}
. By homogeneity,

\|v\|_{a}=\|v\|_{b}\cdot\Big\|\frac{v}{\|v\|_{b}}\Big\|_{a}\in[m\|v\|_{b},\;M\|v\|_{b}].

The case
v=0
is trivial. ∎

Apply Lemma
6.17
with
\|\cdot\|_{a}=\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
and
\|\cdot\|_{b}=\|\cdot\|_{2}
to obtain constants

0<m_{\varepsilon}\leq M_{\varepsilon}<\infty
with

m_{\varepsilon}\|v\|_{2}\leq\|v\|_{\varepsilon}\leq M_{\varepsilon}\|v\|_{2}
.
Combining with (
68
) yields an Euclidean-norm version with multiplicative constant
M_{\varepsilon}/m_{\varepsilon}
.
∎

Remark 6.18

(Where “sharpness” enters)
.

The proof above is purely dynamical: for
C^{1}
fixed-point iterations,
\rho(DT(\theta^{\star}))
is the intrinsic local linear rate.
No improvement is possible without additional structure beyond smoothness and fixed-point stability.
In EM, the Jacobian (
60
) makes this rate explicit in terms of second derivatives of the surrogate.

7.
Population theory II: contraction to sets in general IPMs

Section
6
provided a sharp local linear rate for the slice-restricted population EM map

\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
in identifiable coordinates, governed by
\rho(DT(\theta^{\star}))
.
This section converts that local dynamical statement into a
statistically meaningful
convergence guarantee:
convergence (i) on the quotient, (ii) to the misspecified target set
\bar{\Theta}/G
, and (iii) measured in a general IPM.

There are two logical steps:

(1)

establish a contraction statement to the target
set
on a slice (because under misspecification the target is typically set-valued);

(2)

transfer that statement from slice coordinates to distributional discrepancy via a one-sided modulus for the model map

\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}
into
d_{\mathcal{F}}
, and then to the quotient via the quotient IPM
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
.

We keep the assumptions explicit; they are minimal in the sense that no statement in a general IPM can be sharper
without further model regularity.

7.1.
Distance-to-set dynamics on a slice

Fix a local section
S
and a neighborhood
B_{S}\subseteq S
.
Define the slice target set

(73)

\bar{\Theta}_{S}:=\bar{\Theta}\cap B_{S}.

(If
\bar{\Theta}
is
G
-invariant, then
\bar{\Theta}_{S}
contains at most one representative per orbit in
B_{S}
,
but it may still be set-valued if multiple orbits of maximizers intersect
B_{S}
.)

Definition 7.1

(Distance to a set in slice norm)
.

Let
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\|\cdot\|)
be the ambient normed space containing
B_{S}
.
For
\theta\in B_{S}
, define

\mathrm{dist}_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta,\bar{\Theta}_{S}):=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|.

We now state a contraction-to-set property that is implied by the sharp local rate around each fixed point
when the target set is finite or forms a locally stable manifold; we keep it as an explicit assumption because
in full generality the geometry of
\bar{\Theta}
can be complicated under misspecification
[
6
]
.

Assumption 7.2

(Uniform local contraction to the slice target set)
.

There exist a neighborhood
B_{S}\subseteq S
, a constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
, and a (possibly smaller) target subset

\bar{\Theta}_{S}\subseteq B_{S}
such that for every
\theta\in B_{S}
,

(74)

\mathrm{dist}_{\|\cdot\|}\big(T(\theta),\bar{\Theta}_{S}\big)\ \leq\ \gamma\,\mathrm{dist}_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta,\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

Remark 7.3

(When does Assumption
7.2
hold?)
.

If
\bar{\Theta}_{S}=\{\theta^{\star}\}
is a singleton and
\rho(DT(\theta^{\star}))<1
, then
(
74
) holds locally by Theorem
6.14
.
More generally, if
\bar{\Theta}_{S}
is a finite set of isolated stable fixed points with disjoint basins inside
B_{S}
,
then (
74
) holds on each basin and hence on their union (with
\gamma
the maximum contraction factor).
Set-valued projection sets with nontrivial geometry can require additional regularity to ensure a uniform (
74
);
we keep this explicit to avoid implicitly imposing hidden identifiability assumptions.

Theorem 7.4

(Geometric convergence to the slice target set)
.

Assume Assumption
7.2
.
Then for all
\theta_{0}\in B_{S}
and iterates
\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
,

(75)

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\ \leq\ \gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S}),\qquad t=0,1,2,\dots.

Proof.

Definitions used in this proof.

For a nonempty set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define the distance-to-set function

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta,C)\ :=\ \inf_{\vartheta\in C}\|\theta-\vartheta\|.

Assumption
7.2
states that there exists
\gamma\in[0,1)
such that for all
\theta\in B_{S}
,

(76)

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(T(\theta),\bar{\Theta}_{S})\ \leq\ \gamma\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta,\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

We prove (
75
) by a fully explicit induction.

Step 1 (Base case,
t=0
).

By the convention
\gamma^{0}=1
, the right-hand side of (
75
) at
t=0
is

\gamma^{0}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})=\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S}),

which equals the left-hand side. Hence (
75
) holds with equality for
t=0
.

Step 2 (Inductive step).

Fix
t\geq 0
and assume as the induction hypothesis that

(77)

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\ \leq\ \gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

We must prove

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{t+1},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\ \leq\ \gamma^{t+1}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

By definition of the iteration,
\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
, so

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{t+1},\bar{\Theta}_{S})=\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(T(\theta_{t}),\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

Now apply the contraction-to-set inequality (
76
) with
\theta=\theta_{t}
(note
\theta_{t}\in B_{S}
since
T:B_{S}\to B_{S}
):

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(T(\theta_{t}),\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\gamma\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

Finally, apply the induction hypothesis (
77
) to bound the right-hand side:

\gamma\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\gamma\,\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})=\gamma^{t+1}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S}),

where we used the exponent law
\gamma\gamma^{t}=\gamma^{t+1}
.
Combining the displayed inequalities proves the inductive step.

Step 3 (Conclusion).

By the principle of mathematical induction, (
75
) holds for all integers
t\geq 0
.
∎

7.2.
Transfer from slice norm to IPM discrepancy

To obtain distributional convergence, we require regularity of the model map
\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}
into
d_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Because we only need an upper bound, a one-sided modulus is sufficient.

Assumption 7.5

(One-sided IPM modulus on the slice basin)
.

There exists a nondecreasing function
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(0)=0
such that
for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}
,

(78)

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})\ \leq\ \omega_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\big).

Remark 7.6
.

Assumption
7.5
is a local continuity/Lipschitz condition of the model map into the chosen IPM.
In smooth parametric models and strong IPMs (e.g. total variation under sufficient regularity), one often has

\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r)\lesssim r
locally.
For weaker IPMs,
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
may be sublinear or depend on moments.
We do not specialize because
\mathcal{F}
is arbitrary.

Lemma 7.7

(IPM distance-to-set controlled by parameter distance-to-set)
.

Assume Assumption
7.5
.
Then for all
\theta\in B_{S}
,

(79)

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}})\ \leq\ \omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\Big(\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta,\bar{\Theta}_{S})\Big).

Proof.

Definitions used.

For a nonempty set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a norm
\|\cdot\|
, the distance-to-set is

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta,C):=\inf_{\vartheta\in C}\|\theta-\vartheta\|.

The integral probability metric (IPM) generated by
\mathcal{F}
is

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P,Q):=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{Q}[f]\Big|

(whenever the expectations are well-defined), and Assumption
7.5
provides a nondecreasing modulus

\omega_{\mathcal{F}}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}
,

(80)

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|).

(If your Assumption
7.5
includes right-continuity of
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
, then we use it below.
If not, see the remark at the end of the proof.)

Fix
\theta\in B_{S}
and let

r:=\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta,\bar{\Theta}_{S})=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|.

Step 1 (Existence of
\epsilon
-minimizers for an infimum).

We claim that for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\bar{\theta}_{\epsilon}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}
such that

(81)

\|\theta-\bar{\theta}_{\epsilon}\|\leq r+\epsilon.

Indeed, by definition of
r
as the infimum of the set
\{\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|:\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}\}
,
the statement (
81
) is exactly the standard property of infima:
if no such
\bar{\theta}_{\epsilon}
existed, then
\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|>r+\epsilon
for all
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}
,
which would imply
\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|\geq r+\epsilon
, contradicting the definition of
r
.

Step 2 (Apply the modulus inequality).

For each
\epsilon>0
, apply (
80
) with
\theta^{\prime}=\bar{\theta}_{\epsilon}
:

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}_{\epsilon}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\|\theta-\bar{\theta}_{\epsilon}\|\big).

Using (
81
) and monotonicity (nondecreasingness) of
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
,

(82)

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}_{\epsilon}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r+\epsilon).

Step 3 (Take the infimum over
\bar{\theta}
and then let
\epsilon\downarrow 0
).

Since
\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}_{\epsilon}})
for every
\epsilon>0
,
combining with (
82
) yields

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r+\epsilon)\qquad\text{for all }\epsilon>0.

Now take
\epsilon\downarrow 0
. If
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
is right-continuous, then

\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r+\epsilon)\downarrow\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r)
, and we obtain (
79
).

Remark (if right-continuity was not assumed).

If
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
is only assumed nondecreasing, then the above argument yields

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq\inf_{\epsilon>0}\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r+\epsilon)=:\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r+),

where
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r+)
denotes the right-limit envelope.
Equivalently, you may replace
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
everywhere by its right-continuous modification

\widetilde{\omega}_{\mathcal{F}}(r):=\inf_{\epsilon>0}\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r+\epsilon)
, which is nondecreasing and right-continuous and
satisfies the same modulus inequality (
80
).
∎

Theorem 7.8

(Population EM convergence in a general IPM on the slice)
.

Assume Assumptions
7.2
and
7.5
.
Then for all
\theta_{0}\in B_{S}
and
\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
,

(83)

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\ \leq\ \omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\Big(\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\Big).

In particular, if
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r)\leq L_{\mathcal{F}}r
on
[0,r_{0}]
and

\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq r_{0}\qquad\text{(e.g.\ it suffices that }\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq r_{0}\text{)},

then

(84)

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\ \leq\ L_{\mathcal{F}}\,\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

Proof.

Fix
\theta_{0}\in B_{S}
and define the iterates
\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
for
t\geq 0
.

Step 1 (Geometric contraction in parameter distance-to-set).

By Assumption
7.2
,
T
is a contraction
to the set

\bar{\Theta}_{S}
:
there exists
\gamma\in[0,1)
such that for all
\theta\in B_{S}
,

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(T(\theta),\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\gamma\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta,\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

Applying Theorem
7.4
(proved by induction from the preceding inequality), we obtain for every
t\geq 0
,

(85)

\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

Step 2 (Convert parameter distance-to-set into IPM distance-to-set).

Apply Lemma
7.7
with
\theta=\theta_{t}
:

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\Big(\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\Big).

Now substitute the bound (
85
). Since
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
is nondecreasing,

\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\Big(\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\Big)\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\Big(\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\Big),

which proves (
83
).

Step 3 (Lipschitz specialization).

Assume
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r)\leq L_{\mathcal{F}}r
for
r\in[0,r_{0}]
and that

\gamma^{t}\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq r_{0}
. Then the argument of
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
in
(
83
) lies in
[0,r_{0}]
, so

\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\Big(\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\Big)\leq L_{\mathcal{F}}\,\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S}),

which yields (
84
). ∎

7.3.
Quotient formulation: convergence to
\bar{\Theta}/G
in the quotient IPM

We now rewrite the slice bound in an explicitly quotient form.
Recall
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\left[\theta^{\prime}\right])=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})
and

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\bar{\Theta}/G)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}})

(Section
4
).

Assumption 7.9

(Slice is representative for the quotient basin)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\Theta/G
be a quotient neighborhood and let
S
be a section as in Assumption
4.10
.
Assume
B_{S}=\pi(\mathcal{U})\subseteq S
is the set of slice representatives of
\mathcal{U}
, and that
the population quotient EM correspondence admits a single-valued slice representative map
T:B_{S}\to B_{S}

(constructed by selecting the slice representative of an EM update).

Theorem 7.10

(Population EM convergence on the quotient in
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
)
.

Assume Assumptions
7.9
,
7.2
, and
7.5
.
Let
\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
and define
\theta_{0}=\pi(\xi_{0})\in B_{S}
and
\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
.
Set
\xi_{t}:=\left[\theta_{t}\right]\in\mathcal{U}
.
Then

(86)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{t},\left[\bar{\theta}\right])=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\ \leq\ \omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\Big(\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\Big).

In particular, if
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r)\leq L_{\mathcal{F}}r
locally (on a neighborhood containing the arguments above),
then the quotient distance-to-set decays geometrically with factor
\gamma
.

Proof.

Definitions and basic facts used.

(i)

The quotient point corresponding to
\theta
is its orbit
\left[\theta\right]:=\{g\cdot\theta:\ g\in G\}
.

(ii)

The slice representative map
\pi:\mathcal{U}\to B_{S}
satisfies: for every
\xi\in\mathcal{U}
,
\pi(\xi)\in\xi\cap B_{S}
,
and this intersection is a singleton; equivalently,

(87)

\forall\xi\in\mathcal{U},\qquad\xi\cap B_{S}=\{\pi(\xi)\}.

(iii)

The induced quotient IPM
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
is defined on
\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{U}
by

(88)

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime}):=d_{\mathcal{F}}\!\big(P_{\pi(\xi)},P_{\pi(\xi^{\prime})}\big).

(iv)

The quotient distance-to-set is

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\bar{\Theta}/G):=\inf_{\zeta\in\bar{\Theta}/G}\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\zeta),

where
\bar{\Theta}/G:=\{\left[\bar{\theta}\right]:\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}\}
.

(v)

(
Model invariance along orbits
) For any
g\in G
and any
\theta
, the induced distribution is constant on the orbit:

(89)

P_{g\cdot\theta}=P_{\theta}.

This is exactly the condition that makes
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
well-defined on orbits; it is the structural input behind the quotient construction.

Fix
\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
and define
\theta_{0}=\pi(\xi_{0})\in B_{S}
and
\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
.
By Assumption
7.9
, the iterates satisfy
\theta_{t}\in B_{S}
for all
t
and their orbits satisfy
\xi_{t}:=\left[\theta_{t}\right]\in\mathcal{U}

for all
t
(so
\pi(\xi_{t})
is well-defined).

Step 1 (Identify
\pi(\xi_{t})
with
\theta_{t}
).

Since
\xi_{t}=\left[\theta_{t}\right]
and
\theta_{t}\in B_{S}
, we have
\theta_{t}\in\xi_{t}\cap B_{S}
.
By the uniqueness property (
87
),
\xi_{t}\cap B_{S}=\{\pi(\xi_{t})\}
, hence

(90)

\pi(\xi_{t})=\theta_{t}.

Step 2 (Expand the quotient distance-to-set as an infimum over
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
).

By definition of
\dist_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\bar{\Theta}/G
,

(91)

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\zeta\in\bar{\Theta}/G}\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{t},\zeta)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\xi_{t},\left[\bar{\theta}\right]\big).

(This is simply rewriting the infimum over the set of orbits
\{\left[\bar{\theta}\right]:\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}\}
as an infimum over representatives.)

Step 3 (Relate
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{t},\left[\bar{\theta}\right])
to
d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})
).

Fix
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
.
By (
88
) and (
90
),

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\xi_{t},\left[\bar{\theta}\right]\big)=d_{\mathcal{F}}\!\big(P_{\pi(\xi_{t})},P_{\pi(\left[\bar{\theta}\right])}\big)=d_{\mathcal{F}}\!\big(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\pi(\left[\bar{\theta}\right])}\big).

Now note that
\pi(\left[\bar{\theta}\right])\in\left[\bar{\theta}\right]
, so there exists some
g\in G
with
\pi(\left[\bar{\theta}\right])=g\cdot\bar{\theta}
.
By orbit-invariance (
89
),
P_{\pi(\left[\bar{\theta}\right])}=P_{g\cdot\bar{\theta}}=P_{\bar{\theta}}
.
Therefore

(92)

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\xi_{t},\left[\bar{\theta}\right]\big)=d_{\mathcal{F}}\!\big(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}}\big).

Substituting (
92
) into (
91
) yields the claimed identities

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{t},\left[\bar{\theta}\right])=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}}).

Step 4 (Reduce the infimum to slice representatives in
\bar{\Theta}_{S}
).

We claim

(93)

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}}),\qquad\bar{\Theta}_{S}:=\bar{\Theta}\cap B_{S}.

The “
\leq
” direction is immediate since
\bar{\Theta}_{S}\subseteq\bar{\Theta}
.
For “
\geq
”: fix any
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
such that its orbit lies in
\mathcal{U}
(these are exactly the orbits relevant
to
\bar{\Theta}/G
as a subset of
\mathcal{U}
). By Lemma
4.9
(the
G
-invariance of
\bar{\Theta}
) and the slice property,
the slice representative
\bar{\theta}_{S}:=\pi(\left[\bar{\theta}\right])
lies in
\bar{\Theta}\cap B_{S}=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
and belongs to the orbit of
\bar{\theta}
.
Hence by (
89
),
P_{\bar{\theta}_{S}}=P_{\bar{\theta}}
, and therefore

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}_{S}})\geq\inf_{\tilde{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\tilde{\theta}}).

Taking the infimum over
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
yields the “
\geq
” inequality in (
93
).

Step 5 (Apply the slice IPM convergence bound).

By (
93
) and Theorem
7.8
,

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\Big(\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\Big).

Combining this with Steps 2–3 gives (
86
).

Step 6 (Local Lipschitz specialization).

If
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r)\leq L_{\mathcal{F}}r
on an interval containing

r_{t}:=\gamma^{t}\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})
for the times of interest, then (
86
) implies

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G)\leq L_{\mathcal{F}}\,\gamma^{t}\,\dist_{\|\cdot\|}(\theta_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S}),

i.e. geometric decay with factor
\gamma
. ∎

Remark 7.11

(Two-sided moduli and orbit identification)
.

Theorem
7.10
requires only the one-sided modulus (
78
), because it delivers an upper bound on IPM error.
If one wishes to infer a rate in an orbit-distance such as
d_{\Theta}
(Definition
4.13
), then a two-sided modulus
as in Assumption
4.14
is needed (Theorem
4.15
).

Corollary 7.12

(Objective convergence along population EM)
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
7.10
, the sequence
\{\Phi(\theta_{t})\}_{t\geq 0}
is nondecreasing and bounded above by

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\Phi(\theta)
.
In particular,
\Phi(\theta_{t})
converges as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

Step 1 (Monotonicity of
\Phi(\theta_{t})
).

By Theorem
5.11
(population EM ascent), for every
\theta\in B_{S}
we have

\Phi(T(\theta))\geq\Phi(\theta).

Apply this with
\theta=\theta_{t}
to obtain

\Phi(\theta_{t+1})=\Phi(T(\theta_{t}))\geq\Phi(\theta_{t}),

so
\{\Phi(\theta_{t})\}_{t\geq 0}
is nondecreasing.

Step 2 (Boundedness above).

By definition of the supremum, for every
\theta\in\Theta
we have
\Phi(\theta)\leq\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Phi(\vartheta)
.
In particular, for each
t
,

\Phi(\theta_{t})\leq\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Phi(\vartheta).

(If you prefer to avoid taking
\sup_{\Theta}
globally, it suffices to assume
\Phi
is bounded above on the forward-invariant region
containing
\{\theta_{t}\}
; for instance, if the iterates are confined to a compact subset of
\Theta
and
\Phi
is continuous.)

Step 3 (A bounded monotone sequence converges).

Let
a_{t}:=\Phi(\theta_{t})
. Since
(a_{t})
is nondecreasing and bounded above, the limit exists and equals
\sup_{t\geq 0}a_{t}
.
For completeness: set
L:=\sup_{t\geq 0}a_{t}<\infty
. For any
\epsilon>0
, by definition of supremum there exists
T
such that

L-\epsilon<a_{T}\leq L
. Since
(a_{t})
is nondecreasing, for all
t\geq T
we have

L-\epsilon<a_{T}\leq a_{t}\leq L,

so
|a_{t}-L|<\epsilon
for all
t\geq T
, i.e.
a_{t}\to L
.

Therefore
\Phi(\theta_{t})
converges as
t\to\infty
. ∎

8.
Finite-sample theory: sample EM as a perturbed quotient contraction

This section develops nonasymptotic guarantees for
sample
EM by comparing it to
population
EM on a local basin.
The logical structure mirrors the now-standard population-to-sample program for EM
[
1
]
but is
formulated in a way that is compatible with (i) orbit geometry (quotient state space), (ii) misspecified, potentially set-valued
targets, and (iii) a general IPM error criterion.

There are three components:

(1)

a
deterministic
perturbation lemma for contractions (tight recursion with the unavoidable factor
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
);

(2)

a
metric transfer
step converting slice-coordinate bounds into quotient/IPM distance-to-set bounds;

(3)

an
operator deviation
analysis bounding
\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|
via empirical process
complexity of an EM-induced class (generic chaining / entropy), following the sharp uniform-deviation theory in

[
10
,
11
]
.

8.1.
Sample slice EM map and the operator deviation functional

Fix a local section
S
and a slice basin
B_{S}\subseteq S
as in Sections
4
–
7
.
Assume the population slice EM map
T:B_{S}\to B_{S}
is single-valued (Definition
6.10
).

Assumption 8.1

(Uniform strong concavity for population and sample M-steps)
.

There exists
\lambda>0
such that:

(i)

For every
\theta\in B_{S}
, the population surrogate
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
is twice continuously differentiable on
B_{S}
and

-\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)\ \succeq\ \lambda I\qquad\forall\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}.

(ii)

For the same
\lambda
(or a smaller constant), for every
\theta\in B_{S}
the sample surrogate
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto\hat{Q}(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
is twice continuously differentiable on
B_{S}
and

-\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}\hat{Q}(\theta^{\prime};\theta)\ \succeq\ \lambda I\qquad\forall\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S},

on the event under consideration (typically a high-probability event, quantified later).

Definition 8.2

(Sample slice EM map)
.

Under Assumption
8.1
, define the (single-valued) sample slice EM map
\hat{T}:B_{S}\to B_{S}
by

\hat{T}(\theta):=\argmax_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\hat{Q}(\theta^{\prime};\theta).

We measure sample-to-population discrepancy through the operator deviation functional

(94)

\Delta_{n}\ :=\ \sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|.

Theorems
8.4
–
8.12
are deterministic statements conditional on a bound

\Delta_{n}\leq\varepsilon
; the probabilistic work is to bound
\Delta_{n}
with high probability in
Subsection
8.5
.

8.2.
Deterministic perturbation theory for contractions (tight)

We begin with the basic deterministic inequality controlling the iterates of a perturbed contraction.
This lemma is elementary but central: it yields the sharp amplification factor
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
.

Assumption 8.3

(Population contraction on the slice basin)
.

There exists
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that

(95)

\|T(\theta)-T(\theta^{\prime})\|\ \leq\ \gamma\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\qquad\forall\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}.

Theorem 8.4

(Perturbed contraction recursion (sharp, deterministic))
.

Assume Assumption
8.3
. Let
(\theta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\hat{\theta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be sequences in
B_{S}

satisfying

\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t}),\qquad\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t}),

and suppose
\Delta_{n}
in (
94
) is finite.
Then for every
t\geq 0
,

(96)

\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\theta_{t}\|\ \leq\ \gamma^{t}\|\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}\|+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,\Delta_{n}.

In particular, if
\hat{\theta}_{0}=\theta_{0}
, then

(97)

\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\theta_{t}\|\ \leq\ \frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,\Delta_{n}\ \leq\ \frac{\Delta_{n}}{1-\gamma}.

Proof.

Definitions being used.

Assumption
8.3
provides:

(i)

(
T
is a contraction on
B_{S}
.
) There exists
\gamma\in[0,1)
such that for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}
,

(98)

\|T(\theta)-T(\theta^{\prime})\|\leq\gamma\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|.

(ii)

(
Uniform perturbation size.
) The quantity
\Delta_{n}
is defined by

(99)

\Delta_{n}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|,

and is finite by hypothesis. In particular, for every
\theta\in B_{S}
,

(100)

\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|\leq\Delta_{n}.

(iii)

(
Iterates stay in the domain.
) By assumption of the theorem statement,
\theta_{t},\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
for all
t
,
so (
98
) and (
100
) can be applied at each time.

Define the error sequence

e_{t}:=\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\theta_{t}\|\qquad(t\geq 0),

which is a nonnegative real sequence.

Step 1 (Derive the fundamental one-step inequality).

We start from the definitions of the iterates:

\hat{\theta}_{t+1}-\theta_{t+1}=\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\theta_{t}).

Add and subtract
T(\hat{\theta}_{t})
:

\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\theta_{t})=\big(\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\hat{\theta}_{t})\big)+\big(T(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\theta_{t})\big).

Now apply the triangle inequality
\|a+b\|\leq\|a\|+\|b\|
:

(101)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}_{t+1}-\theta_{t+1}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\hat{\theta}_{t})\|+\|T(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\theta_{t})\|.

Bound each term:

•

By (
100
) applied at
\theta=\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
,

\|\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\hat{\theta}_{t})\|\leq\Delta_{n}.

•

By the contraction property (
98
) applied to
(\hat{\theta}_{t},\theta_{t})\in B_{S}\times B_{S}
,

\|T(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\theta_{t})\|\leq\gamma\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\theta_{t}\|=\gamma e_{t}.

Substituting into (
101
) gives the one-step recursion

(102)

e_{t+1}\leq\Delta_{n}+\gamma e_{t}\qquad(t\geq 0).

Step 2 (Unroll the recursion by repeated substitution).

We now expand (
102
) explicitly.
First apply it once:

e_{1}\leq\Delta_{n}+\gamma e_{0}.

Apply it again to
e_{2}
and substitute the bound for
e_{1}
:

e_{2}\leq\Delta_{n}+\gamma e_{1}\leq\Delta_{n}+\gamma(\Delta_{n}+\gamma e_{0})=\Delta_{n}(1+\gamma)+\gamma^{2}e_{0}.

One more step:

e_{3}\leq\Delta_{n}+\gamma e_{2}\leq\Delta_{n}+\gamma\big(\Delta_{n}(1+\gamma)+\gamma^{2}e_{0}\big)=\Delta_{n}(1+\gamma+\gamma^{2})+\gamma^{3}e_{0}.

The pattern is now clear and can be proved by induction:

Claim 8.5
.

For every integer
t\geq 1
,

(103)

e_{t}\leq\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\Delta_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{k}.

Proof of Claim
8.5
.

We argue by induction on
t
.
For
t=1
, (
103
) reads
e_{1}\leq\gamma e_{0}+\Delta_{n}
, which is exactly (
102
) at
t=0
.
Assume (
103
) holds for some
t\geq 1
. Then by (
102
),

e_{t+1}\leq\Delta_{n}+\gamma e_{t}\leq\Delta_{n}+\gamma\Big(\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\Delta_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{k}\Big)=\gamma^{t+1}e_{0}+\Delta_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{t}\gamma^{k},

which is (
103
) with
t
replaced by
t+1
. This completes the induction.
∎

Thus (
103
) holds for all
t\geq 1
, and it is also true for
t=0
(with the empty sum convention
\sum_{k=0}^{-1}(\cdot)=0
).
Therefore, for all
t\geq 0
,

(104)

e_{t}\leq\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\Delta_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{k}.

Step 3 (Evaluate the geometric series and obtain the closed form).

Since
\gamma\in[0,1)
, the finite geometric sum satisfies

(105)

\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{k}=\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\qquad(t\geq 1),

and for
t=0
the sum is
0
, which is consistent with the right-hand side since
(1-\gamma^{0})/(1-\gamma)=0
.
Substitute (
105
) into (
104
) and recall
e_{t}=\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\theta_{t}\|
and
e_{0}=\|\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}\|
to get

\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\theta_{t}\|\leq\gamma^{t}\|\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}\|+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\Delta_{n},

which is exactly (
96
).

Step 4 (Same-initialization specialization).

If
\hat{\theta}_{0}=\theta_{0}
, then the first term vanishes and (
96
) becomes

\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\theta_{t}\|\leq\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\Delta_{n}.

Since
0\leq\gamma^{t}\leq 1
, we have
1-\gamma^{t}\leq 1
, hence

\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\Delta_{n}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\Delta_{n},

yielding (
97
). ∎

Remark 8.6

(Why
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
is unavoidable (sharpness))
.

The factor
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
cannot be improved under a
uniform additive perturbation
model.
Indeed, even in one dimension, consider the deterministic recursion

e_{t+1}=\gamma e_{t}+\Delta_{n},\qquad e_{0}=0,

which is the equality case of (
102
). Solving it exactly by the same unrolling gives

e_{t}=\Delta_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{k}=\Delta_{n}\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma},

so the upper bound in (
97
) is attained.
Thus any deterministic bound that depends only on
(\gamma,\Delta_{n})
must, in general, contain a factor of order
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
.

8.3.
Distance-to-set version (misspecified targets)

Under misspecification the target is set-valued, so we need a distance-to-set analogue of the previous theorem.
We state it for an arbitrary closed set
A\subseteq B_{S}
(later
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
).

Definition 8.7

(One-step set deviation)
.

For a set
A\subseteq B_{S}
, define

\Delta_{n}(A):=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\mathrm{dist}\big(\hat{T}(\theta),\,T(\theta)\big),\qquad\mathrm{dist}(u,v):=\|u-v\|.

Note
\Delta_{n}(A)
is the same as
\Delta_{n}
in (
94
) (the notation emphasizes the role of sets below).

Assumption 8.8

(Contraction to a set for population map)
.

There exist
\gamma\in(0,1)
and a nonempty set
A\subseteq B_{S}
such that

(106)

\mathrm{dist}\big(T(\theta),A\big)\ \leq\ \gamma\,\mathrm{dist}(\theta,A)\qquad\forall\theta\in B_{S}.

Theorem 8.9

(Perturbed contraction to a set)
.

Assume Assumption
8.8
. Let
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
with
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
for all
t
.
Then for all
t\geq 0
,

(107)

\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)\ \leq\ \gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},A)+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,\Delta_{n}.

Proof.

Definitions used.

For a nonempty set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
(in the ambient norm
\|\cdot\|
fixed throughout this section),

\dist(\theta,A):=\inf_{a\in A}\|\theta-a\|.

Assumption
8.8
provides the following two ingredients:

(i)

(
Contraction to the set
A
for the population map
T
.
) There exists
\gamma\in[0,1)
such that for all
\theta\in B_{S}
,

(108)

\dist(T(\theta),A)\leq\gamma\,\dist(\theta,A).

(ii)

(
Uniform perturbation size.
) With
\Delta_{n}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|<\infty
, we have

(109)

\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|\leq\Delta_{n}\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in B_{S}.

We will also use the following basic inequality for distance-to-set.

Lemma (distance-to-set is 1-Lipschitz).

For any nonempty set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(110)

\big|\dist(x,A)-\dist(y,A)\big|\leq\|x-y\|.

In particular,

(111)

\dist(x,A)\leq\dist(y,A)+\|x-y\|.

Proof.

Fix
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any
a\in A
, the triangle inequality gives

\|x-a\|\leq\|x-y\|+\|y-a\|.

Taking the infimum over
a\in A
on the right-hand side yields

\|x-a\|\leq\|x-y\|+\dist(y,A)\qquad\text{for all }a\in A.

Now take the infimum over
a\in A
on the left-hand side to obtain

\dist(x,A)\leq\|x-y\|+\dist(y,A).

Swapping the roles of
x
and
y
gives
\dist(y,A)\leq\|x-y\|+\dist(x,A)
.
Combining the two inequalities yields (
110
), and (
111
) is the first inequality written explicitly.
∎

Step 1 (One-step recursion for the distance-to-set).

Set

e_{t}:=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)\qquad(t\geq 0),

so
e_{t}\geq 0
is a real sequence. Using
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
, we start with

e_{t+1}=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t+1},A)=\dist(\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A).

Apply (
111
) with
x=\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
and
y=T(\hat{\theta}_{t})
:

(112)

\displaystyle\dist(\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A)

\displaystyle\leq\dist(T(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A)+\|\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\hat{\theta}_{t})\|.

We bound the two terms in (
112
) using the assumptions:

•

By (
109
) (since
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
),

\|\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\hat{\theta}_{t})\|\leq\Delta_{n}.

•

By the contraction-to-set property (
76
) applied at
\theta=\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
,

\dist(T(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A)\leq\gamma\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)=\gamma e_{t}.

Substituting these into (
112
) yields the one-step recursion

(113)

e_{t+1}\leq\Delta_{n}+\gamma e_{t}\qquad(t\geq 0).

Step 2 (Unroll the scalar recursion).

We now unroll (
113
) exactly as for the pointwise perturbation theorem.
For completeness, we state and prove the unrolling bound.

Lemma 8.10

(Unrolling
e_{t+1}\leq\gamma e_{t}+\Delta
)
.

Let
(e_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a nonnegative sequence satisfying
e_{t+1}\leq\gamma e_{t}+\Delta
for all
t\geq 0
,
where
\gamma\in[0,1)
and
\Delta\geq 0
. Then for all
t\geq 0
,

e_{t}\leq\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\Delta\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{k}=\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\Delta\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}.

Proof.

We prove by induction that for all
t\geq 0
,

e_{t}\leq\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\Delta\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{k},

with the convention that
\sum_{k=0}^{-1}\gamma^{k}:=0
.
For
t=0
this reads
e_{0}\leq e_{0}
, which is true.
Assume the bound holds at time
t
. Then

e_{t+1}\leq\gamma e_{t}+\Delta\leq\gamma\Big(\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\Delta\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{k}\Big)+\Delta=\gamma^{t+1}e_{0}+\Delta\sum_{k=0}^{t}\gamma^{k},

which is exactly the inductive claim for
t+1
.
This completes the induction. Evaluating the geometric series gives the closed form.
∎

Apply Lemma
8.10
to (
113
) with
e_{t}=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)
and
\Delta=\Delta_{n}
:

\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)\leq\gamma^{t}\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},A)+\Delta_{n}\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma},

which is (
107
). ∎

8.4.
Transfer to IPM error and to the quotient

We now convert the slice distance-to-set bound into the distributional criterion

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\hat{\theta}_{t}\right],\bar{\Theta}/G)
.
As in Section
7
, only a one-sided modulus is needed.

Assumption 8.11

(IPM modulus on the slice basin)
.

There exists a nondecreasing
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
with
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(0)=0
such that

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})\ \leq\ \omega_{\mathcal{F}}(\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|)\qquad\forall\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}.

Theorem 8.12

(Sample EM: quotient IPM distance-to-set bound)
.

Let
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}:=\bar{\Theta}\cap B_{S}
.
Assume Assumption
8.8
holds with this
A
, and assume Assumption
8.11
.
Let
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
with
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
and define
\hat{\xi}_{t}:=\left[\hat{\theta}_{t}\right]\in\Theta/G
.
Then for all
t\geq 0
,

(114)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\hat{\xi}_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G\big)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\ \leq\ \omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\left(\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,\Delta_{n}\right).

If
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r)\leq L_{\mathcal{F}}r
on the relevant range, then

(115)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\hat{\xi}_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G\big)\ \leq\ L_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,\Delta_{n}\right).

Proof.

Definitions and assumptions used.

(i)

(
Distance-to-set in parameter space.
) For
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
nonempty,

\dist(\theta,C):=\inf_{c\in C}\|\theta-c\|.

(ii)

(
Quotient distance-to-set in IPM.
) For
\xi\in\Theta/G
,

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\bar{\Theta}/G):=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\xi,\left[\bar{\theta}\right]),\qquad\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\left[\theta^{\prime}\right]):=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}}).

In particular, for
\hat{\xi}_{t}=\left[\hat{\theta}_{t}\right]
,

(116)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\hat{\xi}_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}}),

using the orbit-invariance property
P_{g\cdot\theta}=P_{\theta}
that makes
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
well-defined.

(iii)

(
G
-invariance of
\bar{\Theta}
.
) Lemma
4.9
states
g\cdot\bar{\Theta}=\bar{\Theta}
for all
g\in G
.
Combined with orbit-invariance of the model, this implies that replacing
\bar{\theta}
by any orbit-equivalent representative
does not change
P_{\bar{\theta}}
.

(iv)

(
Finite-sample IPM modulus.
) Assumption
8.11
provides a nondecreasing

\omega_{\mathcal{F}}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}
,

(117)

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|).

(As in Lemma
7.7
, if right-continuity is not assumed, one can replace
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
by its right-continuous envelope
without changing (
117
).)

(v)

(
Perturbed contraction to the set
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
.
) Assumption
8.8
and the definition

\Delta_{n}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|<\infty
imply (Theorem
8.9
) that for all
t\geq 0
,

(118)

\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,\Delta_{n}.

Fix
t\geq 0
.

Step 1 (Reduce the quotient infimum to slice representatives in
\bar{\Theta}_{S}
).

We claim that

(119)

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}}),\qquad\bar{\Theta}_{S}:=\bar{\Theta}\cap B_{S}.

The “
\leq
” direction holds because
\bar{\Theta}_{S}\subseteq\bar{\Theta}
.
For “
\geq
”: fix any
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
whose orbit is relevant for
\bar{\Theta}/G
inside the local quotient chart.
Let
\bar{\theta}_{S}:=\pi(\left[\bar{\theta}\right])
. By Lemma
4.9
,
\bar{\theta}_{S}\in\bar{\Theta}
, and by definition of
\pi
,

\bar{\theta}_{S}\in B_{S}
, hence
\bar{\theta}_{S}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}
.
Moreover
\bar{\theta}_{S}
lies in the orbit of
\bar{\theta}
, so by orbit-invariance of the model we have
P_{\bar{\theta}_{S}}=P_{\bar{\theta}}
.
Therefore

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}_{S}})\geq\inf_{\tilde{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\tilde{\theta}}).

Taking the infimum over
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}
yields the “
\geq
” inequality in (
119
),
establishing (
119
).
Combining with (
116
) gives

(120)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\hat{\xi}_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}}).

Step 2 (Control IPM distance-to-set by parameter distance-to-set).

We now prove the analogue of Lemma
7.7
for the present
\hat{\theta}_{t}
.
Let
r_{t}:=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})
. By definition of infimum, for every
\epsilon>0
there exists

\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}
such that

(121)

\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}\|\leq r_{t}+\epsilon.

Apply the modulus inequality (
117
) with
\theta=\hat{\theta}_{t}
and
\theta^{\prime}=\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}
:

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}\|\big)\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r_{t}+\epsilon),

using (
121
) and monotonicity of
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Since
\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}})
, we obtain

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r_{t}+\epsilon)\qquad\text{for all }\epsilon>0.

Letting
\epsilon\downarrow 0
and using right-continuity (or the right-continuous envelope) yields

(122)

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\big).

Combining (
120
) and (
122
) gives

(123)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\hat{\xi}_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G)\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\big).

Step 3 (Insert the perturbed set-contraction bound and conclude).

Apply (
118
) to bound
\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})
, and then apply monotonicity of
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
:

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathcal{F}}(\hat{\xi}_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G)

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\left(\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,\Delta_{n}\right).

This is exactly (
114
).

Step 4 (Lipschitz specialization).

If
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r)\leq L_{\mathcal{F}}r
on an interval containing the argument

r_{t}:=\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\Delta_{n},

then applying this bound to (
114
) yields (
115
). ∎

8.5.
Bounding
\Delta_{n}
via EM-induced empirical processes

The deterministic bounds above reduce the finite-sample problem to controlling
\Delta_{n}
.
We now relate
\Delta_{n}
to uniform deviations of (population vs. empirical) gradients of the surrogate.

8.5.1.
Gradient characterization and a stability inequality

Define the surrogate gradients (in the M-step variable):

(124)

G(\theta^{\prime},\theta):=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta),\qquad\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta):=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\hat{Q}(\theta^{\prime};\theta).

Under Assumption
8.1
, the first-order conditions characterize the updates:

(125)

G(T(\theta),\theta)=0,\qquad\hat{G}(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)=0\qquad(\theta\in B_{S}).

Lemma 8.13

(Strong concavity yields a Lipschitz inverse-gradient bound)
.

Assume Assumption
8.1
(i) for
Q
.
Then for each fixed
\theta\in B_{S}
, the map
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
is
\lambda
-strongly monotone:
for all
\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{2}\in B_{S}
,

(126)

\big\langle G(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime}_{2},\theta),\,\theta^{\prime}_{1}-\theta^{\prime}_{2}\big\rangle\ \geq\ \lambda\|\theta^{\prime}_{1}-\theta^{\prime}_{2}\|^{2}.

Consequently,

(127)

\|\theta^{\prime}_{1}-\theta^{\prime}_{2}\|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{\lambda}\,\|G(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime}_{2},\theta)\|.

The same statements hold for
\hat{G}
under Assumption
8.1
(ii).

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in B_{S}
.

Step 1 (From strong concavity to strong monotonicity).

Assumption
8.1
(i) states
-\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)\succeq\lambda I
.
Equivalently,
\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)\preceq-\lambda I
.
For a
C^{1}
map
G(\cdot,\theta)=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\cdot;\theta)
, this implies strong monotonicity with constant
\lambda
:
integrate the Hessian along the segment between
\theta^{\prime}_{1}
and
\theta^{\prime}_{2}
to obtain (
126
)
(the standard characterization of strong concavity/monotonicity).

Step 2 (Derive the inverse-Lipschitz bound).

Apply Cauchy–Schwarz to (
126
):

\lambda\|\theta^{\prime}_{1}-\theta^{\prime}_{2}\|^{2}\leq\langle G(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime}_{2},\theta),\theta^{\prime}_{1}-\theta^{\prime}_{2}\rangle\leq\|G(\theta^{\prime}_{1},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime}_{2},\theta)\|\,\|\theta^{\prime}_{1}-\theta^{\prime}_{2}\|.

If
\theta^{\prime}_{1}\neq\theta^{\prime}_{2}
, divide by
\|\theta^{\prime}_{1}-\theta^{\prime}_{2}\|
; otherwise the inequality is trivial.
This yields (
127
). ∎

Theorem 8.14

(Operator deviation controlled by gradient deviations)
.

Assume Assumption
8.1
and that
T(\theta),\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}
for all
\theta\in B_{S}
.
Then

(128)

\Delta_{n}\ \leq\ \frac{2}{\lambda}\,\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\ \sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\ \|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in B_{S}
.

Step 1 (Add and subtract
G(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)
).

Using (
125
) and the triangle inequality,

\|G(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)-G(T(\theta),\theta)\|=\|G(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)-0\|\leq\|G(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)-\hat{G}(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)\|+\|\hat{G}(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)\|.

But
\hat{G}(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)=0
by (
125
), so

\|G(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)-G(T(\theta),\theta)\|\leq\|G(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)-\hat{G}(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)\|.

Similarly, adding and subtracting
\hat{G}(T(\theta),\theta)
yields

\|G(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)-G(T(\theta),\theta)\|=\|\hat{G}(T(\theta),\theta)-G(T(\theta),\theta)\|.

Combining the two displays and taking the maximum,

\|G(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)-G(T(\theta),\theta)\|\leq\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|.

Step 2 (Invert the strong monotonicity).

By Lemma
8.13
applied to
G(\cdot,\theta)
,

\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\|G(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)-G(T(\theta),\theta)\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|.

Step 3 (Symmetrize to obtain the factor
2/\lambda
).

The previous bound is already sufficient. A slightly more conservative but uniform form is obtained by noting that

\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|\leq\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|,

and then taking
\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}
of the left-hand side.
Writing the right-hand side with a prefactor
2/\lambda
accommodates the common situation where
one bounds separately
\sup\|\hat{G}-G\|
and an additional event ensuring
\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}
uniformly.
(Keeping
2/\lambda
is harmless and matches standard perturbation statements in the EM literature.)
Thus (
128
) holds. ∎

8.5.2.
EM-induced function class and empirical process bounds

We now express
\hat{G}-G
as an empirical process indexed by an EM-induced class.

Under the standing differentiability conditions (as in Assumptions
5.1
and
6.4
),
the gradient takes the form

G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P^{*}}\Big[g_{\theta^{\prime},\theta}(X)\Big],\qquad\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{n}}\Big[g_{\theta^{\prime},\theta}(X)\Big],

where the
index function

g_{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is

(129)

g_{\theta^{\prime},\theta}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,Z)\big].

Define the EM-induced vector-valued class

(130)

\mathcal{H}:=\{\,g_{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:\ (\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in B_{S}\times B_{S}\,\}.

To apply scalar empirical process results, we use the standard reduction:
for
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, consider the scalar class

\mathcal{H}_{u}:=\{\langle u,g\rangle:g\in\mathcal{H}\}
and take a union bound over a net on the sphere.
We state the cleanest form in terms of a generic complexity functional; see
[
10
,
11
]
.

Theorem 8.15

(Generic chaining bound for the EM-induced class)
.

Assume the functions in
\mathcal{H}
are square-integrable under
P^{*}
and that
\mathcal{H}
is
P^{*}
-centered
(or replace each
h
by
h-\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}h
). Write the
L_{2}(P^{*})
semimetric as

d(h,h^{\prime}):=\|h-h^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(P^{*})}.

(Standard additional concentration hypothesis.)

Assume moreover that the class has
L_{2}(P^{*})
-subgaussian increments
in the following sense: there exists
K<\infty

such that for all
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
, the centered random variable

Z_{h,h^{\prime}}(X):=(h-h^{\prime})(X)-\mathbb{E}_{P^{*}}[(h-h^{\prime})(X)]

satisfies the
\psi_{2}
bound

(131)

\|Z_{h,h^{\prime}}(X)\|_{\psi_{2}}\ \leq\ K\,d(h,h^{\prime}).

(For example, (
131
) holds if every
h(X)
is subgaussian with
\|h(X)-\mathbb{E}h(X)\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K\|h\|_{L_{2}(P^{*})}
.)

Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that for every
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

(132)

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\big|(P_{n}-P^{*})h\big|\ \leq\ CK\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},d)}{\sqrt{n}}+\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},d)\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right),

where
\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},d)
is Talagrand’s
\gamma_{2}
functional of
(\mathcal{H},d)
and

\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},d):=\sup_{h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}d(h,h^{\prime})
.

Remark (on the assumptions).

Without some concentration control like (
131
), one generally cannot expect an exponential tail

\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n}
from only
L_{2}
integrability; the high-probability form (
132
) is the standard
subgaussian-increment regime in generic chaining references.

Proof.

Step 0 (Notation and
\gamma_{2}
definition).

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}}P^{*}
and
P_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
.
For each
h\in\mathcal{H}
define the centered empirical-process coordinate

\mathbb{X}_{h}:=(P_{n}-P^{*})h=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(h(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}h(X)\big).

We will control
\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}|\mathbb{X}_{h}|
.

Recall the (one of several equivalent) definitions of Talagrand’s
\gamma_{2}
functional.
A sequence of subsets
(T_{k})_{k\geq 0}
of
\mathcal{H}
is called
admissible
if
|T_{0}|=1
and

|T_{k}|\leq 2^{2^{k}}
for all
k\geq 1
.
Given an admissible
(T_{k})
define, for each
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

d(h,T_{k}):=\inf_{t\in T_{k}}d(h,t).

Then

(133)

\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},d):=\inf_{(T_{k})\ \text{admissible}}\ \sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\ \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{k/2}\,d(h,T_{k}).

(We may assume separability w.r.t.
d
or otherwise interpret the supremum over a countable dense subclass;
this is standard and does not affect the bound.)

Step 1 (Subgaussian increments for the empirical process).

We first show that the process
\{\mathbb{X}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathcal{H}}
has subgaussian increments w.r.t. the metric
d/\sqrt{n}
.

Lemma 8.16

(Averaging subgaussian variables)
.

If
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
are independent, centered, and
\|Y_{i}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\sigma
, then

\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\ \leq\ \frac{C_{0}\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}

for a universal constant
C_{0}
.

Proof.

A standard characterization of the
\psi_{2}
norm is: there exists a universal
c>0
such that

\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\sigma
implies
\mathbb{E}\exp\!\big(\lambda Y\big)\leq\exp(c\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2})
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
Applying this to each
Y_{i}
and using independence,

\mathbb{E}\exp\!\left(\lambda\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\exp\!\left(\frac{\lambda}{n}Y_{i}\right)\leq\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\!\left(c\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n^{2}}\sigma^{2}\right)=\exp\!\left(c\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n}\sigma^{2}\right).

This shows
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}
is subgaussian with variance proxy
\asymp\sigma^{2}/n
, which is equivalent to the stated

\psi_{2}
bound with some universal
C_{0}
.
∎

Now fix
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
and consider the increment

\mathbb{X}_{h}-\mathbb{X}_{h^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(Z_{h,h^{\prime}}(X_{i})\Big),\qquad Z_{h,h^{\prime}}(x):=(h-h^{\prime})(x)-\mathbb{E}(h-h^{\prime})(X).

By the increment assumption (
131
),
\|Z_{h,h^{\prime}}(X)\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq Kd(h,h^{\prime})
.
Applying Lemma
8.16
with
\sigma=Kd(h,h^{\prime})
yields

(134)

\|\mathbb{X}_{h}-\mathbb{X}_{h^{\prime}}\|_{\psi_{2}}\ \leq\ \frac{C_{1}K}{\sqrt{n}}\,d(h,h^{\prime})\qquad\text{for all }h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H},

for a universal
C_{1}
.
Equivalently, the process has subgaussian increments with respect to the metric

\tilde{d}(h,h^{\prime}):=\frac{K}{\sqrt{n}}\,d(h,h^{\prime}).

Step 2 (A self-contained chaining tail bound for subgaussian-increment processes).

We now prove the high-probability generic chaining inequality in the form needed here.

Lemma 8.17

(Chaining tail bound for subgaussian increments)
.

Let
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in T}
be a centered real-valued process indexed by
(T,\rho)
, and assume

\|X_{t}-X_{s}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\rho(t,s)\qquad\text{for all }s,t\in T.

Then there exists a universal constant
C_{2}>0
such that for every
u\geq 1
,

(135)

\Pr\!\left(\sup_{t\in T}|X_{t}|>C_{2}\Big(\gamma_{2}(T,\rho)+u\,\mathrm{diam}(T,\rho)\Big)\right)\ \leq\ 2e^{-u^{2}}.

Proof.

We prove an explicit chaining inequality by constructing nets and taking a union bound over scales.

(a) Fix an admissible sequence and associated projections.

Let
(T_{k})_{k\geq 0}
be an admissible sequence of subsets of
T

(with
|T_{0}|=1
,
|T_{k}|\leq 2^{2^{k}}
).
For each
t\in T
choose a measurable selector
\pi_{k}(t)\in T_{k}
satisfying

\rho\big(t,\pi_{k}(t)\big)\leq\rho(t,T_{k})+2^{-k}\mathrm{diam}(T,\rho),

which is always possible by definition of the infimum and a standard
2^{-k}
-approximation.
(Any summable slack would do; this choice is convenient.)

Fix a basepoint
t_{0}\in T_{0}
(the unique element).
Since
X_{t_{0}}
is centered and does not affect the supremum up to additive constants,
we will bound
X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}
and then use
|X_{t}|\leq|X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}|+|X_{t_{0}}|
; the latter contributes only a universal constant
absorbed into
C_{2}
.

(b) Telescoping decomposition.

For any
t\in T
and any integer
m\geq 1
, write

X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Big(X_{\pi_{k}(t)}-X_{\pi_{k-1}(t)}\Big)\;+\;\Big(X_{t}-X_{\pi_{m}(t)}\Big).

Letting
m\to\infty
and using separability (or working on a countable dense subset) we may assume

\rho(t,\pi_{m}(t))\to 0
and hence
X_{t}-X_{\pi_{m}(t)}\to 0
in
L_{2}
(and almost surely along a subsequence),
so it suffices to control the series of increments.

(c) Control increments uniformly over the net pairs at each scale.

Fix
k\geq 1
. For any
t\in T
, the increment

X_{\pi_{k}(t)}-X_{\pi_{k-1}(t)}
is a difference of two net points. Define the set of all such pairs:

\mathcal{P}_{k}:=\{(a,b)\in T_{k}\times T_{k-1}\}.

Then
|\mathcal{P}_{k}|\leq|T_{k}||T_{k-1}|\leq 2^{2^{k}}2^{2^{k-1}}\leq 2^{2^{k+1}}
.

By the subgaussian increment hypothesis,

\|X_{a}-X_{b}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\rho(a,b)\qquad\text{for all }(a,b)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}.

Hence there is a universal
c>0
such that for every
(a,b)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
and every
v\geq 0
,

(136)

\Pr\big(|X_{a}-X_{b}|>v\,\rho(a,b)\big)\leq 2e^{-cv^{2}}.

Now set
v_{k}:=u\,2^{k/2}
with
u\geq 1
.
By a union bound over all pairs in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and (
136
),

\Pr\Big(\exists(a,b)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}:\ |X_{a}-X_{b}|>v_{k}\,\rho(a,b)\Big)\leq|\mathcal{P}_{k}|\cdot 2e^{-cv_{k}^{2}}\leq 2\exp\!\Big((\log 2)\,2^{k+1}-cu^{2}2^{k}\Big).

Choose
u\geq 1
and absorb constants so that
cu^{2}2^{k}
dominates
(\log 2)2^{k+1}
; explicitly,
for a universal choice of constants in front of
u
we obtain a bound of the form

\Pr\Big(\exists(a,b)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}:\ |X_{a}-X_{b}|>C_{3}u\,2^{k/2}\rho(a,b)\Big)\leq 2e^{-u^{2}2^{k-1}},

for a universal
C_{3}
and all
k\geq 1
.
Summing over
k\geq 1
gives

\Pr\Big(\exists k\geq 1\ \exists(a,b)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}:\ |X_{a}-X_{b}|>C_{3}u\,2^{k/2}\rho(a,b)\Big)\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}2e^{-u^{2}2^{k-1}}\leq 2e^{-u^{2}},

using that
u\geq 1
makes the series dominated by its first term.

Therefore, on an event
\Omega_{u}
with
\Pr(\Omega_{u})\geq 1-2e^{-u^{2}}
, we have
simultaneously for all
k\geq 1
and all

(a,b)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
,

(137)

|X_{a}-X_{b}|\leq C_{3}u\,2^{k/2}\rho(a,b).

(d) Sum the scale bounds.

Fix
t\in T
and work on
\Omega_{u}
.
Apply (
137
) to
a=\pi_{k}(t)
and
b=\pi_{k-1}(t)
:

|X_{\pi_{k}(t)}-X_{\pi_{k-1}(t)}|\leq C_{3}u\,2^{k/2}\,\rho\big(\pi_{k}(t),\pi_{k-1}(t)\big).

Using the triangle inequality for
\rho
,

\rho\big(\pi_{k}(t),\pi_{k-1}(t)\big)\leq\rho\big(t,\pi_{k}(t)\big)+\rho\big(t,\pi_{k-1}(t)\big).

Thus

\displaystyle|X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}|X_{\pi_{k}(t)}-X_{\pi_{k-1}(t)}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}u\sum_{k\geq 1}2^{k/2}\Big(\rho(t,\pi_{k}(t))+\rho(t,\pi_{k-1}(t))\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}u\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k/2}\rho(t,\pi_{k}(t)),

for a universal
C_{4}
.
By construction of
\pi_{k}(t)
,

\rho(t,\pi_{k}(t))\leq\rho(t,T_{k})+2^{-k}\mathrm{diam}(T,\rho).

Therefore,

|X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}|\leq C_{4}u\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k/2}\rho(t,T_{k})\;+\;C_{4}u\,\mathrm{diam}(T,\rho)\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k/2}2^{-k}.

The last series
\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{-k/2}
converges to a universal constant, so

|X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}|\leq C_{5}u\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k/2}\rho(t,T_{k})+C_{5}u\,\mathrm{diam}(T,\rho),

for universal
C_{5}
.
Taking the supremum over
t\in T
gives, on
\Omega_{u}
,

\sup_{t\in T}|X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}|\leq C_{5}u\left(\sup_{t\in T}\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k/2}\rho(t,T_{k})\right)+C_{5}u\,\mathrm{diam}(T,\rho).

Finally, take the infimum over admissible
(T_{k})
and use the definition (
133
) to obtain

\sup_{t\in T}|X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}|\leq C_{5}u\,\gamma_{2}(T,\rho)+C_{5}u\,\mathrm{diam}(T,\rho).

Absorbing
|X_{t_{0}}|
into constants (or taking
t_{0}
such that
X_{t_{0}}=0
if available) yields (
135
)
with a universal constant
C_{2}
.
∎

Step 3 (Apply Lemma
8.17
to the empirical process).

Apply Lemma
8.17
to the process
\mathbb{X}_{h}
indexed by
T=\mathcal{H}
with the metric

\rho(h,h^{\prime}):=\frac{C_{1}K}{\sqrt{n}}\,d(h,h^{\prime}),

which is valid by (
134
).
Then for every
u\geq 1
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-u^{2}}
,

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}|\mathbb{X}_{h}|\leq C_{2}\left(\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},\rho)+u\,\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},\rho)\right).

Using the scaling properties
\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},c\rho)=c\,\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},\rho)
and
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},c\rho)=c\,\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},\rho)
,
we have

\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},\rho)=\frac{C_{1}K}{\sqrt{n}}\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},d),\qquad\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},\rho)=\frac{C_{1}K}{\sqrt{n}}\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},d).

Therefore, with probability at least
1-2e^{-u^{2}}
,

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}|(P_{n}-P^{*})h|\leq CK\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},d)}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{u\,\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},d)}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

for a universal
C
(absorbing constants
C_{1},C_{2}
).

Set
u:=\sqrt{\log(2/\delta)}\asymp\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}
so that
2e^{-u^{2}}\leq\delta
.
This yields (
132
).
∎

Corollary 8.18

(Bracketing/entropy specialization)
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
8.15
, one may upper bound
\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},d)
by entropy integrals.
If
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{H},d)
denotes covering numbers, then

\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},d)\ \lesssim\ \int_{0}^{\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},d)}\sqrt{\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{H},d)}\,d\epsilon,

yielding a Dudley-type bound.

Proof.

We give a direct construction from covers.

For
k\geq 0
define the scale

\epsilon_{k}:=\inf\Big\{\epsilon>0:\ \log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{H},d)\leq 2^{k}\Big\}.

Then there exists an
\epsilon_{k}
-net
T_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
with

|T_{k}|\leq N(\epsilon_{k},\mathcal{H},d)\leq e^{2^{k}}\leq 2^{2^{k}}
(up to a harmless change of constants in the definition of
\epsilon_{k}
),
so
(T_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is admissible after ensuring
|T_{0}|=1
(replace
T_{0}
by a singleton).

By definition of
\epsilon_{k}
, we have
d(h,T_{k})\leq\epsilon_{k}
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Therefore,

\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},d)\leq\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k/2}d(h,T_{k})\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k/2}\epsilon_{k}.

Now relate the discrete sum to the entropy integral by a standard layer-cake argument:
since
\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{H},d)
is nonincreasing in
\epsilon
, the definition of
\epsilon_{k}
implies that

\epsilon\in(\epsilon_{k+1},\epsilon_{k}]
entails
\sqrt{\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{H},d)}\gtrsim 2^{k/2}
.
Hence

\int_{0}^{\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{H},d)}\sqrt{\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{H},d)}\,d\epsilon\ \gtrsim\ \sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k/2}(\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k+1})\ \asymp\ \sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k/2}\epsilon_{k},

where the last equivalence uses summation by parts and
\epsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
.
Combining the displays yields the claimed bound (absorbing universal constants into
\lesssim
).
∎

8.5.3.
Putting the pieces together

Combining Theorem
8.14
with Theorem
8.15
yields a high-probability bound on
\Delta_{n}
,
and inserting that bound into Theorem
8.12
yields an explicit finite-sample rate.

Theorem 8.19

(Sample EM in a general IPM: high-probability bound)
.

Assume:

(i)

population contraction-to-set (
106
) on
B_{S}
for
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
with constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
;

(ii)

strong concavity Assumption
8.1
with constant
\lambda
;

(iii)

IPM modulus Assumption
8.11
on
B_{S}
;

(iv)

the EM-induced class
\mathcal{H}
in (
130
) satisfies the conditions of Theorem
8.15
.

Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that for every
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,
the sample EM iterates satisfy for all
t\geq 0
,

(138)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\left[\hat{\theta}_{t}\right],\bar{\Theta}/G\big)\ \leq\ \omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\left(\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\cdot\frac{2}{\lambda}\cdot C\left[{\frac{\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))}{\sqrt{n}}}+\diam(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right]\right).

Proof.

Roadmap.

We prove (
138
) by chaining together three deterministic implications:

\text{(empirical-process control of $\hat{G}-G$)}\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{(control of the EM update perturbation $\Delta_{n}$)}\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{(perturbed contraction-to-set $\Rightarrow$ IPM distance-to-set bound).}

Each implication is made explicit below.

Step 0 (Recall the objects and where randomness enters).

The only randomness is through the sample
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}}P^{*}
, hence through
P_{n}
and the sample EM operator
\hat{T}
.
The population objects (
T
,
G
,
\bar{\Theta}_{S}
,
\gamma
,
\lambda
,
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
) are deterministic.

Recall that, by definition of the EM-induced class
\mathcal{H}
in (
130
),

(139)

\sup_{(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in B_{S}\times B_{S}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\|(P_{n}-P^{*})h\|,

where
