\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
is the sample analogue of the population gradient map
G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
, and each coordinate of

\hat{G}-G
is an empirical-process average over a corresponding
h\in\mathcal{H}
.
Identity (
139
) is a definition-level rewriting: it is precisely why
\mathcal{H}
was introduced.

Step 1 (Apply the generic chaining bound to control
\sup\|\hat{G}-G\|
).

By assumption (iv),
\mathcal{H}
satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
8.15
.
Therefore, for every
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

(140)

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\big|(P_{n}-P^{*})h\big|\ \leq\ C\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\diam(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right).

On this event, combine (
140
) with the identity (
139
) to obtain

(141)

\sup_{(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in B_{S}\times B_{S}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|\leq C\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\diam(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right).

Step 2 (Convert gradient-approximation error into a uniform EM-map perturbation bound).

Define the uniform perturbation size

\Delta_{n}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|.

By Theorem
8.14
(a deterministic stability result for strongly concave maximization),
Assumption
8.1
with constant
\lambda
implies

(142)

\Delta_{n}\ \leq\ \frac{2}{\lambda}\,\sup_{(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in B_{S}\times B_{S}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|.

(Here is where strong concavity enters: it converts a gradient perturbation into a parameter perturbation with factor
2/\lambda
.)

Now work on the high-probability event where (
141
) holds. Substituting (
141
) into
(
142
) yields the explicit bound

(143)

\Delta_{n}\ \leq\ \frac{2}{\lambda}\cdot C\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))}{\sqrt{n}}+\diam(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right).

Step 3 (Apply the sample-EM quotient IPM bound with this
\Delta_{n}
).

Under assumption (i) (population contraction-to-set on
B_{S}
with target set
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
) and assumption (iii)
(the IPM modulus on
B_{S}
), Theorem
8.12
gives, deterministically for every
t\geq 0
,

(144)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\left[\hat{\theta}_{t}\right],\bar{\Theta}/G\big)\ \leq\ \omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\left(\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,\Delta_{n}\right).

(Here the factor
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
comes from unrolling the perturbed contraction recursion.)

Finally, substitute the bound (
143
) on
\Delta_{n}
into (
144
). This yields

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\left[\hat{\theta}_{t}\right],\bar{\Theta}/G\big)\ \leq\ \omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\left(\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\cdot\frac{2}{\lambda}\cdot C\left[{\frac{\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))}{\sqrt{n}}}+\diam(\mathcal{H},L_{2}(P^{*}))\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\right]\right),

which is exactly (
138
).

Step 4 (Probability statement).

The bound is valid on the event (
140
), which holds with probability at least
1-\delta
by
Theorem
8.15
. All subsequent steps were deterministic conditional on this event.
Therefore (
138
) holds with probability at least
1-\delta
.
∎

Remark 8.20

(Interpretation of (
138
))
.

Equation (
138
) exhibits the standard “optimization + statistics” decomposition:
a geometric optimization term
\gamma^{t}\,\mathrm{dist}(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})
and a statistical floor of order

\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\big(\gamma_{2}(\mathcal{H})/\sqrt{n}\big)
.
The factor
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
is algorithmically unavoidable for perturbed contractions, while
\lambda^{-1}
reflects
curvature of the M-step surrogate.
The statistical complexity is driven by the EM-induced class
\mathcal{H}
, not by
\mathcal{F}
directly.

9.
Extensions and refinements: inexact EM, basin invariance, and sample-splitting

Section
8
reduced finite-sample EM to a
perturbed contraction
analysis controlled by the operator deviation

\Delta_{n}=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|
.
This section strengthens that theory in three directions that are frequently indispensable in rigorous applications:
(i) explicit
basin invariance
conditions ensuring sample EM remains inside the local basin,
(ii)
inexact
(approximate) E/M steps with time-varying errors, and
(iii)
sample-splitting
(fresh-batch) EM yielding sharper high-probability bounds over long horizons.

Throughout, we retain the maximally general evaluation metric: an arbitrary IPM
d_{\mathcal{F}}
and its quotient form

\bar{d}_{\mathcal{F}}(\left[\theta\right],\left[\theta^{\prime}\right])=d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})
(Section
4
).

9.1.
Basin invariance: when does sample EM stay in
B_{S}
?

The deterministic results in Section
8
implicitly assume
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
for all
t
.
This is not automatic: even a small perturbation can eject an iterate from the basin unless one imposes a margin-to-boundary condition.
We now state and prove the sharp basin-invariance lemma used in most local EM analyses (cf. the basin-based program in
[
1
]
).

Assumption 9.1

(Absorbing basin for the population map)
.

B_{S}\subseteq S
is nonempty and satisfies
T(B_{S})\subseteq B_{S}
.

Assumption 9.2

(Margin to the boundary)
.

There exists
r>0
such that the closed
r
-neighborhood of
T(B_{S})
is contained in
B_{S}
, i.e.

(145)

\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ \mathrm{dist}(u,T(B_{S}))\leq r\}\ \subseteq\ B_{S}.

Lemma 9.3

(Uniform operator perturbation implies basin invariance)
.

Assume Assumption
9.1
and Assumption
9.2
.
If
\Delta_{n}\leq r
, then
\hat{T}(B_{S})\subseteq B_{S}
.
Consequently, if
\hat{\theta}_{0}\in B_{S}
and
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
, then
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

Definitions and assumptions used.

Recall
\Delta_{n}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|
.
Assumption
9.1
states that

(146)

T(B_{S})\subseteq B_{S}.

Assumption
9.2
states that there exists a radius
r>0
such that

(147)

\Big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ \dist\big(x,T(B_{S})\big)\leq r\Big\}\ \subseteq\ B_{S}.

In words: the
r
-neighborhood (Minkowski enlargement) of the image set
T(B_{S})
is contained in the basin
B_{S}
.

Assume
\Delta_{n}\leq r
. We show
\hat{T}(B_{S})\subseteq B_{S}
by proving
\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}
for an arbitrary
\theta\in B_{S}
.

Step 1 (Pointwise proximity of
\hat{T}(\theta)
to the image set
T(B_{S})
).

Fix any
\theta\in B_{S}
.
Since
T(\theta)\in T(B_{S})
by definition of the image set, we can upper bound the distance from
\hat{T}(\theta)
to
T(B_{S})

by a specific candidate point
T(\theta)\in T(B_{S})
:

(148)

\displaystyle\dist\big(\hat{T}(\theta),T(B_{S})\big)

\displaystyle:=\inf_{y\in T(B_{S})}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-y\|\leq\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|.

By definition of
\Delta_{n}
as a supremum over
B_{S}
, and since
\theta\in B_{S}
,

(149)

\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|\leq\Delta_{n}.

Combining (
148
) and (
149
) and using the assumed bound
\Delta_{n}\leq r
gives

(150)

\dist\big(\hat{T}(\theta),T(B_{S})\big)\leq r.

Step 2 (Apply the margin assumption to conclude
\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}
).

Inequality (
150
) says precisely that
\hat{T}(\theta)
belongs to the
r
-neighborhood of
T(B_{S})
:

\hat{T}(\theta)\in\Big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ \dist\big(x,T(B_{S})\big)\leq r\Big\}.

By Assumption
9.2
, the right-hand set is contained in
B_{S}
, hence
\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}
.

Since
\theta\in B_{S}
was arbitrary, we have shown
\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}
for all
\theta\in B_{S}
, i.e.

\hat{T}(B_{S})\subseteq B_{S}.

Step 3 (Forward invariance for the iterates).

Assume
\hat{\theta}_{0}\in B_{S}
and define
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
.
We prove by induction that
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
for all
t\geq 0
.
The base case
t=0
holds by hypothesis.
If
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
, then
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}(\hat{\theta}_{t})\in\hat{T}(B_{S})\subseteq B_{S}
by the first part of the lemma.
Thus, by induction,
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
for all
t\geq 0
.
∎

Remark 9.4

(Sharpness of the margin condition)
.

Assumption
9.2
is essentially tight: if
T(B_{S})
approaches the boundary of
B_{S}
with no positive margin,
then arbitrarily small uniform perturbations can force
\hat{T}(\theta)\notin B_{S}
for some
\theta
.

9.2.
A sharper bound for
\Delta_{n}
(removing the unnecessary factor
2
)

Theorem
8.14
in Section
8
stated a conservative prefactor
2/\lambda
.
Under the same hypotheses one can obtain the optimal prefactor
1/\lambda
without any additional assumptions.

Recall
G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime};\theta)
and
\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\hat{Q}(\theta^{\prime};\theta)

as in (
124
).

Theorem 9.5

(Optimal strong-concavity control of
\Delta_{n}
)
.

Assume Assumption
8.1
with constant
\lambda>0
and that

T(\theta),\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}
for all
\theta\in B_{S}
.
Assume moreover that
B_{S}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(or, more generally, that the maximizers
T(\theta)
and
\hat{T}(\theta)

are interior points of the feasible set so that first-order conditions take the form of vanishing gradients).
Then

(151)

\Delta_{n}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{\lambda}\,\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\ \sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\ \|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|.

Proof.

Step 0 (Set up notation; reduce to a pointwise bound).

Fix an arbitrary
\theta\in B_{S}
. Define the population and sample slice objectives

Q_{\theta}(\theta^{\prime}):=Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta),\qquad\hat{Q}_{\theta}(\theta^{\prime}):=\hat{Q}(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta),

and their gradients (with respect to
\theta^{\prime}
)

\nabla Q_{\theta}(\theta^{\prime})=G(\theta^{\prime},\theta),\qquad\nabla\hat{Q}_{\theta}(\theta^{\prime})=\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta).

Write

v:=T(\theta)\in B_{S},\qquad u:=\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}.

Our goal for this fixed
\theta
is to prove

(152)

\|u-v\|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{\lambda}\,\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|.

Once (
152
) is established, taking
\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}
on both sides yields (
151
)
(because the left-hand side becomes
\Delta_{n}
by definition).

Step 1 (Strong concavity
\Rightarrow
sharp inverse-Lipschitz property of the gradient).

We isolate and prove the quantitative inequality that converts a
gradient difference
into a
parameter difference
.
This is the sharp step where the constant
1/\lambda
is obtained.

Lemma 9.6

(Strong concavity implies inverse-Lipschitz gradient)
.

Let
f:B_{S}\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable and
\lambda
-strongly concave on an open convex set
B_{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then for all
x,y\in B_{S}
,

(153)

\|x-y\|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{\lambda}\,\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|.

Equivalently,
\nabla f
is injective and its inverse (on its range) is
1/\lambda
-Lipschitz.

Proof.

We proceed in two substeps.

(a) Strong concavity
\Rightarrow
strong monotonicity of the gradient.

A standard characterization of
\lambda
-strong concavity is:
for all
x,y\in B_{S}
,

(154)

f(y)\leq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|y-x\|^{2}.

Swap
x
and
y
in (
154
) to get

(155)

f(x)\leq f(y)+\langle\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}.

Add (
154
) and (
155
). The left-hand side becomes
f(x)+f(y)
, and the
f(x),f(y)
terms cancel,
yielding

0\leq\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+\langle\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle-\lambda\|x-y\|^{2}.

Since
\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle=-\langle\nabla f(x),x-y\rangle
and
\langle\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle
is unchanged, this becomes

(156)

\langle\nabla f(y)-\nabla f(x),\,x-y\rangle\ \geq\ \lambda\|x-y\|^{2}.

Equivalently,

(157)

\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),\,x-y\rangle\ \leq\ -\lambda\|x-y\|^{2}.

This is the strong monotonicity (with negative sign, since
f
is concave) of
\nabla f
.

(b) Strong monotonicity
\Rightarrow
inverse-Lipschitz.

Apply Cauchy–Schwarz to the inner product:

\big|\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),\,x-y\rangle\big|\leq\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|\,\|x-y\|.

From (
157
), the quantity
\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle
is
nonpositive
, hence

-\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),\,x-y\rangle\geq\lambda\|x-y\|^{2}.

Also,

-\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),\,x-y\rangle=\langle\nabla f(y)-\nabla f(x),\,x-y\rangle\leq\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|\,\|x-y\|.

Combining the two displayed inequalities gives

\lambda\|x-y\|^{2}\leq\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|\,\|x-y\|.

If
x=y
, the desired inequality (
153
) is trivial. If
x\neq y
, divide by
\|x-y\|>0
to obtain

\|x-y\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|,

which is (
153
).
∎

Apply Lemma
9.6
to
f=Q_{\theta}
(which is
\lambda
-strongly concave by Assumption
8.1
)
and the points
x=u=\hat{T}(\theta)
and
y=v=T(\theta)
:

(158)

\|u-v\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\|\nabla Q_{\theta}(u)-\nabla Q_{\theta}(v)\|=\frac{1}{\lambda}\|G(u,\theta)-G(v,\theta)\|.

Step 2 (Justify the first-order conditions at
T(\theta)
and
\hat{T}(\theta)
).

We now show that the maximizer of a differentiable function over an open set must have vanishing gradient.
This is the point where the assumption “
T(\theta),\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}
” is interpreted as “interior maximizers”.

Lemma 9.7

(Interior maximizer implies zero gradient)
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open and let
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable.
If
x^{\star}\in U
is a (local, hence also global) maximizer of
f
over
U
, then
\nabla f(x^{\star})=0
.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction that
\nabla f(x^{\star})\neq 0
.
Let
v:=\nabla f(x^{\star})/\|\nabla f(x^{\star})\|
so that
\|v\|=1
.
Consider the one-dimensional function
\varphi(t):=f(x^{\star}+tv)
.
Since
U
is open and
x^{\star}\in U
, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
x^{\star}+tv\in U
for all
|t|<\epsilon
.
By the chain rule,

\varphi^{\prime}(0)=\langle\nabla f(x^{\star}),v\rangle=\|\nabla f(x^{\star})\|>0.

Hence for sufficiently small
t\in(0,\epsilon)
we have
\varphi(t)>\varphi(0)
, i.e.
f(x^{\star}+tv)>f(x^{\star})
,
contradicting that
x^{\star}
is a maximizer.
Therefore
\nabla f(x^{\star})=0
.
∎

Apply Lemma
9.7
to
f=Q_{\theta}
and
x^{\star}=v=T(\theta)\in B_{S}
(with
B_{S}
open):

(159)

G(v,\theta)=\nabla Q_{\theta}(v)=0.

Similarly, apply it to
f=\hat{Q}_{\theta}
and
x^{\star}=u=\hat{T}(\theta)\in B_{S}
:

(160)

\hat{G}(u,\theta)=\nabla\hat{Q}_{\theta}(u)=0.

Step 3 (Rewrite
\|G(u,\theta)-G(v,\theta)\|
using the FOCs and bound by a uniform gradient perturbation).

Using (
159
) in (
158
), we have

\|u-v\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\|G(u,\theta)-G(v,\theta)\|=\frac{1}{\lambda}\|G(u,\theta)\|.

Now use (
160
) to express
G(u,\theta)
as a
difference
between population and sample gradients:

G(u,\theta)=G(u,\theta)-\hat{G}(u,\theta)\qquad(\text{since }\hat{G}(u,\theta)=0).

Therefore

(161)

\|u-v\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\,\|G(u,\theta)-\hat{G}(u,\theta)\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\,\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\|G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|.

This is exactly the pointwise bound (
152
).

Step 4 (Take suprema over
\theta
to obtain the bound on
\Delta_{n}
).

Since (
161
) holds for every fixed
\theta\in B_{S}
, taking
\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}
yields

\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|.

By definition, the left-hand side is
\Delta_{n}
, giving (
151
). ∎

Remark 9.8

(If
B_{S}
is closed: variational-inequality version)
.

If
B_{S}
is a
closed
convex set, the exact-gradient equalities
G(T(\theta),\theta)=0
and
\hat{G}(\hat{T}(\theta),\theta)=0

need not hold when the maximizers lie on the boundary. In that case the correct first-order condition is the variational inequality

\langle G(T(\theta),\theta),\theta^{\prime}-T(\theta)\rangle\leq 0\quad\forall\,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S},

(and similarly for
\hat{G}
), or equivalently
0\in G(T(\theta),\theta)+N_{B_{S}}(T(\theta))
with the normal cone
N_{B_{S}}
.
One can still obtain perturbation bounds, but the clean
1/\lambda
constant may require additional interiority/margin assumptions
ensuring the maximizers are not on the boundary (which is exactly what the present theorem assumes implicitly via interiority).

Remark 9.9

(Tightness and the factor-of-
2
improvement)
.

The constant
1/\lambda
is best possible from strong concavity alone. For instance, for any map
m(\theta)
consider the quadratic surrogate

Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)=-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\theta^{\prime}-m(\theta)\|^{2},\qquad\hat{Q}(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)=-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\theta^{\prime}-\hat{m}(\theta)\|^{2}.

Then
T(\theta)=m(\theta)
,
\hat{T}(\theta)=\hat{m}(\theta)
, and

G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=-\lambda(\theta^{\prime}-m(\theta)),\qquad\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=-\lambda(\theta^{\prime}-\hat{m}(\theta)).

Hence
\sup_{\theta^{\prime}}\|\hat{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)-G(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\|=\lambda\|\hat{m}(\theta)-m(\theta)\|
and equality holds in
(
151
).

9.3.
Inexact EM: deterministic and stochastic per-iteration errors

In practice, exact E- and M-steps are often replaced by approximate computations:
truncated posterior computations, approximate maximization, or inner-loop optimization.
We model this by allowing an
inexact update map

T_{t}^{\sharp}
at each iteration
t
, with a controlled error.

Definition 9.10

(Inexact slice EM update with additive error)
.

Let
T:B_{S}\to B_{S}
be the population slice EM map.
An inexact update sequence is any sequence
\{\theta_{t}^{\sharp}\}_{t\geq 0}\subseteq B_{S}
satisfying

(162)

\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}=T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})+\varepsilon_{t},

where
\varepsilon_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an error term (deterministic or random).

Theorem 9.11

(Inexact contraction recursion (time-varying errors; sharp))
.

Assume
T:B_{S}\to B_{S}
is a contraction on
B_{S}
in the sense that there exists
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that

(163)

\|T(\theta)-T(\theta^{\prime})\|\leq\gamma\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\qquad\text{for all }\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}.

Let
\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the exact iterates
\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t})
and let
\{\theta_{t}^{\sharp}\}_{t\geq 0}
satisfy the inexact recursion

(164)

\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}=T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})+\varepsilon_{t},\qquad t\geq 0,

for some error sequence
\{\varepsilon_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the iterates remain in
B_{S}

(e.g. because
B_{S}
is forward-invariant and the errors are small enough).
Then for every
t\geq 0
,

(165)

\|\theta_{t}^{\sharp}-\theta_{t}\|\leq\gamma^{t}\|\theta_{0}^{\sharp}-\theta_{0}\|+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\,\|\varepsilon_{k}\|.

In particular, if
\sup_{k}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|\leq\bar{\varepsilon}
, then

(166)

\|\theta_{t}^{\sharp}-\theta_{t}\|\leq\gamma^{t}\|\theta_{0}^{\sharp}-\theta_{0}\|+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,\bar{\varepsilon}\leq\|\theta_{0}^{\sharp}-\theta_{0}\|+\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}}{1-\gamma}.

Moreover, the bound (
165
) is
sharp
in the sense that equality can occur for suitable one-dimensional examples.

Proof.

Step 0 (Define the error process and record what must be shown).

Define the
tracking error

e_{t}:=\theta_{t}^{\sharp}-\theta_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad E_{t}:=\|e_{t}\|\in[0,\infty).

We will derive a scalar inequality for
E_{t}
and then solve it explicitly.

Step 1 (Exact identity for the one-step error; then a one-step inequality).

Start from the two recursions:

\theta_{t+1}=T(\theta_{t}),\qquad\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}=T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})+\varepsilon_{t}.

Subtract the first from the second:

\displaystyle e_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}-\theta_{t+1}=\Big(T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})+\varepsilon_{t}\Big)-T(\theta_{t})

(167)

\displaystyle=\Big(T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})-T(\theta_{t})\Big)+\varepsilon_{t}.

Now take norms and apply the triangle inequality
\|a+b\|\leq\|a\|+\|b\|
:

(168)

E_{t+1}=\|e_{t+1}\|\leq\|T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})-T(\theta_{t})\|+\|\varepsilon_{t}\|.

Finally apply the contraction property (
163
) to the pair
(\theta_{t}^{\sharp},\theta_{t})
(both in
B_{S}
):

\|T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})-T(\theta_{t})\|\leq\gamma\|\theta_{t}^{\sharp}-\theta_{t}\|=\gamma E_{t}.

Substitute into (
168
) to obtain the fundamental one-step inequality

(169)

E_{t+1}\leq\gamma E_{t}+\|\varepsilon_{t}\|,\qquad t\geq 0.

Step 2 (Solve the scalar inhomogeneous recursion explicitly).

We now unroll (
169
) carefully, keeping track of indices.

For
t=0
, (
169
) gives

E_{1}\leq\gamma E_{0}+\|\varepsilon_{0}\|.

For
t=1
,

E_{2}\leq\gamma E_{1}+\|\varepsilon_{1}\|\leq\gamma(\gamma E_{0}+\|\varepsilon_{0}\|)+\|\varepsilon_{1}\|=\gamma^{2}E_{0}+\gamma\|\varepsilon_{0}\|+\|\varepsilon_{1}\|.

For
t=2
,

E_{3}\leq\gamma E_{2}+\|\varepsilon_{2}\|\leq\gamma(\gamma^{2}E_{0}+\gamma\|\varepsilon_{0}\|+\|\varepsilon_{1}\|)+\|\varepsilon_{2}\|=\gamma^{3}E_{0}+\gamma^{2}\|\varepsilon_{0}\|+\gamma\|\varepsilon_{1}\|+\|\varepsilon_{2}\|.

The pattern suggests the general bound

(170)

E_{t}\leq\gamma^{t}E_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|.

We now prove (
170
) by induction on
t
.

Claim 9.12
.

For every integer
t\geq 0
, inequality (
170
) holds.

Proof of Claim
9.12
.

For
t=0
, the sum is empty and interpreted as
0
, so (
170
) reads
E_{0}\leq E_{0}
, which is true.

Assume (
170
) holds for some
t\geq 0
. Apply the one-step recursion (
169
):

E_{t+1}\leq\gamma E_{t}+\|\varepsilon_{t}\|.

Substitute the induction hypothesis for
E_{t}
:

E_{t+1}\leq\gamma\left(\gamma^{t}E_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|\right)+\|\varepsilon_{t}\|=\gamma^{t+1}E_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-k}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|+\|\varepsilon_{t}\|.

Now observe that
\|\varepsilon_{t}\|=\gamma^{t-t}\|\varepsilon_{t}\|
and that

\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-k}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|+\|\varepsilon_{t}\|=\sum_{k=0}^{t}\gamma^{t-k}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|=\sum_{k=0}^{(t+1)-1}\gamma^{(t+1)-1-k}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|.

Therefore

E_{t+1}\leq\gamma^{t+1}E_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t}\gamma^{t-k}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|,

which is exactly (
170
) with
t
replaced by
t+1
.
This completes the induction.
∎

Since
E_{t}=\|\theta_{t}^{\sharp}-\theta_{t}\|
and
E_{0}=\|\theta_{0}^{\sharp}-\theta_{0}\|
, Claim
9.12
is precisely (
165
).

Step 3 (Uniform error specialization).

Assume
\sup_{k\geq 0}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|\leq\bar{\varepsilon}
.
Then each term in the sum in (
165
) is bounded by
\gamma^{t-1-k}\bar{\varepsilon}
, hence

(171)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\|\varepsilon_{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}=\bar{\varepsilon}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{j}=\bar{\varepsilon}\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma},

where we used the change of variables
j=t-1-k
and the finite geometric-series identity.
Substituting (
171
) into (
165
) yields the first inequality in (
166
).
The second inequality in (
166
) follows because
0\leq\gamma^{t}\leq 1
implies

\gamma^{t}\|\theta_{0}^{\sharp}-\theta_{0}\|\leq\|\theta_{0}^{\sharp}-\theta_{0}\|,\qquad\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}.

Step 4 (Sharpness).

The inequality chain used only (i) triangle inequality and (ii) the contraction inequality.
Both can be tight.
For instance, in one dimension with
T(x)=\gamma x
(which is a contraction with factor
\gamma
on any interval containing
0
),
choose
\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}^{\sharp}=0
and take
\varepsilon_{t}\geq 0
for all
t
.
Then the exact iterates satisfy
\theta_{t}\equiv 0
and the inexact iterates satisfy

\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}=\gamma\theta_{t}^{\sharp}+\varepsilon_{t}
, so
e_{t}=\theta_{t}^{\sharp}
and

e_{t}=\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\varepsilon_{k},

which matches (
165
) with equality. ∎

Corollary 9.13

(Inexact EM to a set and IPM transfer)
.

Assume the population contraction-to-set property (
106
) holds on
B_{S}
for
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
with factor
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
and assume the IPM modulus (
78
) holds on
B_{S}
with modulus
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Let
\{\theta_{t}^{\sharp}\}_{t\geq 0}\subseteq B_{S}
be any sequence such that

(172)

\dist\!\big(\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp},\,T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})\big)\leq\eta_{t}\qquad\text{for all }t\geq 0,

for some nonnegative sequence
\{\eta_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.
Then for all
t\geq 0
,

(173)

\dist(\theta_{t}^{\sharp},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\theta_{0}^{\sharp},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\eta_{k},

and consequently

(174)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\left[\theta_{t}^{\sharp}\right],\bar{\Theta}/G\big)\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\left(\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\theta_{0}^{\sharp},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\eta_{k}\right).

Proof.

Part I: derive the perturbed contraction-to-set recursion.

Define
e_{t}:=\dist(\theta_{t}^{\sharp},\bar{\Theta}_{S})
.
We first show

(175)

e_{t+1}\leq\gamma e_{t}+\eta_{t}.

Fix
t\geq 0
. Start from the definition:

e_{t+1}=\dist(\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp},\bar{\Theta}_{S}).

Apply the 1-Lipschitz property of distance-to-set (Lemma
8.3
from your earlier proof):
for any
x,y
,

\dist(x,\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\dist(y,\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\|x-y\|
.
With
x=\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}
and
y=T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})
,

(176)

\dist(\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\dist(T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp}),\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\|\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}-T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})\|.

By the inexactness assumption (
172
),

\|\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}-T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})\|\geq\dist(\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp},T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp}))\quad\text{and hence}\quad\|\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}-T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})\|\leq\eta_{t}

(the last implication holds if (
172
) is strengthened to the norm bound

\|\theta_{t+1}^{\sharp}-T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp})\|\leq\eta_{t}
; if you only assume distance-to-a-point, these are identical because

\dist(x,\{y\})=\|x-y\|
).
Thus the second term in (
176
) is at most
\eta_{t}
.

For the first term, apply contraction-to-set (
106
) with
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
:

\dist(T(\theta_{t}^{\sharp}),\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\gamma\,\dist(\theta_{t}^{\sharp},\bar{\Theta}_{S})=\gamma e_{t}.

Substituting into (
176
) yields (
175
).

Now unroll (
175
) exactly as in Theorem
9.11
(with
\|\varepsilon_{t}\|
replaced by
\eta_{t}
),
to obtain (
173
).

Part II: transfer distance-to-set in parameters to quotient IPM distance-to-set.

Apply the analogue of Lemma
7.7
(as previously proved) at
\theta=\theta_{t}^{\sharp}
:

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}^{\sharp}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\dist(\theta_{t}^{\sharp},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\big)=\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(e_{t}).

As in your quotient arguments, by
G
-invariance and orbit-invariance,

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\left[\theta_{t}^{\sharp}\right],\bar{\Theta}/G\big)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta_{t}^{\sharp}},P_{\bar{\theta}}),

so

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\left[\theta_{t}^{\sharp}\right],\bar{\Theta}/G\big)\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(e_{t}).

Finally substitute the bound (
173
) on
e_{t}
and use monotonicity of
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
to obtain
(
174
). ∎

9.4.
Sample-splitting EM: independent operator errors and sharper probability control

The analysis in Section
8
treats the
fixed-sample
map
\hat{T}
and controls
\Delta_{n}
uniformly on
B_{S}
.
A complementary regime is
sample-splitting EM
: at iteration
t
one uses a fresh batch of data to form an independent map
\hat{T}_{t}
.
While this can be statistically wasteful, it yields cleaner high-probability statements over long horizons because the operator errors are independent across
t
.
This device is standard in nonasymptotic EM analyses as a proof technique
[
1
]
.

Definition 9.14

(Sample-splitting EM)
.

Let the data be partitioned into
m
disjoint blocks of equal size
b
(so
n=mb
).
For each
t\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
, define the empirical surrogate
\hat{Q}_{t}(\cdot;\cdot)
using only block
t
,
and the associated slice update map

\hat{T}_{t}(\theta):=\argmax_{\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}}\hat{Q}_{t}(\theta^{\prime};\theta).

The sample-splitting EM iterates are

\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t}),\qquad t=0,\dots,m-1.

For each
t
, define the per-iteration operator deviation

\Delta_{t}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}_{t}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|.

Because block
t
is independent of
\hat{\theta}_{t}
(which depends only on earlier blocks), one can apply
conditional concentration at each step and then take a union bound over
t
.

Theorem 9.15

(Sample-splitting perturbed contraction bound)
.

Assume the contraction-to-set property (
106
) holds on
B_{S}
for

A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
with factor
\gamma\in(0,1)
, i.e.

(177)

\dist\!\big(T(\theta),A\big)\leq\gamma\,\dist(\theta,A)\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in B_{S}.

Let
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
for
t=0,\dots,m-1
, where each
\hat{T}_{t}:B_{S}\to B_{S}
is a (possibly random)
approximation of
T
and define the per-step operator deviations

(178)

\Delta_{t}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}_{t}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|\in[0,\infty].

Assume
\Delta_{t}<\infty
and
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
for all
t\leq m
(e.g. by a basin invariance lemma).
Then for every
t\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
,

(179)

\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)\leq\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},A)+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\,\Delta_{k}.

Proof.

Step 0 (A basic tool: distance-to-set is 1-Lipschitz).

We use the inequality (proved earlier as Lemma
8.3
):
for any nonempty set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(180)

\dist(x,A)\leq\dist(y,A)+\|x-y\|.

Step 1 (Derive the one-step scalar recursion).

Fix
t\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
and set
e_{t}:=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)
.
Using the iteration
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
,

e_{t+1}=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t+1},A)=\dist(\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A).

Apply (
180
) with
x=\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
and
y=T(\hat{\theta}_{t})
:

(181)

\dist(\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A)\leq\dist(T(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A)+\|\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\hat{\theta}_{t})\|.

We bound the two terms on the right-hand side separately.

(a) Contraction-to-set term.

Since
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
, the contraction-to-set property (
177
) gives

(182)

\dist(T(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A)\leq\gamma\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)=\gamma e_{t}.

(b) Approximation term.

By definition (
178
) of
\Delta_{t}
as a supremum over
\theta\in B_{S}
and since
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
,

(183)

\|\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\hat{\theta}_{t})\|\leq\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}_{t}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|=\Delta_{t}.

Substitute (
182
) and (
183
) into (
181
) to obtain the one-step recursion

(184)

e_{t+1}\leq\gamma e_{t}+\Delta_{t},\qquad t=0,\dots,m-1.

Step 2 (Unroll the time-varying recursion).

We now solve (
184
) explicitly.
We claim that for every
t\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
,

(185)

e_{t}\leq\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\Delta_{k}.

Claim 9.16
.

Inequality (
185
) holds for all
t\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
.

Proof of Claim
9.16
.

For
t=0
, the sum is empty (interpreted as
0
), and (
185
) reads
e_{0}\leq e_{0}
.
Assume (
185
) holds at time
t
with
t\leq m-1
.
Then by (
184
),

e_{t+1}\leq\gamma e_{t}+\Delta_{t}\leq\gamma\left(\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\Delta_{k}\right)+\Delta_{t}=\gamma^{t+1}e_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-k}\Delta_{k}+\Delta_{t}.

Since
\Delta_{t}=\gamma^{t-t}\Delta_{t}
, the last two terms combine as

\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-k}\Delta_{k}+\Delta_{t}=\sum_{k=0}^{t}\gamma^{t-k}\Delta_{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{(t+1)-1}\gamma^{(t+1)-1-k}\Delta_{k}.

Thus

e_{t+1}\leq\gamma^{t+1}e_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t}\gamma^{t-k}\Delta_{k},

which is exactly (
185
) with
t
replaced by
t+1
.
This completes the induction.
∎

Finally, recalling that
e_{t}=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)
and
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
, we obtain (
179
).
∎

Theorem 9.17

(Sample-splitting EM in a general IPM: uniform high-probability bound)
.

Assume:

(i)

(
IPM modulus on the slice.
) For all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in B_{S}
,

(186)

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|),

where
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is nondecreasing and (without loss) right-continuous.

(ii)

(
Per-step uniform operator deviation control.
) For each
t\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
and each
\delta\in(0,1)
,

(187)

\Pr\!\big(\Delta_{t}\leq a_{b}(\delta)\big)\ \geq\ 1-\delta,

where
\Delta_{t}:=\sup_{\theta\in B_{S}}\|\hat{T}_{t}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|
(cf. (
178
))
and
a_{b}(\delta)
depends on the block size
b
.

(iii)

(
Forward invariance on the horizon.
) With probability one (or on the same event we work on below),
the iterates
\hat{\theta}_{t}
remain in
B_{S}
for all
t\leq m
.
(For instance, this holds whenever a basin invariance lemma applies, e.g. Lemma
9.3
under
\Delta_{t}\leq r
.)

(iv)

(
Population contraction-to-set.
) The population map
T
satisfies, for
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
and all
\theta\in B_{S}
,

(188)

\dist\!\big(T(\theta),A\big)\leq\gamma\,\dist(\theta,A)

with a factor
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

Consider the sample-splitting iterates
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
for
t=0,\dots,m-1
and define
\hat{\xi}_{t}:=\left[\hat{\theta}_{t}\right]
.
Then for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,
simultaneously for all

t\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
,

(189)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\hat{\xi}_{t},\bar{\Theta}/G\big)\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\left(\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,a_{b}(\delta/m)\right).

Proof.

Step 0 (Two reusable facts: a Lipschitz property and the quotient reduction).

Fact A (distance-to-set is 1-Lipschitz).

For any nonempty
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(190)

\dist(x,A)\leq\dist(y,A)+\|x-y\|.

Fact B (quotient IPM distance-to-set equals a slice infimum).

By
G
-invariance of
\bar{\Theta}
and orbit-invariance of the model (as in Theorem
8.12
),
for every
\theta\in B_{S}
,

(191)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\left[\theta\right],\bar{\Theta}/G\big)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}})=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\theta},P_{\bar{\theta}}).

The second equality uses that every orbit in
\bar{\Theta}/G
that meets the local chart has a unique representative in
\bar{\Theta}_{S}

and that replacing
\bar{\theta}
by an orbit-equivalent representative does not change
P_{\bar{\theta}}
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
for the remainder of the proof.

Step 1 (Construct a single high-probability event controlling all steps).

For each
t\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
define

E_{t}:=\{\Delta_{t}\leq a_{b}(\delta/m)\}.

By (
187
) with
\delta/m
in place of
\delta
,

\Pr(E_{t})\geq 1-\delta/m\qquad\text{for each }t.

By the union bound,

(192)

\Pr\Big(E:=\bigcap_{t=0}^{m-1}E_{t}\Big)\geq 1-\sum_{t=0}^{m-1}\Pr(E_{t}^{c})\geq 1-\sum_{t=0}^{m-1}\frac{\delta}{m}=1-\delta.

Hence, on the event
E
(which occurs with probability at least
1-\delta
), we have the
simultaneous
uniform bound

(193)

\Delta_{t}\leq a_{b}(\delta/m)\qquad\text{for all }t=0,\dots,m-1.

Step 2 (Derive the time-varying perturbed contraction-to-set recursion on
E
).

Work on the event
E
and fix any
t\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
.
We first establish the one-step inequality

(194)

\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t+1},A)\leq\gamma\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)+\Delta_{t},\qquad A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}.

Indeed,

\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t+1},A)=\dist(\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A)\leq\dist(T(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A)+\|\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\hat{\theta}_{t})\|

by (
190
) with
x=\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t})
and
y=T(\hat{\theta}_{t})
.
Now, since
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in B_{S}
by forward invariance (assumption (iii)),
the contraction-to-set property (
188
) yields

\dist(T(\hat{\theta}_{t}),A)\leq\gamma\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A),

and by definition of
\Delta_{t}
as a supremum over
B_{S}
,

\|\hat{T}_{t}(\hat{\theta}_{t})-T(\hat{\theta}_{t})\|\leq\Delta_{t}.

Substituting these bounds proves (
194
).

Step 3 (Unroll the recursion with explicit index bookkeeping).

Define
e_{t}:=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)\geq 0
. Then (
194
) becomes

(195)

e_{t+1}\leq\gamma e_{t}+\Delta_{t},\qquad t=0,\dots,m-1.

We claim that for every
t\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
,

(196)

e_{t}\leq\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\Delta_{k}.

Claim 9.18
.

Inequality (
196
) holds for all
t\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
.

Proof of Claim
9.18
.

For
t=0
, the sum is empty and (
196
) reads
e_{0}\leq e_{0}
.
Assume (
196
) holds for some
t\leq m-1
.
Then by (
195
),

e_{t+1}\leq\gamma e_{t}+\Delta_{t}\leq\gamma\left(\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\Delta_{k}\right)+\Delta_{t}=\gamma^{t+1}e_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-k}\Delta_{k}+\Delta_{t}.

Since
\Delta_{t}=\gamma^{t-t}\Delta_{t}
, the last two terms combine into

\sum_{k=0}^{t}\gamma^{t-k}\Delta_{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{(t+1)-1}\gamma^{(t+1)-1-k}\Delta_{k}
,
so

e_{t+1}\leq\gamma^{t+1}e_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t}\gamma^{t-k}\Delta_{k},

which is (
196
) with
t
replaced by
t+1
.
∎

Thus (
196
) holds. Now invoke the uniform bound (
193
) on
E
:
for each
t\in\{0,\dots,m\}
,

\displaystyle e_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}\Delta_{k}\leq\gamma^{t}e_{0}+a_{b}(\delta/m)\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-k}

(197)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{t}e_{0}+a_{b}(\delta/m)\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\gamma^{j}=\gamma^{t}e_{0}+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,a_{b}(\delta/m),

where we used the change of variables
j=t-1-k
and the finite geometric-series identity.

Recalling
e_{t}=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},A)
and
A=\bar{\Theta}_{S}
gives, on
E
,

(198)

\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\leq\gamma^{t}\,\dist(\hat{\theta}_{0},\bar{\Theta}_{S})+\frac{1-\gamma^{t}}{1-\gamma}\,a_{b}(\delta/m).

Step 4 (Convert parameter distance-to-set into quotient IPM distance-to-set).

Fix
t\in\{0,\dots,m\}
and work on the event
E
.
By Fact B (
191
),

(199)

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\left[\hat{\theta}_{t}\right],\bar{\Theta}/G\big)=\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}}).

Let
r_{t}:=\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})
.
By the definition of infimum, for each
\epsilon>0
there exists
\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}
such that

(200)

\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}\|\leq r_{t}+\epsilon.

Apply the modulus (
186
) and monotonicity of
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
:

d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\big(\|\hat{\theta}_{t}-\bar{\theta}_{t,\epsilon}\|\big)\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r_{t}+\epsilon).

Since the left-hand side is an upper bound on the infimum over
\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}
, we obtain

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r_{t}+\epsilon)\qquad\text{for all }\epsilon>0.

Let
\epsilon\downarrow 0
and use right-continuity of
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}
to conclude

(201)

\inf_{\bar{\theta}\in\bar{\Theta}_{S}}d_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\hat{\theta}_{t}},P_{\bar{\theta}})\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}(r_{t})=\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\big(\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\big).

Combining (
199
) and (
201
) yields, on
E
,

\dist_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\left[\hat{\theta}_{t}\right],\bar{\Theta}/G\big)\leq\omega_{\mathcal{F}}\!\big(\dist(\hat{\theta}_{t},\bar{\Theta}_{S})\big).

Finally substitute (
198
) into the right-hand side and use monotonicity of
\omega_{\mathcal{F}}

to obtain (
189
) on
E
.

Step 5 (Conclude the probability statement and uniformity in
t
).

By (
192
),
\Pr(E)\geq 1-\delta
.
All inequalities in Steps 2–4 hold simultaneously for all
t\in\{0,\dots,m\}
on the
same
event
E

(because (
193
) is uniform in
t
and the recursion is deterministic given the
\Delta_{t}
’s).
Hence, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the bound (
189
) holds simultaneously for all
t\leq m
.
∎

Remark 9.19

(Comparison with fixed-sample EM)
.

Fixed-sample EM reuses the same
n
observations across all iterations and can yield smaller
per-iteration
statistical error,
but the induced dependence between
(\hat{\theta}_{t})
and the empirical fluctuations typically forces either
(i) uniform empirical-process control that is stable over a data-dependent trajectory, or (ii) stability/algorithmic arguments.
Sample-splitting EM avoids these issues: conditional on the past, each update uses fresh data, so per-step bounds such as
(
187
) follow from a one-shot empirical-process inequality at block size
b
.
The cost is explicit in (
189
): a
\delta/m
union-bound penalty and statistical scaling
1/\sqrt{b}

(with
b\approx n/m
if the sample is split evenly).
Which regime is preferable depends on the horizon
m
and whether one can obtain fixed-sample bounds that hold uniformly over
t
.

Appendix A
Measure-theoretic and conditional-expectation preliminaries

Throughout, let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
and
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
be measurable spaces for the observed and latent variables, respectively, and let
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a parameter set. Write
P_{\theta}^{XZ}
for the joint law of
(X,Z)
under parameter
\theta
, and
P_{\theta}^{X}
for the marginal law of
X
. The data-generating law of
X
is denoted by
P^{\star}
(not assumed to lie in the model).

For any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
, we use the standard EM functionals

\Phi(\theta)\ :=\ \mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X)\big],\qquad Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\ :=\ \mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\big]\Big],

whenever the expectations are well-defined as extended real numbers (possibly equal to
-\infty
). Here
\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,\cdot)
denotes a regular conditional law of
Z
given
X=x
under parameter
\theta^{\prime}
(constructed below), and
p_{\theta}
denotes a chosen density under domination (Assumption
A.1
).

Extended-real integration conventions.

For a measurable function
h:\Omega\to[-\infty,\infty]
and a measure
\mathsf{M}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
, the integral
\int h\,d\mathsf{M}
is defined iff at least one of
\int h_{+}\,d\mathsf{M}
and
\int h_{-}\,d\mathsf{M}
is finite; in that case
\int h\,d\mathsf{M}:=\int h_{+}\,d\mathsf{M}-\int h_{-}\,d\mathsf{M}\in[-\infty,\infty]
. We also use
0\log 0:=0
and, when needed,
0\log(0/q):=0
for
q>0
.

A.1.
Dominating measures and versions of conditional laws

We impose a standing domination/regularity condition ensuring densities and conditional laws can be manipulated via kernels.

Assumption A.1

(Joint domination and standard Borel latent space)
.

There exist
\sigma
-finite measures
\mu
on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
and
\nu
on
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
such that, for every
\theta\in\Theta
,

P_{\theta}^{XZ}\ \ll\ \mu\otimes\nu,\qquad\text{with density}\qquad p_{\theta}(x,z)\ :=\ \frac{dP_{\theta}^{XZ}}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)}(x,z).

Moreover,
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
is a standard Borel space.

A basic measurability fact (used repeatedly).

Lemma A.2

(Measurability of section integrals)
.

Let
h:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to[0,\infty]
be
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
-measurable and let
\nu
be
\sigma
-finite on
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
. Then the map

x\ \longmapsto\ \int_{\mathsf{Z}}h(x,z)\,\nu(dz)\in[0,\infty]

is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable (as an extended real-valued function).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the class of all nonnegative measurable functions
h
such that

x\mapsto\int h(x,z)\nu(dz)
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable. We show
\mathcal{H}
contains all nonnegative
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
-measurable functions.

Step 1 (rectangles).
If
h=\mathbf{1}_{A\times B}
with
A\in\mathcal{F}
,
B\in\mathcal{G}
, then

\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\mathbf{1}_{A\times B}(x,z)\nu(dz)=\mathbf{1}_{A}(x)\nu(B),

which is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable. Hence indicators of measurable rectangles lie in
\mathcal{H}
.

Step 2 (simple functions).
By linearity, all finite linear combinations of rectangle indicators (i.e. nonnegative simple functions on
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}
) lie in
\mathcal{H}
.

Step 3 (monotone limits).
Suppose
h_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
and
h_{n}\uparrow h
pointwise. Then for every
x
,

\int h_{n}(x,z)\nu(dz)\uparrow\int h(x,z)\nu(dz)

by monotone convergence in
z
. The pointwise supremum of measurable functions is measurable, so
h\in\mathcal{H}
.

Step 4 (conclusion).
Every nonnegative
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
-measurable
h
is the pointwise limit of an increasing sequence of nonnegative simple functions. By Steps 2–3,
h\in\mathcal{H}
.
∎

Marginal densities.

Under Assumption
A.1
, the
X
-marginal is also dominated:
P_{\theta}^{X}\ll\mu
, with density

(202)

p_{\theta}(x)\ :=\ \frac{dP_{\theta}^{X}}{d\mu}(x)\ =\ \int_{\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz),\qquad\mu\text{-a.e. }x,

where the integral is well-defined in
[0,\infty]
.

Proof of (
202
).

Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and define
\tilde{p}_{\theta}(x):=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\nu(dz)\in[0,\infty]
. By Lemma
A.2
,
\tilde{p}_{\theta}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

Let
A\in\mathcal{F}
. Using the definition of the marginal and the Radon–Nikodym representation of
P_{\theta}^{XZ}
,

P_{\theta}^{X}(A)=P_{\theta}^{XZ}(A\times\mathsf{Z})=\int_{A\times\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,(\mu\otimes\nu)(dx,dz).

Since
p_{\theta}\geq 0
, Tonelli’s theorem applies:

\int_{A\times\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,(\mu\otimes\nu)(dx,dz)=\int_{A}\Big(\int_{\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\nu(dz)\Big)\mu(dx)=\int_{A}\tilde{p}_{\theta}(x)\,\mu(dx).

Thus
P_{\theta}^{X}(A)=\int_{A}\tilde{p}_{\theta}\,d\mu
for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
, which implies
P_{\theta}^{X}\ll\mu
with Radon–Nikodym derivative
dP_{\theta}^{X}/d\mu=\tilde{p}_{\theta}

\mu
-a.e., i.e. (
202
).
∎

Regular conditional laws.

We next construct a regular conditional probability of
Z
given
X
under
P_{\theta}^{XZ}
. The construction is completely explicit (via a Borel isomorphism to a Borel subset of
[0,1]
and conditional distribution functions), and yields the full disintegration identity.

Proposition A.3

(Existence of a regular conditional law)
.

Assume
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
is standard Borel. Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and let
(X,Z)\sim P_{\theta}^{XZ}
on some probability space. Then there exists a Markov kernel

\kappa_{\theta}:\ \mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{G}\to[0,1]

such that for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
and
B\in\mathcal{G}
,

(203)

P_{\theta}^{XZ}(A\times B)\ =\ \int_{A}\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx).

Moreover, if
\tilde{\kappa}_{\theta}
is another kernel satisfying (
203
), then for every
B\in\mathcal{G}
,

\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)=\tilde{\kappa}_{\theta}(x,B)\quad\text{for }P_{\theta}^{X}\text{-a.e.\ }x.

Proof.

We proceed in five steps.

Step 1 (reduce to a Borel subset of
[0,1]
).

Since
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
is standard Borel, there exists a Borel subset
E\subseteq[0,1]
and a bimeasurable bijection (Borel isomorphism)
b:\mathsf{Z}\to E
such that both
b
and
b^{-1}
are measurable. Define
Y:=b(Z)
taking values in
E\subseteq[0,1]
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be the trace
\sigma
-field on
E
:
\mathcal{E}=\{E\cap B:B\in\mathcal{B}([0,1])\}
. Then
Y
is
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{E})
-measurable and
\sigma(Y)=\sigma(Z)
.

It suffices to construct a regular conditional distribution of
Y
given
X
, say
\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
on
(E,\mathcal{E})
, and then define for
B\in\mathcal{G}

\kappa_{\theta}(x,B):=\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,b(B)).

Measurability and the disintegration identity then transfer by the bimeasurability of
b
.

Step 2 (conditional distribution functions on rational cutpoints).

For each rational
q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
, define the random variable
\mathbf{1}_{\{Y\leq q\}}
and take its conditional expectation given
\sigma(X)
:

g_{q}(X)\ :=\ \mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\{Y\leq q\}}\mid\sigma(X)\big].

By definition,
g_{q}(X)
is
\sigma(X)
-measurable and satisfies, for all
A\in\sigma(X)
,

(204)

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{A}g_{q}(X)\big]\ =\ \mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{A}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y\leq q\}}\big].

Since
g_{q}(X)
is
\sigma(X)
-measurable, there exists an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable function
F_{q}:\mathsf{X}\to[0,1]
such that

F_{q}(X)=g_{q}(X)\quad\text{a.s.}

(Indeed,
\sigma(X)
-measurability of
g_{q}(X)
means
g_{q}(X)=\tilde{F}_{q}(X)
for some
\tilde{F}_{q}
measurable on
\mathsf{X}
; choose one such version and call it
F_{q}
.)

Because
\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
is countable, we can (and do) choose the versions
F_{q}
simultaneously for all rationals.

Step 3 (enforce monotonicity and right-continuity in
q
pointwise in
x
).

For rationals
q_{1}<q_{2}
, we have
\mathbf{1}_{\{Y\leq q_{1}\}}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\{Y\leq q_{2}\}}
, hence by monotonicity of conditional expectation

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{Y\leq q_{1}\}}\mid\sigma(X)]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{Y\leq q_{2}\}}\mid\sigma(X)]\quad\text{a.s.}

Thus
F_{q_{1}}(X)\leq F_{q_{2}}(X)
a.s. For each pair
(q_{1},q_{2})
this inequality may fail on a null set; by countability of pairs of rationals, there exists a single
P_{\theta}^{X}
-null set
N\subseteq\mathsf{X}
such that for all
x\notin N
and all rationals
q_{1}<q_{2}
,

(205)

F_{q_{1}}(x)\leq F_{q_{2}}(x).

For each
x\notin N
, define a function
F_{x}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
by

(206)

F_{x}(t)\ :=\ \inf\{F_{q}(x):q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1],\ q>t\},

with the convention that the infimum over an empty set is
1
(which occurs only at
t=1
). For
x\in N
, define
F_{x}(t):=\mathbf{1}_{\{t\geq 1\}}
(any distribution function will do; this set will be null under
P_{\theta}^{X}
).

For each fixed
x\notin N
, the function
t\mapsto F_{x}(t)
is nondecreasing and right-continuous on
[0,1]
:
- nondecreasing: if
t_{1}<t_{2}
, then
\{q>t_{2}\}\subseteq\{q>t_{1}\}
, hence
\inf_{q>t_{1}}F_{q}(x)\leq\inf_{q>t_{2}}F_{q}(x)
;
- right-continuity: for
t_{n}\downarrow t
, the sets
\{q>t_{n}\}
increase to
\{q>t\}
, so the infima decrease to the infimum over
\{q>t\}
, which equals
F_{x}(t)
by definition.

Also, for
t<0
define
F_{x}(t):=0
and for
t>1
define
F_{x}(t):=1
; then
F_{x}
is a distribution function on
\mathbb{R}
.

Step 4 (define a measure from
F_{x}
and verify measurability in
x
).

For each
x\notin N
, define a probability measure
\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
on
([0,1],\mathcal{B}([0,1]))
by the standard Lebesgue–Stieltjes construction:

\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,(a,b])\ :=\ F_{x}(b)-F_{x}(a),\qquad 0\leq a<b\leq 1,

and extend uniquely to
\mathcal{B}([0,1])
(Carathéodory extension theorem). Since
F_{x}
is right-continuous and nondecreasing with
F_{x}(0^{-})=0
and
F_{x}(1)=1
, the resulting measure is a probability measure. For
x\in N
, set
\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
equal to any fixed probability measure on
[0,1]
(e.g.
\delta_{0}
).

Now restrict to
E\subseteq[0,1]
by defining for
C\in\mathcal{E}
,

\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,C)\ :=\ \kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,\tilde{C}),

where
\tilde{C}\in\mathcal{B}([0,1])
is any Borel set with
\tilde{C}\cap E=C
; this is well-defined because
\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
can be replaced by its restriction to
E
(equivalently, consider the pushforward measure on
E
).

We now verify the kernel measurability: for each
C\in\mathcal{E}
, the map
x\mapsto\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,C)
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
It suffices to check this on a
\pi
-system generating
\mathcal{E}
and then extend by the monotone class theorem.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be the collection of half-intervals in
E
of the form

(-\infty,t]\cap E,\qquad t\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1].

This is a
\pi
-system generating
\mathcal{E}
(since
E
is a Borel subset of
[0,1]
and such intervals generate
\mathcal{B}([0,1])
). For
t\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
and
x\notin N
, we claim

(207)

\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}\bigl(x,(-\infty,t]\cap E\bigr)=F_{t}(x).

Indeed, by definition of
F_{x}
and right-continuity regularization, for rational
t
we have

F_{x}(t)=\inf_{q>t,\ q\in\mathbb{Q}}F_{q}(x)=F_{t}(x)

because monotonicity (
205
) implies
F_{q}(x)\downarrow F_{t}(x)
as
q\downarrow t
along rationals, hence the infimum equals the limit
F_{t}(x)
. For a distribution function
F_{x}
, the associated Stieltjes measure satisfies
\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,(-\infty,t])=F_{x}(t)
, so (
207
) holds.

Since each
F_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable, the map
x\mapsto\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,I)
is measurable for each
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Let

\mathcal{M}:=\{C\in\mathcal{E}:\ x\mapsto\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,C)\ \text{is }\mathcal{F}\text{-measurable}\}.

Then
\mathcal{M}
is a monotone class: if
C_{n}\uparrow C
with
C_{n}\in\mathcal{M}
, then
\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,C_{n})\uparrow\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,C)
(continuity from below), so measurability passes to the limit; similarly for
C_{n}\downarrow C
using continuity from above and boundedness by
1
. Since
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
and
\sigma(\mathcal{I})=\mathcal{E}
, the monotone class theorem yields
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{E}
. Thus
x\mapsto\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,C)
is measurable for all
C\in\mathcal{E}
, proving that
\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}
is a kernel.

Step 5 (verify the defining disintegration identity).

We show that for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
and all
C\in\mathcal{E}
,

(208)

\mathbb{P}\big(X\in A,\ Y\in C\big)\ =\ \int_{A}\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,C)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx).

It suffices to verify (
208
) on the generating
\pi
-system
\mathcal{I}
and then extend by a
\lambda
-system argument.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
and let
C_{t}:=(-\infty,t]\cap E\in\mathcal{I}
. By (
207
),

\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,C_{t})=F_{t}(x)\quad\text{for }P_{\theta}^{X}\text{-a.e.\ }x.

Therefore, for any
A\in\mathcal{F}
,

\int_{A}\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,C_{t})\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx)=\int_{A}F_{t}(x)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx)=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\{X\in A\}}F_{t}(X)\big].

By construction
F_{t}(X)=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{Y\leq t\}}\mid\sigma(X)]
a.s., hence by the defining property of conditional expectation (
204
),

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\{X\in A\}}F_{t}(X)\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\{X\in A\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y\leq t\}}\big]=\mathbb{P}(X\in A,\ Y\in C_{t}).

Thus (
208
) holds for all
C_{t}\in\mathcal{I}
.

Now define

\Lambda:=\{C\in\mathcal{E}:\ \text{\eqref{eq:disintegration-Y} holds for all }A\in\mathcal{F}\}.

We show
\Lambda
is a
\lambda
-system: clearly
E\in\Lambda
since both sides equal
P_{\theta}^{X}(A)
; if
C_{1}\subseteq C_{2}
with
C_{1},C_{2}\in\Lambda
, then by additivity of probability measures and of
x\mapsto\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
,

\mathbb{P}(X\in A,\ Y\in C_{2}\setminus C_{1})=\mathbb{P}(X\in A,\ Y\in C_{2})-\mathbb{P}(X\in A,\ Y\in C_{1})

and similarly for the integral, so
C_{2}\setminus C_{1}\in\Lambda
; and if
C_{n}\uparrow C
, then both sides of (
208
) converge to the corresponding limit by monotone convergence (bounded by
1
), hence
C\in\Lambda
. Since
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\Lambda
and
\sigma(\mathcal{I})=\mathcal{E}
, the
\pi
-
\lambda
theorem yields
\Lambda=\mathcal{E}
, establishing (
208
) for all
C\in\mathcal{E}
.

Finally, return to
Z
via
b
. For
B\in\mathcal{G}
, set
\kappa_{\theta}(x,B):=\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,b(B))
. Then measurability in
x
and the disintegration identity transfer immediately:

\mathbb{P}(X\in A,\ Z\in B)=\mathbb{P}(X\in A,\ Y\in b(B))=\int_{A}\kappa^{Y}_{\theta}(x,b(B))\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx)=\int_{A}\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx),

which is (
203
).

Uniqueness.

Let
\kappa_{\theta}
and
\tilde{\kappa}_{\theta}
satisfy (
203
). Fix
B\in\mathcal{G}
and define
h(x):=\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)-\tilde{\kappa}_{\theta}(x,B)
, which is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable. For every
A\in\mathcal{F}
,

\int_{A}h(x)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx)=\int_{A}\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx)-\int_{A}\tilde{\kappa}_{\theta}(x,B)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx)=P_{\theta}^{XZ}(A\times B)-P_{\theta}^{XZ}(A\times B)=0.

Taking
A=\{h>0\}
gives
\int_{\{h>0\}}h\,dP_{\theta}^{X}=0
, but
h\geq 0
on
\{h>0\}
, hence
P_{\theta}^{X}(h>0)=0
. Similarly
P_{\theta}^{X}(h<0)=0
. Therefore
P_{\theta}^{X}(h\neq 0)=0
, i.e.
\kappa_{\theta}(\cdot,B)=\tilde{\kappa}_{\theta}(\cdot,B)

P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.s.
∎

Density-based version.

When
p_{\theta}(x)\in(0,\infty)
, a canonical kernel is obtained by normalizing the joint density.

Lemma A.4

(Conditional density representation)
.

Assume Assumption
A.1
. Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and define
p_{\theta}(x)
by (
202
). For
x
such that
p_{\theta}(x)\in(0,\infty)
define

(209)

p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\ :=\ \frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}.

Then for
\mu
-a.e.
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)\in(0,\infty)
:

(1)

z\mapsto p_{\theta}(z\mid x)
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable, nonnegative, and
\int p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)=1
;

(2)

\kappa_{\theta}(x,B):=\int_{B}p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)
defines a kernel on
\{p_{\theta}>0\}
;

(3)

extending
\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
arbitrarily on
\{p_{\theta}=0\}
yields a kernel satisfying (
203
).

Proof.

(1)
Joint measurability of
(x,z)\mapsto p_{\theta}(x,z)
and measurability of
x\mapsto p_{\theta}(x)
imply measurability of
(x,z)\mapsto p_{\theta}(x,z)/p_{\theta}(x)
on
\{p_{\theta}(x)>0\}
; in particular for each fixed
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)>0
,
z\mapsto p_{\theta}(z\mid x)
is measurable and nonnegative. Moreover,

\int_{\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)=\frac{1}{p_{\theta}(x)}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\nu(dz)=\frac{p_{\theta}(x)}{p_{\theta}(x)}=1

by (
202
).

(2)
For each
B\in\mathcal{G}
, the map
x\mapsto\int_{B}p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable on
\{p_{\theta}>0\}
by Lemma
A.2
applied to
h(x,z)=\mathbf{1}_{B}(z)p_{\theta}(z\mid x)
(extend by
0
on
\{p_{\theta}=0\}
if desired). For each such
x
,
B\mapsto\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)
is a probability measure since it is given by integrating a density.

(3)
On
\{p_{\theta}=0\}
define
\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
as any fixed probability measure; this preserves the kernel property. Since
P_{\theta}^{X}\ll\mu
with density
p_{\theta}
, we have
P_{\theta}^{X}(\{p_{\theta}=0\})=0
. Finally, for
A\in\mathcal{F}
,
B\in\mathcal{G}
,

\int_{A}\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx)=\int_{A}\Big(\int_{B}\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}\nu(dz)\Big)p_{\theta}(x)\mu(dx)=\int_{A\times B}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,(\mu\otimes\nu)(dx,dz)=P_{\theta}^{XZ}(A\times B),

where Tonelli applies (nonnegative integrand). This is (
203
).
∎

Kernel–density equivalence.

Conversely, specifying a marginal density and a conditional density kernel reconstructs a dominated joint law.

Lemma A.5

(Reconstruction from marginal and conditional)
.

Fix
\theta
and suppose we are given:
(i) a nonnegative measurable
p_{\theta}:\mathsf{X}\to[0,\infty)
with
\int p_{\theta}\,d\mu=1
; and
(ii) a measurable function
p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid\cdot):\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to[0,\infty)
such that for each
x
,
z\mapsto p_{\theta}(z\mid x)
is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable and
\int p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)=1
.
Define

(210)

p_{\theta}(x,z)\ :=\ p_{\theta}(x)\,p_{\theta}(z\mid x).

Then
p_{\theta}(x,z)
is a
(\mu\otimes\nu)
-density, hence defines a probability law
P_{\theta}^{XZ}\ll\mu\otimes\nu
. Moreover: (a) its
X
-marginal has density
p_{\theta}(x)
; and (b) a regular conditional law of
Z
given
X=x
is
\kappa_{\theta}(x,dz)=p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)
.

Proof.

Measurability and nonnegativity of
p_{\theta}(x,z)
are immediate. Its total mass is

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,(\mu\otimes\nu)(dx,dz)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}p_{\theta}(x)\Big(\int_{\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)\Big)\mu(dx)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}p_{\theta}(x)\mu(dx)=1,

by Tonelli. Thus
p_{\theta}
is a probability density on
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}
.

For the marginal, for any
A\in\mathcal{F}
,

P_{\theta}^{X}(A)=P_{\theta}^{XZ}(A\times\mathsf{Z})=\int_{A\times\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(x)p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\,(\mu\otimes\nu)(dx,dz)=\int_{A}p_{\theta}(x)\mu(dx),

since the inner integral over
\mathsf{Z}
is
1
. Hence the marginal density is
p_{\theta}(x)
.

For the conditional law, define
\kappa_{\theta}(x,B):=\int_{B}p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)
. Then for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
,
B\in\mathcal{G}
,

\int_{A}\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx)=\int_{A}\Big(\int_{B}p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)\Big)p_{\theta}(x)\mu(dx)=\int_{A\times B}p_{\theta}(x)p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\,(\mu\otimes\nu)(dx,dz)=P_{\theta}^{XZ}(A\times B),

which is exactly the disintegration identity (
203
).
∎

A.2.
Interchange of integrals, limits, and differentiation

This subsection records the routine interchange steps used repeatedly for
\Phi
and
Q
.

Kernel integrals and joint measures.

Definition A.6

(Kernel)
.

A map
\kappa:\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{G}\to[0,1]
is a (Markov) kernel from
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
to
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
if (i) for each
x\in\mathsf{X}
,
\kappa(x,\cdot)
is a probability measure on
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
, and (ii) for each
B\in\mathcal{G}
,
x\mapsto\kappa(x,B)
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

Lemma A.7

(Measurability of kernel integrals)
.

Let
\kappa
be a kernel and
h:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to[0,\infty]
be
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
-measurable. Then the map

x\ \longmapsto\ \int_{\mathsf{Z}}h(x,z)\,\kappa(x,dz)\in[0,\infty]

is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of all nonnegative measurable
h
for which
x\mapsto\int h(x,z)\kappa(x,dz)
is measurable. We verify
\mathcal{H}
contains a generating class and is closed under monotone limits.

If
h=\mathbf{1}_{A\times B}
with
A\in\mathcal{F}
,
B\in\mathcal{G}
, then

\int\mathbf{1}_{A\times B}(x,z)\kappa(x,dz)=\mathbf{1}_{A}(x)\kappa(x,B),

which is measurable by Definition
A.6
. Hence rectangle indicators lie in
\mathcal{H}
, and by linearity, all nonnegative simple functions lie in
\mathcal{H}
. If
h_{n}\uparrow h
with
h_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
, then by monotone convergence (in
z
),

\int h_{n}(x,z)\kappa(x,dz)\uparrow\int h(x,z)\kappa(x,dz)

pointwise in
x
, and the limit of measurable functions is measurable. Thus
\mathcal{H}
is a monotone class containing all rectangle indicators, hence contains all nonnegative measurable functions.
∎

Definition A.8

(Joint measure induced by a marginal and a kernel)
.

Let
P
be a probability measure on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
and
\kappa
a kernel from
\mathsf{X}
to
\mathsf{Z}
. Define a set function on rectangles by

(P\otimes\kappa)(A\times B)\ :=\ \int_{A}\kappa(x,B)\,P(dx),\qquad A\in\mathcal{F},\ B\in\mathcal{G}.

Then
P\otimes\kappa
extends uniquely to a probability measure on
(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
.

Proof.

We must verify that the rectangle set function is a pre-measure on the algebra generated by rectangles. Fix
B\in\mathcal{G}
. The map
A\mapsto\int_{A}\kappa(x,B)P(dx)
is a (finite) measure on
\mathcal{F}
, because it is integration of the nonnegative measurable function
\kappa(\cdot,B)
. Linearity in
B
is inherited pointwise from the measure property of
\kappa(x,\cdot)
. Standard arguments (check countable additivity on disjoint unions of rectangles and extend by Carathéodory) yield a unique measure on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}
. Total mass is

(P\otimes\kappa)(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z})=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\kappa(x,\mathsf{Z})\,P(dx)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}1\,P(dx)=1,

so the extension is a probability measure.
∎

Lemma A.9

(Tonelli/Fubini for nested expectations)
.

Let
h:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to[-\infty,\infty]
be
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
-measurable, let
P
be a probability measure on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
, and let
\kappa
be a kernel from
\mathsf{X}
to
\mathsf{Z}
.

(1)

If
h\geq 0
, then

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}}h(x,z)\,(P\otimes\kappa)(dx,dz)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h(x,z)\,\kappa(x,dz)\,P(dx)\in[0,\infty].

(2)

If
\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}}|h|\,d(P\otimes\kappa)<\infty
, then

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}}h\,d(P\otimes\kappa)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h(x,z)\,\kappa(x,dz)\,P(dx)

and the integral is finite. In particular, iterated integration is unambiguous and standard Fubini manipulations are valid.

Proof.

(1)
Let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of all nonnegative measurable
h
for which the identity holds. For
h=\mathbf{1}_{A\times B}
,

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}}\mathbf{1}_{A\times B}\,d(P\otimes\kappa)=(P\otimes\kappa)(A\times B)=\int_{A}\kappa(x,B)P(dx)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\mathbf{1}_{A\times B}(x,z)\kappa(x,dz)P(dx),

so rectangle indicators are in
\mathcal{H}
. By linearity, nonnegative simple functions are in
\mathcal{H}
.

If
h_{n}\uparrow h
pointwise with
h_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
, then by monotone convergence applied to the measure
P\otimes\kappa
,

\int h_{n}\,d(P\otimes\kappa)\uparrow\int h\,d(P\otimes\kappa),

and by monotone convergence applied inside the iterated integral (nonnegative integrand),

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h_{n}(x,z)\kappa(x,dz)P(dx)\uparrow\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h(x,z)\kappa(x,dz)P(dx).

Thus the identity passes to the limit, so
\mathcal{H}
is closed under monotone limits. Since every nonnegative measurable function is an increasing limit of nonnegative simple functions,
\mathcal{H}
contains all
h\geq 0
.

(2)
Write
h=h_{+}-h_{-}
with
h_{\pm}\geq 0
measurable. If
\int|h|\,d(P\otimes\kappa)<\infty
, then both
\int h_{+}\,d(P\otimes\kappa)
and
\int h_{-}\,d(P\otimes\kappa)
are finite. By part (1) applied to
h_{+}
and
h_{-}
,

\int h_{\pm}\,d(P\otimes\kappa)=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h_{\pm}(x,z)\kappa(x,dz)P(dx).

Subtracting yields the desired identity for
h
, and finiteness follows.
∎

Lemma A.10

(Dominated convergence under nested expectations)
.

Let
h_{n},h:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable with
h_{n}(x,z)\to h(x,z)
pointwise.
Let
P
be a probability on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
and
\kappa
a kernel from
\mathsf{X}
to
\mathsf{Z}
.
Assume there exists an envelope
H:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to[0,\infty)
such that
|h_{n}|\leq H
for all
n
and

\int_{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}}H(x,z)\,(P\otimes\kappa)(dx,dz)\ <\ \infty.

Then

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h_{n}(x,z)\,\kappa(x,dz)\,P(dx)\ \to\ \int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h(x,z)\,\kappa(x,dz)\,P(dx).

Proof.

By Lemma
A.9
(2), since
H
is integrable under
P\otimes\kappa
, each
h_{n}
and
h
is integrable and

\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h_{n}\,d\kappa\,dP=\int h_{n}\,d(P\otimes\kappa),\qquad\int_{\mathsf{X}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h\,d\kappa\,dP=\int h\,d(P\otimes\kappa).

Apply the standard dominated convergence theorem to the probability space
(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G},P\otimes\kappa)
with dominating function
H
to obtain
\int h_{n}\,d(P\otimes\kappa)\to\int h\,d(P\otimes\kappa)
. Translating back yields the claimed convergence of iterated integrals.
∎

Differentiation under marginalization.

We state a vector-valued Leibniz rule sufficient for differentiating
p_{\theta}(x)=\int p_{\theta}(x,z)\nu(dz)
.

Assumption A.11

(Differentiability and domination of the joint density gradient)
.

For
(\mu\otimes\nu)
-a.e.
(x,z)
, the map
\theta\mapsto p_{\theta}(x,z)
is differentiable on
\Theta
.
Moreover, for every compact
K\subset\Theta
there exists a measurable envelope
G_{K}:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to[0,\infty)
such that

\sup_{\theta\in K}\|\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x,z)\|\ \leq\ G_{K}(x,z)\quad\text{for }(\mu\otimes\nu)\text{-a.e.\ }(x,z),

and such that for
\mu
-a.e.
x
,

\int_{\mathsf{Z}}G_{K}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)\ <\ \infty.

Lemma A.12

(Differentiation of the marginal density)
.

Under Assumption
A.11
, for every compact
K\subset\Theta
and
\mu
-a.e.
x
, the map
\theta\mapsto p_{\theta}(x)
is differentiable on
K
, and

\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)\ =\ \int_{\mathsf{Z}}\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz),\qquad\theta\in K.

Proof.

Fix a compact
K\subset\Theta
, a point
\theta\in K
, and a coordinate
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. Let
e_{j}
be the
j
th standard basis vector. For
t\neq 0
such that
\theta+te_{j}\in K
, define

D_{t,j}(x)\ :=\ \frac{p_{\theta+te_{j}}(x)-p_{\theta}(x)}{t}.

Using (
202
) and linearity of the integral,

D_{t,j}(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\frac{p_{\theta+te_{j}}(x,z)-p_{\theta}(x,z)}{t}\,\nu(dz).

For
(\mu\otimes\nu)
-a.e.
(x,z)
, differentiability of
\theta\mapsto p_{\theta}(x,z)
implies

\frac{p_{\theta+te_{j}}(x,z)-p_{\theta}(x,z)}{t}\ \to\ \partial_{\theta_{j}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\qquad\text{as }t\to 0.

We now produce an integrable dominating function in
z
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
. For such
(x,z)
and sufficiently small
t
(so that the segment between
\theta
and
\theta+te_{j}
lies in
K
), the one-dimensional mean value theorem gives

\frac{p_{\theta+te_{j}}(x,z)-p_{\theta}(x,z)}{t}=\partial_{\theta_{j}}p_{\theta+s_{t}e_{j}}(x,z)

for some
s_{t}
between
0
and
t
. Hence

\Big|\frac{p_{\theta+te_{j}}(x,z)-p_{\theta}(x,z)}{t}\Big|\leq\sup_{\vartheta\in K}\big|\partial_{\theta_{j}}p_{\vartheta}(x,z)\big|\leq\sup_{\vartheta\in K}\|\nabla_{\theta}p_{\vartheta}(x,z)\|\leq G_{K}(x,z),

for
(\mu\otimes\nu)
-a.e.
(x,z)
. By assumption, for
\mu
-a.e.
x
,
\int G_{K}(x,z)\nu(dz)<\infty
, so dominated convergence (in
z
) yields, for such
x
,

\lim_{t\to 0}D_{t,j}(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\partial_{\theta_{j}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz).

Thus
\partial_{\theta_{j}}p_{\theta}(x)
exists and equals the integral of
\partial_{\theta_{j}}p_{\theta}(x,z)
, for
\mu
-a.e.
x
. Doing this for each
j
yields the vector identity for
\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)
.
∎

Differentiating
\Phi
and
Q
.

To differentiate log-likelihood terms under
P^{\star}
and under the joint measure
P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}
, we impose local score envelopes.

Assumption A.13

(Positivity and score domination)
.

Fix a compact set
K\subset\Theta
.

(1)

(
Positivity
) For each
\theta\in K
,
p_{\theta}(x)\in(0,\infty)
for
P^{\star}
-a.e.
x
; and for each
\theta\in K
,
p_{\theta}(x,z)\in(0,\infty)
for
(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}})(dx,dz)
-a.e.
(x,z)
whenever
Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
is evaluated.

(2)

(
Differentiability
) For
P^{\star}
-a.e.
x
,
\theta\mapsto\log p_{\theta}(x)
is differentiable on
K
; and for
(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}})(dx,dz)
-a.e.
(x,z)
,
\theta\mapsto\log p_{\theta}(x,z)
is differentiable on
K
.

(3)

(
Integrable envelopes
) There exist measurable
S_{K}:\mathsf{X}\to[0,\infty)
and
T_{K}:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to[0,\infty)
such that

\sup_{\theta\in K}\|\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x)\|\ \leq\ S_{K}(x)\quad\text{for }P^{\star}\text{-a.e.\ }x,

\sup_{\theta\in K}\|\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\|\ \leq\ T_{K}(x,z)\quad\text{for }(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}\text{)-a.e.\ }(x,z),

and
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[S_{K}(X)]<\infty
and
\int T_{K}\,d(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}})<\infty
.

Lemma A.14

(Differentiation of
\Phi
)
.

Fix a compact
K\subset\Theta
and assume Assumption
A.13
holds for this
K
. Then
\Phi
is differentiable on
K
and, for each
\theta\in K
,

\nabla_{\theta}\Phi(\theta)\ =\ \mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(X)\big].

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in K
and coordinate
j
. For
t\neq 0
small enough so that
\theta+te_{j}\in K
, define

\Delta_{t,j}(x)\ :=\ \frac{\log p_{\theta+te_{j}}(x)-\log p_{\theta}(x)}{t}.

By Assumption
A.13
(2),
\Delta_{t,j}(x)\to\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p_{\theta}(x)
for
P^{\star}
-a.e.
x
.

We now dominate
\Delta_{t,j}
by an integrable envelope. For
P^{\star}
-a.e.
x
, the map
s\mapsto\log p_{\theta+se_{j}}(x)
is differentiable for
s
in a neighborhood of
0
(since
K
is compact and
\theta
is an interior point of the segment for small
t
). By the mean value theorem applied to
s\mapsto\log p_{\theta+se_{j}}(x)
on
[0,t]
(or
[t,0]
if
t<0
), there exists
s_{t}(x)
between
0
and
t
such that

\Delta_{t,j}(x)=\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p_{\theta+s_{t}(x)e_{j}}(x).

Therefore

|\Delta_{t,j}(x)|\leq\|\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta+s_{t}(x)e_{j}}(x)\|\leq\sup_{\vartheta\in K}\|\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\vartheta}(x)\|\leq S_{K}(x)

for
P^{\star}
-a.e.
x
. Since
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[S_{K}(X)]<\infty
, dominated convergence yields

\lim_{t\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[\Delta_{t,j}(X)]=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p_{\theta}(X)].

Finally, by definition of
\Phi
,

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[\Delta_{t,j}(X)]=\frac{1}{t}\Big(\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[\log p_{\theta+te_{j}}(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[\log p_{\theta}(X)]\Big)=\frac{\Phi(\theta+te_{j})-\Phi(\theta)}{t}.

Thus
\partial_{\theta_{j}}\Phi(\theta)
exists and equals
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p_{\theta}(X)]
. Since this holds for all
j
,
\Phi
is differentiable at
\theta
with the stated gradient. As
\theta\in K
was arbitrary,
\Phi
is differentiable on
K
.
∎

Lemma A.15

(Differentiation of
Q
)
.

Fix a compact
K\subset\Theta
and assume Assumption
A.13
holds for this
K
. Then for each
\theta\in K
,

\nabla_{\theta}Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\ =\ \mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\big]\Big],

provided the right-hand side exists in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(equivalently,
\int\|\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}\|\,d(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}})<\infty
).

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in K
and coordinate
j
. For small
t\neq 0
with
\theta+te_{j}\in K
, define

\Delta_{t,j}(x,z):=\frac{\log p_{\theta+te_{j}}(x,z)-\log p_{\theta}(x,z)}{t}.

By Assumption
A.13
(2),
\Delta_{t,j}(x,z)\to\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)
for
(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}})
-a.e.
(x,z)
.

By the mean value theorem applied pointwise in
(x,z)
to
s\mapsto\log p_{\theta+se_{j}}(x,z)
, there exists
s_{t}(x,z)
between
0
and
t
such that

\Delta_{t,j}(x,z)=\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p_{\theta+s_{t}(x,z)e_{j}}(x,z),

and hence

|\Delta_{t,j}(x,z)|\leq\sup_{\vartheta\in K}\|\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\vartheta}(x,z)\|\leq T_{K}(x,z)

for
(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}})
-a.e.
(x,z)
. The envelope
T_{K}
is integrable under
P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}
by Assumption
A.13
(3). Therefore dominated convergence (Lemma
A.10
applied under the joint measure
P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}
) yields

\lim_{t\to 0}\int\Delta_{t,j}(x,z)\,(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}})(dx,dz)=\int\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\,(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}})(dx,dz).

Using Lemma
A.9
(2), the left-hand side equals

\frac{1}{t}\Big(Q(\theta+te_{j}\mid\theta^{\prime})-Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\Big),

since
Q(\cdot\mid\theta^{\prime})
is defined as an integral of
\log p_{\theta}(x,z)
under
P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}
.
Thus
\partial_{\theta_{j}}Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
exists and equals

\int\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\,(P^{\star}\otimes\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}})(dx,dz)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\mathbb{E}_{\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}\big[\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\big].

Collecting coordinates gives the claimed gradient formula.
∎

Fisher identity.

Lemma A.16

(Fisher identity)
.

Assume Assumption
A.1
. Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and suppose:

(1)

p_{\theta}(x)\in(0,\infty)
for
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
x
;

(2)

Assumption
A.11
holds on some compact neighborhood
K\ni\theta
;

(3)

\int\|\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\|\,\kappa_{\theta}(x,dz)<\infty
for
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
x
, where
\kappa_{\theta}(x,dz)=p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\nu(dz)
as in Lemma
A.4
.

Then for
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
x
,

\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)}\big[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,Z)\big].

Proof.

By Lemma
A.12
, for
\mu
-a.e.
x
(hence for
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
x
since
P_{\theta}^{X}\ll\mu
),

\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz).

Fix such an
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)>0
. Divide by
p_{\theta}(x)
:

\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x)=\frac{\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x)}{p_{\theta}(x)}=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\frac{\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}\,\nu(dz).

Multiply and divide inside the integral by
p_{\theta}(x,z)
(which is positive
\nu
-a.e. under the conditional law by construction of
\kappa_{\theta}
):

\frac{\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}=\frac{\nabla_{\theta}p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x,z)}\cdot\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}=\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\cdot p_{\theta}(z\mid x).

Hence

\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\,p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\kappa_{\theta}(x,dz)=\mathbb{E}_{\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)}[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(x,Z)],

where the final expression is well-defined by the assumed integrability of the complete-data score under
\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
.
∎

A.3.
Auxiliary inequalities

We collect inequalities used repeatedly, with full proofs.

Lemma A.17

(Jensen’s inequality)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space, let
U:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be integrable, and let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to(-\infty,\infty]
be convex. Then

\varphi(\mathbb{E}[U])\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}[\varphi(U)]

whenever
\mathbb{E}[\varphi(U)]
is well-defined in
(-\infty,\infty]
. In particular, since
\log
is concave, for any nonnegative
W
with
\mathbb{E}[W]\in(0,\infty)
,

\mathbb{E}[\log W]\ \leq\ \log\mathbb{E}[W],

allowing
\mathbb{E}[\log W]
to equal
-\infty
.

Proof.

If
\varphi(\mathbb{E}[U])=+\infty
, the inequality is trivial. Assume
\varphi(\mathbb{E}[U])<\infty
and set
m:=\mathbb{E}[U]\in\mathbb{R}
.

A real-valued convex function on
\mathbb{R}
has finite left and right derivatives at every point in the interior of its effective domain, and admits at least one subgradient at such points. Concretely, define the left and right slopes

\varphi^{\prime}_{-}(m):=\lim_{t\uparrow 0}\frac{\varphi(m+t)-\varphi(m)}{t},\qquad\varphi^{\prime}_{+}(m):=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{\varphi(m+t)-\varphi(m)}{t},

which exist in
[-\infty,\infty]
with
\varphi^{\prime}_{-}(m)\leq\varphi^{\prime}_{+}(m)
. Choose any
s\in[\varphi^{\prime}_{-}(m),\varphi^{\prime}_{+}(m)]\cap\mathbb{R}
(such an
s
exists whenever
\varphi(m)<\infty
). Then the supporting line inequality holds:

(211)

\varphi(u)\ \geq\ \varphi(m)+s(u-m)\qquad\text{for all }u\in\mathbb{R}.

(To verify (
211
): for
u>m
, convexity implies
(\varphi(u)-\varphi(m))/(u-m)\geq\varphi^{\prime}_{-}(m)\geq s
; for
u<m
, convexity implies
(\varphi(u)-\varphi(m))/(u-m)\leq\varphi^{\prime}_{+}(m)\leq s
, which rearranges to the same inequality.)

Apply (
211
) to
u=U(\omega)
and take expectations:

\mathbb{E}[\varphi(U)]\ \geq\ \mathbb{E}[\varphi(m)+s(U-m)]=\varphi(m)+s(\mathbb{E}[U]-m)=\varphi(\mathbb{E}[U]).

This proves Jensen.

For the
\log
specialization, apply Jensen to the convex function
-\log
on
(0,\infty)
(or equivalently, use concavity of
\log
) with
U=W
.
∎

Lemma A.18

(Elementary log bounds)
.

For all
t>0
,

\log t\ \leq\ t-1,\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad-\log t\ \geq\ 1-t.

More generally, for any
t_{0}>0
,

\log t\ \leq\ \log t_{0}\ +\ \frac{t-t_{0}}{t_{0}}.

Proof.

Define
f(t):=t-1-\log t
for
t>0
. Then
f^{\prime}(t)=1-\frac{1}{t}
and
f^{\prime\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{t^{2}}>0
, so
f
is strictly convex. Its unique minimizer satisfies
f^{\prime}(t)=0
, i.e.
t=1
, and
f(1)=0
. Hence
f(t)\geq 0
for all
t>0
, which is
\log t\leq t-1
.

For the supporting-hyperplane inequality, concavity of
\log
implies that for any
t_{0}>0
,

\log t\leq\log t_{0}+\log^{\prime}(t_{0})(t-t_{0})=\log t_{0}+\frac{t-t_{0}}{t_{0}}.

∎

Lemma A.19

(KL nonnegativity)
.

Let
P,Q
be probability measures on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
with
P\ll Q
. Define

\KL(P\|Q)\ :=\ \int_{\Omega}\log\Big(\frac{dP}{dQ}\Big)\,dP\ \in[0,\infty].

Then
\KL(P\|Q)\geq 0
, with equality if and only if
P=Q
.

Proof.

Let
r:=\frac{dP}{dQ}
, so
r\geq 0
and
\int r\,dQ=1
. Then

\KL(P\|Q)=\int r\log r\,dQ,

with the convention
0\log 0:=0
.

Apply Lemma
A.18
to
t=r
to obtain
\log r\geq 1-\frac{1}{r}
for
r>0
, equivalently

r\log r\geq r-1\qquad\text{for all }r\geq 0,

where the
r=0
case holds by continuity (
\lim_{r\downarrow 0}r\log r=0
and
r-1=-1
). Integrate both sides with respect to
Q
:

\KL(P\|Q)=\int r\log r\,dQ\geq\int(r-1)\,dQ=\int r\,dQ-1=0.

If
\KL(P\|Q)=0
, then
\int(r\log r-(r-1))\,dQ=0
with a nonnegative integrand. Thus

r\log r-(r-1)=0\quad Q\text{-a.e.}

But the function
g(r):=r\log r-(r-1)
is strictly convex on
(0,\infty)
, satisfies
g(r)\geq 0
, and
g(r)=0
iff
r=1
. Hence
r=1

Q
-a.e., which implies
P=Q
.
∎

Lemma A.20

(Integral log-sum inequality)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\lambda)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space and let
a,b:\Omega\to[0,\infty)
be measurable with
