A:=\int a\,d\lambda\in(0,\infty),\qquad B:=\int b\,d\lambda\in(0,\infty).

Then

\int a\log\frac{a}{b}\,d\lambda\ \geq\ A\log\frac{A}{B},

with the conventions
0\log(0/b):=0
and the inequality interpreted as valid if the left-hand side is
+\infty
.

Proof.

Define probability measures
P
and
Q
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
by

P(d\omega)=\frac{a(\omega)}{A}\,\lambda(d\omega),\qquad Q(d\omega)=\frac{b(\omega)}{B}\,\lambda(d\omega).

Then
P,Q
are probability measures (since
\int a\,d\lambda=A
and
\int b\,d\lambda=B
). If
P\not\ll Q
, then
\KL(P\|Q)=+\infty
by definition, and the desired inequality is immediate. Assume
P\ll Q
.

Compute the Radon–Nikodym derivative:

\frac{dP}{dQ}(\omega)=\frac{a(\omega)/A}{b(\omega)/B}=\frac{B}{A}\cdot\frac{a(\omega)}{b(\omega)}\qquad Q\text{-a.e.}

Therefore,

\KL(P\|Q)=\int\log\Big(\frac{dP}{dQ}\Big)\,dP=\int\log\Big(\frac{B}{A}\cdot\frac{a}{b}\Big)\,dP=\int\log\Big(\frac{a}{b}\Big)\,dP+\log\Big(\frac{B}{A}\Big)\int 1\,dP.

Since
\int 1\,dP=1
and
dP=(a/A)\,d\lambda
, this becomes

\KL(P\|Q)=\frac{1}{A}\int a\log\Big(\frac{a}{b}\Big)\,d\lambda+\log\Big(\frac{B}{A}\Big).

By Lemma
A.19
,
\KL(P\|Q)\geq 0
, hence

\frac{1}{A}\int a\log\Big(\frac{a}{b}\Big)\,d\lambda\geq\log\Big(\frac{A}{B}\Big),

which is the claimed inequality after multiplying by
A
.
∎

Lemma A.21

(KL chain rule and measurability of conditional KL)
.

Assume
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
is standard Borel. Let
P,Q
be probability measures on
(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
with
P\ll Q
. Let
P^{X},Q^{X}
be the
\mathsf{X}
-marginals, and let
P(\cdot\mid x),Q(\cdot\mid x)
be regular conditional laws of
Z
given
X=x
under
P,Q
, respectively.
Then:

(1)

The map
x\mapsto\KL(P(\cdot\mid x)\|Q(\cdot\mid x))
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable as an extended real-valued function.

(2)

The chain rule holds:

\KL(P\|Q)\ =\ \KL(P^{X}\|Q^{X})\ +\ \mathbb{E}_{P^{X}}\big[\KL(P(\cdot\mid X)\|Q(\cdot\mid X))\big],

with both sides possibly equal to
+\infty
.

Proof.

Step 1 (disintegrations and marginal absolute continuity).

Since
\mathsf{Z}
is standard Borel, regular conditional laws exist, so we can write the disintegrations

P(dx,dz)=P^{X}(dx)\,P(dz\mid x),\qquad Q(dx,dz)=Q^{X}(dx)\,Q(dz\mid x).

From
P\ll Q
it follows that
P^{X}\ll Q^{X}
: if
Q^{X}(A)=0
, then
Q(A\times\mathsf{Z})=0
, hence
P(A\times\mathsf{Z})=0
, i.e.
P^{X}(A)=0
.

Let
r_{X}:=dP^{X}/dQ^{X}
be the marginal Radon–Nikodym derivative.

Step 2 (conditional absolute continuity for
P^{X}
-a.e.
x
).

We show that for
P^{X}
-a.e.
x
,
P(\cdot\mid x)\ll Q(\cdot\mid x)
. Let

\Gamma:=\Big\{x\in\mathsf{X}:\ P(\cdot\mid x)\not\ll Q(\cdot\mid x)\Big\}.

For each
x\in\Gamma
, there exists a measurable set
B_{x}\in\mathcal{G}
such that
Q(B_{x}\mid x)=0
but
P(B_{x}\mid x)>0
.
To avoid measurability issues from the choice
x\mapsto B_{x}
, use the following countable reduction: since
\mathsf{Z}
is standard Borel,
\mathcal{G}
is countably generated, so there exists a countable
\pi
-system
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
generating
\mathcal{G}
. If
P(\cdot\mid x)\not\ll Q(\cdot\mid x)
, then there exists some
B\in\mathcal{C}
with
Q(B\mid x)=0
and
P(B\mid x)>0
(because otherwise
P
would be absolutely continuous on the generating class and hence on all of
\mathcal{G}
by a monotone class argument). Therefore

\Gamma=\bigcup_{B\in\mathcal{C}}\Gamma_{B},\qquad\Gamma_{B}:=\{x:\ Q(B\mid x)=0,\ P(B\mid x)>0\}.

Each
\Gamma_{B}\in\mathcal{F}
because
x\mapsto P(B\mid x)
and
x\mapsto Q(B\mid x)
are measurable (kernel property). Now compute

Q(\Gamma_{B}\times B)=\int_{\Gamma_{B}}Q(B\mid x)\,Q^{X}(dx)=\int_{\Gamma_{B}}0\,Q^{X}(dx)=0.

Since
P\ll Q
, we also have
P(\Gamma_{B}\times B)=0
, i.e.

0=P(\Gamma_{B}\times B)=\int_{\Gamma_{B}}P(B\mid x)\,P^{X}(dx).

But
P(B\mid x)>0
on
\Gamma_{B}
by definition, hence
P^{X}(\Gamma_{B})=0
. Since
\mathcal{C}
is countable,

P^{X}(\Gamma)\leq\sum_{B\in\mathcal{C}}P^{X}(\Gamma_{B})=0.

Thus
P(\cdot\mid x)\ll Q(\cdot\mid x)
for
P^{X}
-a.e.
x
.

Step 3 (factorization of the joint Radon–Nikodym derivative).

For
P^{X}
-a.e.
x
, define
r_{Z\mid X}(x,\cdot):=\frac{dP(\cdot\mid x)}{dQ(\cdot\mid x)}
(a version), which is
\mathcal{G}
-measurable in
z
and satisfies
\int r_{Z\mid X}(x,z)\,Q(dz\mid x)=1
.

Define

r(x,z):=r_{X}(x)\,r_{Z\mid X}(x,z),

interpreting
r_{Z\mid X}(x,z)=0
when
r_{X}(x)=0
(which can only occur on a
P^{X}
-null set anyway). We claim
r=dP/dQ

Q
-a.e.

To verify, it suffices (by uniqueness of Radon–Nikodym derivatives) to check that for all rectangles
A\times B
,

(212)

P(A\times B)=\int_{A\times B}r(x,z)\,Q(dx,dz).

Compute the right-hand side using disintegration of
Q
and then the definition of conditional Radon–Nikodym derivatives:

\int_{A\times B}r(x,z)\,Q(dx,dz)=\int_{A}\Big(\int_{B}r_{X}(x)r_{Z\mid X}(x,z)\,Q(dz\mid x)\Big)Q^{X}(dx)=\int_{A}r_{X}(x)\Big(\int_{B}r_{Z\mid X}(x,z)\,Q(dz\mid x)\Big)Q^{X}(dx)

=\int_{A}r_{X}(x)\,P(B\mid x)\,Q^{X}(dx)=\int_{A}P(B\mid x)\,P^{X}(dx)=P(A\times B),

where we used
P^{X}(dx)=r_{X}(x)Q^{X}(dx)
. This proves (
212
), hence
r=dP/dQ

Q
-a.e.

Step 4 (chain rule identity).

Using
dP=r\,dQ
and
\log r=\log r_{X}+\log r_{Z\mid X}
,

\KL(P\|Q)=\int\log r\,dP=\int\log r\;r\,dQ.

A more transparent decomposition is obtained by integrating with respect to
P
directly:

\KL(P\|Q)=\int\log\Big(\frac{dP}{dQ}\Big)\,dP=\int\big(\log r_{X}(x)+\log r_{Z\mid X}(x,z)\big)\,P(dx,dz)

=\int\log r_{X}(x)\,P^{X}(dx)+\int\Big(\int\log r_{Z\mid X}(x,z)\,P(dz\mid x)\Big)P^{X}(dx).

The first term equals
\KL(P^{X}\|Q^{X})
because
r_{X}=dP^{X}/dQ^{X}
and
P^{X}=r_{X}Q^{X}
:

\int\log r_{X}\,dP^{X}=\int\log\Big(\frac{dP^{X}}{dQ^{X}}\Big)\,dP^{X}=\KL(P^{X}\|Q^{X}).

For the second term, the inner integral is exactly
\KL(P(\cdot\mid x)\|Q(\cdot\mid x))
for
P^{X}
-a.e.
x
, by definition of
r_{Z\mid X}(x,\cdot)
. Thus

\KL(P\|Q)=\KL(P^{X}\|Q^{X})+\mathbb{E}_{P^{X}}\big[\KL(P(\cdot\mid X)\|Q(\cdot\mid X))\big],

with the understanding that if any term is
+\infty
then the equality holds in
[0,\infty]
(all quantities are nonnegative by Lemma
A.19
).

Step 5 (measurability of conditional KL).

We prove that
x\mapsto\KL(P(\cdot\mid x)\|Q(\cdot\mid x))
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

Since
\mathsf{Z}
is standard Borel,
\mathcal{G}
is countably generated. Let
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
be a countable algebra that generates
\mathcal{G}
, and let
\Pi
be the countable set of all finite measurable partitions
\pi=\{A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\}
with each
A_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

For probability measures
R,S
on
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
, define the partition divergence

\KL_{\pi}(R\|S):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}R(A_{i})\log\frac{R(A_{i})}{S(A_{i})},

with the conventions
0\log(0/s):=0
and
r\log(r/0):=+\infty
for
r>0
. A standard characterization of KL (proved below) is

(213)

\KL(R\|S)=\sup_{\pi}\KL_{\pi}(R\|S),

where the supremum is over all finite measurable partitions. Moreover, because
\mathcal{A}_{0}
generates
\mathcal{G}
, it suffices to take the supremum over
\Pi
:

(214)

\KL(R\|S)=\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}\KL_{\pi}(R\|S).

Measurability from (
214
).

For each fixed
\pi=\{A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\}\in\Pi
, the map

x\mapsto\KL_{\pi}(P(\cdot\mid x)\|Q(\cdot\mid x))=\sum_{i=1}^{m}P(A_{i}\mid x)\log\frac{P(A_{i}\mid x)}{Q(A_{i}\mid x)}

is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable because
x\mapsto P(A_{i}\mid x)
and
x\mapsto Q(A_{i}\mid x)
are measurable (kernel property) and the map
(u,v)\mapsto u\log(u/v)
is Borel on
[0,1]^{2}
with the stated conventions. Since
\Pi
is countable, the pointwise supremum

x\mapsto\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}\KL_{\pi}(P(\cdot\mid x)\|Q(\cdot\mid x))

is measurable. By (
214
), this supremum equals
\KL(P(\cdot\mid x)\|Q(\cdot\mid x))
, proving measurability.

Proof of (
213
) and (
214
).

Let
R\ll S
with density
r=dR/dS
(else KL is
+\infty
and the partition supremum also yields
+\infty
by choosing a cell where
S
is zero and
R
positive). For any finite partition
\pi=\{A_{i}\}
, define the conditional expectation of
r
on each cell:

\bar{r}_{i}:=\frac{1}{S(A_{i})}\int_{A_{i}}r\,dS\quad\text{if }S(A_{i})>0,\qquad\bar{r}_{i}:=0\ \text{if }S(A_{i})=0.

Then
R(A_{i})=\int_{A_{i}}r\,dS=\bar{r}_{i}S(A_{i})
. Hence

\KL_{\pi}(R\|S)=\sum_{i}\bar{r}_{i}S(A_{i})\log\bar{r}_{i}.

On the other hand,

\KL(R\|S)=\int r\log r\,dS=\sum_{i}\int_{A_{i}}r\log r\,dS.

By Jensen’s inequality (Lemma
A.17
) applied to the convex function
u\mapsto u\log u
under the probability measure
S(\cdot\mid A_{i})=S(\cdot\cap A_{i})/S(A_{i})
(for cells with
S(A_{i})>0
),

\frac{1}{S(A_{i})}\int_{A_{i}}r\log r\,dS\ \geq\ \Big(\frac{1}{S(A_{i})}\int_{A_{i}}r\,dS\Big)\log\Big(\frac{1}{S(A_{i})}\int_{A_{i}}r\,dS\Big)=\bar{r}_{i}\log\bar{r}_{i},

thus
\int_{A_{i}}r\log r\,dS\geq\bar{r}_{i}S(A_{i})\log\bar{r}_{i}
. Summing over
i
yields

\KL(R\|S)\ \geq\ \KL_{\pi}(R\|S)\qquad\text{for every finite partition }\pi,

so
\KL(R\|S)\geq\sup_{\pi}\KL_{\pi}(R\|S)
.

For the reverse inequality, approximate
r
by simple functions measurable with respect to a refining sequence of finite partitions: since
\mathcal{G}
is countably generated, there exists an increasing sequence of finite sub-
\sigma
-fields
\mathcal{G}_{n}
with
\sigma(\cup_{n}\mathcal{G}_{n})=\mathcal{G}
. Let
r_{n}:=\mathbb{E}_{S}[r\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}]
be the conditional expectation under
S
. Then
r_{n}\to r
in
L^{1}(S)
and
r_{n}\to r

S
-a.e. (martingale convergence theorem). Moreover, by convexity of
u\mapsto u\log u
and Jensen applied conditionally,

\int r_{n}\log r_{n}\,dS\ \uparrow\ \int r\log r\,dS,

because
(r_{n})
is the
S
-martingale of conditional expectations and
u\mapsto u\log u
is convex with appropriate integrability handled by truncation (standard; one may first prove for bounded
r
, then pass by monotone truncation
r\wedge M
and let
M\uparrow\infty
).

For each
n
,
r_{n}
is constant on atoms of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
, i.e.
r_{n}=\sum_{i}\bar{r}_{n,i}\mathbf{1}_{A_{n,i}}
for a finite partition
\pi_{n}=\{A_{n,i}\}
with
A_{n,i}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
. Then

\int r_{n}\log r_{n}\,dS=\sum_{i}\bar{r}_{n,i}S(A_{n,i})\log\bar{r}_{n,i}=\KL_{\pi_{n}}(R\|S).

Taking
n\to\infty
gives

\KL(R\|S)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\KL_{\pi_{n}}(R\|S)\ \leq\ \sup_{\pi}\KL_{\pi}(R\|S),

hence equality (
213
).

Finally, because the generating algebra
\mathcal{A}_{0}
generates
\mathcal{G}
, every finite partition can be approximated (refined) by partitions from
\Pi
without decreasing the supremum; more precisely, for any finite partition
\pi
and any
\varepsilon>0
one can find
\tilde{\pi}\in\Pi
such that
\KL_{\tilde{\pi}}(R\|S)\geq\KL_{\pi}(R\|S)-\varepsilon
(using approximation of sets in
\mathcal{G}
by sets in the generating algebra and continuity of the partition functional). Therefore the supremum over all finite partitions equals the supremum over
\Pi
, proving (
214
).
∎

How these inequalities are used in EM arguments.

Lemma
A.17
yields the standard EM lower bound (Jensen in the conditional expectation defining
Q
); Lemmas
A.19
and
A.21
convert log-likelihood gaps into KL divergences and split them into marginal and conditional terms; Lemma
A.18
provides first-order (supporting-hyperplane) controls for log terms in stability and perturbation bounds.

Appendix B
Quotient constructions and orbit-level measurability

This appendix collects measure-theoretic and geometric facts about orbit spaces and local slices.
The goal is to make precise (i) how to put a canonical
\sigma
-field on the orbit space
\Theta/G
,
(ii) when
\Theta/G
is standard Borel (globally or locally), and (iii) how orbit-level distances relate
to distances between chosen representatives.

Standing notation.

Let
G
be a group acting on
\Theta
by a left action

a:G\times\Theta\to\Theta,\qquad a(g,\theta)=g\cdot\theta.

Write
\left[(\right]\theta)=\{g\cdot\theta:g\in G\}
, let
\Theta/G
denote the orbit set, and let

q:\Theta\to\Theta/G,\qquad q(\theta)=\left[(\right]\theta)

be the quotient map.

Unless stated otherwise,
(\Theta,\mathcal{B}(\Theta))
is a measurable space, typically
standard Borel
.
When
\Theta
is Polish,
\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
denotes its Borel
\sigma
-field.

B.1.
Standard Borel preliminaries used repeatedly

We record a few elementary facts used throughout.

Lemma B.1

(Diagonals are Borel)
.

If
Y
is a standard Borel space, then the diagonal

\mathrm{Diag}_{Y}:=\{(y,y):y\in Y\}\subseteq Y\times Y

is Borel in
Y\times Y
.

Proof.

Choose a Polish topology on a set
Y^{\prime}
and a Borel isomorphism
\varphi:Y\to Y^{\prime}
(existence is part of the definition of standard Borel).
Then
\mathrm{Diag}_{Y^{\prime}}
is closed in the Polish space
Y^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}
and hence Borel.
Since
\varphi\times\varphi
is a Borel isomorphism,
\mathrm{Diag}_{Y}=(\varphi\times\varphi)^{-1}(\mathrm{Diag}_{Y^{\prime}})
is Borel.
∎

Lemma B.2

(Graphs of Borel maps are Borel)
.

Let
X,Y
be standard Borel spaces and let
f:X\to Y
be Borel. Then

\mathrm{Graph}(f):=\{(x,y)\in X\times Y:\ y=f(x)\}

is Borel in
X\times Y
.

Proof.

The map
F:X\times Y\to Y\times Y
,
F(x,y)=(y,f(x))
, is Borel because
f
is Borel and products preserve measurability.
Then
\mathrm{Graph}(f)=F^{-1}(\mathrm{Diag}_{Y})
, which is Borel by Lemma
B.1
.
∎

Lemma B.3

(Analyticity via projection)
.

Let
X,Y
be standard Borel and let
A\subseteq X\times Y
be Borel. Then
\pi_{X}(A)\subseteq X
is analytic,
where
\pi_{X}(x,y)=x
is the projection.

Proof.

By definition (one of the equivalent ones), analytic sets in a standard Borel space are precisely projections of Borel sets in a product with a standard Borel space.
∎

Lemma B.4

(Fixed-point sets of measurable idempotents)
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{A})
be measurable and let
r:X\to X
be
\mathcal{A}
-measurable. Then the fixed-point set

\mathrm{Fix}(r):=\{x\in X:r(x)=x\}

is in
\mathcal{A}
whenever the diagonal
\mathrm{Diag}_{X}\subseteq X\times X
is measurable in
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}

(e.g. when
X
is standard Borel).

Proof.

Consider the measurable map
x\mapsto(x,r(x))
from
(X,\mathcal{A})
to
(X\times X,\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})
.
Then
\mathrm{Fix}(r)=(x\mapsto(x,r(x)))^{-1}(\mathrm{Diag}_{X})
.
∎

B.2.
Measurability of the quotient map and orbit relation

Quotient
\sigma
-field

We equip
\Theta/G
with the
quotient
\sigma
-field

(215)

\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)\ :=\ \{A\subseteq\Theta/G:\ q^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)\}.

Lemma B.5

(Basic properties of the quotient
\sigma
-field)
.

The collection
\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
in (
215
) is a
\sigma
-field on
\Theta/G
. Moreover:

(1)

q:(\Theta,\mathcal{B}(\Theta))\to(\Theta/G,\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G))
is measurable.

(2)

\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
is the
largest

\sigma
-field on
\Theta/G
for which
q
is measurable:
if
\mathcal{A}
is a
\sigma
-field on
\Theta/G
such that
q
is
(\mathcal{B}(\Theta),\mathcal{A})
-measurable, then

\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
.

Proof.

Step 1 (Show
\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
is a
\sigma
-field).

•

\emptyset,\Theta/G\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
since
q^{-1}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
and
q^{-1}(\Theta/G)=\Theta
lie in
\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.

•

If
A\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
then
q^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
and

q^{-1}(A^{c})=\Theta\setminus q^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta),

so
A^{c}\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
.

•

If
A_{n}\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
, then
q^{-1}(A_{n})\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
and

q^{-1}\Big(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}A_{n}\Big)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}q^{-1}(A_{n})\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta),

so
\bigcup_{n\geq 1}A_{n}\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
.

Step 2 (Measurability of
q
).

For each
A\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
, the preimage
q^{-1}(A)
is in
\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
by definition.
This is exactly measurability of
q
.

Step 3 (Maximality).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\sigma
-field on
\Theta/G
such that
q
is measurable into
(\Theta/G,\mathcal{A})
.
Then for any
A\in\mathcal{A}
,
q^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
, hence
A\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
by (
215
).
Thus
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
.
∎

Saturated sets and pullbacks

A subset
B\subseteq\Theta
is
G
-invariant
(or
saturated
) if
g\cdot B=B
for all
g\in G
.
Equivalently,
B=q^{-1}(q(B))
.

Lemma B.6

(Saturated sets are exactly quotient pullbacks)
.

A set
B\subseteq\Theta
is saturated if and only if
B=q^{-1}(A)
for some
A\subseteq\Theta/G

(namely
A=q(B)
). Moreover, if
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
is saturated, then
q(B)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
.

Proof.

Step 1 (
q^{-1}(A)
is saturated).

If
B=q^{-1}(A)
then
q(B)=q(q^{-1}(A))=A
(surjectivity of
q
), hence
q^{-1}(q(B))=q^{-1}(A)=B
.

Step 2 (Saturated implies pullback).

If
B
is saturated, define
A:=q(B)
. Then
B=q^{-1}(q(B))=q^{-1}(A)
.

Step 3 (Borelness on the quotient).

If
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
and
B=q^{-1}(q(B))
, then
q^{-1}(q(B))\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
, so
q(B)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
by definition.
∎

The orbit equivalence relation

Define

E\subseteq\Theta\times\Theta,\qquad(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\in E\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \exists g\in G:\ \theta^{\prime}=g\cdot\theta.

Assumption B.7

(Borel action)
.

\Theta
is standard Borel. The action map
a(g,\theta)=g\cdot\theta
is measurable from

(G\times\Theta,\mathcal{B}(G)\otimes\mathcal{B}(\Theta))
to
(\Theta,\mathcal{B}(\Theta))
for some
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{B}(G)
on
G
.
Typical cases: (i)
G
countable,
\mathcal{B}(G)=2^{G}
; (ii)
G
second-countable topological group,
\mathcal{B}(G)
its Borel
\sigma
-field,
acting Borel (in particular continuously) on a Polish
\Theta
.

Lemma B.8

(Orbit relation is analytic; Borel for countable groups)
.

Under Assumption
B.7
,
E
is analytic in
\Theta\times\Theta
.
If
G
is countable and
\mathcal{B}(G)=2^{G}
, then
E
is Borel.

Proof.

Step 1 (Encode
E
as a projection of a Borel set).

Consider the set

A:=\{(g,\theta,\theta^{\prime})\in G\times\Theta\times\Theta:\ \theta^{\prime}=g\cdot\theta\}.

Define the measurable map

F:G\times\Theta\times\Theta\to\Theta\times\Theta,\qquad F(g,\theta,\theta^{\prime})=(\theta^{\prime},\,g\cdot\theta).

Measurability of
F
follows from measurability of the coordinate projection
(g,\theta,\theta^{\prime})\mapsto\theta^{\prime}
and the action map.
Since
\Theta
is standard Borel, Lemma
B.1
gives that the diagonal
\mathrm{Diag}_{\Theta}
is Borel.
Hence

A=F^{-1}(\mathrm{Diag}_{\Theta})

is Borel in
G\times\Theta\times\Theta
.

Step 2 (Project to obtain
E
).

Let
\pi:G\times\Theta\times\Theta\to\Theta\times\Theta
be
\pi(g,\theta,\theta^{\prime})=(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
.
Then

\pi(A)=\{(\theta,\theta^{\prime}):\exists g\in G,\ \theta^{\prime}=g\cdot\theta\}=E.

By Lemma
B.3
,
\pi(A)
is analytic, hence
E
is analytic.

Step 3 (Countable
G
gives Borel).

Assume
G
is countable and
\mathcal{B}(G)=2^{G}
.
For each fixed
g\in G
, the map
\theta\mapsto g\cdot\theta
is measurable (as a section of the jointly measurable action),
hence Borel. By Lemma
B.2
,

E_{g}:=\{(\theta,\theta^{\prime}):\theta^{\prime}=g\cdot\theta\}

is Borel in
\Theta\times\Theta
.
Finally,

E=\bigcup_{g\in G}E_{g},

a countable union of Borel sets, so
E
is Borel.
∎

Standard-Borel quotients under a measurable section

Proposition B.9

(Standard-Borel quotient under a measurable section)
.

Assume
\Theta
is standard Borel and
\Theta/G
is equipped with
\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
from (
215
).
Suppose there exists a measurable section
s:\Theta/G\to\Theta
such that
q\circ s=\mathrm{id}_{\Theta/G}
.
Let
S:=s(\Theta/G)\subseteq\Theta
. Then:

(1)

s
is injective, and
q|_{S}:S\to\Theta/G
is a bijection with inverse
s
.

(2)

S\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.

(3)

The restriction
q|_{S}:(S,\mathcal{B}(S))\to(\Theta/G,\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G))
is a Borel isomorphism, where

\mathcal{B}(S)=\{S\cap B:B\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)\}
.
In particular,
(\Theta/G,\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G))
is standard Borel.

Proof.

Step 1 (Injectivity and bijection).

If
s(\bar{\theta})=s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})
, apply
q
to obtain

\bar{\theta}=q(s(\bar{\theta}))=q(s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime}))=\bar{\theta}^{\prime}
, so
s
is injective.
Since
q(s(\bar{\theta}))=\bar{\theta}
for all
\bar{\theta}
, the map
q|_{S}
is surjective and has inverse
s
.
Injectivity of
q|_{S}
follows from injectivity of
s
, so
q|_{S}
is bijective.

Step 2 (Borelness of
S
).

Define
r:\Theta\to\Theta
by

r:=s\circ q.

This is measurable because
q
is measurable (Lemma
B.5
) and
s
is measurable by assumption.
Moreover,
r
is idempotent:

r(r(\theta))=s(q(s(q(\theta))))=s(q(\theta))=r(\theta).

We claim that
S=\mathrm{Fix}(r)
.
Indeed, if
\theta\in S
then
\theta=s(\bar{\theta})
for some
\bar{\theta}
and hence

r(\theta)=s(q(s(\bar{\theta})))=s(\bar{\theta})=\theta
.
Conversely, if
r(\theta)=\theta
, then
\theta=s(q(\theta))\in S
.

Since
\Theta
is standard Borel,
\mathrm{Diag}_{\Theta}
is Borel and Lemma
B.4
yields
\mathrm{Fix}(r)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.
Thus
S\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.

Step 3 (Borel isomorphism).

The map
q|_{S}
is measurable as the restriction of a measurable map to a measurable subset.
Its inverse is
s
, which is measurable and takes values in
S
.
Hence
q|_{S}
is a measurable bijection with measurable inverse, i.e. a Borel isomorphism.

Step 4 (Standard Borelness).

Because
S
is Borel in the standard Borel space
\Theta
, the measurable space
(S,\mathcal{B}(S))
is standard Borel.
A Borel isomorphism preserves standard-Borel structure, hence
(\Theta/G,\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G))
is standard Borel.
∎

Remark B.10

(Smoothness and selectors)
.

In descriptive set theory, existence of a Borel section is equivalent to smoothness of the orbit equivalence relation
(Borel reducible to equality on a standard Borel space). Proposition
B.9
is the corresponding
standard-Borel consequence. In the main text we only need local sections (Definition
B.11
).

B.3.
Local sections (slices): existence and construction templates

Definition B.11

(Local measurable section)
.

Let
\bar{\theta}_{0}\in\Theta/G
. A
local measurable section at
\bar{\theta}_{0}
consists of

U\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
with
\bar{\theta}_{0}\in U
and a measurable map
s:U\to\Theta
such that

q(s(\bar{\theta}))=\bar{\theta},\qquad\forall\bar{\theta}\in U.

Lemma B.12

(Equivalent slice formulation)
.

Let
U\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
and
s:U\to\Theta
. Set
S:=s(U)
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

s
is a local measurable section on
U
.

(2)

S\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
,
S\subseteq q^{-1}(U)
, and for each
\bar{\theta}\in U
the fiber
q^{-1}(\bar{\theta})
meets
S
in exactly one point.
Equivalently,
q|_{S}:S\to U
is a measurable bijection with measurable inverse
s
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2).

Assume
s
is measurable and
q\circ s=\mathrm{id}_{U}
.

Step 1 (Fiberwise uniqueness).

Fix
\bar{\theta}\in U
.
If
\theta\in S\cap q^{-1}(\bar{\theta})
then
\theta=s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})
for some
\bar{\theta}^{\prime}\in U
and

\bar{\theta}=q(\theta)=q(s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime}))=\bar{\theta}^{\prime}.

Hence
\theta=s(\bar{\theta})
. So
S\cap q^{-1}(\bar{\theta})=\{s(\bar{\theta})\}
.

Step 2 (
S
is Borel).

Define
r:q^{-1}(U)\to\Theta
by
r:=s\circ q
(well-defined on
q^{-1}(U)
).
Then
r
is measurable and idempotent on
q^{-1}(U)
, and

S=\{\theta\in q^{-1}(U):r(\theta)=\theta\}.

Because
q^{-1}(U)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
and
\Theta
is standard Borel locally on
q^{-1}(U)
,
Lemma
B.4
implies the fixed-point set is measurable; hence
S\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.

Step 3 (Measurable bijection).

By Step 1,
q|_{S}
is bijective onto
U
with inverse
s
.
Measurability of
q|_{S}
follows from measurability of
q
; measurability of
s
is assumed.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1).

Assume (2). Since
q|_{S}:S\to U
is a measurable bijection with measurable inverse, define
s
to be this inverse
followed by the inclusion
S\hookrightarrow\Theta
. Then
q\circ s=\mathrm{id}_{U}
and
s
is measurable.
∎

Finite group actions: a global Borel section by tie-breaking

Assumption B.13

(Ordered parameter space)
.

\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is Borel. Fix the lexicographic order
\preceq
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma B.14

(Lexicographic order is Borel)
.

The set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:x\preceq y\}
is Borel in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
Consequently, if
f,g:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
are Borel, then
\{\theta:f(\theta)\preceq g(\theta)\}
is Borel in
\Theta
.

Proof.

Write

\{(x,y):x\preceq y\}=\{(x,y):x=y\}\ \cup\ \bigcup_{j=1}^{d}\{(x,y):x_{1}=y_{1},\ldots,x_{j-1}=y_{j-1},\ x_{j}<y_{j}\}.

Each equality constraint is closed; each strict inequality is open; finite unions preserve Borelness.
The second claim follows by pulling back this Borel set under the Borel map
\theta\mapsto(f(\theta),g(\theta))
.
∎

Lemma B.15

(Borel minimum over finitely many Borel maps)
.

Let
Y
be standard Borel with a Borel total order
\preceq
and let
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}:\Theta\to Y
be Borel.
Then the pointwise minimum
M(\theta)=\min_{\preceq}\{f_{1}(\theta),\dots,f_{m}(\theta)\}
is Borel.
Moreover, the tie-broken argmin

I(\theta):=\min\{i:\ f_{i}(\theta)=M(\theta)\}

is Borel as a map into the discrete space
\{1,\dots,m\}
.

Proof.

Step 1 (Borel selector sets).

For each
i
define

A_{i}:=\Big(\bigcap_{j<i}\{f_{j}\prec f_{i}\}^{c}\Big)\cap\Big(\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}\{f_{i}\preceq f_{j}\}\Big),

where
f_{j}\prec f_{i}
means
f_{j}\preceq f_{i}
and not
f_{i}\preceq f_{j}
.
Each comparison set
\{f_{i}\preceq f_{j}\}
is Borel because
(f_{i},f_{j})
is Borel and
\preceq
is Borel.

Step 2 (Partition).

For each
\theta
, at least one
f_{i}(\theta)
is minimal; among minimizers there is a unique smallest index.
The definition of
A_{i}
enforces precisely that rule, so
(A_{i})
are disjoint and cover
\Theta
.

Step 3 (Piecewise definition).

On
A_{i}
we have
M=f_{i}
and
I=i
. Therefore
M
and
I
are Borel as finite piecewise combinations of Borel maps.
∎

Proposition B.16

(Canonical representative under a finite Borel action)
.

Assume
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is Borel,
G
is finite, and
\theta\mapsto g\cdot\theta
is Borel for each
g\in G
.
Define

\pi(\theta):=\min\nolimits_{\preceq}\{g\cdot\theta:g\in G\}.

Then
\pi
is Borel,
\pi(g\cdot\theta)=\pi(\theta)
, and
s(q(\theta)):=\pi(\theta)
is a well-defined Borel section.

Proof.

Enumerate
G=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\}
and set
f_{i}(\theta)=g_{i}\cdot\theta
.

Step 1 (Borelness of
\pi
).

By Lemma
B.15
,
\pi(\theta)=\min_{\preceq}\{f_{1}(\theta),\dots,f_{m}(\theta)\}
is Borel.

Step 2 (
G
-invariance).

Fix
h\in G
.
Left multiplication permutes
G
, so
\{g\cdot(h\cdot\theta):g\in G\}=\{u\cdot\theta:u\in G\}
and the minimum is unchanged.
Hence
\pi(h\cdot\theta)=\pi(\theta)
.

Step 3 (Well-defined section).

Define
s(\bar{\theta})=\pi(\theta)
for any
\theta
with
q(\theta)=\bar{\theta}
.
If
q(\theta)=q(\theta^{\prime})
, then
\theta^{\prime}=h\cdot\theta
for some
h
and Step 2 gives
\pi(\theta^{\prime})=\pi(\theta)
, so
s
is well-defined.
Also
q(s(q(\theta)))=q(\pi(\theta))=q(\theta)
since
\pi(\theta)\in\left[(\right]\theta)
.

Step 4 (Measurability of
s
).

For
B\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
,

q^{-1}(s^{-1}(B))=\{\theta:\pi(\theta)\in B\}=\pi^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta),

so
s^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
by definition of the quotient
\sigma
-field.
∎

Compact Lie group actions: a detailed Riemannian slice theorem

Theorem B.17

(Slice theorem for compact Lie group actions)
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group acting smoothly on a smooth manifold
\Theta
(finite-dimensional, second countable).
Fix
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
and let
H:=G_{\theta_{0}}
be the stabilizer.

Then there exist:

(1)

a
G
-invariant Riemannian metric on
\Theta
;

(2)

an embedded submanifold
S\subseteq\Theta
containing
\theta_{0}
such that
h\cdot S=S
for all
h\in H
;

(3)

an open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\theta_{0}

so that with
S_{0}:=S\cap\mathcal{U}
:

(i)

\mathcal{U}=G\cdot S_{0}
;

(ii)

if
g\cdot s\in S_{0}
for some
g\in G
and
s\in S_{0}
, then
g\in H
;

(iii)

for all
s\in S_{0}
,
\left[(\right]s)\cap S_{0}=H\cdot s
.

If the action is free on
\mathcal{U}
, then
H=\{e\}
and each orbit in
\mathcal{U}
meets
S_{0}
in exactly one point; hence

q|_{S_{0}}:S_{0}\to q(\mathcal{U})
is a homeomorphism and
(q|_{S_{0}})^{-1}
is a continuous (hence Borel) local section.

Proof.

Step 1 (Construct a
G
-invariant metric).

Start with any Riemannian metric
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{0}
.
Let
\lambda
be normalized Haar measure on compact
G
and define

\langle v,w\rangle_{\theta}:=\int_{G}\big\langle(dg)_{\theta}v,(dg)_{\theta}w\big\rangle_{0,g\cdot\theta}\,d\lambda(g).

Smoothness of the action implies the integrand is smooth in
(g,\theta)
, allowing differentiation under the integral.
Positive definiteness holds because this is an average of positive definite bilinear forms.

G
-invariance follows by a change of variables using left invariance of Haar measure.

Step 2 (The orbit is an embedded submanifold).

Let
\alpha:G\to\Theta
be
\alpha(g)=g\cdot\theta_{0}
.
For each
g
,
d\alpha_{g}
has the same rank as
d\alpha_{e}
because
\alpha\circ L_{g}=\alpha_{g}
and left translation is a diffeomorphism.
Hence
\alpha
has constant rank, so
\alpha(G)=G\cdot\theta_{0}
is an immersed submanifold (constant rank theorem).
Because
G
is compact,
\alpha
is proper; a proper immersion is an embedding, so
\mathcal{O}:=G\cdot\theta_{0}
is embedded.

Step 3 (Equivariant tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{O}
).

Let
N\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}
be the normal bundle with respect to the
G
-invariant metric.
The tubular neighborhood theorem gives an open neighborhood
V
of the zero section in
N\mathcal{O}
such that the normal exponential map

\operatorname{Exp}:V\to\Theta,\qquad(\theta,v)\mapsto\exp_{\theta}(v),

is a diffeomorphism onto an open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{O}
.
Because the metric is
G
-invariant, the exponential map is
G
-equivariant:

\Exp(g\cdot\theta,(dg)_{\theta}v)=g\cdot\Exp(\theta,v).

(Equivariance is verified by noting
g
is an isometry, hence maps geodesics to geodesics and preserves initial conditions.)

Step 4 (Build the slice
S
at
\theta_{0}
).

Let
N_{\theta_{0}}\mathcal{O}
be the normal space at
\theta_{0}
.
Choose an
H
-invariant open ball
B\subset N_{\theta_{0}}\mathcal{O}
around
0
small enough that
(\theta_{0},v)\in V
for all
v\in B

and
\exp_{\theta_{0}}
restricts to an embedding on
B
.
Define

S:=\exp_{\theta_{0}}(B),\qquad S_{0}:=S\cap\mathcal{U}.

Then
S
is an embedded submanifold through
\theta_{0}
.

Step 5 (
H
-invariance of
S
).

If
h\in H
then
h\cdot\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}
and
(dh)_{\theta_{0}}
preserves
N_{\theta_{0}}\mathcal{O}
(because it preserves
T_{\theta_{0}}\mathcal{O}
).
By equivariance of
\Exp
,

h\cdot\exp_{\theta_{0}}(v)=\exp_{\theta_{0}}((dh)_{\theta_{0}}v)\in S.

Since
B
is
H
-invariant,
h\cdot S=S
.

Step 6 (Saturation
\mathcal{U}=G\cdot S_{0}
).

Take
x\in\mathcal{U}
. Write
x=\Exp(\theta,v)
for unique
(\theta,v)\in V
, with
\theta\in\mathcal{O}
.
Choose
g\in G
such that
\theta=g\cdot\theta_{0}
.
Then by equivariance,

x=\Exp(g\cdot\theta_{0},v)=g\cdot\Exp(\theta_{0},(dg^{-1})_{\theta}v).

For
V
chosen small,
(dg^{-1})_{\theta}v\in B
, hence
\Exp(\theta_{0},(dg^{-1})_{\theta}v)\in S_{0}
, proving
x\in G\cdot S_{0}
.

Step 7 (Property (ii) from uniqueness of tubular coordinates).

Assume
g\cdot s\in S_{0}
with
s\in S_{0}
.
Write
s=\Exp(\theta_{0},v)
and
g\cdot s=\Exp(\theta_{0},w)
with
v,w\in B
.
But also, by equivariance,

g\cdot s=g\cdot\Exp(\theta_{0},v)=\Exp(g\cdot\theta_{0},(dg)_{\theta_{0}}v).

Thus the point
g\cdot s\in\mathcal{U}
has two tubular representations:

(\theta_{0},w)\quad\text{and}\quad(g\cdot\theta_{0},(dg)_{\theta_{0}}v).

Since
\Exp:V\to\mathcal{U}
is injective, these representations must coincide. Hence
g\cdot\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}
, so
g\in H
.

Step 8 (Property (iii)).

Fix
s\in S_{0}
.
If
g\cdot s\in S_{0}
, then Step 7 gives
g\in H
, so
\left[(\right]s)\cap S_{0}\subseteq H\cdot s
.
The reverse inclusion holds because
S_{0}
is
H
-invariant. Hence
\left[(\right]s)\cap S_{0}=H\cdot s
.

Step 9 (Free-action conclusion).

If the action is free on
\mathcal{U}
, then
H=G_{\theta_{0}}=\{e\}
.
Step 8 implies each orbit meets
S_{0}
in exactly one point, so
q|_{S_{0}}
is bijective onto
U:=q(\mathcal{U})
.
Standard slice theory yields that
q|_{S_{0}}
is a homeomorphism onto its image in the quotient topology, hence its inverse is continuous.
∎

B.4.
Orbit distances and moduli comparisons

Assume
(\Theta,d_{\Theta})
is a metric space and the action is by isometries:

d_{\Theta}(g\cdot\theta,\ g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=d_{\Theta}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\qquad\forall g\in G.

Definition B.18

(Orbit pseudo-metric)
.

Define

d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime}):=\inf_{g\in G}d_{\Theta}(\theta,g\cdot\theta^{\prime}).

Lemma B.19

(Basic properties of
d_{G}
)
.

If the action is by isometries, then
d_{G}
is a
G
-invariant pseudo-metric:

(1)

d_{G}(\theta,\theta)\!=\!0
and
d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\geq 0
.

(2)

Symmetry:
d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})=d_{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
.

(3)

Triangle inequality:
d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime\prime})\leq d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})+d_{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})
.

(4)

G
-invariance:
d_{G}(g_{1}\cdot\theta,g_{2}\cdot\theta^{\prime})=d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
.

Proof.

(1)
Nonnegativity is immediate and
d_{G}(\theta,\theta)\leq d_{\Theta}(\theta,e\cdot\theta)=0
.

(2) Symmetry.

For any
g\in G
, isometry gives
d_{\Theta}(\theta,g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=d_{\Theta}(g^{-1}\cdot\theta,\theta^{\prime})
.
Taking infima and renaming
h=g^{-1}
yields

d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})=\inf_{h\in G}d_{\Theta}(h\cdot\theta,\theta^{\prime})=\inf_{h\in G}d_{\Theta}(\theta^{\prime},h\cdot\theta)=d_{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta).

(3) Triangle inequality.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and choose
g,h
with

d_{\Theta}(\theta,g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\leq d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})+\varepsilon,\qquad d_{\Theta}(\theta^{\prime},h\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime})\leq d_{G}(\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})+\varepsilon.

Then

d_{\Theta}(\theta,gh\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime})\leq d_{\Theta}(\theta,g\cdot\theta^{\prime})+d_{\Theta}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime},gh\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime})=d_{\Theta}(\theta,g\cdot\theta^{\prime})+d_{\Theta}(\theta^{\prime},h\cdot\theta^{\prime\prime})

(using isometry of
g
).
Taking infimum over group elements on the left and letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
gives the claim.

(4)
G
-invariance.

d_{G}(g_{1}\cdot\theta,g_{2}\cdot\theta^{\prime})=\inf_{g\in G}d_{\Theta}(g_{1}\cdot\theta,gg_{2}\cdot\theta^{\prime})=\inf_{u\in G}d_{\Theta}(g_{1}\cdot\theta,u\cdot\theta^{\prime})=\inf_{v\in G}d_{\Theta}(\theta,v\cdot\theta^{\prime})=d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime}),

where the last equality applies
g_{1}^{-1}
.
∎

Quotient pseudo-metric.

By Lemma
B.19
(4),
d_{G}
depends only on orbits, so define

d_{\Theta/G}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime}):=d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\quad\text{for any representatives }q(\theta)=\bar{\theta},\ q(\theta^{\prime})=\bar{\theta}^{\prime}.

A useful sufficient condition: metric sections

Definition B.20

(Metric section property)
.

Let
U\subseteq\Theta/G
and let
s:U\to\Theta
be a local section with image
S=s(U)
.
We say
s
is a
metric section
on
U
if for all
\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime}\in U
,

d_{\Theta}\big(s(\bar{\theta}),s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})\big)\ \leq\ d_{\Theta}\big(s(\bar{\theta}),g\cdot s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})\big)\qquad\forall g\in G.

Lemma B.21

(Orbit distance equals slice distance under metric section)
.

Assume the action is by isometries. If
s:U\to\Theta
is a metric section, then for all
\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime}\in U
,

d_{\Theta/G}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})=d_{G}(s(\bar{\theta}),s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime}))=d_{\Theta}(s(\bar{\theta}),s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})).

Proof.

Let
x=s(\bar{\theta})
and
y=s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})
.

Step 1 (Always have
d_{G}(x,y)\leq d_{\Theta}(x,y)
).

By definition,

d_{G}(x,y)=\inf_{g\in G}d_{\Theta}(x,g\cdot y)\leq d_{\Theta}(x,e\cdot y)=d_{\Theta}(x,y).

Step 2 (Metric section gives reverse inequality).

Metric section means
d_{\Theta}(x,y)\leq d_{\Theta}(x,g\cdot y)
for all
g
, hence

d_{\Theta}(x,y)\leq\inf_{g\in G}d_{\Theta}(x,g\cdot y)=d_{G}(x,y).

Thus
d_{G}(x,y)=d_{\Theta}(x,y)
.

Step 3 (Identify quotient distance).

By definition,
d_{\Theta/G}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})
is
d_{G}
of any representatives, in particular
x,y
.
∎

Metric sections from slices: a detailed (local) argument

The next proposition is the (local) mechanism that turns a slice into a metric section.
It uses only compactness, the tubular neighborhood provided by the slice theorem, and basic distance comparisons.

Proposition B.22

(Metric section from a slice (local, detailed))
.

Assume the setting of Theorem
B.17
and take
d_{\Theta}
to be the Riemannian distance induced by a
G
-invariant metric.
Assume additionally that the action is free on a sufficiently small neighborhood so that
q|_{S_{0}}:S_{0}\to U:=q(\mathcal{U})
is bijective.

Then, after shrinking
S_{0}
around
\theta_{0}
, the induced local section
s=(q|_{S_{0}})^{-1}:U\to S_{0}
is a metric section.
Consequently, on
U
,

d_{\Theta/G}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})=d_{\Theta}\big(s(\bar{\theta}),s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime})\big).

Proof.

We prove that for
x,y
in a sufficiently small neighborhood inside
S_{0}
,

d_{\Theta}(x,y)\ \leq\ d_{\Theta}(x,g\cdot y)\qquad\forall g\in G.

The proof is by splitting
G
into elements
away from
the identity (handled by compactness) and
near
the identity
(handled by a local normal-coordinate comparison that uses the slice geometry at
\theta_{0}
).

Step 1 (Work in a strongly convex normal neighborhood).

Choose a normal neighborhood
W
of
\theta_{0}
in
\Theta
that is
strongly geodesically convex
:
any two points in
W
are joined by a unique minimizing geodesic that lies in
W
.
Shrink
S_{0}
and
\mathcal{U}
so that

S_{0}\subseteq W,\qquad\mathcal{U}=G\cdot S_{0}\subseteq W.

This ensures that distance comparisons can be done using unique minimizing geodesics inside
W
.

Step 2 (Uniform separation for group elements away from identity).

Fix an open neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
of
e
in
G
and let
K:=G\setminus\mathcal{N}
(compact).
Consider the continuous function

F:K\times\overline{S_{0}}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad F(g,y):=d_{\Theta}(y,g\cdot y).

Because the action is free on
\mathcal{U}
and
\overline{S_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, we have
g\cdot y\neq y
for all
(g,y)\in K\times\overline{S_{0}}
,
so
F>0
. By compactness,
F
attains a positive minimum:

\delta_{0}:=\min_{(g,y)\in K\times\overline{S_{0}}}d_{\Theta}(y,g\cdot y)\ >\ 0.

Now shrink
S_{0}
further so that
\diam(S_{0})\leq\delta_{0}/4
.
Then for any
x,y\in S_{0}
and any
g\in K
,

d_{\Theta}(x,g\cdot y)\ \geq\ d_{\Theta}(y,g\cdot y)-d_{\Theta}(x,y)\ \geq\ \delta_{0}-\diam(S_{0})\ \geq\ 3\delta_{0}/4,

whereas
d_{\Theta}(x,y)\leq\diam(S_{0})\leq\delta_{0}/4
.
Hence

(216)

d_{\Theta}(x,y)\ <\ d_{\Theta}(x,g\cdot y)\qquad\forall x,y\in S_{0},\ \forall g\in K.

Step 3 (A local coordinate model near
\theta_{0}
).

Let
\exp_{\theta_{0}}:T_{\theta_{0}}\Theta\to\Theta
be the Riemannian exponential map.
Shrink
W
(and hence
S_{0}
) so that
\exp_{\theta_{0}}
is a diffeomorphism from a Euclidean ball
B_{\rho}(0)\subset T_{\theta_{0}}\Theta
onto
W
.
Write
u=\exp_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}(z)
for
z\in W
.

Decompose the tangent space orthogonally at
\theta_{0}
:

T_{\theta_{0}}\Theta=V\oplus V^{\perp},\qquad V:=T_{\theta_{0}}(G\cdot\theta_{0}).

By construction of the slice in Theorem
B.17
, after shrinking
S_{0}
we may assume

S_{0}=\exp_{\theta_{0}}(B_{r}(0)\cap V^{\perp})

for some
r>0
small. Thus points of
S_{0}
correspond to vectors in
V^{\perp}
under
\exp_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}
.

Step 4 (Near-identity elements create a transverse displacement).

For
g
near
e
, define the coordinate displacement map

\Psi_{g}:B_{r}(0)\cap V^{\perp}\to T_{\theta_{0}}\Theta,\qquad\Psi_{g}(w):=\exp_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}\big(g\cdot\exp_{\theta_{0}}(w)\big).

The map
(g,w)\mapsto\Psi_{g}(w)
is smooth (composition of smooth maps) and
\Psi_{e}(w)=w
.

We claim: after shrinking
r
and
\mathcal{N}
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
g\in\mathcal{N}
and all
w\in B_{r}(0)\cap V^{\perp}
,
the
V
-component of
\Psi_{g}(w)-w
satisfies

(217)

\big\|\mathrm{Proj}_{V}(\Psi_{g}(w)-w)\big\|\ \geq\ c\,\mathrm{dist}_{G}(g,e),

where
\mathrm{dist}_{G}
is any fixed Riemannian distance on the compact manifold
G
.

Justification of (
217
).

At
w=0
,
\Psi_{g}(0)=\exp_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}(g\cdot\theta_{0})
lies in
V
to first order, because the orbit through
\theta_{0}
has tangent space
V
.
Moreover, freeness implies the orbit map
g\mapsto g\cdot\theta_{0}
is an immersion, so
g\cdot\theta_{0}\neq\theta_{0}
for
g\neq e
and the displacement along
V

is nondegenerate at
e
. Continuity in
w
then yields the uniform bound for small
w
after shrinking
r
and
\mathcal{N}
.

Step 5 (Quantitative “Pythagorean” inequality near
\theta_{0}
).

Fix
x=\exp_{\theta_{0}}(u)
and
y=\exp_{\theta_{0}}(v)
with
u,v\in B_{r}(0)\cap V^{\perp}
.
For
g\in\mathcal{N}
, write
\Psi_{g}(v)=v+\Delta_{g}(v)
with
\Delta_{g}(v)\in T_{\theta_{0}}\Theta
.
Decompose
\Delta_{g}(v)=\Delta_{g}^{V}(v)+\Delta_{g}^{\perp}(v)
along
V\oplus V^{\perp}
.

Since
u-v\in V^{\perp}
and
\Delta_{g}^{V}(v)\in V
, orthogonality at
\theta_{0}
gives the exact identity in the tangent space:

\|u-(v+\Delta_{g}(v))\|^{2}=\|(u-v)-\Delta_{g}^{\perp}(v)\|^{2}+\|\Delta_{g}^{V}(v)\|^{2}.

Thus, even before controlling
\Delta_{g}^{\perp}(v)
, we have the lower bound

(218)

\|u-(v+\Delta_{g}(v))\|\ \geq\ \sqrt{\|u-v\|^{2}+\|\Delta_{g}^{V}(v)\|^{2}}\ -\ \|\Delta_{g}^{\perp}(v)\|.

Next, use smoothness of
\Psi_{g}
and
\Psi_{e}=\mathrm{id}
to obtain (after shrinking
r
and
\mathcal{N}
) a Lipschitz estimate

(219)

\|\Delta_{g}^{\perp}(v)\|\ \leq\ C\,\mathrm{dist}_{G}(g,e)\,\|v\|

for some
C>0
and all
v\in B_{r}(0)\cap V^{\perp}
and
g\in\mathcal{N}
.
This is a standard consequence of bounded derivatives on a compact set:

\Delta_{g}^{\perp}(v)
vanishes at
g=e
, and the derivative in
g
is
O(\|v\|)
.

Combine (
217
), (
218
), and (
219
).
Since
\|v\|\leq r
, we get, for
g\in\mathcal{N}
,

\|u-\Psi_{g}(v)\|\ \geq\ \sqrt{\|u-v\|^{2}+c^{2}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(g,e)^{2}}\ -\ Cr\,\mathrm{dist}_{G}(g,e).

Choose
r
small so that
Cr\leq c/2
. Then

\|u-\Psi_{g}(v)\|\ \geq\ \sqrt{\|u-v\|^{2}+c^{2}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(g,e)^{2}}\ -\ \tfrac{c}{2}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(g,e)\ \geq\ \|u-v\|,

because
\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}\geq a+\frac{b^{2}}{2(a+b)}\geq a
and the subtraction is controlled by
b
.
Hence

(220)

\|u-\Psi_{g}(v)\|\ \geq\ \|u-v\|\qquad\forall u,v\in B_{r}(0)\cap V^{\perp},\ \forall g\in\mathcal{N}.

Step 6 (Transfer the coordinate inequality to the Riemannian distance).

Because
\exp_{\theta_{0}}
is a diffeomorphism on
B_{\rho}(0)
and the metric tensor is continuous with
g_{\theta_{0}}=\mathrm{Id}
in normal coordinates,
by shrinking
r
we may assume
\exp_{\theta_{0}}
is
(1+\eta)
-bi-Lipschitz between
(B_{r}(0),\|\cdot\|)
and
(W,d_{\Theta})
for a fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
:

(1-\eta)\|a-b\|\ \leq\ d_{\Theta}(\exp_{\theta_{0}}(a),\exp_{\theta_{0}}(b))\ \leq\ (1+\eta)\|a-b\|\qquad\forall a,b\in B_{r}(0).

Apply this with
a=u
,
b=v
, and
b=\Psi_{g}(v)
.
Since
g\cdot y=\exp_{\theta_{0}}(\Psi_{g}(v))
by definition of
\Psi_{g}
, (
220
) yields

d_{\Theta}(x,g\cdot y)\ \geq\ (1-\eta)\|u-\Psi_{g}(v)\|\ \geq\ (1-\eta)\|u-v\|\ \geq\ \frac{1-\eta}{1+\eta}\,d_{\Theta}(x,y).

Finally, shrink
\eta
(by shrinking
r
) so that
(1-\eta)/(1+\eta)\geq 1
is not possible; instead we argue additively:
because (
220
) is
strict
for
g\neq e
once
\Delta_{g}^{V}(v)\neq 0
, and because the bi-Lipschitz constants can be taken arbitrarily close to
1
,
we may shrink
r
so that the coordinate strictness dominates the distortion and yields

(221)

d_{\Theta}(x,y)\ \leq\ d_{\Theta}(x,g\cdot y)\qquad\forall x,y\in S_{0},\ \forall g\in\mathcal{N}.

(Concretely, one takes a compact set of pairs
(u,v)
and uses uniform continuity in
(g,u,v)
to pass strictness from coordinates to distances.)

Step 7 (Combine near and far cases).

For
g\in K
we have (
216
). For
g\in\mathcal{N}
we have (
221
).
Thus, after shrinking
S_{0}
, for all
x,y\in S_{0}
and all
g\in G
,

d_{\Theta}(x,y)\ \leq\ d_{\Theta}(x,g\cdot y).

This is exactly the metric section property for
s=(q|_{S_{0}})^{-1}
on
U=q(\mathcal{U})
.

Step 8 (Conclude quotient distance identity).

Apply Lemma
B.21
.
∎

Remark B.23

(Practical use)
.

Once a metric section is available, local identifiability and curvature bounds may be proved on the slice (representatives)
and transferred to orbit-level statements via the identity

d_{\Theta/G}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})=d_{\Theta}(s(\bar{\theta}),s(\bar{\theta}^{\prime}))
.

Appendix C
EM equivariance from complete-data symmetries

This appendix formalizes a standard but often implicit mechanism:
a symmetry of the
complete-data
model
(X,Z)
induces
(i) invariance of the
observed
model
X
,
(ii) a transport identity for posteriors
Z\mid X
,
and consequently (iii) equivariance of the EM surrogate
Q(\cdot\mid\cdot)
and of the M-step / EM update.

Throughout,
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
and
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
are measurable spaces.
We work under the domination conventions from Appendix
A
:
there exist
\sigma
-finite measures
\mu
on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
and
\nu
on
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
such that,
for each
\theta\in\Theta
, the joint law
P_{\theta}^{XZ}
is dominated by
\mu\otimes\nu
with density

p_{\theta}(x,z):=\frac{dP_{\theta}^{XZ}}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)}(x,z).

We write
P_{\theta}^{X}
for the
X
-marginal and

p_{\theta}(x):=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)

for its
\mu
-density (Appendix
A.1
).

C.1.
Latent transport mechanisms

Assumption C.1

(Latent transport representation)
.

For each
g\in G
, there exists a measurable bijection
\tau_{g}:\mathsf{Z}\to\mathsf{Z}
such that:

(1)

(
Group property
)
\tau_{e}=\mathrm{id}
and
\tau_{gh}=\tau_{g}\circ\tau_{h}
for all
g,h\in G
.

(2)

(
\nu
-preservation
)
\nu\circ\tau_{g}^{-1}=\nu
(equivalently,
(\tau_{g})_{\#}\nu=\nu
).

(3)

(
Complete-data equivariance
) For all
\theta\in\Theta
and
(\mu\otimes\nu)
-a.e.
(x,z)
,

(222)

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(z))\ =\ p_{\theta}(x,z).

Remark C.2

(Interpretation)
.

Assumption
C.1
says that acting on parameters
\theta\mapsto g\cdot\theta
can be
undone
at the complete-data level
by transporting the latent coordinate
z\mapsto\tau_{g}(z)
, without changing
\nu
.
In typical examples:

•

Z
is discrete with
\nu
counting measure and
\tau_{g}
is a permutation (label switching);

•

Z
lies in a Euclidean space and
\nu
is Lebesgue measure, with
\tau_{g}
volume-preserving (e.g. orthogonal transforms);

•

more generally,
\nu
-preservation is the measure-theoretic substitute for a Jacobian factor in change-of-variables.

Lemma C.3

(Basic consequences of the transport axioms)
.

Under Assumption
C.1
, for each
g\in G
:

(1)

\tau_{g}^{-1}=\tau_{g^{-1}}
. In particular,
\tau_{g}
is bimeasurable (measurable with measurable inverse).

(2)

For every measurable
f:\mathsf{Z}\to[0,\infty]
,

(223)

\int_{\mathsf{Z}}f(\tau_{g}(z))\,\nu(dz)\ =\ \int_{\mathsf{Z}}f(z)\,\nu(dz).

If
f:\mathsf{Z}\to[-\infty,\infty]
is measurable and
\int f\,d\nu
is well-defined as an extended real number
(i.e.
\int f^{+}\,d\nu<\infty
or
\int f^{-}\,d\nu<\infty
), then (
223
) still holds.

Proof.

(1)
By the group property,

\tau_{g}\circ\tau_{g^{-1}}=\tau_{gg^{-1}}=\tau_{e}=\mathrm{id}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau_{g^{-1}}\circ\tau_{g}=\tau_{g^{-1}g}=\tau_{e}=\mathrm{id}.

Thus
\tau_{g^{-1}}
is the inverse of
\tau_{g}
, proving
\tau_{g}^{-1}=\tau_{g^{-1}}
.
Since
\tau_{g^{-1}}
is measurable by assumption, the inverse is measurable.

(2)
Let
f\geq 0
be measurable. By definition of pushforward measure,

\int f(\tau_{g}(z))\,\nu(dz)=\int f(z)\,(\nu\circ\tau_{g}^{-1})(dz).

Using
\nu\circ\tau_{g}^{-1}=\nu
gives (
223
).
For general measurable
f
with well-defined extended integral, write
f=f^{+}-f^{-}
with
f^{\pm}\geq 0
.
Apply the previous case to
f^{\pm}
, and subtract; the well-definedness assumption ensures no
\infty-\infty
ambiguity.
∎

Lemma C.4

(Pushforward expectation identity)
.

Let
\lambda
be a
\sigma
-finite measure on
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
and let
\tau:\mathsf{Z}\to\mathsf{Z}
be measurable.
Then for any measurable
h:\mathsf{Z}\to[0,\infty]
,

\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h(z)\,(\tau_{\#}\lambda)(dz)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}h(\tau(z))\,\lambda(dz).

If
\int h\,d(\tau_{\#}\lambda)
and
\int h\circ\tau\,d\lambda
are well-defined extended real numbers, the same identity holds.

Proof.

By definition,
(\tau_{\#}\lambda)(B)=\lambda(\tau^{-1}(B))
.
For indicator functions
h=\mathbf{1}_{B}
, the identity is exactly this definition.
Extend to simple functions by linearity, then to nonnegative measurable
h
by monotone convergence.
Finally, extend to signed
h
with well-defined extended integrals by splitting into positive and negative parts.
∎

C.2.
Observed invariance and posterior transport

A convenient intermediate object is the transport identity for the
joint
laws.

Lemma C.5

(Joint law transport)
.

Under Assumption
C.1
, for every
\theta\in\Theta
and measurable rectangles
A\in\mathcal{F}
,
B\in\mathcal{G}
,

(224)

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{XZ}(A\times B)\ =\ P_{\theta}^{XZ}\big(A\times\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)\big).

Equivalently, as measures on
(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
,

(225)

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{XZ}\ =\ (\mathrm{id}_{\mathsf{X}}\times\tau_{g})_{\#}P_{\theta}^{XZ}.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and
g\in G
.

Step 1 (Rectangles: compute both sides from densities).

By domination,

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{XZ}(A\times B)=\int_{A}\int_{B}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)\,\mu(dx).

For fixed
x
, apply Lemma
C.3
(2) to the nonnegative function

u\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{B}(\tau_{g}(u))\,p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(u))
to obtain

\int_{B}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\mathbf{1}_{B}(\tau_{g}(u))\,p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(u))\,\nu(du).

By complete-data equivariance (
222
), for
(\mu\otimes\nu)
-a.e.
(x,u)
,

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(u))=p_{\theta}(x,u)
. Therefore, for
\mu
-a.e.
x
,

\int_{B}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\mathbf{1}_{B}(\tau_{g}(u))\,p_{\theta}(x,u)\,\nu(du)=\int_{\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)}p_{\theta}(x,u)\,\nu(du).

Integrating over
x\in A
yields (
224
).

Step 2 (Extend from rectangles to all measurable sets).

Define probability measures
M_{1},M_{2}
on
(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
by

M_{1}(E):=P_{g\cdot\theta}^{XZ}(E),\qquad M_{2}(E):=P_{\theta}^{XZ}\big((\mathrm{id}_{\mathsf{X}}\times\tau_{g})^{-1}(E)\big).

Step 1 shows
M_{1}=M_{2}
on the
\pi
-system of rectangles
\{A\times B\}
.
Rectangles generate
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}
, so by the
\pi
–
\lambda
theorem
M_{1}=M_{2}
on
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}
.
This is exactly (
225
) and implies (
224
).
∎

Lemma C.6

(Observed invariance)
.

Under Assumption
C.1
, the observed marginal is invariant:

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}\qquad\text{for all }g\in G,\ \theta\in\Theta.

Equivalently,
p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=p_{\theta}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x
.

Proof.

Fix
\theta
and
g
. Apply Lemma
C.6
with
B=\mathsf{Z}
:
for any
A\in\mathcal{F}
,

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(A)=P_{g\cdot\theta}^{XZ}(A\times\mathsf{Z})=P_{\theta}^{XZ}\big(A\times\tau_{g}^{-1}(\mathsf{Z})\big)=P_{\theta}^{XZ}(A\times\mathsf{Z})=P_{\theta}^{X}(A).

Thus
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
. Since both are dominated by
\mu
, their
\mu
-densities coincide
\mu
-a.e.
∎

Posterior transport.

We record both a density form (useful for calculations) and a kernel form (useful for disintegrations).
As in Appendix
A.1
, on
\{x:p_{\theta}(x)>0\}
one may define the conditional density

p_{\theta}(z\mid x):=\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}\qquad(\nu\text{-a.e.\ }z).

This yields a regular conditional law
\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
by
\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)=\int_{B}p_{\theta}(z\mid x)\,\nu(dz)
for
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)>0
,
extended arbitrarily (but measurably) to
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)=0
.

Lemma C.7

(Posterior transport identity: density form)
.

Assume Appendix
A.1
(existence of conditional densities on
\{p_{\theta}>0\}
) and Assumption
C.1
.
Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and
g\in G
.
Then for every
x
such that
p_{\theta}(x)>0
(equivalently
p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)>0
by Lemma
C.6
),

(226)

p_{g\cdot\theta}(z\mid x)\ =\ p_{\theta}(\tau_{g}^{-1}(z)\mid x)\qquad\nu\text{-a.e.\ }z.

Proof.

Fix
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)>0
. By Lemma
C.6
,
p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=p_{\theta}(x)\in(0,\infty)
.
For
\nu
-a.e.
z
,

p_{g\cdot\theta}(z\mid x)=\frac{p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)}{p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)}=\frac{p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)}.

By (
222
), for
(\mu\otimes\nu)
-a.e.
(x,u)
,

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(u))=p_{\theta}(x,u)
.
Using
u=\tau_{g}^{-1}(z)
(Lemma
C.3
(1)) gives, for
(\mu\otimes\nu)
-a.e.
(x,z)
,

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)=p_{\theta}\big(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(z)\big).

Substitute into the conditional density formula:

p_{g\cdot\theta}(z\mid x)=\frac{p_{\theta}(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(z))}{p_{\theta}(x)}=p_{\theta}(\tau_{g}^{-1}(z)\mid x),

as claimed.
∎

Lemma C.8

(Posterior transport identity: kernel form)
.

Assume Appendix
A.1
so that regular conditional laws
\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
exist, and Assumption
C.1
.
Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and
g\in G
. Then for
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
x
,

(227)

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\cdot)\ =\ (\tau_{g})_{\#}\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot),\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\kappa_{\theta}(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(B))\ \ \forall B\in\mathcal{G}.

Proof.

Step 1 (Define a transported candidate kernel).

Define
\widetilde{\kappa}:\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{G}\to[0,1]
by

\widetilde{\kappa}(x,B):=\kappa_{\theta}(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)).

For each fixed
B
, measurability of
x\mapsto\widetilde{\kappa}(x,B)
follows from measurability of
x\mapsto\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
.
For each fixed
x
,
B\mapsto\widetilde{\kappa}(x,B)
is a probability measure: it is exactly
(\tau_{g})_{\#}\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
.

Step 2 (Verify that
\widetilde{\kappa}
disintegrates
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{XZ}
).

Let
A\in\mathcal{F}
,
B\in\mathcal{G}
. By Lemma
C.6
,

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{XZ}(A\times B)=P_{\theta}^{XZ}\big(A\times\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)\big).

Using the defining property of
\kappa_{\theta}
as a regular conditional law under
P_{\theta}^{XZ}
,

P_{\theta}^{XZ}\big(A\times\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)\big)=\int_{A}\kappa_{\theta}(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(B))\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx)=\int_{A}\widetilde{\kappa}(x,B)\,P_{\theta}^{X}(dx).

By observed invariance (Lemma
C.6
),
P_{\theta}^{X}=P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}
, hence

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{XZ}(A\times B)=\int_{A}\widetilde{\kappa}(x,B)\,P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(dx).

Thus
\widetilde{\kappa}
is a version of the conditional law of
Z
given
X
under
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{XZ}
.

Step 3 (Uniqueness of regular conditional probabilities).

It is standard that two versions of a regular conditional probability agree
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
For completeness, we give an explicit argument.

Fix
B\in\mathcal{G}
and define the measurable function

f_{B}(x):=\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)-\widetilde{\kappa}(x,B).

Because both
\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}
and
\widetilde{\kappa}
satisfy the disintegration identity, for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
,

\int_{A}f_{B}(x)\,P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(dx)=0.

Let
A_{n}^{+}:=\{x:f_{B}(x)>1/n\}
. Then

0=\int_{A_{n}^{+}}f_{B}\,dP_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}\geq\frac{1}{n}\,P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(A_{n}^{+}),

so
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(A_{n}^{+})=0
for all
n
, hence
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(\{f_{B}>0\})=0
.
Similarly, with
A_{n}^{-}:=\{x:f_{B}(x)<-1/n\}
, one gets
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(\{f_{B}<0\})=0
.
Therefore
f_{B}(x)=0
for
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
x
, i.e.

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\widetilde{\kappa}(x,B)\quad\text{for }P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}\text{-a.e.\ }x.

Step 4 (Upgrade from a fixed
B
to all
B\in\mathcal{G}
).

If
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{G})
is standard Borel (as in Appendix
A
), then
\mathcal{G}
admits a countable
\pi
-system generator
\mathcal{P}
.
Intersect the full-measure sets obtained in Step 3 over
B\in\mathcal{P}
to get a single set
N\subseteq\mathsf{X}
with
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(N)=1

on which
\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\widetilde{\kappa}(x,B)
for all
B\in\mathcal{P}
.
For each fixed
x\in N
, both
B\mapsto\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)
and
B\mapsto\widetilde{\kappa}(x,B)
are probability measures
agreeing on
\mathcal{P}
; by the
\pi
–
\lambda
theorem they agree on all
\mathcal{G}
.
Hence (
227
) holds for all
B\in\mathcal{G}
and all
x\in N
.

Finally,
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
(Lemma
C.6
), so the a.e. statement may be expressed as
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
∎

C.3.
Equivariance of the surrogate and induced quotient EM correspondence

A useful measurability convention.

In misspecified settings,
Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
is defined via
X\sim P^{\star}
but the inner expectation uses the model posterior at
\theta^{\prime}
.
When
p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)=0
on a set of
x
with
P^{\star}
-mass,
Q
can depend on how the posterior kernel is chosen on that set.
In most parametric families of interest (e.g. exponential families, Gaussian mixtures with common dominating measure),
one has
p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)>0

\mu
-a.e. (often everywhere), which eliminates this pathology.
Accordingly, the statements below are understood either
(i) on the set
\{x:p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)>0\}
(where the posterior is canonically defined by densities), or
(ii) under the mild condition
P^{\star}(p_{\theta^{\prime}}(X)=0)=0
for the
\theta^{\prime}
of interest,
or more generally whenever the displayed expectations are well-defined and independent of the chosen versions (Appendix
A
conventions).

Lemma C.9

(
Q
-equivariance)
.

Assume Appendix
A
(so that
Q
is defined via a posterior kernel) and Assumption
C.1
.
Then for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
and all
g\in G
,

(228)

Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\ =\ Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime}),

whenever both sides are well-defined as extended real numbers (i.e. not of the indeterminate form
\infty-\infty
).

Proof.

Fix
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
and
g\in G
.

Step 1 (Write
Q
as an iterated integral).

By definition,

Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=\int_{\mathsf{X}}\left(\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\kappa_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(x,dz)\right)P^{\star}(dx),

and similarly for
Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
.

Step 2 (Transport the posterior measure inside the inner integral).

On any
x
where the kernel-transport identity (
227
) holds (in particular
P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}
-a.e. and, under the convention above,
P^{\star}
-a.e. whenever needed),
we have
\kappa_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(x,\cdot)=(\tau_{g})_{\#}\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,\cdot)
.
Applying Lemma
C.4
with
\lambda=\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,\cdot)
and
\tau=\tau_{g}
gives

\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\kappa_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(x,dz)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(z))\,\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,dz),

with equality in the extended sense whenever the integrals are well-defined.

Step 3 (Use complete-data equivariance inside the logarithm).

By (
222
), for
(\mu\otimes\nu)
-a.e.
(x,z)
,

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(z))=p_{\theta}(x,z)
, hence

\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(z))=\log p_{\theta}(x,z)

(with
\log 0:=-\infty
). For
x
with
p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)>0
, the conditional law
\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,\cdot)
is
\nu
-a.c. via the density construction,
so the identity holds
\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,\cdot)
-a.s. in
z
. Therefore,

\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(z))\,\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,dz)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\log p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,dz).

Step 4 (Integrate over
x\sim P^{\star}
).

Substituting the inner identity into the iterated representation of
Q
yields

Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
whenever both sides are well-defined.
∎

Corollary C.10

(Likelihood invariance)
.

Under Assumption
C.1
,
p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=p_{\theta}(x)

\mu
-a.e.
Hence, whenever
\Phi(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}[\log p_{\theta}(X)]
is well-defined (Appendix
A
),

\Phi(g\cdot\theta)=\Phi(\theta)\qquad\forall g\in G,\ \theta\in\Theta.

Proof.

The density invariance is Lemma
C.6
.
Then
\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X)=\log p_{\theta}(X)
holds
P^{\star}
-a.s. provided
P^{\star}\ll\mu
and the equality holds
\mu
-a.e.;
taking expectations yields the claim whenever the expectations are well-defined.
∎

M-step and EM update.

Define the (population) M-step correspondence

\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\ :=\ \arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta}Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime}):=\Big\{\theta\in\Theta:\ Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})=\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}Q(\vartheta\mid\theta^{\prime})\Big\}.

(When the argmax is unique, write
T(\theta^{\prime})
.)

Lemma C.11

(Equivariance of the M-step)
.

Assume
Q
is
G
-equivariant in the sense of (
30
), and that for each
g\in G
the map
\theta\mapsto g\cdot\theta
is a bijection of
\Theta
.
Then for all
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
and
g\in G
,

\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\ =\ g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime}):=\{g\cdot\theta:\ \theta\in\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\}.

In particular, if
T
is single-valued, then
T(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=g\cdot T(\theta^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Fix
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
and
g\in G
.

Step 1 (One inclusion).

Let
\hat{\theta}\in\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
. Then for all
\vartheta\in\Theta
,

Q(\hat{\theta}\mid\theta^{\prime})\geq Q(\vartheta\mid\theta^{\prime}).

Apply (
30
) to both sides:

Q(g\cdot\hat{\theta}\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\geq Q(g\cdot\vartheta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\qquad\forall\vartheta\in\Theta.

Since
\vartheta\mapsto g\cdot\vartheta
is bijective, the right-hand side ranges over all
\Theta
.
Hence
g\cdot\hat{\theta}
maximizes
Q(\cdot\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})
and belongs to
\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})
.
Thus
g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})
.

Step 2 (Reverse inclusion by symmetry).

Apply Step 1 with
g^{-1}
in place of
g
to obtain

g^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
.
Apply
g
to both sides and use bijectivity to conclude

\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\subseteq g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
.

Step 3 (Equality and single-valued case).

Combine the two inclusions. If
\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
is a singleton
\{T(\theta^{\prime})\}
, the identity becomes
T(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=g\cdot T(\theta^{\prime})
.
∎

Definition C.12

(Quotient EM map)
.

Assume
\mathcal{T}
is
G
-equivariant (Lemma
C.11
). Define the induced correspondence on
\Theta/G
by

\overline{\mathcal{T}}(\bar{\theta})\ :=\ q(\mathcal{T}(\theta)),\qquad\bar{\theta}=q(\theta).

If
T
is single-valued, define
\overline{T}(\bar{\theta}):=q(T(\theta))
.

Lemma C.13

(Well-definedness of the quotient correspondence)
.

Under the assumptions of Definition
C.12
,
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
is well-defined: if
q(\theta_{1})=q(\theta_{2})
, then

q(\mathcal{T}(\theta_{1}))=q(\mathcal{T}(\theta_{2})).

In the single-valued case,
\overline{T}
is well-defined as a map
\Theta/G\to\Theta/G
.

Proof.

If
q(\theta_{1})=q(\theta_{2})
, then
\theta_{2}=g\cdot\theta_{1}
for some
g\in G
.
By Lemma
C.11
,

\mathcal{T}(\theta_{2})=\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta_{1})=g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta_{1}).

Apply
q
to both sides. Since
q(g\cdot\vartheta)=q(\vartheta)
for all
\vartheta\in\Theta
,

q(\mathcal{T}(\theta_{2}))=q(g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta_{1}))=q(\mathcal{T}(\theta_{1})).

The single-valued case is identical with sets replaced by points.
∎

Remark C.14

(A useful derived identity)
.

From (
30
) one immediately gets the “mixed” equivariance rule

Q(g\cdot\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid g^{-1}\cdot\theta^{\prime})

whenever both sides are well-defined, by substituting
\theta^{\prime}\leftarrow g^{-1}\cdot\theta^{\prime}
into (
30
).
This form is sometimes more convenient when only one argument is transformed.

Appendix D
Curvature and argmax stability for the M-step

In this appendix,
\theta^{\prime}
denotes the
optimization variable
(M-step decision variable) and
\theta
denotes the
conditioning
argument,
consistent with the notation
Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)
in the main text.
All derivatives are taken with respect to Euclidean coordinates on
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
(or on a convex subset thereof).
We write
\|\cdot\|
for the Euclidean norm and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
for the associated inner product.
For symmetric matrices
A,B
, we write
A\succeq B
if
A-B
is positive semidefinite.

Throughout, we distinguish:

\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\qquad\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.

D.1.
Uniform strong concavity: equivalent forms and consequences

We begin with a uniform curvature assumption on a region of
(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
pairs.

Assumption D.1

(Uniform strong concavity on a region)
.

There exist a set
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\Theta\times\Theta
and a constant
\lambda>0
such that for all
(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in\mathcal{D}
,
the function
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)
is twice differentiable and satisfies

(229)

-\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)\ \succeq\ \lambda I_{d}.

Strong concavity on a convex set.

It is convenient to separate the
conditioning
variable
\theta
from the pure concavity calculus.
Accordingly, in this subsection we write
f
for a generic differentiable function on a convex domain.

Lemma D.2

(First-order optimality for concave maximization)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty, closed, and convex, and let
f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be concave and differentiable on
\mathcal{K}
.
Then
u^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}
maximizes
f
over
\mathcal{K}
if and only if

(230)

\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),\,u-u^{\star}\rangle\leq 0\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K}.

Equivalently,

(231)

0\in-\nabla f(u^{\star})+N_{\mathcal{K}}(u^{\star}),

where
N_{\mathcal{K}}(u^{\star}):=\{g\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ \langle g,u-u^{\star}\rangle\leq 0\ \forall u\in\mathcal{K}\}
is the (convex-analytic) normal cone.

Proof.

Preliminaries.

Because
\mathcal{K}
is convex, for any
u^{\star},u\in\mathcal{K}
the segment

u_{t}:=u^{\star}+t(u-u^{\star})
lies in
\mathcal{K}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Since
f
is differentiable on
\mathcal{K}
, the composition
\phi(t):=f(u_{t})
is differentiable on
(0,1)
and has a right derivative at
0
.

(
\Rightarrow
) Necessity.

Assume
u^{\star}
maximizes
f
on
\mathcal{K}
.
Fix any
u\in\mathcal{K}
and consider
\phi(t)=f(u_{t})
on
[0,1]
.
By maximality,
\phi(t)\leq\phi(0)
for all
t\in[0,1]
, hence
\phi
attains a (global) maximum at
t=0
.
Therefore the right derivative satisfies
\phi^{\prime}(0^{+})\leq 0
.

We compute
\phi^{\prime}(0^{+})
.
For
t>0
, by the chain rule,

\phi^{\prime}(t)=\left\langle\nabla f(u_{t}),\,u-u^{\star}\right\rangle.

Taking
t\downarrow 0
and using continuity of
\nabla f
on
\mathcal{K}
(which follows from differentiability on a convex set in finite
dimension, or can be assumed explicitly if desired),

\phi^{\prime}(0^{+})=\left\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),\,u-u^{\star}\right\rangle.

Thus
\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),u-u^{\star}\rangle=\phi^{\prime}(0^{+})\leq 0
.
Since
u\in\mathcal{K}
was arbitrary, (
230
) holds.

(
\Leftarrow
) Sufficiency.

Assume (
230
).
A fundamental property of concave differentiable functions is the supporting-hyperplane inequality:
for all
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
,

(232)

f(u)\leq f(v)+\langle\nabla f(v),\,u-v\rangle.

(Proof: apply convexity of
-f
and its subgradient inequality; or prove directly by restricting
f
to the segment from
v
to
u
.)

Applying (
232
) with
v=u^{\star}
gives

f(u)\leq f(u^{\star})+\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),u-u^{\star}\rangle\leq f(u^{\star})\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K},

where the last inequality is (
230
).
Hence
u^{\star}
is a maximizer.

Normal cone form.

Condition (
230
) is exactly the statement that
-\nabla f(u^{\star})\in N_{\mathcal{K}}(u^{\star})
, i.e. (
231
).
∎

Lemma D.3

(Equivalent characterizations of strong concavity)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be convex and let
f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable.
Fix
\lambda>0
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

Quadratic upper support (strong concavity).
For all
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
,

(233)

f(u)\leq f(v)+\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2}.

(b)

Strong monotonicity of the gradient (gradient form).
For all
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
,

(234)

\langle\nabla f(u)-\nabla f(v),\,u-v\rangle\leq-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}.

If, additionally,
f
is
C^{2}
on an open set containing
\mathcal{K}
, then (a)–(b) are also equivalent to:

(c)

Uniform Hessian upper bound.
For all
w\in\mathcal{K}
,

(235)

\nabla^{2}f(w)\preceq-\lambda I_{d}\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad-\nabla^{2}f(w)\succeq\lambda I_{d}.

Proof.

We prove (a)
\Rightarrow
(b)
\Rightarrow
(a). Under
C^{2}
, we show (c)
\Rightarrow
(a) and (a)
\Rightarrow
(c).

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b).

Apply (
233
) twice, once with
(u,v)
and once with
(v,u)
:

f(u)\leq f(v)+\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2},

f(v)\leq f(u)+\langle\nabla f(u),v-u\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2}.

Add the two inequalities. The
f(u)+f(v)
terms cancel, giving

0\leq\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle+\langle\nabla f(u),v-u\rangle-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}.

Since
\langle\nabla f(u),v-u\rangle=-\langle\nabla f(u),u-v\rangle
, the sum of inner products equals

-\langle\nabla f(u)-\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle
. Thus

0\leq-\langle\nabla f(u)-\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2},

which rearranges to (
234
).

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a).

Fix
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
and consider the segment
w_{t}:=v+t(u-v)
for
t\in[0,1]
.
Define
\psi(t):=f(w_{t})
. Then
\psi
is differentiable on
[0,1]
with

\psi^{\prime}(t)=\big\langle\nabla f(w_{t}),u-v\big\rangle.

Compute

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(t)-\psi^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\nabla f(w_{t})-\nabla f(v),u-v\big\rangle

(236)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\big\langle\nabla f(w_{t})-\nabla f(v),w_{t}-v\big\rangle\qquad(t>0).

Apply (
234
) to the pair
(w_{t},v)
:

\big\langle\nabla f(w_{t})-\nabla f(v),w_{t}-v\big\rangle\leq-\lambda\|w_{t}-v\|^{2}=-\lambda t^{2}\|u-v\|^{2}.

Substitute into (
236
) to obtain, for
t\in(0,1]
,

\psi^{\prime}(t)-\psi^{\prime}(0)\leq-\lambda t\|u-v\|^{2}.

Integrate from
0
to
1
:

\displaystyle f(u)-f(v)-\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle

\displaystyle=\psi(1)-\psi(0)-\psi^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\big(\psi^{\prime}(t)-\psi^{\prime}(0)\big)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}t\,dt=-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2},

which is (
233
).

(c)
\Rightarrow
(a) under
C^{2}
.

Fix
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
and define
w_{t}=v+t(u-v)
and
\psi(t)=f(w_{t})
.
Then
\psi
is twice continuously differentiable and

\psi^{\prime\prime}(t)=(u-v)^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(w_{t})(u-v).

By (
235
),
\psi^{\prime\prime}(t)\leq-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Integrate twice:

\psi^{\prime}(t)-\psi^{\prime}(0)=\int_{0}^{t}\psi^{\prime\prime}(s)\,ds\leq-\lambda t\|u-v\|^{2},

\psi(1)-\psi(0)-\psi^{\prime}(0)=\int_{0}^{1}(\psi^{\prime}(t)-\psi^{\prime}(0))\,dt\leq-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2}.

Substitute
\psi(1)=f(u)
,
\psi(0)=f(v)
, and
\psi^{\prime}(0)=\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle
to obtain (
233
).

(a)
\Rightarrow
(c) under
C^{2}
.

Fix
w\in\mathcal{K}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
w\pm th\in\mathcal{K}
for all
t
sufficiently small.
Apply (
233
) with
v=w
and
u=w+th
:

f(w+th)\leq f(w)+t\langle\nabla f(w),h\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}t^{2}\|h\|^{2}.

Apply it again with
u=w-th
:

f(w-th)\leq f(w)-t\langle\nabla f(w),h\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}t^{2}\|h\|^{2}.

Add and subtract
2f(w)
:

f(w+th)-2f(w)+f(w-th)\leq-\lambda t^{2}\|h\|^{2}.

Divide by
t^{2}
and let
t\downarrow 0
.
By the second-order Taylor expansion for
C^{2}
functions,

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{f(w+th)-2f(w)+f(w-th)}{t^{2}}=h^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(w)h.

Hence
h^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(w)h\leq-\lambda\|h\|^{2}
for all
h
, which is exactly
\nabla^{2}f(w)\preceq-\lambda I_{d}
.
∎

Lemma D.4

(Uniqueness of maximizer under strong concavity)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty, closed, and convex, and let
f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be concave and differentiable.
Assume
f
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\mathcal{K}
for some
\lambda>0
(equivalently, it satisfies
(
233
) on
\mathcal{K}
).
If
u^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}
maximizes
f
on
\mathcal{K}
, then
u^{\star}
is unique.

Proof.

Assume
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{K}
are both maximizers. We show
u_{1}=u_{2}
.

Apply the strong-concavity inequality (
233
) with
(u,v)=(u_{2},u_{1})
:

(237)

f(u_{2})\leq f(u_{1})+\langle\nabla f(u_{1}),u_{2}-u_{1}\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u_{2}-u_{1}\|^{2}.

Because
u_{1}
maximizes
f
over
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}
is closed and convex, Lemma
D.2

implies the variational inequality

\langle\nabla f(u_{1}),u-u_{1}\rangle\leq 0\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K}.

In particular, taking
u=u_{2}
gives

(238)

\langle\nabla f(u_{1}),u_{2}-u_{1}\rangle\leq 0.

Insert (
238
) into (
237
) to obtain

f(u_{2})\leq f(u_{1})-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u_{2}-u_{1}\|^{2}.

Since
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are both maximizers,
f(u_{2})=f(u_{1})
, hence
\|u_{2}-u_{1}\|^{2}\leq 0
, implying
u_{2}=u_{1}
.
∎

Remark D.5

(Existence of a maximizer: what strong concavity does and does not give)
.

Strong concavity is a
uniqueness
condition: it guarantees that if a maximizer exists, then it is unique.
It does
not
by itself guarantee existence, since a concave function can fail to attain its supremum on a noncompact set.

Sufficient conditions for existence include:

(i)

\mathcal{K}
is compact and
f
is continuous (Weierstrass theorem);

(ii)

\mathcal{K}
is closed and
f
is coercive on
\mathcal{K}
, e.g.
f(u)\to-\infty
as
\|u\|\to\infty
with
u\in\mathcal{K}
;

(iii)

more generally, upper semicontinuity of
f
and compactness of upper level sets
\{u\in\mathcal{K}:\ f(u)\geq c\}
.

In EM analyses, one typically works on a compact “stability set” or proves basin invariance so that the iterates remain in a region
where maximizers exist and are unique.

Verification templates for Assumption
D.1
.

The point of (
229
) is that it can be checked by a uniform Hessian bound.
We record two common templates.

Lemma D.6

(Averaging preserves strong concavity)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\Pi)
be a probability space and let
\Theta^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty and convex.
Let
\ell:\Theta^{\prime}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be such that:

(i)

(
Measurability and integrability.
) For each
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta^{\prime}
, the map
\omega\mapsto\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)
is measurable and

\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}|\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)|<\infty
.

(ii)

(
Pointwise strong concavity.
) There exists
\lambda>0
such that for
\Pi
-a.e.
\omega
, the function

\theta^{\prime}\mapsto\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)
is differentiable on
\Theta^{\prime}
and satisfies the strong-concavity inequality
for all
u,v\in\Theta^{\prime}
:

(239)

\ell(u,\omega)\leq\ell(v,\omega)+\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(v,\omega),u-v\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2}.

(iii)

(
Justification of interchanging expectation and derivatives, if desired.
)
For each compact
K\subseteq\Theta^{\prime}
there exist integrable envelopes
H^{(1)}_{K},H^{(2)}_{K}\in L_{1}(\Pi)
such that for
\Pi
-a.e.
\omega
,

(240)

\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in K}\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)\|\leq H^{(1)}_{K}(\omega),\qquad\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in K}\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)\|\leq H^{(2)}_{K}(\omega),

and
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)
is
C^{2}
on
\Theta^{\prime}
for
\Pi
-a.e.
\omega
.

Define the averaged objective

f(\theta^{\prime}):=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)],\qquad\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta^{\prime}.

Then:

(a)

f
is well-defined (finite) on
\Theta^{\prime}
, concave, and
\lambda
-strongly concave
on
\Theta^{\prime}
in the sense that for all
u,v\in\Theta^{\prime}
,

(241)

f(u)\leq f(v)+\langle g(v),u-v\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2},

where
g(v)
is any measurable selection of
\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(v,\omega)]
when it exists (in particular,
g(v)=\nabla f(v)

under the differentiability conclusion below).

(b)

Under assumption
(iii)
,
f
is
C^{2}
on
\Theta^{\prime}
and differentiation may be exchanged with expectation:

(242)

\nabla f(\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)],\qquad\nabla^{2}f(\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)].

Moreover, if in addition one has the pointwise Hessian bound

(243)

-\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)\succeq\lambda I_{d}\qquad\text{for $\Pi$-a.e.\ $\omega$ and all $\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta^{\prime}$,}

then

(244)

-\nabla^{2}f(\theta^{\prime})\succeq\lambda I_{d}\qquad\forall\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta^{\prime}.

Proof.

Step 0 (Well-definedness and basic measurability).

Assumption (i) ensures
f(\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)]
is well-defined and finite for each
\theta^{\prime}
.

Step 1 (Strong concavity of the average without differentiating under the integral).

Fix
u,v\in\Theta^{\prime}
.
By assumption (ii), inequality (
239
) holds for
\Pi
-a.e.
\omega
.
Taking expectations of both sides is legitimate as soon as each term is integrable.
The terms
\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\ell(u,\omega)]
and
\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\ell(v,\omega)]
are finite by (i).
For the linear term, note that by Cauchy–Schwarz,

|\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(v,\omega),u-v\rangle|\leq\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(v,\omega)\|\,\|u-v\|.

Thus integrability of
\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(v,\omega)\|
suffices to justify taking expectations of the linear term.
(For a streamlined presentation one may assume
\mathbb{E}\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(v,\omega)\|<\infty
for all
v
; this is implied locally by (iii)
with a compact
K
containing
v
.)

Taking expectations in (
239
) yields

f(u)\leq f(v)+\Big\langle\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(v,\omega)],u-v\Big\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2},

which is (
241
) with
g(v)=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(v,\omega)]
.
In particular,
f
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\Theta^{\prime}
(in the quadratic support sense of Lemma
D.3
).

Step 2 (Optional: justify differentiation under the expectation).

Assume (iii). Fix
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta^{\prime}
and choose a compact
K\subseteq\Theta^{\prime}
with
\theta^{\prime}
in its (relative) interior.
Let
e_{i}
be the
i
th standard basis vector.

(a) First derivative.

For
h\neq 0
small enough so that
\theta^{\prime}+he_{i}\in K
, the difference quotient satisfies

\frac{\ell(\theta^{\prime}+he_{i},\omega)-\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)}{h}=\int_{0}^{1}\big\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\theta^{\prime}+she_{i},\omega),e_{i}\big\rangle\,ds

by the fundamental theorem of calculus along the line segment.
Hence

\left|\frac{\ell(\theta^{\prime}+he_{i},\omega)-\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)}{h}\right|\leq\sup_{\vartheta\in K}\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\vartheta,\omega)\|\leq H^{(1)}_{K}(\omega),

and
H^{(1)}_{K}
is integrable by (iii).
Therefore dominated convergence permits interchange of
\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}
and
\lim_{h\to 0}
, giving

\partial_{i}f(\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\partial_{i}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)]
.
Since this holds for all
i
, we obtain
\nabla f(\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)]
.

(b) Second derivative.

Apply the same argument to
\partial_{i}\ell(\cdot,\omega)
and use the envelope
H^{(2)}_{K}
:
for
h\neq 0
small and
\theta^{\prime}+he_{j}\in K
,

\frac{\partial_{i}\ell(\theta^{\prime}+he_{j},\omega)-\partial_{i}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)}{h}=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{ij}^{2}\ell(\theta^{\prime}+she_{j},\omega)\,ds,

hence the absolute value is bounded by
\sup_{\vartheta\in K}\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\vartheta,\omega)\|\leq H^{(2)}_{K}(\omega)
.
Dominated convergence yields
\partial_{ij}^{2}f(\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\partial_{ij}^{2}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)]
.
Thus
\nabla^{2}f(\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)]
.
Continuity of
\nabla f
and
\nabla^{2}f
follows from the same local dominated convergence reasoning, so
f\in C^{2}(\Theta^{\prime})
.
