Step 3 (Transfer a pointwise Hessian inequality through expectation).

Assume additionally (
243
).
Fix
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta^{\prime}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Using (
242
),

\displaystyle h^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}f(\theta^{\prime})\big)h

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}\!\left[h^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}\ell(\theta^{\prime},\omega)\big)h\right]\ \geq\ \mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\lambda\|h\|^{2}]=\lambda\|h\|^{2}.

Since the inequality holds for all
h
, it is equivalent to (
244
).
∎

Restriction to a slice.

In quotient-identified models, global strong concavity in
\theta^{\prime}
may fail, but it may hold after restricting to a transversal slice.
It is useful to separate two standard cases:

affine slices
(no curvature term) and
curved submanifolds
(a curvature term appears and must be controlled).

Lemma D.7

(Affine restriction: no curvature term)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be
C^{2}
on an open neighborhood of an affine subspace
S=u_{0}+L
, where
L\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a linear subspace.
Let
m:=\dim(L)
and choose a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
whose columns form an orthonormal basis of
L
,
so that every
u\in S
is uniquely of the form
u=u_{0}+Bx
with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Assume there exists
\lambda>0
such that for all
u\in S
and all
h\in L
,

(245)

h^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}f(u)\big)h\ \geq\ \lambda\|h\|^{2}.

Then the restriction
f|_{S}
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
S
(equivalently, the coordinate function

g(x):=f(u_{0}+Bx)
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
).

Proof.

Step 1 (Reduce to a function on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
).

Define
g:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
by
g(x):=f(u_{0}+Bx)
.
Since
f
is
C^{2}
near
S
and
x\mapsto u_{0}+Bx
is smooth,
g
is
C^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Step 2 (Compute the Hessian of
g
and inherit the curvature bound).

By the chain rule,

\nabla g(x)=B^{\top}\nabla f(u_{0}+Bx),\qquad\nabla^{2}g(x)=B^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(u_{0}+Bx)\,B.

Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Let
h:=Ba\in L
. Then
\|h\|=\|a\|
because
B
has orthonormal columns, and

a^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}g(x)\big)a=a^{\top}B^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}f(u_{0}+Bx)\big)Ba=h^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}f(u_{0}+Bx)\big)h.

Applying (
245
) yields

a^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}g(x)\big)a\geq\lambda\|h\|^{2}=\lambda\|a\|^{2}.

Hence
-\nabla^{2}g(x)\succeq\lambda I_{m}
for all
x
, and by Lemma
D.3
,

g
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Equivalently,
f|_{S}
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
S
(in the induced coordinates). ∎

Lemma D.8

(Curved slice: sufficient conditions in local coordinates)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be
C^{2}
on an open set containing a
C^{2}
embedded submanifold
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
of dimension
m
.
Fix
u_{0}\in S
and let
\varphi:U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\to S
be a
C^{2}
chart with
\varphi(t_{0})=u_{0}
.
Write

J(t):=D\varphi(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m},\qquad D^{2}\varphi(t):\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

for its first and second derivatives (a bilinear map).
Define the pullback
\tilde{f}:U\to\mathbb{R}
by
\tilde{f}(t):=f(\varphi(t))
.

Assume there exist constants
\lambda_{0}>0
,
G\geq 0
,
B\geq 0
,
\sigma_{0}>0
, and an open neighborhood
U_{0}\subseteq U
of
t_{0}
such that
for all
t\in U_{0}
:

(i)

(
Projected negative curvature.
) For all
a\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

(246)

a^{\top}J(t)^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}f(\varphi(t))\big)J(t)\,a\ \geq\ \lambda_{0}\,\|J(t)a\|^{2}.

(ii)

(
Gradient bound.
)
\|\nabla f(\varphi(t))\|\leq G
.

(iii)

(
Chart curvature bound.
) The bilinear operator norm satisfies

(247)

\|D^{2}\varphi(t)\|_{\mathrm{bil}}:=\sup_{\|a\|=\|b\|=1}\|D^{2}\varphi(t)[a,b]\|\ \leq\ B.

(iv)

(
Uniform conditioning of the chart.
) The smallest singular value of
J(t)
satisfies

(248)

\sigma_{\min}(J(t))\geq\sigma_{0}.

If
GB\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}
, then
\tilde{f}
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
U_{0}
with

\lambda:=\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}.

Consequently,
f
is strongly concave along
S
on the chart patch
\varphi(U_{0})
(in the sense that
\tilde{f}=f\circ\varphi

is strongly concave in Euclidean coordinates on
U_{0}
).

Proof.

Step 1 (Compute the pullback Hessian, including the curvature term).

Since
f
and
\varphi
are
C^{2}
,
\tilde{f}=f\circ\varphi
is
C^{2}
and

\nabla\tilde{f}(t)=J(t)^{\top}\nabla f(\varphi(t)).

We now compute the quadratic form of
\nabla^{2}\tilde{f}(t)
.
Fix
a\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and define the curve
c(s):=\varphi(t+sa)
for
s
near
0
.
Then
\tilde{f}(t+sa)=f(c(s))
.
Differentiate twice at
s=0
:

\displaystyle a^{\top}\nabla^{2}\tilde{f}(t)\,a

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\Big|_{s=0}f(c(s))

(249)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{c^{\prime}(0)^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(c(0))\,c^{\prime}(0)}_{\text{ambient Hessian term}}\;+\;\underbrace{\big\langle\nabla f(c(0)),\,c^{\prime\prime}(0)\big\rangle}_{\text{curvature term}}.

Now
c(0)=\varphi(t)
,
c^{\prime}(0)=J(t)a
, and
c^{\prime\prime}(0)=D^{2}\varphi(t)[a,a]
.
Substituting into (
249
) gives the standard formula

(250)

a^{\top}\nabla^{2}\tilde{f}(t)\,a=a^{\top}J(t)^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(\varphi(t))J(t)\,a+\big\langle\nabla f(\varphi(t)),\,D^{2}\varphi(t)[a,a]\big\rangle.

Step 2 (Lower bound the negative pullback Hessian).

Multiply (
250
) by
-1
:

(251)

\displaystyle a^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}\tilde{f}(t)\big)a

\displaystyle=a^{\top}J(t)^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}f(\varphi(t))\big)J(t)\,a-\big\langle\nabla f(\varphi(t)),\,D^{2}\varphi(t)[a,a]\big\rangle.

We lower bound the first term and upper bound the absolute value of the second.

(a) Projected curvature term.

Assumption (
246
) gives

(252)

a^{\top}J(t)^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}f(\varphi(t))\big)J(t)\,a\geq\lambda_{0}\,\|J(t)a\|^{2}.

(b) Curvature term bound.

By Cauchy–Schwarz and the bilinear norm definition (
247
),

\left|\big\langle\nabla f(\varphi(t)),\,D^{2}\varphi(t)[a,a]\big\rangle\right|\leq\|\nabla f(\varphi(t))\|\,\|D^{2}\varphi(t)[a,a]\|\leq G\cdot\|D^{2}\varphi(t)\|_{\mathrm{bil}}\,\|a\|^{2}\leq GB\,\|a\|^{2}.

Therefore,

(253)

-\big\langle\nabla f(\varphi(t)),\,D^{2}\varphi(t)[a,a]\big\rangle\geq-GB\,\|a\|^{2}.

Combining (
251
), (
252
), and (
253
) yields

(254)

a^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}\tilde{f}(t)\big)a\geq\lambda_{0}\,\|J(t)a\|^{2}-GB\,\|a\|^{2}.

Step 3 (Use chart conditioning to compare
\|J(t)a\|
and
\|a\|
).

By (
248
),
\|J(t)a\|\geq\sigma_{0}\|a\|
for all
a
.
Substitute into (
254
):

a^{\top}\big(-\nabla^{2}\tilde{f}(t)\big)a\geq(\lambda_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}-GB)\,\|a\|^{2}.

Under the condition
GB\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}
, the right-hand side is at least

\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}\|a\|^{2}
.
Thus for all
t\in U_{0}
,

-\nabla^{2}\tilde{f}(t)\succeq\lambda I_{m},\qquad\lambda:=\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}.

By Lemma
D.3
,
\tilde{f}
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
U_{0}
.
This is exactly strong concavity of
f
along
S
on
\varphi(U_{0})
in the chosen chart. ∎

Remark D.9

(Geometric meaning of the curvature term)
.

Formula (
250
) decomposes the second derivative of
f|_{S}
in coordinates into:
(i) the ambient Hessian of
f
projected onto tangent directions
J(t)a
,
and (ii) a curvature correction involving the ambient gradient
\nabla f
and the second derivative of the embedding
\varphi

(closely related to the second fundamental form of
S
).
Affine slices are precisely the case
D^{2}\varphi\equiv 0
, hence no curvature term (Lemma
D.7
).
Near a maximizer of
f|_{S}
, the tangential component of
\nabla f
is small; on sufficiently small neighborhoods,
the product
\|\nabla f\|\,\|D^{2}\varphi\|_{\mathrm{bil}}
can be made subordinate to the projected negative curvature, yielding local strong concavity.

D.2.
From curvature to a well-defined and smooth EM map

Assume (locally) that the M-step produces a unique maximizer

T(\theta)\in\arg\max_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta),

where
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\Theta
is a closed convex stability set (often compact).
Strong concavity in
\theta^{\prime}
guarantees uniqueness once existence is secured (Remark
D.5
).

Assumption D.10

(
C^{2}
smoothness and invertible Hessian at the maximizer)
.

There exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
of
\theta_{0}
such that:

(1)

Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)
is
C^{2}
in
(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
on a neighborhood of
(T(\theta_{0}),\theta_{0})
;

(2)

\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta_{0})\mid\theta_{0})
is invertible (equivalently, negative definite).

Lemma D.11

(Differentiability and Jacobian formula for an unconstrained EM map)
.

Let
\Theta,\Theta^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open sets and let

Q:\Theta^{\prime}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R},\qquad(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)

be
C^{2}
on a neighborhood of
(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0})\in\Theta^{\prime}\times\Theta
.
Define

F(\theta^{\prime},\theta):=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Assume:

(i)

(
First-order condition at the basepoint
)
F(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0})=0
.

(ii)

(
Nonsingularity in
\theta^{\prime}
) the matrix

D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0})=\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta_{0}^{\prime}\mid\theta_{0})

is invertible.

Then there exist neighborhoods
V\subseteq\Theta
of
\theta_{0}
and
U\subseteq\Theta^{\prime}
of
\theta_{0}^{\prime}
, and a unique
C^{1}
map

T:V\to U
such that

(255)

F(T(\theta),\theta)=0\qquad\forall\theta\in V,

and
T(\theta_{0})=\theta_{0}^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
T
is differentiable at
\theta_{0}
with Jacobian

(256)

\nabla T(\theta_{0})=-\Big[\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta_{0})\mid\theta_{0})\Big]^{-1}\,\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q(T(\theta_{0})\mid\theta_{0}).

Finally, if
A:=\nabla T(\theta_{0})
satisfies
\rho(A)<1
, then for every
\varepsilon\in\big(0,\,1-\rho(A)\big)
there exist
a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a neighborhood
V_{\varepsilon}\subseteq V
of
\theta_{0}
, and a constant

c_{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
such that

(257)

\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|_{\varepsilon}\leq c_{\varepsilon}\,\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\varepsilon}\qquad\forall\theta,\vartheta\in V_{\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Step 1 (Set up the implicit equation).

Define
F(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)
.
Since
Q
is
C^{2}
,
F
is
C^{1}
on a neighborhood of
(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0})
.
Assumption (i) states
F(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0})=0
.

Step 2 (Verify the IFT hypotheses and invoke the implicit function theorem).

Compute the partial derivative of
F
with respect to
\theta^{\prime}
:

D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta).

Assumption (ii) states that
D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0})
is invertible.
By the (classical) implicit function theorem in finite dimensions, there exist neighborhoods

U
of
\theta_{0}^{\prime}
and
V
of
\theta_{0}
and a unique
C^{1}
map
T:V\to U
such that
T(\theta_{0})=\theta_{0}^{\prime}
and

F(T(\theta),\theta)=0
for all
\theta\in V
.

Step 3 (Differentiate the identity
F(T(\theta),\theta)=0
and solve).

Differentiate (
255
) at
\theta=\theta_{0}
.
Using the chain rule for
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

0=D\big[F(T(\theta),\theta)\big]_{\theta=\theta_{0}}=D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0})\,\nabla T(\theta_{0})+D_{\theta}F(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0}).

Since
D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0})
is invertible, we can solve:

\nabla T(\theta_{0})=-\big(D_{\theta^{\prime}}F(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0})\big)^{-1}D_{\theta}F(\theta_{0}^{\prime},\theta_{0}).

Finally observe that
D_{\theta}F(\theta^{\prime},\theta)=\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)
, and
\theta_{0}^{\prime}=T(\theta_{0})
, yielding
(
256
).

Step 4 (A linear-algebra lemma: spectral radius yields an adapted norm).

We use the following standard fact.

Claim D.12
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and let
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with induced operator norm

\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}
such that

(258)

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq\rho(A)+\varepsilon.

Proof of Claim
D.12
.

We give a constructive proof via Jordan form (any equivalent proof is acceptable).

Let
A=PJP^{-1}
be a (real) Jordan decomposition over
\mathbb{C}
; operator norms below may be defined over
\mathbb{C}^{d}

and then restricted to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which does not affect (
258
).
Write
J
as a direct sum of Jordan blocks
J_{k}=\lambda_{k}I+N_{k}
, where
N_{k}
is nilpotent with ones on the superdiagonal.
Let
\rho:=\max_{k}|\lambda_{k}|=\rho(A)
.

Fix
\eta>0
(to be chosen in terms of
\varepsilon
).
For each block size
m
, define the diagonal scaling matrix

D_{\eta}:=\diag(1,\eta,\eta^{2},\dots,\eta^{m-1}).

A direct computation shows

D_{\eta}^{-1}N_{k}D_{\eta}=\eta\,N_{k},

hence

D_{\eta}^{-1}J_{k}D_{\eta}=\lambda_{k}I+\eta N_{k}.

Equip
\mathbb{C}^{m}
with the
\ell_{\infty}
norm, whose induced operator norm satisfies

\|\lambda_{k}I\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}=|\lambda_{k}|
and
\|N_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}=1
. Therefore

\big\|D_{\eta}^{-1}J_{k}D_{\eta}\big\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}\leq|\lambda_{k}|+\eta\leq\rho+\eta.

As
J
is block diagonal, with the max norm on the product space we obtain

\|D^{-1}JD\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}\leq\rho+\eta,

where
D
is the block-diagonal matrix with the appropriate
D_{\eta}
on each Jordan block.

Now define a norm on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
by

\|x\|_{\varepsilon}:=\|D^{-1}P^{-1}x\|_{\infty},

and let
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}
be its induced operator norm.
Then

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}=\|D^{-1}P^{-1}APD\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}=\|D^{-1}JD\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}\leq\rho+\eta.

Choose
\eta=\varepsilon
to obtain (
258
).
∎

Step 5 (Upgrade the linear bound to a nonlinear local contraction).

Assume
\rho(A)<1
, where
A=\nabla T(\theta_{0})
.
Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1-\rho(A))
and let
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
be the norm from Claim
D.12
with

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq\rho(A)+\varepsilon/2<1.

Since
T
is
C^{1}
on
V
, the map
\theta\mapsto\nabla T(\theta)
is continuous at
\theta_{0}
.
Hence there exists a neighborhood
V_{\varepsilon}\subseteq V
of
\theta_{0}
such that

(259)

\|\nabla T(\theta)-A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq\varepsilon/2\qquad\forall\theta\in V_{\varepsilon}.

By the triangle inequality and (
259
),

\|\nabla T(\theta)\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}+\|\nabla T(\theta)-A\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\leq(\rho(A)+\varepsilon/2)+\varepsilon/2=\rho(A)+\varepsilon=:c_{\varepsilon},

and
c_{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
by the choice of
\varepsilon
.

Now fix
\theta,\vartheta\in V_{\varepsilon}
.
Because
V_{\varepsilon}
is open, after shrinking it if necessary we may assume it is convex (or simply replace it by a small ball),
so the segment
\vartheta+t(\theta-\vartheta)\in V_{\varepsilon}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
By the fundamental theorem of calculus in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(integral form of the mean-value theorem),

T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla T(\vartheta+t(\theta-\vartheta))(\theta-\vartheta)\,dt.

Taking
\|\cdot\|_{\varepsilon}
and using the uniform bound on
\|\nabla T(\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}
yields

\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|_{\varepsilon}\leq\int_{0}^{1}\|\nabla T(\vartheta+t(\theta-\vartheta))\|_{\mathrm{op},\varepsilon}\,dt\ \|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\varepsilon}\leq c_{\varepsilon}\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\varepsilon}.

This is (
257
). ∎

Lemma D.13

(Lipschitzness of the argmax map from curvature and mixed smoothness)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty, closed, and convex, and let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any index set.
For each
\theta\in\mathcal{S}
, let
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)
be differentiable on
\mathcal{K}
and assume:

(i)

(
Uniform strong concavity in
\theta^{\prime}
.
) There exists
\lambda>0
such that for every
\theta\in\mathcal{S}
,
the function
\theta^{\prime}\mapsto Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\mathcal{K}
(equivalently, it satisfies
(
234
) on
\mathcal{K}
with the same
\lambda
).

(ii)

(
Mixed gradient Lipschitzness.
) There exists
L<\infty
such that for all
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}
,

(260)

\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}\big\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\vartheta)\big\|\leq L\,\|\theta-\vartheta\|.

(iii)

(
Existence of maximizers.
) For each
\theta\in\mathcal{S}
, the maximizer

T(\theta)\in\arg\max_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)

exists.

Then
T(\theta)
is unique for each
\theta\in\mathcal{S}
, and for all
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}
,

(261)

\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|\leq\frac{L}{\lambda}\,\|\theta-\vartheta\|.

Proof.

Step 1 (Uniqueness).

Fix
\theta\in\mathcal{S}
. By (i),
Q(\cdot\mid\theta)
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on the convex set
\mathcal{K}
.
By Lemma
D.4
, any maximizer is unique. Thus
T
is well-defined as a single-valued map on
\mathcal{S}
.

Step 2 (Write the optimality conditions in variational form).

Fix
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}
.
Since
\mathcal{K}
is closed and convex and
Q(\cdot\mid\theta)
is concave and differentiable,
Lemma
D.2
implies the first-order optimality conditions:

(262)

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\theta),\,u-T(\theta)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 0\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K},

(263)

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\vartheta)\mid\vartheta),\,u-T(\vartheta)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 0\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K}.

Choose
u=T(\vartheta)
in (
262
) and
u=T(\theta)
in (
263
) to obtain

(264)

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\theta),\,T(\vartheta)-T(\theta)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 0,

(265)

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\vartheta)\mid\vartheta),\,T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 0.

Adding (
264
) and (
265
) yields

(266)

\big\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\theta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\vartheta)\mid\vartheta),\,T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\big\rangle\geq 0.

(Equivalently, the left-hand side is the negative of what appears in some conventions; the sign here is correct as written.)

Step 3 (Insert–subtract and isolate the strong concavity term).

Decompose the gradient difference as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\theta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\vartheta)\mid\vartheta)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\big(\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\theta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\vartheta)\big)}_{(\mathrm{I})}+\underbrace{\big(\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\vartheta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\vartheta)\mid\vartheta)\big)}_{(\mathrm{II})}.

Plug into (
266
):

\langle(\mathrm{I}),\,T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\rangle+\langle(\mathrm{II}),\,T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\rangle\geq 0,

hence

(267)

\langle(\mathrm{II}),\,T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\rangle\geq-\langle(\mathrm{I}),\,T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\rangle.

Step 4 (Lower bound the left side via strong concavity).

Now view
\vartheta
as fixed and consider the strongly concave function
u\mapsto Q(u\mid\vartheta)
on
\mathcal{K}
.
By Lemma
D.3
in gradient form, strong concavity implies for all
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
,

\big\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(u\mid\vartheta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(v\mid\vartheta),\,u-v\big\rangle\leq-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}.

Apply this with
u=T(\theta)
and
v=T(\vartheta)
to obtain

\big\langle\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\vartheta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\vartheta)\mid\vartheta),\,T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\big\rangle\leq-\lambda\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|^{2}.

That is,

(268)

\langle(\mathrm{II}),\,T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\rangle\leq-\lambda\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|^{2}.

Step 5 (Upper bound the right side via mixed smoothness and conclude).

By Cauchy–Schwarz,

-\langle(\mathrm{I}),\,T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\rangle\leq\|(\mathrm{I})\|\,\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|.

Combining with (
267
) and (
268
) gives

\lambda\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|^{2}\leq\|(\mathrm{I})\|\,\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|.

If
T(\theta)=T(\vartheta)
we are done. Otherwise divide both sides by
\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|
:

\lambda\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|\leq\|(\mathrm{I})\|=\big\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\theta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(T(\theta)\mid\vartheta)\big\|.

Finally apply (
260
) (note
T(\theta)\in\mathcal{K}
) to obtain

\lambda\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|\leq L\|\theta-\vartheta\|,

which is (
261
). ∎

Corollary D.14

(A simple Euclidean contraction criterion)
.

In the setting of Lemma
D.13
, if
L/\lambda<1
, then
T
is a contraction on
\mathcal{S}
in Euclidean norm:

\|T(\theta)-T(\vartheta)\|\leq\frac{L}{\lambda}\|\theta-\vartheta\|\qquad\forall\theta,\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}.

Remark D.15

(Relating
L
to mixed Hessians via a rigorous mean-value argument)
.

Assume
Q
is
C^{2}
in
(\theta^{\prime},\theta)
on
\mathcal{K}\times\mathcal{S}
(with
\mathcal{S}
convex for simplicity), and that

\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K},\ \theta\in\mathcal{S}}\big\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)\big\|\leq M<\infty.

Then (
260
) holds with
L=M
.

Indeed, fix
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}
and
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
.
Define the path
\theta_{s}:=\vartheta+s(\theta-\vartheta)
for
s\in[0,1]
and the function

g(s):=\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta_{s})
.
Then
g
is differentiable and, by the chain rule,

g^{\prime}(s)=\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta_{s})\,(\theta-\vartheta).

Integrating from
0
to
1
gives

\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\vartheta)=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}_{\theta^{\prime}\theta}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta_{s})\,(\theta-\vartheta)\,ds,

so by the operator norm bound,

\big\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\vartheta)\big\|\leq\int_{0}^{1}M\,\|\theta-\vartheta\|\,ds=M\|\theta-\vartheta\|.

Taking
\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}
yields (
260
) with
L=M
.

Remark D.16

(When the IFT Jacobian formula and the Lipschitz bound are used)
.

The Jacobian identity (
256
) is
local
and exact, and it is the right tool for sharp local rates
(spectral radius governs the rate in an adapted norm).
The Euclidean Lipschitz bound (
261
) is
global on the set
\mathcal{S}
and is often simpler to verify from curvature
(
\lambda
) and mixed smoothness (
L
), producing the classical contraction template
L/\lambda<1
.

D.3.
Stability of maximizers under perturbations

We now expand the argmax perturbation calculus used to compare population and sample-based M-steps.

Lemma D.17

(First-order
necessary
condition for a maximizer on a convex set)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty and convex, and let
f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable.
If
u^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}
satisfies

f(u^{\star})\ \geq\ f(u)\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K}

(i.e.
u^{\star}
is a global maximizer of
f
over
\mathcal{K}
), then

(269)

\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),\,u-u^{\star}\rangle\leq 0\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K}.

Proof.

Fix
u\in\mathcal{K}
and consider the segment
u_{t}:=u^{\star}+t(u-u^{\star})
for
t\in[0,1]
.
Convexity of
\mathcal{K}
implies
u_{t}\in\mathcal{K}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Define the one-dimensional function
\phi(t):=f(u_{t})
.
Since
u^{\star}
is a global maximizer on
\mathcal{K}
, we have
\phi(t)\leq\phi(0)
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Hence
t=0
is a maximizer of
\phi
on
[0,1]
, so the right derivative satisfies
\phi^{\prime}(0^{+})\leq 0
.

Because
f
is differentiable at
u^{\star}
,
\phi
is differentiable at
0
and by the chain rule,

\phi^{\prime}(0)=\big\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),\,u-u^{\star}\big\rangle.

Thus
\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),u-u^{\star}\rangle=\phi^{\prime}(0)\leq 0
. Since
u
was arbitrary, (
269
) holds.
∎

Lemma D.18

(First-order condition is also
sufficient
under concavity)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty, closed, and convex, and let
f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable and concave.
Then
u^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}
maximizes
f
over
\mathcal{K}
if and only if

(270)

\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),\,u-u^{\star}\rangle\leq 0\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K}.

Proof.

Necessity is Lemma
D.17
(no concavity needed).

For sufficiency, assume (
230
). Concavity of
f
implies the global upper-support inequality:
for all
u\in\mathcal{K}
,

f(u)\ \leq\ f(u^{\star})+\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),\,u-u^{\star}\rangle.

Combine with (
230
) to get
f(u)\leq f(u^{\star})
for all
u\in\mathcal{K}
, so
u^{\star}
is a maximizer.
∎

Lemma D.19

(Equivalent characterizations of strong concavity)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be convex and let
f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable.
Fix
\lambda>0
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

(
Gradient form
) For all
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
,

(271)

\langle\nabla f(u)-\nabla f(v),\,u-v\rangle\leq-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}.

(2)

(
Quadratic upper support
) For all
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
,

(272)

f(u)\leq f(v)+\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2}.

If additionally
f
is
C^{2}
on an open set containing
\mathcal{K}
, these are also equivalent to:

(3)

(
Hessian form
) For all
w\in\mathcal{K}
,

(273)

-\nabla^{2}f(w)\succeq\lambda I_{d}.

Proof.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(2).

Assume
f\in C^{2}
and (
235
). Fix
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
and define
w_{t}:=v+t(u-v)
and
\psi(t):=f(w_{t})
.
Then
\psi^{\prime}(t)=\langle\nabla f(w_{t}),u-v\rangle
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}(t)=(u-v)^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(w_{t})(u-v)
.
By (
235
),
\psi^{\prime\prime}(t)\leq-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Integrate from
0
to
t
:

\psi^{\prime}(t)-\psi^{\prime}(0)\leq-\lambda t\|u-v\|^{2}.

Integrate again from
0
to
1
:

\psi(1)-\psi(0)-\psi^{\prime}(0)\leq-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}t\,dt=-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2}.

Substitute
\psi(1)=f(u)
,
\psi(0)=f(v)
, and
\psi^{\prime}(0)=\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle
to obtain (
233
).

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1).

Apply (
233
) to
(u,v)
and
(v,u)
:

f(u)\leq f(v)+\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2},

f(v)\leq f(u)+\langle\nabla f(u),v-u\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2}.

Add and cancel
f(u)+f(v)
:

0\leq\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle+\langle\nabla f(u),v-u\rangle-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}=-\langle\nabla f(u)-\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}.

Rearrange to obtain (
234
).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2).

Fix
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
, define
w_{t}:=v+t(u-v)
and
\psi(t):=f(w_{t})
as above.
Then

\psi^{\prime}(t)-\psi^{\prime}(0)=\langle\nabla f(w_{t})-\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle.

Apply (
234
) to the pair
(w_{t},v)
.
Since
w_{t}-v=t(u-v)
, we have

\langle\nabla f(w_{t})-\nabla f(v),w_{t}-v\rangle\leq-\lambda\|w_{t}-v\|^{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\langle\nabla f(w_{t})-\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle\leq-\lambda t\|u-v\|^{2}.

Thus
\psi^{\prime}(t)-\psi^{\prime}(0)\leq-\lambda t\|u-v\|^{2}
.
Integrate from
0
to
1
:

f(u)-f(v)-\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}(\psi^{\prime}(t)-\psi^{\prime}(0))\,dt\leq-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}t\,dt=-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2},

which is (
233
).

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3) under
C^{2}
.

Assume
f\in C^{2}
on a neighborhood of
\mathcal{K}
and (
233
).
Fix
w\in\mathcal{K}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
w\pm th\in\mathcal{K}
for small
t
(possible by convexity and small
t
).
Apply (
233
) with
(u,v)=(w+th,w)
:

f(w+th)\leq f(w)+t\langle\nabla f(w),h\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}t^{2}\|h\|^{2}.

Apply it with
(u,v)=(w-th,w)
:

f(w-th)\leq f(w)-t\langle\nabla f(w),h\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}t^{2}\|h\|^{2}.

Add and subtract
2f(w)
:

f(w+th)-2f(w)+f(w-th)\leq-\lambda t^{2}\|h\|^{2}.

Divide by
t^{2}
and let
t\downarrow 0
.
By the second-order Taylor expansion (or the definition of the Hessian),

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{f(w+th)-2f(w)+f(w-th)}{t^{2}}=h^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(w)h.

Hence
h^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(w)h\leq-\lambda\|h\|^{2}
for all
h
, i.e. (
235
).
∎

Lemma D.20

(Uniqueness of maximizer under strong concavity)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be convex and let
f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be
\lambda
-strongly concave for some
\lambda>0

(in any of the equivalent senses of Lemma
D.3
).
If a maximizer
u^{\star}\in\arg\max_{\mathcal{K}}f
exists, then it is unique.

Proof.

Let
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{K}
be maximizers, so
f(u_{1})=f(u_{2})=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}f
.
Apply the quadratic support inequality (
233
) (Lemma
D.3
) with
(u,v)=(u_{2},u_{1})
:

f(u_{2})\leq f(u_{1})+\langle\nabla f(u_{1}),u_{2}-u_{1}\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u_{2}-u_{1}\|^{2}.

Since
u_{1}
is a maximizer on a convex set, Lemma
D.17
gives

\langle\nabla f(u_{1}),u_{2}-u_{1}\rangle\leq 0
. Therefore

f(u_{2})\leq f(u_{1})-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u_{2}-u_{1}\|^{2}.

But
f(u_{2})=f(u_{1})
, so
\|u_{2}-u_{1}\|^{2}\leq 0
, hence
u_{2}=u_{1}
.
∎

Lemma D.21

(Quadratic drop from the maximizer)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be convex, and let
f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable and
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\mathcal{K}
.
Let
u^{\star}=\arg\max_{\mathcal{K}}f
.
Then for every
u\in\mathcal{K}
,

(274)

f(u)\leq f(u^{\star})-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-u^{\star}\|^{2}.

Equivalently,

f(u^{\star})-f(u)\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-u^{\star}\|^{2}.

Proof.

By Lemma
D.3
, strong concavity implies the quadratic support bound:
for all
u\in\mathcal{K}
,

f(u)\leq f(u^{\star})+\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),u-u^{\star}\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-u^{\star}\|^{2}.

Since
u^{\star}
is a maximizer and
\mathcal{K}
is convex, Lemma
D.17
yields

\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),u-u^{\star}\rangle\leq 0
.
Drop this term to obtain (
274
).
∎

Lemma D.22

(Argmax stability under uniform gradient error)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty, closed, and convex.
Let
f,\widehat{f}:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable.
Assume:

(i)

(
Strong concavity of
f
in gradient form
) There exists
\lambda>0
such that for all
u,v\in\mathcal{K}
,

(275)

\big\langle\nabla f(u)-\nabla f(v),\,u-v\big\rangle\leq-\lambda\|u-v\|^{2}.

(ii)

(
Existence of maximizers
) There exist

u^{\star}\in\arg\max_{u\in\mathcal{K}}f(u),\qquad\hat{u}\in\arg\max_{u\in\mathcal{K}}\widehat{f}(u).

(iii)

(
Uniform gradient perturbation
) There exists
\varepsilon\geq 0
such that

(276)

\sup_{u\in\mathcal{K}}\|\nabla\widehat{f}(u)-\nabla f(u)\|\leq\varepsilon.

Then
u^{\star}
is unique and

(277)

\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda}.

Proof.

Uniqueness of
u^{\star}
follows from Lemma
D.4
.

Fix the maximizers
u^{\star}
and
\hat{u}
.
We prove (
277
).

Step 1 (First-order necessary conditions at
u^{\star}
and
\hat{u}
).

Since
u^{\star}
maximizes
f
on the convex set
\mathcal{K}
and
f
is differentiable, Lemma
D.17
gives

(278)

\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),\,u-u^{\star}\rangle\leq 0\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K}.

In particular, taking
u=\hat{u}
,

(279)

\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),\,\hat{u}-u^{\star}\rangle\leq 0.

Likewise, since
\hat{u}
maximizes
\widehat{f}
on
\mathcal{K}
and
\widehat{f}
is differentiable,

(280)

\langle\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u}),\,u-\hat{u}\rangle\leq 0\qquad\forall u\in\mathcal{K}.

Taking
u=u^{\star}
gives

(281)

\langle\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u}),\,u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle\leq 0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\langle\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u}),\,\hat{u}-u^{\star}\rangle\geq 0.

Step 2 (Use strong concavity to control the displacement).

Apply (
275
) with
(u,v)=(\hat{u},u^{\star})
:

\langle\nabla f(\hat{u})-\nabla f(u^{\star}),\,\hat{u}-u^{\star}\rangle\leq-\lambda\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}.

Rearrange:

\displaystyle\lambda\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\langle\nabla f(u^{\star})-\nabla f(\hat{u}),\,\hat{u}-u^{\star}\rangle

(282)

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla f(u^{\star}),\hat{u}-u^{\star}\rangle-\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),\hat{u}-u^{\star}\rangle.

By (
279
), the first inner product is
\leq 0
, hence

(283)

\lambda\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}\leq-\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),\hat{u}-u^{\star}\rangle=\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle.

Step 3 (Insert
\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u})
and exploit (
281
)).

Write

\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle=\langle\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle+\langle\nabla f(\hat{u})-\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle.

By (
281
),
\langle\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle\leq 0
, therefore

\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle\leq\langle\nabla f(\hat{u})-\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle.

Combine with (
283
) to get

(284)

\lambda\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}\leq\langle\nabla f(\hat{u})-\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle.

Step 4 (Cauchy–Schwarz and the uniform gradient bound).

By Cauchy–Schwarz,

\langle\nabla f(\hat{u})-\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle\leq\|\nabla f(\hat{u})-\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u})\|\,\|u^{\star}-\hat{u}\|.

By (
276
),
\|\nabla f(\hat{u})-\nabla\widehat{f}(\hat{u})\|\leq\varepsilon
.
Thus

\lambda\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}\leq\varepsilon\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|.

If
\hat{u}=u^{\star}
we are done; otherwise divide both sides by
\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|
to obtain

\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|\leq\varepsilon/\lambda
.
∎

Remark D.23

(Tightness of the constant
\varepsilon/\lambda
)
.

The factor
1/\lambda
is unimprovable in general.
For instance, on
\mathcal{K}=\mathbb{R}
, take
f(u)=-\frac{\lambda}{2}(u-u^{\star})^{2}
and

\widehat{f}(u)=f(u)+\varepsilon u
.
Then
\sup_{u}|\widehat{f}^{\prime}(u)-f^{\prime}(u)|=\varepsilon
, and the maximizer shifts by exactly
\varepsilon/\lambda
.

Lemma D.24

(Argmax stability from uniform function-value error)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty, closed, and convex.
Let
f,\widehat{f}:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be arbitrary functions (no differentiability assumed).
Assume:

(i)

f
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\mathcal{K}
(e.g. in the sense of Lemma
D.3

if
f
is differentiable, or via the midpoint/segment inequality definition).

(ii)

Maximizers
u^{\star}\in\arg\max_{\mathcal{K}}f
and
\hat{u}\in\arg\max_{\mathcal{K}}\widehat{f}
exist.

(iii)

\sup_{u\in\mathcal{K}}|\widehat{f}(u)-f(u)|\leq\delta
for some
\delta\geq 0
.

Then

\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|\leq\sqrt{\frac{4\delta}{\lambda}}.

Proof.

Step 1 (Relate
f(\hat{u})
to
f(u^{\star})
using the uniform error).

Since
\hat{u}
maximizes
\widehat{f}
,

\widehat{f}(\hat{u})\geq\widehat{f}(u^{\star}).

By the uniform bound
|\widehat{f}-f|\leq\delta
,

f(\hat{u})-\delta\leq\widehat{f}(\hat{u})\geq\widehat{f}(u^{\star})\geq f(u^{\star})-\delta.

Hence

(285)

f(u^{\star})-f(\hat{u})\leq 2\delta.

Step 2 (Strong concavity gives a quadratic gap lower bound).

Because
f
is
\lambda
-strongly concave and
u^{\star}
is a maximizer, the quadratic drop inequality holds:

(286)

f(u^{\star})-f(\hat{u})\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}.

To prove (
286
) directly without differentiability assumptions:
set
u=\hat{u}
and
v=u^{\star}
in the defining strong concavity inequality along the segment, namely for
t\in(0,1)
,

f\big(t\hat{u}+(1-t)u^{\star}\big)\ \geq\ tf(\hat{u})+(1-t)f(u^{\star})+\frac{\lambda}{2}t(1-t)\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}.

Since
u^{\star}
is a maximizer, the left-hand side is at most
f(u^{\star})
.
Thus

f(u^{\star})\geq tf(\hat{u})+(1-t)f(u^{\star})+\frac{\lambda}{2}t(1-t)\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}.

Rearrange and divide by
t>0
:

f(u^{\star})-f(\hat{u})\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}(1-t)\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}.

Let
t\downarrow 0
to obtain (
286
).

Step 3 (Combine upper and lower bounds).

Combine (
285
) and (
286
):

\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}\leq 2\delta,

so
\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|^{2}\leq 4\delta/\lambda
, i.e.
\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|\leq\sqrt{4\delta/\lambda}
.
∎

Lemma D.25

(Approximate stationarity implies proximity to the maximizer)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty, closed, and convex.
Let
f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be differentiable and
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\mathcal{K}
, meaning it satisfies

(287)

f(u)\ \leq\ f(v)+\langle\nabla f(v),u-v\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-v\|^{2}\qquad\forall u,v\in\mathcal{K}.

Assume a maximizer
u^{\star}\in\arg\max_{u\in\mathcal{K}}f(u)
exists (hence is unique).
Suppose
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{K}
satisfies the approximate first-order condition

(288)

\sup_{u\in\mathcal{K}}\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),\,u-\hat{u}\rangle\leq\eta

for some
\eta\geq 0
.
Then

\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|\ \leq\ \sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\lambda}}.

Proof.

Step 0 (Uniqueness of
u^{\star}
).

Since
f
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on the convex set
\mathcal{K}
, it has at most one maximizer.
Indeed, if
u_{1},u_{2}
are maximizers, apply (
287
) with
(u,v)=(u_{2},u_{1})
:

f(u_{2})\leq f(u_{1})+\langle\nabla f(u_{1}),u_{2}-u_{1}\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u_{2}-u_{1}\|^{2}.

Because
u_{1}
maximizes
f
over the convex set
\mathcal{K}
and
f
is differentiable, the first-order
necessary
condition holds:

\langle\nabla f(u_{1}),u_{2}-u_{1}\rangle\leq 0.

Thus

f(u_{2})\leq f(u_{1})-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u_{2}-u_{1}\|^{2}.

But
f(u_{2})=f(u_{1})
, so
\|u_{2}-u_{1}\|^{2}\leq 0
, hence
u_{2}=u_{1}
.

Step 1 (Quadratic support inequality centered at
\hat{u}
).

Apply (
287
) with
v=\hat{u}
and arbitrary
u\in\mathcal{K}
:

(289)

f(u)\ \leq\ f(\hat{u})+\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),u-\hat{u}\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u-\hat{u}\|^{2}.

This is valid for every
u\in\mathcal{K}
because
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{K}
.

Step 2 (Plug in
u=u^{\star}
and rearrange).

Since
u^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}
, we may take
u=u^{\star}
in (
289
):

f(u^{\star})\ \leq\ f(\hat{u})+\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle-\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u^{\star}-\hat{u}\|^{2}.

Move terms to the other side:

(290)

\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u^{\star}-\hat{u}\|^{2}\ \leq\ f(\hat{u})-f(u^{\star})+\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle.

Step 3 (Control the two terms on the right).

(a) Control
f(\hat{u})-f(u^{\star})
.

Because
u^{\star}
maximizes
f
over
\mathcal{K}
and
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{K}
,

f(\hat{u})\ \leq\ f(u^{\star}),

hence

(291)

f(\hat{u})-f(u^{\star})\ \leq\ 0.

(b) Control
\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle
.

By the hypothesis (
288
),

\sup_{u\in\mathcal{K}}\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),u-\hat{u}\rangle\leq\eta.

Since
u^{\star}\in\mathcal{K}
, the supremum dominates the particular choice
u=u^{\star}
, giving

(292)

\langle\nabla f(\hat{u}),u^{\star}-\hat{u}\rangle\leq\eta.

Step 4 (Conclude).

Insert (
291
) and (
292
) into (
290
):

\frac{\lambda}{2}\|u^{\star}-\hat{u}\|^{2}\leq 0+\eta=\eta.

Thus
\|u^{\star}-\hat{u}\|^{2}\leq 2\eta/\lambda
, i.e.

\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\lambda}}.\qed

Corollary D.26

(EM-style perturbation bound for the M-step)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be nonempty, closed, and convex. Fix
\theta
and define

f(\theta^{\prime}):=Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta),\qquad\widehat{f}(\theta^{\prime}):=\widehat{Q}(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta),\qquad\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}.

Assume:

(i)

Q(\cdot\mid\theta)
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\mathcal{K}
for some
\lambda>0
.

(ii)

Maximizers exist:

T(\theta)\in\arg\max_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta),\qquad\widehat{T}(\theta)\in\arg\max_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}\widehat{Q}(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta).

(iii)

The gradients are uniformly close on
\mathcal{K}
:

\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}}\big\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\widehat{Q}(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}Q(\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta)\big\|\leq\varepsilon.

Then

\|\widehat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda}.

Proof.

Define
f
and
\widehat{f}
as in the statement.
Assumption (i) means
f
is
\lambda
-strongly concave on
\mathcal{K}
.
Assumption (ii) provides maximizers

u^{\star}:=T(\theta)\in\arg\max_{\mathcal{K}}f,\qquad\hat{u}:=\widehat{T}(\theta)\in\arg\max_{\mathcal{K}}\widehat{f}.

Assumption (iii) is exactly the uniform gradient perturbation condition

\sup_{u\in\mathcal{K}}\|\nabla\widehat{f}(u)-\nabla f(u)\|\leq\varepsilon.

Therefore Lemma
D.22
applies, yielding

\|\widehat{T}(\theta)-T(\theta)\|=\|\hat{u}-u^{\star}\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda}.\qed

Appendix E
Empirical-process tools for operator deviation bounds

Let
P
be a probability measure on a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
and let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}P
with empirical measure

P_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
. For a measurable map
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
(scalar
m=1
, vector
m=d
, or matrix
m=d^{2}
via vectorization)
we write

(P_{n}-P)f\ :=\ \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[f(X)].

We use
\|\cdot\|
for the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
for the associated inner product.
For matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we write
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
for the spectral/operator norm induced by
\|\cdot\|
and
\|A\|_{\mathcal{F}}
for the Frobenius norm.
We denote the unit sphere by
\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}:=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|u\|=1\}
.

Function classes and measurability convention.

For a scalar class
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\}
define

Z(\mathcal{F})\ :=\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P)f,\qquad Z_{\pm}(\mathcal{F})\ :=\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\big|(P_{n}-P)f\big|.

To avoid measurability pathologies of
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
in full generality, we adopt the standard convention:

Assumption E.1

(Pointwise measurability / separability)
.

Every class
\mathcal{F}
(and each scalarized class derived from it below) is
pointwise measurable
:
there exists a countable subset
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
such that for every
f\in\mathcal{F}
there is a sequence
f_{k}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}

with
f_{k}(x)\to f(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Under Assumption
E.1
, the random variables
Z(\mathcal{F})
and
Z_{\pm}(\mathcal{F})
are measurable and one may freely apply
expectation and concentration inequalities. (Otherwise, all statements below remain valid with the standard “outer expectation/probability” modifications.)

E.1.
Scalarization for vector and operator norms

The basic mechanism behind operator deviation bounds is
scalarization
:
reduce a vector- or matrix-valued supremum to finitely many scalar suprema by discretizing the unit spheres.

Lemma E.2

(Scalarization via sphere nets: vectors)
.

Let
\eta\in(0,1)
and let
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}\subseteq\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
be an
\eta
-net in Euclidean norm, i.e.
for every
u\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
there exists
v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
with
\|u-v\|\leq\eta
.
Then for every (possibly random)
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(293)

\|Y\|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{1-\eta}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle v,Y\rangle.

Consequently, for any class
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\{h:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
and any probability measures
P_{n},P
for which
(P_{n}-P)h
is defined,

(294)

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\|(P_{n}-P)h\|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{1-\eta}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}(P_{n}-P)\langle v,h\rangle.

Moreover, there exists an
\eta
-net
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}\subseteq\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
with cardinality

(295)

|\mathcal{N}_{\eta}|\leq(1+2/\eta)^{d}.

Proof.

Step 1 (Support-function representation of the Euclidean norm).

We claim that for every
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(296)

\|Y\|\ =\ \sup_{u\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}\langle u,Y\rangle.

Indeed, by Cauchy–Schwarz,
\langle u,Y\rangle\leq\|u\|\,\|Y\|=\|Y\|
for all
u\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
, so the supremum is
\leq\|Y\|
.
If
Y\neq 0
, take
u=Y/\|Y\|\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
to attain
\langle u,Y\rangle=\|Y\|
, so the supremum is
\geq\|Y\|
.
If
Y=0
, both sides equal
0
. This proves (
296
).

Step 2 (Discretize the sphere and derive the
(1-\eta)^{-1}
factor).

Fix
u\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
. By the
\eta
-net property, choose
v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
with
\|u-v\|\leq\eta
.
Then, for any
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\langle u,Y\rangle=\langle v,Y\rangle+\langle u-v,Y\rangle\leq\langle v,Y\rangle+\|u-v\|\,\|Y\|\leq\langle v,Y\rangle+\eta\|Y\|,

where we used Cauchy–Schwarz in the middle inequality.
Since
v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
, we have
\langle v,Y\rangle\leq\max_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle w,Y\rangle
, hence

\langle u,Y\rangle\leq\max_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle w,Y\rangle+\eta\|Y\|.

Now take
\sup_{u\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}
of the left-hand side and use (
296
):

\|Y\|\leq\max_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle w,Y\rangle+\eta\|Y\|.

Rearrange (noting
1-\eta>0
):

(1-\eta)\|Y\|\leq\max_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle w,Y\rangle,

which is exactly (
293
).

Step 3 (Apply to empirical-process vectors).

Apply (
293
) with
Y=(P_{n}-P)h
to obtain, for each fixed
h
,

\|(P_{n}-P)h\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle v,(P_{n}-P)h\rangle.

Take
\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}
of both sides:

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\|(P_{n}-P)h\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle v,(P_{n}-P)h\rangle.

Since
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
is finite, for any real-valued function
a(h,v)
we have

\sup_{h}\max_{v}a(h,v)\leq\max_{v}\sup_{h}a(h,v)
(because for every
h
,
\max_{v}a(h,v)\leq\max_{v}\sup_{h^{\prime}}a(h^{\prime},v)
).
Thus

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\|(P_{n}-P)h\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\langle v,(P_{n}-P)h\rangle.

Finally, by linearity of
(P_{n}-P)
and the definition of
\langle v,h\rangle
as a scalar function,

\langle v,(P_{n}-P)h\rangle=(P_{n}-P)\langle v,h\rangle,

which yields (
294
).

Step 4 (Existence of an
\eta
-net and the volumetric cardinality bound).

We show existence and (
295
) by a standard maximal-packing argument.

(a) Existence of a maximal
\eta
-separated subset and net property.

Call a subset
S\subseteq\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}

\eta
-separated
if
\|x-y\|>\eta
for all distinct
x,y\in S
.
Because
\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
is compact, there exists a finite maximal
\eta
-separated subset
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}

(e.g. by a greedy construction: iteratively add a point outside the union of existing
\eta
-balls; compactness forces termination).
We claim that any maximal
\eta
-separated subset is an
\eta
-net:
if there existed
u\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
with
\|u-v\|>\eta
for all
v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
, then
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}\cup\{u\}

would still be
\eta
-separated, contradicting maximality. Hence the net property holds.

(b) Disjointness of small balls.

For distinct
v,w\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
, the
\eta
-separation gives
\|v-w\|>\eta
, so the Euclidean balls

B(v,\eta/2)
and
B(w,\eta/2)
are disjoint (otherwise a point in the intersection would yield
\|v-w\|\leq\eta
by the triangle inequality).

(c) Containment in a slightly larger ball.

For any
x\in B(v,\eta/2)
with
v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
, we have
\|x\|\leq\|v\|+\|x-v\|\leq 1+\eta/2
.
Thus

\bigcup_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}B(v,\eta/2)\ \subseteq\ B(0,1+\eta/2).

(d) Volume comparison.

Let
\Vol(\cdot)
denote Lebesgue volume in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
By disjointness and containment,

|\mathcal{N}_{\eta}|\,\Vol\!\big(B(0,\eta/2)\big)=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\Vol\!\big(B(v,\eta/2)\big)=\Vol\!\left(\bigcup_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}B(v,\eta/2)\right)\leq\Vol\!\big(B(0,1+\eta/2)\big).

Using
\Vol(B(0,r))=r^{d}\Vol(B(0,1))
,

|\mathcal{N}_{\eta}|\left(\frac{\eta}{2}\right)^{d}\Vol(B(0,1))\leq(1+\eta/2)^{d}\Vol(B(0,1)),

hence

|\mathcal{N}_{\eta}|\leq\left(\frac{1+\eta/2}{\eta/2}\right)^{d}=(1+2/\eta)^{d},

which is (
295
).
∎

Lemma E.3

(Scalarization via sphere nets: operator norm)
.

Let
\eta\in(0,1)
and let
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}\subseteq\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
be an
\eta
-net.
Then for any matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,

(297)

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{(1-\eta)^{2}}\max_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\ \langle u,Av\rangle.

If
A
is symmetric, then for any
\eta\in(0,1/2)
one may take a single net and obtain

(298)

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{1-2\eta}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\ \big|\langle v,Av\rangle\big|.

If additionally
A\succeq 0
, the absolute value in (
298
) may be dropped.

Proof.

Step 1 (Two equivalent variational characterizations of
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
).

We use:

(299)

\displaystyle\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}\|Av\|,

(300)

\displaystyle\|Av\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{u\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}\langle u,Av\rangle\qquad\text{for each fixed }v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}.

Identity (
299
) is the definition of the induced operator norm.
Identity (
300
) is Lemma
E.2
Step 1 (support-function representation) applied to the vector
Av
:
indeed, by Cauchy–Schwarz,
\langle u,Av\rangle\leq\|Av\|
, with equality at
u=Av/\|Av\|
if
Av\neq 0
.

Step 2 (General case: discretize both left and right singular directions).

Fix
v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
and apply Lemma
E.2
(
293
) to the vector
Y=Av
:

(301)

\|Av\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}\max_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle u,Av\rangle.

Now take
\sup_{v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}
of both sides and use (
299
):

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}\|Av\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}\sup_{v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}\max_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle u,Av\rangle.

As in Lemma
E.2
Step 3, since
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
is finite we may exchange
\sup
and
\max
:

\sup_{v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}\max_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle u,Av\rangle\leq\max_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\sup_{v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}\langle u,Av\rangle.

For each fixed
u
,
\langle u,Av\rangle=\langle A^{\top}u,v\rangle
, hence by (
300
) applied to
A^{\top}u
,

\sup_{v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}\langle u,Av\rangle=\sup_{v\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}\langle A^{\top}u,v\rangle=\|A^{\top}u\|.

Therefore,

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}\max_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\|A^{\top}u\|.

Apply Lemma
E.2
again to each vector
Y=A^{\top}u
:

\|A^{\top}u\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle v,A^{\top}u\rangle=\frac{1}{1-\eta}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\langle u,Av\rangle.

Combining the last two displays yields (
297
).

Step 3 (Symmetric case: reduction to a single net via Rayleigh quotients).

Assume
A=A^{\top}
and fix
\eta\in(0,1/2)
.

(a) Rayleigh-quotient characterization.

We claim

(302)

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{x\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}}|\langle x,Ax\rangle|.

Indeed, since
A
is symmetric it admits an orthonormal eigenbasis with real eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{i}\}
.
Writing
x=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}e_{i}
with
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}=1
,

\langle x,Ax\rangle=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2},

so the supremum of
\langle x,Ax\rangle
over
\|x\|=1
equals
\lambda_{\max}(A)
, attained at a top eigenvector,
and the infimum equals
\lambda_{\min}(A)
.
Therefore
\sup_{\|x\|=1}|\langle x,Ax\rangle|=\max\{\lambda_{\max}(A),-\lambda_{\min}(A)\}=\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, proving (
302
).

(b) Net approximation and rearrangement.

Let
x^{\star}\in\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
achieve the supremum in (
302
) (existence holds since
\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
is compact and

x\mapsto|\langle x,Ax\rangle|
is continuous). Choose
v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
with
\|x^{\star}-v\|\leq\eta
.
Then

\displaystyle|\langle x^{\star},Ax^{\star}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle v,Av\rangle|+|\langle x^{\star},Ax^{\star}\rangle-\langle v,Av\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle v,Av\rangle|+|\langle x^{\star}-v,Ax^{\star}\rangle+\langle v,A(x^{\star}-v)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle v,Av\rangle|+|\langle x^{\star}-v,Ax^{\star}\rangle|+|\langle v,A(x^{\star}-v)\rangle|.

Bound each error term by Cauchy–Schwarz and the operator norm:

|\langle x^{\star}-v,Ax^{\star}\rangle|\leq\|x^{\star}-v\|\cdot\|Ax^{\star}\|\leq\eta\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}},

and similarly (using
\|v\|=1
),

|\langle v,A(x^{\star}-v)\rangle|\leq\|v\|\cdot\|A(x^{\star}-v)\|\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|x^{\star}-v\|\leq\eta\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Thus

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}=|\langle x^{\star},Ax^{\star}\rangle|\leq|\langle v,Av\rangle|+2\eta\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Rearrange using
1-2\eta>0
:

(1-2\eta)\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq|\langle v,Av\rangle|\leq\max_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}|\langle w,Aw\rangle|,

which yields (
298
).

(c) Positive semidefinite case.

If
A\succeq 0
, then
\langle v,Av\rangle\geq 0
for all
v
, so
|\langle v,Av\rangle|=\langle v,Av\rangle
and the absolute value may be dropped.
∎

Corollary E.4

(Matrix-valued classes: reduction to scalar classes)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\{M:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\}
and let
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}\subseteq\lx@sectionsign^{d-1}
be an
\eta
-net with
\eta\in(0,1)
.
Then

\sup_{M\in\mathcal{M}}\big\|(P_{n}-P)M\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{(1-\eta)^{2}}\max_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\ \sup_{M\in\mathcal{M}}(P_{n}-P)\,\langle u,Mv\rangle.

If each
M(x)
is symmetric, then for any
\eta\in(0,1/2)
one also has the single-net bound

\sup_{M\in\mathcal{M}}\big\|(P_{n}-P)M\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{1-2\eta}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\ \sup_{M\in\mathcal{M}}(P_{n}-P)\,\big|\langle v,Mv\rangle\big|.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
E.3
to the deterministic matrix
A=(P_{n}-P)M
for each fixed
M\in\mathcal{M}
:

\|(P_{n}-P)M\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{1}{(1-\eta)^{2}}\max_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\ \langle u,(P_{n}-P)Mv\rangle.

Take
\sup_{M\in\mathcal{M}}
of both sides. Since
(P_{n}-P)
is linear,

\langle u,(P_{n}-P)Mv\rangle=(P_{n}-P)\langle u,Mv\rangle,

and the first bound follows. The symmetric-case bound follows similarly from (
298
).
∎

Remark E.5

(Why nets are useful for operator deviation bounds)
.

Corollary
E.4
reduces operator norms to finitely many scalar empirical processes
indexed by the classes
\{\langle u,Mv\rangle:M\in\mathcal{M}\}
(or
\{\langle v,Mv\rangle\}
in the symmetric case).
One then applies scalar tools (symmetrization, contraction, entropy bounds, concentration) and finally union-bounds
over
|\mathcal{N}_{\eta}|^{2}\leq(1+2/\eta)^{2d}
directions (or
|\mathcal{N}_{\eta}|
in the symmetric case).

E.2.
Symmetrization, contraction, and Rademacher complexity

The workhorse bounds on
\mathbb{E}[Z(\mathcal{F})]
proceed by symmetrization to Rademacher processes, followed by a complexity estimate.

Lemma E.6

(Symmetrization)
.

Let
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be i.i.d. with law
P
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
and let
P_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\}
satisfy Assumption
E.1
so that the random variable

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P)f
is measurable.
1
1

1

If one does not wish to impose measurability, the same proof
goes through with outer expectations throughout.

Let
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n}
be i.i.d. Rademacher signs independent of
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
.
Then

(303)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P)f\Big]\ \leq\ 2\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(X_{i})\Big].

Moreover, letting
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}:=\mathcal{F}\cup(-\mathcal{F})
,

(304)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|(P_{n}-P)f|\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}^{\pm}}(P_{n}-P)g\Big]\ \leq\ 2\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}^{\pm}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}g(X_{i})\Big].

Proof.

Step 0 (Ghost sample and notation).

Let
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}P
be an independent copy (“ghost sample”), independent of
X_{1}^{n}
.
Write
P_{n}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}^{\prime}}
.

Step 1 (Replace
P
by the conditional expectation of
P_{n}^{\prime}
).

Fix
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Since
X_{1}^{\prime n}
is i.i.d. with law
P
and independent of
X_{1}^{n}
,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[P_{n}^{\prime}f\,\middle|\,X_{1}^{n}\right]=\mathbb{E}[P_{n}^{\prime}f]=Pf.

Hence, for each fixed
f
,

(P_{n}-P)f=P_{n}f-\mathbb{E}\!\left[P_{n}^{\prime}f\,\middle|\,X_{1}^{n}\right]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})f\,\middle|\,X_{1}^{n}\right].

Taking
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
and using the elementary inequality

\sup_{f}\mathbb{E}[Z_{f}\mid X]\leq\mathbb{E}[\sup_{f}Z_{f}\mid X]
(a direct consequence of Jensen since
\sup
is convex, or simply because

\sup_{f}Z_{f}\geq Z_{f_{0}}
for each
f_{0}
and then take conditional expectations), we obtain

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P)f=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}\!\left[(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})f\,\middle|\,X_{1}^{n}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\!\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})f\,\middle|\,X_{1}^{n}\right].

Taking expectations yields

(305)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P)f\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})f\Big].

Step 2 (Random sign-swap identity).

Let
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n}
be i.i.d. Rademachers independent of
(X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}
.
Define the swapped pairs

(\widetilde{X}_{i},\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\prime}):=\begin{cases}(X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime})&\text{if }\epsilon_{i}=+1,\\
(X_{i}^{\prime},X_{i})&\text{if }\epsilon_{i}=-1.\end{cases}

Because
(X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime})
is exchangeable and
\epsilon_{i}
is symmetric, the joint law of

(\widetilde{X}_{i},\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}
is the same as that of
(X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})f\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f(X_{i})-f(X_{i}^{\prime})\big)\Big]

(306)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f(\widetilde{X}_{i})-f(\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\prime})\big)\Big].

But by the definition of
(\widetilde{X}_{i},\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\prime})
,

f(\widetilde{X}_{i})-f(\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\prime})=\begin{cases}f(X_{i})-f(X_{i}^{\prime})&\epsilon_{i}=+1,\\
f(X_{i}^{\prime})-f(X_{i})&\epsilon_{i}=-1,\end{cases}=\epsilon_{i}\big(f(X_{i})-f(X_{i}^{\prime})\big).

Plugging this into (
306
) gives the exact identity

(307)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})f\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\big(f(X_{i})-f(X_{i}^{\prime})\big)\Big].

Step 3 (Split and use symmetry).

Using
\sup(A+B)\leq\sup A+\sup B
,

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\big(f(X_{i})-f(X_{i}^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(X_{i})+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-\epsilon_{i})f(X_{i}^{\prime}).

Take expectations. Since
(X_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}
is an independent copy of
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and
(-\epsilon_{i})
has the same law as
(\epsilon_{i})
,
the two terms have the same expectation. Hence from (
307
),

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})f\Big]\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(X_{i})\Big].

Combine with (
305
) to obtain (
303
).

Step 4 (Absolute-value form).

For any real collection
(a_{f})_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
,

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|a_{f}|=\max\Big\{\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}a_{f},\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(-a_{f})\Big\}=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}\cup(-\mathcal{F})}a_{g}=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}^{\pm}}a_{g}.

Apply this with
a_{f}=(P_{n}-P)f
to get the equality in (
304
), and then apply (
303
)
to the class
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
to get the inequality.
∎

Rademacher complexity.

Define the expected empirical Rademacher complexity

\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F})\ :=\ \mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(X_{i})\Big].

Lemma
E.6
implies
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{f}(P_{n}-P)f]\leq 2\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F})
(and similarly for
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
).

Lemma E.7

(Contraction inequality (scalar Lipschitz maps; correct constants))
.

Let
\phi_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be
L
-Lipschitz and satisfy
\phi_{i}(0)=0
for
i=1,\dots,n
.
Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\}
satisfy Assumption
E.1
.
Then

(308)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\,\phi_{i}\big(f(X_{i})\big)\Big]\ \leq\ 2L\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\,f(X_{i})\Big].

If additionally
\mathcal{F}
is
symmetric
(i.e.
f\in\mathcal{F}\Rightarrow-f\in\mathcal{F}
), then the factor
2
may be removed:

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\,\phi_{i}\big(f(X_{i})\big)\Big]\ \leq\ L\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\,f(X_{i})\Big].

Proof.

This is the classical Ledoux–Talagrand contraction principle.
A fully detailed proof (including the measurability reduction under Assumption
E.1
)
appears, for example, as Theorem 4.12 in Ledoux–Talagrand (Probability in Banach Spaces, 1991) and in many modern empirical-process texts.
We record the key reductions to make explicit what is used.

Step 1 (Reduction to a finite set of coordinate vectors).

Condition on
X_{1}^{n}
. Consider the (random) subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

T:=\big\{(f(X_{1}),\dots,f(X_{n})):\ f\in\mathcal{F}\big\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}.

By separability/pointwise-measurability (Assumption
E.1
), there exists a countable
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{F}

such that the suprema over
\mathcal{F}
equal those over
\mathcal{F}_{0}
almost surely; then approximate
\mathcal{F}_{0}
by finite truncations and
use monotone convergence to reduce to a finite
T
(this is standard and ensures measurability at each step).

Step 2 (Apply the contraction principle on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
).

For a finite
T\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, define

Z_{\phi}(T):=\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{t\in T}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi_{i}(t_{i})\Big],\qquad Z_{\mathrm{id}}(T):=\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{t\in T}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}t_{i}\Big].

The Ledoux–Talagrand contraction principle states that if each
\phi_{i}
is
L
-Lipschitz and
\phi_{i}(0)=0
, then

Z_{\phi}(T)\ \leq\ 2L\,Z_{\mathrm{id}}(T),

and if
T
is symmetric (i.e.
T=-T
, equivalent to symmetry of
\mathcal{F}
), then

Z_{\phi}(T)\leq L\,Z_{\mathrm{id}}(T)
.
Divide by
n
and uncondition to obtain (
308
) (and its symmetric refinement).

Step 3 (Why
\phi_{i}(0)=0
matters).

If
\phi_{i}(0)\neq 0
, then
\sum_{i}\epsilon_{i}\phi_{i}(f(X_{i}))
contains an uncontrolled term

\sum_{i}\epsilon_{i}\phi_{i}(0)
independent of
f
, which has typical size
\asymp\sqrt{n}
and is not bounded by the RHS.
Centering
\phi_{i}
at
0
removes this additive constant.
∎

Remark E.8

(Vector and matrix contraction)
.

In many EM applications one first scalarizes vector/matrix deviations using sphere nets (Appendix
E.1
) and then applies
Lemma
E.7
to the resulting scalar classes. This typically suffices.
There are also genuinely vector-valued contraction inequalities (with Lipschitz maps
\Phi_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
),
but scalarization usually avoids needing them.

E.3.
Entropy integrals and chaining bounds

Setup.

Let
(\mathcal{F},d)
be a pseudo-metric space. For
\varepsilon>0
, let
N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon)
denote the covering number:
the minimum cardinality of an
\varepsilon
-net in
(\mathcal{F},d)
, and write
\diam(\mathcal{F}):=\sup_{f,g}d(f,g)
.

Lemma E.9

(Finite sub-Gaussian maximum)
.

Let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m}
be centered random variables such that for some
\sigma>0
,

\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda Z_{j}}\big]\ \leq\ \exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}\Big)\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\ \forall j\leq m.

Then

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq j\leq m}Z_{j}\Big]\ \leq\ \sigma\sqrt{2\log m}.

Proof.

For any
\lambda>0
, by Jensen and
\max_{j}a_{j}\leq\log\sum_{j}e^{a_{j}}
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{j}Z_{j}\Big]=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}\Big[\log e^{\lambda\max_{j}Z_{j}}\Big]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\lambda\max_{j}Z_{j}}\Big]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{j=1}^{m}e^{\lambda Z_{j}}\Big]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\Big(me^{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}/2}\Big)=\frac{\log m}{\lambda}+\frac{\lambda\sigma^{2}}{2}.

Optimize over
\lambda>0
at
\lambda=\sqrt{2\log m}/\sigma
.
∎

Lemma E.10

(Dudley entropy integral for Gaussian processes)
.

Let
\{G_{f}\}_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
be a centered Gaussian process such that

\mathbb{E}\big[(G_{f}-G_{g})^{2}\big]=d(f,g)^{2}\qquad\forall f,g\in\mathcal{F}.

Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

(309)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}G_{f}\Big]\ \leq\ C\int_{0}^{\diam(\mathcal{F})}\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon)}\,d\varepsilon.

Proof.

Step 0 (Basepoint and compactness reduction).

Fix
f_{0}\in\mathcal{F}
and note

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}G_{f}=G_{f_{0}}+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(G_{f}-G_{f_{0}}).

Since
\mathbb{E}[G_{f_{0}}]=0
, it suffices to bound
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{f}(G_{f}-G_{f_{0}})]
.
Replace
G_{f}
by
G_{f}-G_{f_{0}}
so that
G_{f_{0}}=0
.

If
N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon)=\infty
for some
\varepsilon
, then the RHS is
+\infty
and the bound is trivial.
Hence assume finite covering numbers at all scales of interest (e.g.
d
-totally bounded).

Step 1 (Dyadic scales and nets).

Let
\Delta:=\diam(\mathcal{F})
and define
\varepsilon_{k}:=2^{-k}\Delta
for
k\geq 0
.
For each
k\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{F}_{k}
be a minimal
\varepsilon_{k}
-net, so
|\mathcal{F}_{k}|=N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon_{k})
.
For each
f\in\mathcal{F}
, choose a selector
\pi_{k}(f)\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
with
d(f,\pi_{k}(f))\leq\varepsilon_{k}
.
Under Assumption
E.1
(or outer-expectation conventions), one can choose these selectors measurably on a
countable dense subset and extend by approximation; we treat this standard measurability point as handled by the assumption.

Step 2 (Chaining decomposition).

For any
K\geq 1
,

G_{f}=G_{\pi_{0}(f)}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\big(G_{\pi_{k}(f)}-G_{\pi_{k-1}(f)}\big)+\big(G_{f}-G_{\pi_{K}(f)}\big).

Taking
\sup_{f}
and using
\sup(A+B)\leq\sup A+\sup B
gives

(310)

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}G_{f}\leq\sup_{f}G_{\pi_{0}(f)}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sup_{f}\big(G_{\pi_{k}(f)}-G_{\pi_{k-1}(f)}\big)+\sup_{f}\big(G_{f}-G_{\pi_{K}(f)}\big).

Step 3 (Remainder term).

For each fixed
f
,
G_{f}-G_{\pi_{K}(f)}
is centered Gaussian with variance
d(f,\pi_{K}(f))^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{K}^{2}
.
Thus it is
\varepsilon_{K}
-sub-Gaussian:

\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda(G_{f}-G_{\pi_{K}(f)}))]\leq\exp(\lambda^{2}\varepsilon_{K}^{2}/2)
.
By Lemma
E.9
applied on a finite
\varepsilon_{K}
-net approximation and then letting the net refine (or by standard Gaussian
continuity arguments), one obtains

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(G_{f}-G_{\pi_{K}(f)})\Big]\ \leq\ C_{0}\,\varepsilon_{K}

for a universal
C_{0}
. In particular,
\varepsilon_{K}\to 0
implies this remainder vanishes as
K\to\infty
.

Step 4 (Control each increment level by a finite maximum).

Fix
k\geq 1
. For any
f
,

\Var\!\big(G_{\pi_{k}(f)}-G_{\pi_{k-1}(f)}\big)=d(\pi_{k}(f),\pi_{k-1}(f))^{2}\leq(d(\pi_{k}(f),f)+d(f,\pi_{k-1}(f)))^{2}\leq(\varepsilon_{k}+\varepsilon_{k-1})^{2}\leq(2\varepsilon_{k-1})^{2}.

Also, the pair
(\pi_{k}(f),\pi_{k-1}(f))
ranges over
\mathcal{F}_{k}\times\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
, hence

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\big(G_{\pi_{k}(f)}-G_{\pi_{k-1}(f)}\big)\leq\max_{(a,b)\in\mathcal{F}_{k}\times\mathcal{F}_{k-1}}(G_{a}-G_{b}).

The collection
\{G_{a}-G_{b}\}_{(a,b)}
is centered Gaussian and each element has variance
\leq(2\varepsilon_{k-1})^{2}
.
Apply Lemma
E.9
(Gaussian is sub-Gaussian with the same parameter) with

m\leq|\mathcal{F}_{k}|\,|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}|
and
\sigma=2\varepsilon_{k-1}
:

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f}(G_{\pi_{k}(f)}-G_{\pi_{k-1}(f)})\Big]\leq 2\varepsilon_{k-1}\sqrt{2\log(|\mathcal{F}_{k}|\,|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}|)}\leq C_{1}\,\varepsilon_{k-1}\Big(\sqrt{\log|\mathcal{F}_{k}|}+\sqrt{\log|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}|}\Big)

for a universal constant
C_{1}
.

Step 5 (Sum and compare to an integral).

Take expectations in (
310
), let
K\to\infty
using Step 3, and use
|\mathcal{F}_{k}|\leq N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon_{k})
:

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}G_{f}\Big]\leq C_{2}\sum_{k\geq 1}\varepsilon_{k-1}\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon_{k})}\leq C_{3}\sum_{k\geq 1}\varepsilon_{k}\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon_{k})}.

Since
\varepsilon\mapsto N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon)
is nonincreasing, for
\varepsilon\in(\varepsilon_{k+1},\varepsilon_{k}]
,

\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon)}\geq\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon_{k})}
. Therefore

\int_{\varepsilon_{k+1}}^{\varepsilon_{k}}\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon)}\,d\varepsilon\geq(\varepsilon_{k}-\varepsilon_{k+1})\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon_{k})}=\varepsilon_{k+1}\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon_{k})}\asymp\varepsilon_{k}\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon_{k})}.

Summing over
k
yields (
309
) up to a universal constant.
∎

Lemma E.11

(Dudley entropy integral for sub-Gaussian increment processes)
.

Let
\{Z_{f}\}_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
be a centered process such that for some pseudo-metric
d
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\big(\lambda(Z_{f}-Z_{g})\big)\Big]\ \leq\ \exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}d(f,g)^{2}}{2}\Big)\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\ \forall f,g\in\mathcal{F}.

Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}Z_{f}\Big]\ \leq\ C\int_{0}^{\diam(\mathcal{F})}\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d,\varepsilon)}\,d\varepsilon.

Proof.

Repeat the proof of Lemma
E.10
, replacing Gaussianity by the assumed sub-Gaussian increment bound, and replacing the Gaussian
maximum step by Lemma
E.9
. The increment variance calculation in Step 4 is replaced by the corresponding
sub-Gaussian parameter bound
d(\pi_{k}(f),\pi_{k-1}(f))\leq 2\varepsilon_{k-1}
, which follows identically from the triangle inequality in
d
.
∎

Corollary E.12

(Conditional Dudley bound for Rademacher averages)
.

Fix a sample
X_{1}^{n}
and define the empirical
L_{2}
pseudo-metric

d_{n}(f,g)\ :=\ \Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f(X_{i})-g(X_{i}))^{2}\Big)^{1/2}.

Let

\mathcal{R}_{n}(f):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(X_{i}).

Then conditional on
X_{1}^{n}
, the process
\{\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)\}_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
has sub-Gaussian increments w.r.t. the metric

\tilde{d}_{n}(f,g):=d_{n}(f,g)/\sqrt{n}
, and hence

(311)

\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)\ \Big|\ X_{1}^{n}\Big]\ \leq\ \frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{0}^{\diam(\mathcal{F},d_{n})}\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d_{n},\varepsilon)}\,d\varepsilon

for a universal constant
C>0
.

Proof.

Step 1 (Sub-Gaussian increments).

Fix
f,g\in\mathcal{F}
and write
a_{i}:=f(X_{i})-g(X_{i})
.
Then, conditional on
X_{1}^{n}
,

\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)-\mathcal{R}_{n}(g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}a_{i}.

Using independence and
\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda\epsilon a}]=\cosh(\lambda a)\leq\exp(\lambda^{2}a^{2}/2)
,

\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\exp\Big(\lambda(\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)-\mathcal{R}_{n}(g))\Big)\,\Big|\,X_{1}^{n}\Big]=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda}{n}\epsilon_{i}a_{i}\Big)\Big]\leq\\
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}a_{i}^{2}}{2n^{2}}\Big)=\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}\Big)=\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{d_{n}(f,g)^{2}}{n}\Big).

Thus the increment is sub-Gaussian with parameter
\tilde{d}_{n}(f,g)=d_{n}(f,g)/\sqrt{n}
.

Step 2 (Apply Lemma
E.11
conditionally).

Apply Lemma
E.11
(conditionally on
X_{1}^{n}
) with metric
\tilde{d}_{n}
.
Since
N(\mathcal{F},\tilde{d}_{n},\varepsilon)=N(\mathcal{F},d_{n},\varepsilon\sqrt{n})
, one obtains (
311
)
after the change of variables
\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}
.
∎

Remark E.13

(From Dudley to empirical-process bounds)
.

Combining Corollary
E.12
with Lemma
E.6
yields

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P)f\Big]\leq\frac{2C}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\diam(\mathcal{F},d_{n})}\sqrt{\log N(\mathcal{F},d_{n},\varepsilon)}\,d\varepsilon\right].

To obtain deterministic entropies (e.g. in
L_{2}(P)
rather than
L_{2}(P_{n})
), one typically upper bounds

N(\mathcal{F},d_{n},\varepsilon)
by
N(\mathcal{F},L_{2}(P),c\varepsilon)
on a high-probability event, or uses bracketing entropies.

E.4.
Concentration inequalities for suprema

We now record concentration inequalities that upgrade an expectation bound into a high-probability deviation bound.

Assumption E.14

(Bounded envelope)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\}
satisfy
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|f\|_{\infty}\leq b
and define the variance proxy

v\ :=\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Var(f(X))\ \leq\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}[f(X)^{2}].

Lemma E.15

(Bousquet/Talagrand inequality for suprema)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent with common law
P
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
and let

P_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\}
satisfy Assumption
E.1
so that the supremum below is measurable.
Define the supremum functional

Z\ :=\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}(P_{n}-P)f.

Assume that there exist finite constants
b\geq 0
and
v\geq 0
such that the following two properties hold:

(A) Bounded differences (one-step Lipschitz).

For each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and every replacement
X_{i}\mapsto X_{i}^{\prime}
(with
X_{i}^{\prime}
independent of all else and distributed as
X_{i}
),
the resulting value
Z^{(i)}
satisfies

(312)

Z-Z^{(i)}\ \leq\ \frac{b}{n}\qquad\text{a.s.}

(B) Predictable quadratic variation.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\sigma(X_{1},\dots,X_{i})
and set the Doob martingale
M_{i}:=\mathbb{E}[Z\mid\mathcal{F}_{i}]
with differences

D_{i}:=M_{i}-M_{i-1}
(
M_{0}:=\mathbb{E}Z
).
Assume the predictable quadratic variation satisfies

(313)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\!\left[D_{i}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\ \leq\ \frac{v}{n}\qquad\text{a.s.}

Then for all
t>0
, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

(314)

Z\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}[Z]+\sqrt{\frac{2vt}{n}}+\frac{bt}{3n}.

Moreover, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

(315)

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\big|(P_{n}-P)f\big|\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\big|(P_{n}-P)f\big|\Big]+\sqrt{\frac{2vt}{n}}+\frac{bt}{3n}.

Proof.

We prove (
314
); the absolute-value form (
315
) is then immediate.

Step 0 (Reduce to a martingale tail bound).

By construction,
(M_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
is a martingale with respect to
(\mathcal{F}_{i})
and

Z-\mathbb{E}Z\ =\ M_{n}-M_{0}\ =\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}.

Thus it suffices to prove a Bernstein/Freedman-type tail bound for
\sum_{i}D_{i}
.

Step 1 (Derive a uniform bound on each martingale increment).

We claim that, under (
312
),

(316)

D_{i}\ \leq\ \frac{b}{n}\qquad\text{a.s. for each }i.

To see this, fix
i
and condition on
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
.
Consider two possible values
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
for
X_{i}
, keeping
X_{-i}
fixed.
Let
Z(x)
denote the value of the supremum functional when
X_{i}
is set to
x
(and all other coordinates fixed).
Then the conditional random variable
Z
given
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
can be viewed as
Z(X_{i})
.
The bounded-differences assumption (
312
) says that for any such pair
(x,x^{\prime})
,

Z(x)-Z(x^{\prime})\ \leq\ \frac{b}{n}.

Taking conditional expectations with respect to
X_{i}
given
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
and using that
M_{i}=\mathbb{E}[Z\mid\mathcal{F}_{i}]=Z(X_{i})

and
M_{i-1}=\mathbb{E}[Z\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]=\mathbb{E}[Z(X_{i})\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]
, we obtain the essential supremum bound

M_{i}-M_{i-1}\leq b/n
, i.e. (
316
).
(Equivalently: a random variable supported in an interval of length
b/n
differs from its conditional mean by at most
b/n
above.)

Step 2 (A sharp one-step Laplace bound for bounded increments).

We prove the following deterministic inequality.

Claim E.16

(Bennett-type upper envelope)
.

Fix
c>0
. For every
\lambda\geq 0
and every real
x\leq c
,

(317)

e^{\lambda x}\ \leq\ 1+\lambda x+\frac{e^{\lambda c}-\lambda c-1}{c^{2}}\,x^{2}.

Proof of Claim
E.16
.

Fix
\lambda\geq 0
and define the function

\psi(x)\ :=\ e^{\lambda x}-1-\lambda x-\alpha x^{2},\qquad\text{where}\qquad\alpha:=\frac{e^{\lambda c}-\lambda c-1}{c^{2}}.

Then
\psi(0)=0
and also
\psi(c)=0
by the definition of
\alpha
.
Moreover,

\psi^{\prime\prime}(x)=\lambda^{2}e^{\lambda x}-2\alpha.

Since
x\mapsto e^{\lambda x}
is increasing,
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is increasing in
x
.
We also have
\psi^{\prime\prime}(c)=\lambda^{2}e^{\lambda c}-2\alpha\geq 0
because
\alpha\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}e^{\lambda c}
(Taylor remainder bound).
Hence
\psi
is convex on
(-\infty,c]
once
x
is sufficiently close to
c
, and, with
\psi(0)=\psi(c)=0
, this implies
\psi(x)\leq 0

for all
x\in[0,c]
; for
x<0
the inequality is even easier since
e^{\lambda x}\leq 1+\lambda x
and the quadratic term is nonnegative.
Thus
\psi(x)\leq 0
for all
x\leq c
, which is (
317
).
∎

Step 3 (Conditional mgf control for the martingale differences).

Fix
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
and set
c:=b/n
.
Apply Claim
E.16
to
x=D_{i}
and
c=b/n
and then take
\mathbb{E}[\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]
.
Using
\mathbb{E}[D_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]=0
(martingale difference property) and the bound
D_{i}\leq c
from (
316
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{\lambda D_{i}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+\lambda\mathbb{E}[D_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]+\frac{e^{\lambda c}-\lambda c-1}{c^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[D_{i}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=1+\frac{e^{\lambda c}-\lambda c-1}{c^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[D_{i}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

(318)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\!\left(\frac{e^{\lambda c}-\lambda c-1}{c^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[D_{i}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\right),

where the last step uses
1+u\leq e^{u}
for all
u
.

Step 4 (Iterate the conditional mgf bound).

Iterating (
318
) and using the tower property yields

(319)

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\exp\!\left(\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}\right)\right]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\!\left[\exp\!\left(\frac{e^{\lambda c}-\lambda c-1}{c^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[D_{i}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\right)\right].

Now invoke the predictable-variation assumption (
313
):

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[D_{i}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\ \leq\ \frac{v}{n}\quad\text{a.s.}

Therefore the exponent on the right-hand side of (
319
) is bounded above by the deterministic constant

\frac{e^{\lambda c}-\lambda c-1}{c^{2}}\cdot\frac{v}{n},

so

(320)

\log\mathbb{E}\!\left[\exp\!\left(\lambda(Z-\mathbb{E}Z)\right)\right]=\log\mathbb{E}\!\left[\exp\!\left(\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}\right)\right]\ \leq\ \frac{e^{\lambda c}-\lambda c-1}{c^{2}}\cdot\frac{v}{n},\qquad c=\frac{b}{n}.

Step 5 (Convert Bennett form to sub-gamma form).

We use the standard inequality (proved below)

(321)

e^{u}-u-1\ \leq\ \frac{u^{2}}{2(1-u/3)}\qquad\forall u\in[0,3).

Applying (
321
) with
u=\lambda c
in (
320
) gives, for all
\lambda\in[0,3/c)
,

(322)

\log\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{\lambda(Z-\mathbb{E}Z)}\right]\ \leq\ \frac{\lambda^{2}}{2\big(1-\lambda c/3\big)}\cdot\frac{v}{n},\qquad c=\frac{b}{n}.

Claim E.17

(Proof of (
321
))
.

For all
u\in[0,3)
,
e^{u}-u-1\leq\frac{u^{2}}{2(1-u/3)}
.

Proof of Claim
E.17
.

For
u\in[0,3)
, expand
e^{u}-u-1=\sum_{k\geq 2}u^{k}/k!
and note that for all
k\geq 3
,

\frac{1}{k!}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2\cdot 3^{k-2}},

since
k!=2\cdot 3\cdot 4\cdots k\ \geq\ 2\cdot 3^{k-2}
.
Hence

e^{u}-u-1=\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sum_{k\geq 3}\frac{u^{k}}{k!}\leq\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sum_{k\geq 3}\frac{u^{k}}{2\cdot 3^{k-2}}=\frac{u^{2}}{2}\left(1+\sum_{m\geq 1}\left(\frac{u}{3}\right)^{m}\right)=\frac{u^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1-u/3},

which is (
321
).
∎

Step 6 (Chernoff bound and explicit optimization).

Fix
t>0
and
s>0
. By Chernoff’s method and (
322
), for any
\lambda\in(0,3/c)
,

\Pr(Z-\mathbb{E}Z\geq s)\leq\exp\!\left(-\lambda s+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda c/3)}\cdot\frac{v}{n}\right).

A standard sub-gamma optimization (and it can be checked by direct substitution) shows that taking

s\ :=\ \sqrt{\frac{2vt}{n}}+\frac{ct}{3}\qquad\Big(\text{recall }c=\frac{b}{n}\Big)

yields
\Pr(Z-\mathbb{E}Z\geq s)\leq e^{-t}
.
Equivalently, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

Z\leq\mathbb{E}Z+\sqrt{\frac{2vt}{n}}+\frac{bt}{3n},

which is (
314
).

Step 7 (Absolute-value version).

Let
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}:=\mathcal{F}\cup(-\mathcal{F})
. Then

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|(P_{n}-P)f|=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}^{\pm}}(P_{n}-P)g.

Assumptions (
312
)–(
313
) are inherited by
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
with the same constants
b,v

(since replacing
f
by
-f
does not change ranges or the martingale increment bounds).
Applying (
314
) to
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
yields (
315
).
∎

Remark E.18

(How to use Lemma
E.15
in empirical-process proofs)
.

Lemma
E.15
is deliberately modular: to apply it, you verify (A) and (B) for your specific supremum functional.

•
