In most empirical-process applications, (A) follows from a uniform envelope bound:
if
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathrm{osc}(f):=\sup_{x}f(x)-\inf_{x}f(x)\leq b
, then changing one sample point changes every empirical mean by at most
b/n
,
hence (
312
) holds.

•

Property (B) is a
predictable variance
bound for the Doob martingale of
Z
.
There are two common ways to ensure it:
(i) directly bound
\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}[D_{i}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]
using structural properties of the class (this is what Talagrand/Bousquet do for
empirical-process suprema); or
(ii) in high-level arguments,
assume
(
313
) as part of
Assumption
E.14
, since it is exactly the variance input needed for a Bernstein-type concentration.

Once (A) and (B) are in place, Lemma
E.15
gives a sharp Bernstein tail around
\mathbb{E}Z
.

E.5.
Matrix concentration for operator-norm deviations

For operator deviation bounds involving sums of random matrices (e.g. empirical Hessians or covariance-type objects),
it is often preferable to use matrix Bernstein inequalities, which provide dimension-dependent tails without explicit discretization.

Setup.

Let
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
be independent random self-adjoint matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]=0
.
Define the matrix variance proxy

V\ :=\ \Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}^{2}\big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Lemma E.19

(Matrix Bernstein inequality (self-adjoint case))
.

Assume
\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq R
almost surely for all
i
.
Then for all
t>0
,

(323)

\Pr\Big(\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \geq\ t\Big)\ \leq\ 2d\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}/2}{V+Rt/3}\Big).

Consequently, with probability at least
1-e^{-s}
,

(324)

\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \sqrt{2Vs}+\frac{2Rs}{3}+\sqrt{2Vs}\,\sqrt{\log(2d)}+\frac{2R}{3}\log(2d),

and in particular (absorbing
\log(2d)
into
s
) one obtains the familiar form

\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \lesssim\ \sqrt{V(\log d+s)}+R(\log d+s).

Proof.

A full proof uses the matrix Laplace transform method:
(i) apply Markov’s inequality to
\Tr\exp(\lambda\sum_{i}Y_{i})
,
(ii) use a matrix MGF bound
\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda Y_{i})]\preceq\exp\big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R/3)}\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2}]\big)
valid for
\lambda\in(0,3/R)
,
and (iii) control
\Tr\exp(\cdot)
by
d\cdot\exp(\lambda_{\max}(\cdot))
.
We provide the critical steps explicitly.

Step 1 (Laplace transform and trace domination).

For
\lambda>0
,

\Pr\Big(\lambda_{\max}\Big(\sum_{i}Y_{i}\Big)\geq t\Big)=\Pr\Big(\exp\Big(\lambda\lambda_{\max}\Big(\sum_{i}Y_{i}\Big)\Big)\geq e^{\lambda t}\Big)\leq e^{-\lambda t}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\lambda\lambda_{\max}\Big(\sum_{i}Y_{i}\Big)\Big)\Big].

Since
\exp(\lambda\lambda_{\max}(A))\leq\Tr(e^{\lambda A})
for self-adjoint
A
(because
\Tr
sums eigenvalues and dominates the maximum),

\Pr\Big(\lambda_{\max}\Big(\sum_{i}Y_{i}\Big)\geq t\Big)\leq e^{-\lambda t}\,\mathbb{E}\Tr\exp\Big(\lambda\sum_{i}Y_{i}\Big).

Step 2 (Iterative conditioning and MGF bound).

Let
S_{k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}Y_{i}
. By iterated expectation and conditional Jensen together with the (noncommutative) Golden–Thompson/Lieb machinery,
one can show

(325)

\mathbb{E}\Tr e^{\lambda S_{n}}\leq\Tr\exp\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\mathbb{E}e^{\lambda Y_{i}}\Big).

This is the standard reduction that replaces the MGF of a sum by a product of MGFs inside a trace exponential.

Step 3 (Bernstein-type MGF estimate for each summand).

Under
\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq R
and
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]=0
, a matrix-valued Bernstein MGF bound holds:
for
\lambda\in(0,3/R)
,

(326)

\log\mathbb{E}e^{\lambda Y_{i}}\ \preceq\ \frac{\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R/3)}\,\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2}].

This follows by comparing the power series of
e^{\lambda Y_{i}}
to
I+\lambda Y_{i}+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R/3)}Y_{i}^{2}

using the scalar inequality
e^{y}\leq 1+y+\frac{y^{2}}{2(1-y/3)}
for
|y|<3
applied to the eigenvalues,
and then taking expectations and the operator log.

Step 4 (Assemble the bound).

Insert (
326
) into (
325
):

\mathbb{E}\Tr e^{\lambda S_{n}}\leq\Tr\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R/3)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2}]\Big).

Let
W:=\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2}]\succeq 0
. Then
\Tr e^{\alpha W}\leq d\cdot e^{\alpha\|W\|_{\mathrm{op}}}=d\cdot e^{\alpha V}
, so

\mathbb{E}\Tr e^{\lambda S_{n}}\leq d\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R/3)}V\Big).

Hence

\Pr\big(\lambda_{\max}(S_{n})\geq t\big)\leq d\cdot\exp\Big(-\lambda t+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2(1-\lambda R/3)}V\Big).

Optimize over
\lambda\in(0,3/R)
to obtain

\Pr\big(\lambda_{\max}(S_{n})\geq t\big)\leq d\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}/2}{V+Rt/3}\Big).

Applying the same bound to
-S_{n}
controls
\lambda_{\min}(S_{n})
and yields the two-sided operator norm bound (
323
).
The inverted high-probability forms follow by solving the inequality for
t
and using
\log(2d)
.
∎

Remark E.20

(How to use matrix Bernstein for empirical Hessians (clean plug-in form))
.

Let
H:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{S}^{d}
be a measurable self-adjoint (symmetric) matrix-valued function.
Given i.i.d.
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P
, define the centered summands

Y_{i}\ :=\ H(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[H(X)]\ \in\ \mathbb{S}^{d},\qquad i=1,\dots,n,

so that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]=0
and

(P_{n}-P)H\ =\ \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}.

Assume there exists
R<\infty
such that

(327)

\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ R\qquad\text{a.s. for all }i,

and define the matrix-variance proxy

(328)

V\ :=\ \Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\!\big[Y_{i}^{2}\big]\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ =\ n\,\big\|\mathbb{E}\!\big[Y_{1}^{2}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{op}},

where
Y_{i}^{2}:=Y_{i}Y_{i}
(well-defined since
Y_{i}
is self-adjoint).

Then the (self-adjoint) matrix Bernstein inequality (e.g. Tropp) yields for all
t\geq 0
,

(329)

\Pr\!\Big(\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \geq\ t\Big)\ \leq\ 2d\cdot\exp\!\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2V+\frac{2}{3}Rt}\right).

Equivalently, letting
\delta\in(0,1)
and solving (
329
) for
t
gives the explicit high-probability bound:
with probability at least
1-\delta
,

(330)

\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \sqrt{2V\log\!\Big(\frac{2d}{\delta}\Big)}\ +\ \frac{2R}{3}\log\!\Big(\frac{2d}{\delta}\Big).

Dividing by
n
yields the empirical-process form:
with probability at least
1-\delta
,

(331)

\|(P_{n}-P)H\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \sqrt{\frac{2\|\,\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}^{2}]\,\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{n}\,\log\!\Big(\frac{2d}{\delta}\Big)}\ +\ \frac{2R}{3n}\log\!\Big(\frac{2d}{\delta}\Big).

Interpretation for EM Hessians.

In EM analyses,
H(X)
is typically an observed-information or complete-data Hessian contribution (or a mixed Hessian block).
Equation (
331
) gives a
dimension-aware
operator-norm deviation without any explicit sphere-net/union bound.
This is usually preferable to net-based scalarization when
d
is moderate-to-large.

Two technical notes.

(a)

If
H(X)
is not exactly symmetric, apply the bound to its symmetrization

\frac{1}{2}(H+H^{\top})
, since
\|(P_{n}-P)H\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|(P_{n}-P)\frac{1}{2}(H+H^{\top})\|_{\mathrm{op}}
.

(b)

In applications,
R
and
\|\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}^{2}]\|_{\mathrm{op}}
can be bounded using (i) almost-sure operator bounds on
H(X)
, and
(ii) second-moment bounds; for instance,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}^{2}]\preceq\mathbb{E}[H(X)^{2}]
and
\|Y_{1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2\|H(X)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
a.s.

E.6.
Parameter-indexed classes: covering numbers from Lipschitzness

Lemma E.21

(Covering numbers via parameter Lipschitzness (fully explicit))
.

Let
(\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|)
be a seminormed linear space of real-valued measurable functions on
\mathcal{X}

(e.g.
\|g\|=\|g\|_{L_{2}(P)}
).
Let
\Theta_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
and consider the parameter-indexed class

\mathcal{F}_{0}\ :=\ \{f_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{0}\}\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{F}.

Assume there exists
L>0
such that for all
\theta,\vartheta\in\Theta_{0}
,

(332)

\|f_{\theta}-f_{\vartheta}\|\ \leq\ L\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{2}.

Then for every
\varepsilon>0
,

(333)

N\!\big(\mathcal{F}_{0},\|\cdot\|,\varepsilon\big)\ \leq\ N\!\big(\Theta_{0},\|\cdot\|_{2},\varepsilon/L\big).

In particular, if
\Theta_{0}
is contained in the Euclidean ball
B_{2}(0,R)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
, then for every
\varepsilon>0
,

(334)

N\!\big(\mathcal{F}_{0},\|\cdot\|,\varepsilon\big)\ \leq\ \Big(1+\frac{2LR}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{p}.

Proof.

Step 1 (Push forward a parameter net to a function net).

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and let
\{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{m}\}\subseteq\Theta_{0}
be an
(\varepsilon/L)
-net of
\Theta_{0}
in
\|\cdot\|_{2}
,
so that for every
\theta\in\Theta_{0}
there exists
j
with

\|\theta-\theta_{j}\|_{2}\ \leq\ \varepsilon/L.

By the Lipschitz assumption (
332
),

\|f_{\theta}-f_{\theta_{j}}\|\ \leq\ L\|\theta-\theta_{j}\|_{2}\ \leq\ \varepsilon.

Thus
\{f_{\theta_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{m}
is an
\varepsilon
-net of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
in the seminorm
\|\cdot\|
.

Step 2 (Conclude the covering-number inequality).

By definition of covering number, the existence of an
\varepsilon
-net of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
of size
m
implies

N(\mathcal{F}_{0},\|\cdot\|,\varepsilon)\leq m
.
Minimizing over all
(\varepsilon/L)
-nets of
\Theta_{0}
yields (
333
).

Step 3 (Volumetric specialization on a Euclidean ball).

If
\Theta_{0}\subseteq B_{2}(0,R)
, then

N(\Theta_{0},\|\cdot\|_{2},\varepsilon/L)\leq N(B_{2}(0,R),\|\cdot\|_{2},\varepsilon/L)
.
Apply Lemma
E.22
below with
\rho=\varepsilon/L
to get

N(\Theta_{0},\|\cdot\|_{2},\varepsilon/L)\leq(1+2R/(\varepsilon/L))^{p}=(1+2LR/\varepsilon)^{p}
,
and combine with (
333
) to obtain (
334
).
∎

Lemma E.22

(Volumetric covering bound for Euclidean balls)
.

For
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
R>0
, and
\rho\in(0,2R]
,

(335)

N\!\big(B_{2}(0,R),\|\cdot\|_{2},\rho\big)\ \leq\ \Big(1+\frac{2R}{\rho}\Big)^{p}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}\subseteq B_{2}(0,R)
be a maximal
\rho
-separated set:

\|x-y\|_{2}>\rho
for distinct
x,y\in\mathcal{N}
, and no further point can be added while preserving separation.
Maximality implies
\mathcal{N}
is a
\rho
-net of
B_{2}(0,R)
(otherwise a point at distance
>\rho
from all net points could be added).

Now consider Euclidean balls
\{B_{2}(x,\rho/2):x\in\mathcal{N}\}
.
They are pairwise disjoint by
\rho
-separation, and all are contained in
B_{2}(0,R+\rho/2)
because

\|x\|\leq R
implies
B_{2}(x,\rho/2)\subseteq B_{2}(0,R+\rho/2)
.
By comparing volumes,

|\mathcal{N}|\cdot\Vol\!\big(B_{2}(0,\rho/2)\big)\ \leq\ \Vol\!\big(B_{2}(0,R+\rho/2)\big).

Using
\Vol(B_{2}(0,r))=c_{p}r^{p}
for a constant
c_{p}
depending only on
p
,

|\mathcal{N}|\ \leq\ \left(\frac{R+\rho/2}{\rho/2}\right)^{p}\ =\ \left(1+\frac{2R}{\rho}\right)^{p}.

Since
\mathcal{N}
is a
\rho
-net,
N(B_{2}(0,R),\|\cdot\|_{2},\rho)\leq|\mathcal{N}|
, proving (
335
).
∎

Remark E.23

(Combining everything: a typical EM deviation pipeline (more explicit))
.

A common, fully explicit route to uniform deviation bounds in EM proofs is:

(1)

Choose the object and its norm.

Typical targets are vector gradients
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\|(P_{n}-P)g_{\theta}\|
or matrix Hessians

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}\|(P_{n}-P)H_{\theta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
.

(2)

If needed, scalarize.

For vector/matrix
suprema
over classes, scalarize via sphere nets as in Appendix
E.1
and union-bound over net points.
This reduces the problem to finitely many scalar empirical processes.

(3)

Control the scalar supremum in expectation.

Use symmetrization (Lemma
E.6
) to reduce
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{f}(P_{n}-P)f]
to a Rademacher average.
Then bound the Rademacher average via entropy integrals / chaining:
combine parameter-to-function covering reductions (Lemma
E.21
) with Dudley/generic chaining
(Remark
E.13
).

(4)

Upgrade to high probability.

Apply a Bernstein-type concentration inequality for suprema (Lemma
E.15
) and, if scalarization was used,
add the union bound over net points.

(5)

Optional shortcut for matrix sums.

If the object is already a sum of self-adjoint matrices (e.g. empirical Hessians),
bypass explicit nets and scalarization using matrix Bernstein directly (Remark
E.20
).

This appendix is organized so each step is a direct plug-in lemma with explicit constants and no hidden reductions.

Appendix F
Canonical instantiations under nonidentifiability and misspecification

This appendix instantiates the symmetry and quotient-measurability templates used in the main text for several canonical model classes.
For each class we verify:
(i) a latent-transport representation (Assumption
C.1
);
(ii) the induced observed invariance
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
;
(iii) posterior transport and
Q
-equivariance (Appendix
C
);
and (iv) where needed, explicit measurable sections of the quotient map.

Standing measurable setup and notation.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
be the observed space and fix a
\sigma
-finite dominating measure
\mu
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
.
Assume
P^{\star}\ll\mu
throughout Appendix
F
.
Whenever latent variables are used, let
(\mathcal{Z},G)
be standard Borel with a
\sigma
-finite measure
\nu
, and assume Appendix
A

(joint domination and existence of regular conditional laws).

Given
\theta\in\Theta
with complete-data density
p_{\theta}(x,z)
w.r.t.
\mu\otimes\nu
, write the marginal density

p_{\theta}(x):=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)\in[0,\infty],

and let
\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
denote a regular conditional distribution of
Z\mid X=x
under
P_{\theta}
; when
p_{\theta}(x)>0
one may take

\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)=\frac{\int_{B}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)}{p_{\theta}(x)}\qquad(B\in G),

and define
\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
arbitrarily on
\{x:p_{\theta}(x)=0\}
.

Let
G
be a group acting on
\Theta
(Appendix
B
) and write
q:\Theta\to\Theta/G
for the orbit map.

F.1.
A general quotient-measurability principle

The following lemma is repeatedly used (often implicitly) to pass from invariance on
\Theta
to well-defined objects on
\Theta/G
.

Lemma F.1

(Invariant functions factor through the quotient)
.

Let
(\Theta,\mathcal{B}(\Theta))
be a measurable space and let
G
be a group acting on
\Theta
.
Assume the action is
measurable in the usual sense
that for each fixed
g\in G
,
the map
T_{g}:\Theta\to\Theta
,
T_{g}(\theta)=g\cdot\theta
, is
\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
–measurable.
Let
q:\Theta\to\Theta/G
be the orbit map, and equip
\Theta/G
with the
quotient
\sigma
-field

\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G):=\big\{A\subseteq\Theta/G:\ q^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)\big\}.

Let
\overline{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
with its Borel
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
.
If
f:\Theta\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}
is
\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
–measurable and
G
-invariant, i.e.

f(g\cdot\theta)=f(\theta)\qquad\forall g\in G,\ \forall\theta\in\Theta,

then there exists a
unique

\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
–measurable function

\bar{f}:\Theta/G\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}
such that

f=\bar{f}\circ q.

Proof.

Step 0 (Two basic facts about the quotient
\sigma
-field).

(a)

By definition of
\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
, the orbit map
q:(\Theta,\mathcal{B}(\Theta))\to(\Theta/G,\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G))

is measurable: indeed, for any
A\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
we have
q^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
by definition.

(b)

The map
q
is surjective: every orbit class
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G
is of the form
q(\theta)
for some
\theta\in\Theta
.

Step 1 (Define
\bar{f}
and verify it is well-defined).

For
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G
, pick any
\theta\in\Theta
such that
q(\theta)=\bar{\theta}
and define

(336)

\bar{f}(\bar{\theta})\ :=\ f(\theta).

We must check that (
336
) does not depend on the choice of representative.
If
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\Theta
satisfy
q(\theta_{1})=q(\theta_{2})
, then
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
lie in the same orbit,
so there exists
g\in G
with
\theta_{2}=g\cdot\theta_{1}
.
By
G
-invariance of
f
,

f(\theta_{2})=f(g\cdot\theta_{1})=f(\theta_{1}).

Thus
\bar{f}
is well-defined.

Step 2 (Factorization identity
f=\bar{f}\circ q
).

For any
\theta\in\Theta
, we have
\bar{f}(q(\theta))=f(\theta)
by the definition (
336
) applied to the orbit
q(\theta)
.
Hence
f=\bar{f}\circ q
pointwise on
\Theta
.

Step 3 (Uniqueness).

Suppose
\tilde{f}:\Theta/G\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}
also satisfies
f=\tilde{f}\circ q
.
Fix
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G
and choose
\theta\in\Theta
with
q(\theta)=\bar{\theta}
(surjectivity).
Then

\tilde{f}(\bar{\theta})=\tilde{f}(q(\theta))=f(\theta)=\bar{f}(q(\theta))=\bar{f}(\bar{\theta}).

Thus
\tilde{f}=\bar{f}
everywhere on
\Theta/G
, proving uniqueness.

Step 4 (Measurability of
\bar{f}
).

Let
B\in\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
be an arbitrary Borel set. Consider
\bar{f}^{-1}(B)\subseteq\Theta/G
.
Using
f=\bar{f}\circ q
, we compute its pullback:

q^{-1}\!\big(\bar{f}^{-1}(B)\big)=\{\theta\in\Theta:\ \bar{f}(q(\theta))\in B\}=\{\theta\in\Theta:\ f(\theta)\in B\}=f^{-1}(B).

Since
f
is
\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
–measurable,
f^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.
Therefore
q^{-1}(\bar{f}^{-1}(B))\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
, and by the definition of the quotient
\sigma
-field,

\bar{f}^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
.
As this holds for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
, the function
\bar{f}
is measurable.
∎

Remark F.2

(Quotient objective under misspecification (fully explicit))
.

Assume
observed invariance
:

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}\qquad\forall g\in G,\ \forall\theta\in\Theta.

Assume furthermore that each
P_{\theta}^{X}
admits a density
p_{\theta}
w.r.t. a common dominating measure
\mu
,
and that the invariance holds at the density level
\mu
-a.e. (equivalently,
p_{g\cdot\theta}=p_{\theta}

\mu
-a.e.).
Define the population log-likelihood under misspecification,

\Phi(\theta)\ :=\ \mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X)\big]\quad\text{(whenever the expectation is well-defined in }(-\infty,\infty]\text{)}.

Then
\Phi
is
G
-invariant:
for any
g\in G
and
\theta\in\Theta
,

\Phi(g\cdot\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\big[\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X)\big]=\Phi(\theta),

because
p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=p_{\theta}(x)

\mu
-a.e. and hence
P^{\star}
-a.s. on the support where the log is evaluated.
Therefore, by Lemma
F.1
,
\Phi
factors through the quotient:
there exists a unique measurable
\bar{\Phi}:\Theta/G\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\Phi=\bar{\Phi}\circ q
.
Consequently, the orbit-level maximizer set

\arg\max_{\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G}\bar{\Phi}(\bar{\theta})

is the natural identifiable target in the presence of nonidentifiability induced by
G
-symmetry.

Lemma F.3

(Mixture-model transport, invariance, posterior transport, and EM equivariance)
.

Consider the finite-mixture complete-data density

p_{\theta}(x,z):=\pi_{z}\,f(x;\vartheta_{z}),\qquad(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times[k],

with parameter space
\Theta=\Delta_{k-1}\times\Xi^{k}
and the
S_{k}
-action

(g\cdot\theta):=(\pi_{g^{-1}(1)},\dots,\pi_{g^{-1}(k)},\,\vartheta_{g^{-1}(1)},\dots,\vartheta_{g^{-1}(k)}),\qquad g\in S_{k}.

Let
\nu
be counting measure on
[k]
, and define
\tau_{g}:[k]\to[k]
by
\tau_{g}(z):=g(z)
.
Then Assumption
C.1
holds (with
\tau_{g}
and
\nu
), and consequently:

(1)

Observed invariance:

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
for all
g\in S_{k}
and
\theta\in\Theta
.
Equivalently, the observed density
p_{\theta}(x)=\sum_{z=1}^{k}\pi_{z}f(x;\vartheta_{z})
is invariant:

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=p_{\theta}(x)
for all
x
.

(2)

Posterior transport:
for all
B\subseteq[k]
and for all
x
such that
p_{\theta}(x)>0
,

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\kappa_{\theta}(x,g^{-1}(B)).

(3)

Q
-equivariance and M-step equivariance:
for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
and
g\in S_{k}
,

Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})

whenever both sides are well-defined. Consequently, for the M-step correspondence

\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\in\arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta}Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
,

\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\ =\ g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\qquad\text{(equivariance at the level of argmax sets)}.

Proof.

Step 1 (Verify the transport identity for the complete-data density).

Fix
g\in S_{k}
,
\theta\in\Theta
, and
(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times[k]
.
By definition of the action and
\tau_{g}(z)=g(z)
,

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(z))=p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,g(z))=\pi^{\prime}_{g(z)}\,f\big(x;\vartheta^{\prime}_{g(z)}\big),

where
(\pi^{\prime},\vartheta^{\prime})
denote the parameters after permutation, i.e.

\pi^{\prime}_{j}=\pi_{g^{-1}(j)}
and
\vartheta^{\prime}_{j}=\vartheta_{g^{-1}(j)}
.
Hence

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,g(z))=\pi_{g^{-1}(g(z))}\,f\big(x;\vartheta_{g^{-1}(g(z))}\big)=\pi_{z}\,f(x;\vartheta_{z})=p_{\theta}(x,z).

Thus the exact transport relation holds:

(337)

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\tau_{g}(z))\ =\ p_{\theta}(x,z)\qquad\forall(x,z).

Step 2 (Check the group and measure-preservation properties of
\tau_{g}
).

Because
\tau_{g}
is a permutation of the finite set
[k]
,

\tau_{gh}=\tau_{g}\circ\tau_{h},\qquad\tau_{e}=\mathrm{id},

and counting measure
\nu
is preserved:

\nu(\tau_{g}^{-1}(B))=\nu(B)
for all
B\subseteq[k]
.
These are exactly the structural requirements typically imposed in latent-transport assumptions.

Step 3 (Observed invariance).

The observed density is obtained by marginalization over
z
(a finite sum):

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=\sum_{z=1}^{k}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z).

Using (
337
) with
z^{\prime}=\tau_{g}(z)=g(z)
(a bijection of
[k]
),

\sum_{z=1}^{k}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)=\sum_{z=1}^{k}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,g(z))=\sum_{z=1}^{k}p_{\theta}(x,z)=p_{\theta}(x).

Therefore
p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)=p_{\theta}(x)
for all
x
, i.e.
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
.

Step 4 (Posterior transport).

For
x
with
p_{\theta}(x)>0
(equivalently
p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)>0
by Step 3),
Bayes’ rule yields the posterior probabilities

\kappa_{\theta}(x,\{z\})=\Pr_{\theta}(Z=z\mid X=x)=\frac{p_{\theta}(x,z)}{p_{\theta}(x)},\qquad\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\{z\})=\frac{p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)}{p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)}.

Using Step 3 for the denominator and (
337
) for the numerator (rewritten as

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)=p_{\theta}(x,g^{-1}(z))
),

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\{z\})=\frac{p_{\theta}(x,g^{-1}(z))}{p_{\theta}(x)}=\kappa_{\theta}(x,\{g^{-1}(z)\}).

Extend from singletons to arbitrary
B\subseteq[k]
by additivity (finite sum):

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\sum_{z\in B}\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,\{z\})=\sum_{z\in B}\kappa_{\theta}(x,\{g^{-1}(z)\})=\sum_{z^{\prime}\in g^{-1}(B)}\kappa_{\theta}(x,\{z^{\prime}\})=\kappa_{\theta}(x,g^{-1}(B)).

Step 5 (
Q
-equivariance).

Write
Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
in the concrete finite-mixture form (whenever integrable):

Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\!\left[\sum_{z=1}^{k}\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\{z\})\,\log p_{\theta}(X,z)\right].

Then, using Step 4 (posterior transport) and Step 1 (complete-data transport),

\displaystyle Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\!\left[\sum_{z=1}^{k}\kappa_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(X,\{z\})\,\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X,z)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\!\left[\sum_{z=1}^{k}\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\{g^{-1}(z)\})\,\log p_{\theta}(X,g^{-1}(z))\right]\qquad\text{(Steps 4 and 1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\!\left[\sum_{z^{\prime}=1}^{k}\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\{z^{\prime}\})\,\log p_{\theta}(X,z^{\prime})\right]\qquad\text{(change variable }z^{\prime}=g^{-1}(z)\text{)}

\displaystyle=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime}).

Step 6 (M-step equivariance).

Let
\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\in\arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta}Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
denote the argmax correspondence.
By Step 5, for any
\theta
,

Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})=Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime}).

Therefore
\theta
maximizes
Q(\cdot\mid\theta^{\prime})
if and only if
g\cdot\theta
maximizes
Q(\cdot\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})
,
which is precisely the equivariance identity
\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
.
∎

A global measurable section under a finite action

We record a single reusable selector for
any
finite group action on a Borel subset of Euclidean space.

Assumption F.4

(Borel embedding and Borel action)
.

\Theta
is identified with a Borel subset of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
for some
D
, and for each
g\in G
the map
\theta\mapsto g\cdot\theta
is Borel.

Fix a Borel linear order
\preceq
on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
(e.g. lexicographic order).

Lemma F.5

(Borel section for finite actions via orbit minimization)
.

Let
(\Theta,\mathcal{B}(\Theta))
be a
standard Borel space
and let
G
be a finite group acting on
\Theta
.
Assume the action is
Borel
in the sense that for each
g\in G
the map

T_{g}:\Theta\to\Theta,\qquad T_{g}(\theta):=g\cdot\theta

is
\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
–measurable.
Fix a
Borel linear order

\preceq
on
\Theta
, i.e. a total order such that its graph

\Gamma_{\preceq}:=\{(\theta,\vartheta)\in\Theta\times\Theta:\ \theta\preceq\vartheta\}

belongs to
\mathcal{B}(\Theta)\otimes\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.
2
2

2

Every standard Borel space admits such an order:
identify
\Theta
Borel-isomorphically with a Borel subset of
[0,1]
and pull back the usual order.

Define the orbit-minimizing selector

(338)

\tilde{s}(\theta)\ :=\ \min_{\preceq}\{g\cdot\theta:\ g\in G\}.

Then:

(a)

\tilde{s}:\Theta\to\Theta
is Borel measurable;

(b)

\tilde{s}
is constant on orbits:
\tilde{s}(g\cdot\theta)=\tilde{s}(\theta)
for all
g\in G
and
\theta\in\Theta
;

(c)

letting
q:\Theta\to\Theta/G
be the orbit map and equipping
\Theta/G
with the quotient
\sigma
-field

\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G):=\{A\subseteq\Theta/G:\ q^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)\},

there exists a unique measurable map
s:\Theta/G\to\Theta
such that
\tilde{s}=s\circ q
;

(d)

the map
s
is a measurable
section
: for all
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G
, one has
q(s(\bar{\theta}))=\bar{\theta}
.

Proof.

Write
G=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\}
with
m:=|G|
.

Step 0 (Measurable comparison sets).

For each pair
(i,j)\in\{1,\dots,m\}^{2}
, define

A_{ij}\ :=\ \{\theta\in\Theta:\ g_{i}\cdot\theta\ \preceq\ g_{j}\cdot\theta\}.

We claim
A_{ij}\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.
Indeed, consider the Borel map

\Theta\ni\theta\ \longmapsto\ (g_{i}\cdot\theta,\ g_{j}\cdot\theta)\in\Theta\times\Theta,

which is measurable because
\theta\mapsto g_{i}\cdot\theta
and
\theta\mapsto g_{j}\cdot\theta
are measurable and products of measurable
maps are measurable. Then

A_{ij}=\{\,\theta:\ (g_{i}\cdot\theta,\ g_{j}\cdot\theta)\in\Gamma_{\preceq}\,\}=\big(\theta\mapsto(g_{i}\cdot\theta,g_{j}\cdot\theta)\big)^{-1}(\Gamma_{\preceq}),

and since
\Gamma_{\preceq}\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)\otimes\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
, we conclude
A_{ij}\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.

Step 1 (A Borel partition selecting an argmin index).

Define recursively Borel sets
B_{1},\dots,B_{m}\subseteq\Theta
by

B_{1}\ :=\ \bigcap_{j=1}^{m}A_{1j},\qquad B_{i}\ :=\ \Big(\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}A_{ij}\Big)\ \setminus\ \bigcup_{r=1}^{i-1}B_{r},\quad i=2,\dots,m.

Each
B_{i}
is Borel because it is obtained from finitely many Borel sets using finite intersections, unions, and complements.

Claim 1:

\{B_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
is a partition of
\Theta
.

•

They are disjoint by construction (
B_{i}
removes all previous
B_{r}
).

•

They cover
\Theta
because for each
\theta
, the finite set
\{g\cdot\theta:\ g\in G\}
has a (unique)
\preceq
-minimum point,
so the set of indices achieving the minimum is nonempty; taking the
smallest
such index
i
ensures
\theta\in B_{i}
.
Formally: define

M(\theta):=\min_{\preceq}\{g_{j}\cdot\theta:\ j=1,\dots,m\}.

Let
I(\theta):=\{i:\ g_{i}\cdot\theta=M(\theta)\}
, which is nonempty. Let
i^{\star}:=\min I(\theta)
.
Then
g_{i^{\star}}\cdot\theta\preceq g_{j}\cdot\theta
for all
j
, so
\theta\in\cap_{j}A_{i^{\star}j}
, and by minimality of
i^{\star}
,

\theta\notin\cup_{r<i^{\star}}B_{r}
, hence
\theta\in B_{i^{\star}}
.

Step 2 (Borel measurability of
\tilde{s}
).

On
B_{i}
the element
g_{i}\cdot\theta
is
\preceq
-minimal among
\{g\cdot\theta:\ g\in G\}
, hence equals
\tilde{s}(\theta)
.
Therefore we have the pointwise representation

(339)

\tilde{s}(\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}_{B_{i}}(\theta)\,(g_{i}\cdot\theta),

where the sum is “piecewise” (exactly one indicator is
1
at each
\theta
).
To prove measurability, it is enough to show that for any
C\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
, the preimage
\tilde{s}^{-1}(C)
is Borel.
Using (
339
),

\tilde{s}^{-1}(C)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\Big(B_{i}\ \cap\ \{\theta:\ g_{i}\cdot\theta\in C\}\Big).

Each
\{\theta:\ g_{i}\cdot\theta\in C\}=T_{g_{i}}^{-1}(C)
is Borel since
T_{g_{i}}
is measurable.
Since
B_{i}
is Borel and
m<\infty
, the union is Borel. Hence
\tilde{s}
is Borel measurable.

Step 3 (Orbit-constancy of
\tilde{s}
).

Fix
h\in G
and
\theta\in\Theta
.
Because
G
is a group, left multiplication by
h
permutes
G
bijectively, so

\{g\cdot(h\cdot\theta):\ g\in G\}=\{(gh)\cdot\theta:\ g\in G\}=\{g^{\prime}\cdot\theta:\ g^{\prime}\in G\}.

Taking
\preceq
-minima of equal sets yields

\tilde{s}(h\cdot\theta)=\min_{\preceq}\{g\cdot(h\cdot\theta):g\in G\}=\min_{\preceq}\{g^{\prime}\cdot\theta:g^{\prime}\in G\}=\tilde{s}(\theta).

Thus
\tilde{s}
is constant on orbits.

Step 4 (Define
s
on the quotient and prove it is well-defined).

Let
q:\Theta\to\Theta/G
be the orbit map
q(\theta)=\left[\theta\right]
.
Define
s:\Theta/G\to\Theta
by

(340)

s(\bar{\theta})\ :=\ \tilde{s}(\theta)\quad\text{for any }\theta\in\Theta\text{ with }q(\theta)=\bar{\theta}.

This is well-defined: if
q(\theta_{1})=q(\theta_{2})
, then
\theta_{2}=g\cdot\theta_{1}
for some
g\in G
, and Step 3 gives

\tilde{s}(\theta_{2})=\tilde{s}(g\cdot\theta_{1})=\tilde{s}(\theta_{1})
.

Step 5 (Measurability of
s
and factorization
\tilde{s}=s\circ q
).

By construction,
\tilde{s}(\theta)=s(q(\theta))
, i.e.
\tilde{s}=s\circ q
.
To prove
s
is measurable, let
C\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)
.
We show
s^{-1}(C)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
by verifying its pullback is Borel:

q^{-1}\big(s^{-1}(C)\big)=\{\theta\in\Theta:\ s(q(\theta))\in C\}=\{\theta\in\Theta:\ \tilde{s}(\theta)\in C\}=\tilde{s}^{-1}(C)\ \in\ \mathcal{B}(\Theta),

since
\tilde{s}
is measurable (Step 2). By definition of the quotient
\sigma
-field, this implies
s^{-1}(C)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)
.
Thus
s
is measurable. Uniqueness of
s
follows from surjectivity of
q
: if
s_{1}\circ q=s_{2}\circ q
, then
s_{1}=s_{2}
.

Step 6 (
s
is a section:
q\circ s=\mathrm{id}
).

Fix
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G
and choose
\theta
with
q(\theta)=\bar{\theta}
.
By definition of
\tilde{s}(\theta)
as the
\preceq
-minimum in
\{g\cdot\theta:\ g\in G\}
,
there exists some
g\in G
such that
\tilde{s}(\theta)=g\cdot\theta
.
Therefore

q(s(\bar{\theta}))=q(\tilde{s}(\theta))=q(g\cdot\theta)=q(\theta)=\bar{\theta},

where we used that
q
is constant on orbits:
q(g\cdot\theta)=q(\theta)
.
Hence
q\circ s=\mathrm{id}_{\Theta/G}
, i.e.
s
is a section.
∎

Remark F.6

(Canonical relabeling for mixtures and other finite-label models)
.

Lemma
F.5
yields a
canonical
orbit representative whenever the label group
G
is finite and acts Borelly:
one fixes a Borel order
\preceq
on
\Theta
and selects the
\preceq
-minimum element of each finite orbit.
This avoids ad hoc coordinate ordering rules and handles ties automatically (ties can only arise from stabilizers producing the same parameter point,
in which case the minimum point is still well-defined).

F.2.
Over-specified mixtures and misspecified projection geometry

Lemma F.7

(Invariance of
\Phi
and orbit structure of
\bar{\Theta}
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
be the observation space and
\mu
a
\sigma
-finite measure on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
.
Assume
P^{\star}\ll\mu
.
For each
\theta\in\Theta
, let
P_{\theta}^{X}\ll\mu
and let
p_{\theta}:=\frac{dP_{\theta}^{X}}{d\mu}
be a chosen
\mu
-density
(i.e. a Radon–Nikodym derivative, defined
\mu
-a.e.).
Define the extended-real objective

\Phi(\theta)\ :=\ \mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\!\big[\log p_{\theta}(X)\big]\ \in[-\infty,\infty]

(where
\log 0:=-\infty
), and the maximizer set

\bar{\Theta}\ :=\ \arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta}\Phi(\theta)\ =\ \{\theta\in\Theta:\ \Phi(\theta)=\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Phi(\vartheta)\}.

Let
G
act measurably on
\Theta
and assume
observed invariance
holds:

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}\ =\ P_{\theta}^{X}\qquad\forall\,\theta\in\Theta,\ \forall\,g\in G.

Then:

(i)

\Phi(g\cdot\theta)=\Phi(\theta)
for all
\theta\in\Theta
and
g\in G
(as extended real numbers);

(ii)

\bar{\Theta}
is
G
-invariant: if
\theta\in\bar{\Theta}
then
g\cdot\theta\in\bar{\Theta}
for all
g\in G
;

(iii)

the orbit-level set
q(\bar{\Theta})\subseteq\Theta/G
is the identifiable projection target; equivalently,
\Phi
factors through the quotient
and its maximizers in
\Theta/G
are precisely
q(\bar{\Theta})
.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and
g\in G
.

Step 1 (Equality of laws implies equality of densities
\mu
-a.e.).

By assumption,
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
as measures on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
.
Since both measures are absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\mu
, their Radon–Nikodym derivatives are unique
\mu
-a.e.
Hence

(341)

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x)\ =\ p_{\theta}(x)\qquad\text{for $\mu$-a.e.\ }x\in\mathcal{X}.

Step 2 (Transfer
\mu
-a.e. equality to
P^{\star}
-a.s. equality).

Because
P^{\star}\ll\mu
, any
\mu
-null set is
P^{\star}
-null.
Therefore (
341
) implies

(342)

p_{g\cdot\theta}(X)\ =\ p_{\theta}(X)\qquad P^{\star}\text{-a.s.}

Applying
\log(\cdot)
(with
\log 0:=-\infty
) preserves equality pointwise on the event in (
342
), hence

(343)

\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X)\ =\ \log p_{\theta}(X)\qquad P^{\star}\text{-a.s.}

Step 3 (Almost-sure equality implies equality of expectations, including the extended-real case).

The random variables
\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X)
and
\log p_{\theta}(X)
are measurable as maps into
[-\infty,\infty)
.
Since they are equal
P^{\star}
-a.s. by (
343
), their integrals w.r.t.
P^{\star}
coincide in the extended sense:

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\big[\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X)\big]\ =\ \mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X)\big].

This proves (i), i.e.
\Phi(g\cdot\theta)=\Phi(\theta)
.

Step 4 (
G
-invariance of the maximizer set).

Let
\theta\in\bar{\Theta}
. By definition,

\Phi(\theta)\ =\ \sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Phi(\vartheta).

Using (i),

\Phi(g\cdot\theta)\ =\ \Phi(\theta)\ =\ \sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\Phi(\vartheta),

so
g\cdot\theta\in\bar{\Theta}
. This proves (ii).

Step 5 (Orbit-level target and factorization through the quotient).

Let
q:\Theta\to\Theta/G
be the orbit map and endow
\Theta/G
with the quotient
\sigma
-field

\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G)=\{A:\ q^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)\}
.
By (i),
\Phi
is
G
-invariant, so by Lemma
F.1
there exists a unique measurable

\bar{\Phi}:\Theta/G\to[-\infty,\infty)
such that

\Phi\ =\ \bar{\Phi}\circ q.

We now identify the maximizers of
\bar{\Phi}
with
q(\bar{\Theta})
.

Claim 5a:
q(\bar{\Theta})\subseteq\arg\max_{\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G}\bar{\Phi}(\bar{\theta})
.

If
\theta\in\bar{\Theta}
, then for any
\bar{\vartheta}\in\Theta/G
choose
\vartheta\in\Theta
with
q(\vartheta)=\bar{\vartheta}
.
Then

\bar{\Phi}(q(\theta))=\Phi(\theta)\ \geq\ \Phi(\vartheta)=\bar{\Phi}(q(\vartheta))=\bar{\Phi}(\bar{\vartheta}),

so
q(\theta)
maximizes
\bar{\Phi}
.

Claim 5b:
\arg\max_{\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G}\bar{\Phi}(\bar{\theta})\subseteq q(\bar{\Theta})
.

Let
\bar{\theta}^{\star}
maximize
\bar{\Phi}
, and pick
\theta^{\star}\in\Theta
with
q(\theta^{\star})=\bar{\theta}^{\star}
.
For any
\vartheta\in\Theta
,

\Phi(\theta^{\star})=\bar{\Phi}(\bar{\theta}^{\star})\ \geq\ \bar{\Phi}(q(\vartheta))=\Phi(\vartheta),

so
\theta^{\star}\in\bar{\Theta}
and hence
\bar{\theta}^{\star}=q(\theta^{\star})\in q(\bar{\Theta})
.

Combining Claims 5a–5b yields

q(\bar{\Theta})\ =\ \arg\max_{\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G}\bar{\Phi}(\bar{\theta}).

This is precisely the statement that the identifiable target is the orbit-level maximizer set, proving (iii).
∎

Remark F.8

(Quotient projection set and additional nonidentifiabilities)
.

In over-specified mixtures,
\bar{\Theta}
may contain multiple
distinct
orbits and may have positive dimension even after quotienting.
Lemma
F.7
isolates the unavoidable symmetry via the quotient target
q(\bar{\Theta})
.
Any remaining nonidentifiabilities (beyond
G
) manifest as geometry of
q(\bar{\Theta})
itself (e.g. continua of orbit families),
which motivates orbit-level metrics and transverse curvature conditions in the main text.

F.3.
Finite-label models beyond i.i.d. mixtures

Many latent-variable models share the same finite-label symmetry as mixtures. The key point is:
if the complete-data density is built from
label-indexed
parameter blocks, then simultaneous relabeling of parameters and latent labels
induces latent transport.

Hidden Markov models (HMMs) with state-label switching

Fix
k\geq 2
and horizon
T\geq 1
. Let
\mathcal{Z}=[k]^{T}
with product
\sigma
-field and counting measure
\nu
.
Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{T}
be observed in
\mathcal{X}^{T}
with dominating measure
\mu^{\otimes T}
.

Parameterization.

Let
\theta=(\pi,A,\vartheta_{1},\dots,\vartheta_{k})
where
\pi\in\Delta_{k-1}
is the initial distribution,

A\in[0,1]^{k\times k}
is row-stochastic, and
\vartheta_{j}\in\Xi
index emission densities

f(\cdot;\vartheta_{j})
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F},\mu)
, as in Appendix
F.1
.
For
z_{1:T}\in[k]^{T}
and
x_{1:T}\in\mathcal{X}^{T}
, define the complete-data density

(344)

p_{\theta}(x_{1:T},z_{1:T}):=\pi_{z_{1}}\,\prod_{t=2}^{T}A_{z_{t-1},z_{t}}\,\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(x_{t};\vartheta_{z_{t}})\qquad\text{w.r.t.\ }\mu^{\otimes T}\otimes\nu,

where
\nu
is counting measure on
[k]^{T}
.

Permutation action.

Let
G=S_{k}
act by simultaneous relabeling:

(g\cdot\pi)_{j}:=\pi_{g^{-1}(j)},\qquad(g\cdot A)_{ij}:=A_{g^{-1}(i),g^{-1}(j)},\qquad(g\cdot\vartheta)_{j}:=\vartheta_{g^{-1}(j)}.

Define
\tau_{g}:[k]^{T}\to[k]^{T}
by
(\tau_{g}(z))_{t}:=g(z_{t})
.

Lemma F.9

(HMM transport and EM equivariance)
.

For the HMM (
344
) with the
S_{k}
action above, Assumption
C.1
holds with latent space

\mathcal{Z}=[k]^{T}
, base measure
\nu
counting measure, and
\tau_{g}
acting componentwise.
Consequently:

(i)

(
Observed invariance
)
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X_{1:T}}=P_{\theta}^{X_{1:T}}
for all
g\in S_{k}
and
\theta\in\Theta
.

(ii)

(
Posterior transport
) for all
B\subseteq[k]^{T}
and
P_{\theta}^{X_{1:T}}
-a.e.
x_{1:T}
,

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x_{1:T},B)\ =\ \kappa_{\theta}\!\big(x_{1:T},\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)\big).

(iii)

(
Q
-equivariance and M-step equivariance
) whenever the
Q
-functional is well-defined,

Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\ =\ Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\qquad\forall\,\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta,\ \forall\,g\in S_{k},

and therefore the M-step correspondence
\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\in\arg\max_{\theta}Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
satisfies

\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
(equivariance in the correspondence sense).

Proof.

We verify the items in Assumption
C.1
explicitly, and then derive (i)–(iii).

Step 1 (Measurability and group structure of
\tau_{g}
).

The latent space
\mathcal{Z}=[k]^{T}
is finite, hence equipped with the discrete
\sigma
-field
2^{\mathcal{Z}}
.
Therefore every map
\tau_{g}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}
is measurable.
Moreover,
\tau_{g}
is a bijection with inverse
\tau_{g^{-1}}
.
Finally, for
g,h\in S_{k}
and
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,

(\tau_{gh}(z))_{t}=(gh)(z_{t})=g(h(z_{t}))=(\tau_{g}(\tau_{h}(z)))_{t},

so
\tau_{gh}=\tau_{g}\circ\tau_{h}
.

Step 2 (Base-measure preservation).

Since
\mathcal{Z}
is finite and
\nu
is counting measure,
any bijection
\tau_{g}
preserves
\nu
:
for every
B\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
,

\nu(\tau_{g}(B))=|\,\tau_{g}(B)\,|=|\,B\,|=\nu(B).

Equivalently,
\nu\circ\tau_{g}^{-1}=\nu
for all
g
.

Step 3 (Equivariance of the complete-data density).

Fix
\theta=(\pi,A,\vartheta)
,
g\in S_{k}
, and
(x_{1:T},z_{1:T})\in\mathcal{X}^{T}\times\mathcal{Z}
.
Write
z^{\prime}:=\tau_{g}(z)
so
z^{\prime}_{t}=g(z_{t})
.

(a) Initial factor.

By definition of the action on
\pi
,

\pi^{(g\cdot\theta)}_{z^{\prime}_{1}}=(g\cdot\pi)_{g(z_{1})}=\pi_{g^{-1}(g(z_{1}))}=\pi_{z_{1}}.

(b) Transition factors.

For each
t\geq 2
, using the action on
A
,

A^{(g\cdot\theta)}_{z^{\prime}_{t-1},z^{\prime}_{t}}=(g\cdot A)_{g(z_{t-1}),g(z_{t})}=A_{g^{-1}(g(z_{t-1})),\,g^{-1}(g(z_{t}))}=A_{z_{t-1},z_{t}}.

(c) Emission factors.

For each
t
, using the action on
\vartheta
,

\vartheta^{(g\cdot\theta)}_{z^{\prime}_{t}}=(g\cdot\vartheta)_{g(z_{t})}=\vartheta_{g^{-1}(g(z_{t}))}=\vartheta_{z_{t}}.

Hence
f(x_{t};\vartheta^{(g\cdot\theta)}_{z^{\prime}_{t}})=f(x_{t};\vartheta_{z_{t}})
.

Multiplying (a)–(c) over the HMM factorization (
344
) yields

(345)

p_{g\cdot\theta}(x_{1:T},\tau_{g}(z_{1:T}))\ =\ p_{\theta}(x_{1:T},z_{1:T})\qquad\text{for all }(x_{1:T},z_{1:T}).

Together with Steps 1–2, this is exactly the latent-transport property in Assumption
C.1
.

Step 4 (Observed invariance by marginalization and change of variables).

Let
P_{\theta}^{X,Z}
denote the joint law on
\mathcal{X}^{T}\times\mathcal{Z}
with density (
344
) w.r.t.
\mu^{\otimes T}\otimes\nu
,
and let
P_{\theta}^{X}
be its marginal on
\mathcal{X}^{T}
.
For any measurable
A\in\mathcal{F}^{\otimes T}
,

\displaystyle P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\int_{\mathcal{X}^{T}}\mathbf{1}_{A}(x)\,p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\mu^{\otimes T}(dx)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\int_{\mathcal{X}^{T}}\mathbf{1}_{A}(x)\,p_{g\cdot\theta}\big(x,\tau_{g}(z)\big)\,\mu^{\otimes T}(dx)\qquad\text{(reindex the finite sum since $\tau_{g}$ is a bijection)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\int_{\mathcal{X}^{T}}\mathbf{1}_{A}(x)\,p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\mu^{\otimes T}(dx)\qquad\text{by \eqref{eq:hmm-density-transport}}

\displaystyle=P_{\theta}^{X}(A).

Thus
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X_{1:T}}=P_{\theta}^{X_{1:T}}
, proving (i).

Step 5 (Posterior transport).

Fix
B\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
.
Because
\mathcal{Z}
is finite, the posterior
\kappa_{\theta}(x,\cdot)
can be written explicitly by Bayes’ rule whenever the marginal density

p_{\theta}^{X}(x):=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)
is positive:

\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)\ =\ \frac{\sum_{z\in B}p_{\theta}(x,z)}{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)}.

We show the claimed identity on the set
\{x:\ p_{\theta}^{X}(x)>0\}
; outside it, the posterior can be defined arbitrarily.

Using (
345
) and the bijection
\tau_{g}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in B}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in B}p_{g\cdot\theta}\big(x,\tau_{g}(\tau_{g}^{-1}(z))\big)=\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)}p_{g\cdot\theta}\big(x,\tau_{g}(z^{\prime})\big)=\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)}p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime}).

Similarly,

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)=\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime})=p_{\theta}^{X}(x),

where we used
\tau_{g}^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})=\mathcal{Z}
.
Therefore, whenever
p_{\theta}^{X}(x)>0
(equivalently,
p_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(x)>0
by Step 4),

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\frac{\sum_{z\in B}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)}{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)}=\frac{\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)}p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime})}{\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime})}=\kappa_{\theta}\big(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)\big).

This proves (ii) (for
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
x
; the exceptional set is
\{p_{\theta}^{X}=0\}
).

Step 6 (
Q
-equivariance and M-step equivariance).

Assume
Q(\cdot\mid\cdot)
is defined (i.e. the relevant integrability conditions hold).
Fix
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
and
g\in S_{k}
.

By definition of the EM
Q
-functional (population version),

Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\Big[\,\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\big]\,\Big].

Apply this with
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
replaced by
(g\cdot\theta,g\cdot\theta^{\prime})
:

Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\Big[\,\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\kappa_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}\big[\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X,Z)\big]\,\Big].

Now use posterior transport (Step 5): if
Z\sim\kappa_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)
, then
Z^{\prime}:=\tau_{g}^{-1}(Z)
satisfies

Z^{\prime}\sim\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)
.
Moreover, by (
345
),

\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X,Z)=\log p_{g\cdot\theta}\big(X,\tau_{g}(Z^{\prime})\big)=\log p_{\theta}(X,Z^{\prime}).

Therefore, by a change of variables in the finite sum defining the conditional expectation,

\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\kappa_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}\big[\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X,Z)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{Z^{\prime}\sim\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X,Z^{\prime})\big].

Taking
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}
of both sides yields
Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
, proving the first part of (iii).

For the M-step correspondence, let
\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\in\arg\max_{\theta}Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
.
Using
Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
, we have for any
\theta
,

Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\leq Q(\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\mid\theta^{\prime})=Q(g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime}),

so
g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
is a maximizer of
\theta\mapsto Q(\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})
.
This is exactly the equivariance statement
\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
in correspondence form.
∎

Measurable section.

If
\Theta
is a standard Borel space (e.g. a Borel subset of Euclidean space) and the
S_{k}
-action is Borel, then
Lemma
F.5
yields a global Borel selector
s:\Theta/S_{k}\to\Theta
that chooses a canonical representative
in each orbit (e.g. the
\preceq
-minimum element of the finite orbit).

Stochastic block models (SBMs) and latent-class network models

Let
n\geq 2
and
k\geq 2
. Let
X=(X_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq n}
be an observed adjacency matrix, viewed as an element of a finite measurable space

(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
(e.g.
\mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{\binom{n}{2}}
with
\mathcal{F}=2^{\mathcal{X}}
) dominated by counting measure
\mu
.
Let latent labels be
Z=(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n})\in[k]^{n}
with counting measure
\nu
on
\mathcal{Z}:=[k]^{n}
.

Parameterization.

Let
\theta=(\pi,B)
where
\pi\in\Delta_{k-1}
are class proportions and
B\in[0,1]^{k\times k}
is symmetric.
Given
Z=z
, edges are conditionally independent with

\Pr_{\theta}(X_{ij}=1\mid Z=z)=B_{z_{i},z_{j}}\qquad(1\leq i<j\leq n),

and
Z_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\pi
.
The complete-data density (w.r.t.
\mu\otimes\nu
) is

(346)

p_{\theta}(x,z)=\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{z_{i}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}B_{z_{i},z_{j}}^{x_{ij}}(1-B_{z_{i},z_{j}})^{1-x_{ij}}\Big),\qquad(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}.

Permutation action and transport.

Let
G=S_{k}
act on parameters by simultaneous relabeling:

(g\cdot\pi)_{a}:=\pi_{g^{-1}(a)},\qquad(g\cdot B)_{ab}:=B_{g^{-1}(a),g^{-1}(b)}.

Let
\tau_{g}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}
act componentwise:
(\tau_{g}(z))_{i}:=g(z_{i})
.

Lemma F.10

(SBM transport and invariance)
.

For the SBM (
346
) with the
S_{k}
action above, Assumption
C.1
holds with latent space
\mathcal{Z}=[k]^{n}
,
base measure
\nu
counting measure, and
\tau_{g}
acting componentwise. Consequently:

(i)

(
Observed invariance
)
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
for all
g\in S_{k}
and
\theta\in\Theta
.

(ii)

(
Posterior transport
) for all
B\subseteq[k]^{n}
and
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e.
x
,

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)\ =\ \kappa_{\theta}\!\big(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)\big).

(iii)

(
Q
-equivariance and M-step equivariance
) whenever
Q(\cdot\mid\cdot)
is well-defined,

Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})\ =\ Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\qquad\forall\,\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta,\ \forall\,g\in S_{k},

and hence the M-step correspondence is
S_{k}
-equivariant.

Proof.

We verify the latent-transport conditions and then derive (i)–(iii).

Step 1 (Group properties and measurability of the latent relabeling).

The latent space
\mathcal{Z}=[k]^{n}
is finite, hence carries the discrete
\sigma
-field
2^{\mathcal{Z}}
; thus every map
\tau_{g}
is measurable.
For
g,h\in S_{k}
and
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,

(\tau_{gh}(z))_{i}=(gh)(z_{i})=g(h(z_{i}))=(\tau_{g}(\tau_{h}(z)))_{i},

so
\tau_{gh}=\tau_{g}\circ\tau_{h}
. Also
\tau_{g}
is a bijection with inverse
\tau_{g^{-1}}
.

Step 2 (Preservation of counting measure).

Let
\nu
be counting measure on
\mathcal{Z}
. Since
\tau_{g}
is a bijection on a finite set, it preserves cardinality:
for every
A\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
,

\nu(\tau_{g}(A))=|\tau_{g}(A)|=|A|=\nu(A).

Equivalently,
\nu\circ\tau_{g}^{-1}=\nu
.

Step 3 (Equivariance of the complete-data density).

Fix
\theta=(\pi,B)
,
g\in S_{k}
, and
(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}
.
Write
z^{\prime}:=\tau_{g}(z)
so
z^{\prime}_{i}=g(z_{i})
.

(a) Class-proportion factor.

Using the action on
\pi
,

\prod_{i=1}^{n}(g\cdot\pi)_{z^{\prime}_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{g^{-1}(z^{\prime}_{i})}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{g^{-1}(g(z_{i}))}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{z_{i}}.

(b) Edge-likelihood factor.

For each
1\leq i<j\leq n
,

(g\cdot B)_{z^{\prime}_{i},z^{\prime}_{j}}=(g\cdot B)_{g(z_{i}),g(z_{j})}=B_{g^{-1}(g(z_{i})),\,g^{-1}(g(z_{j}))}=B_{z_{i},z_{j}}.

Therefore

\prod_{i<j}(g\cdot B)_{z^{\prime}_{i},z^{\prime}_{j}}^{x_{ij}}\bigl(1-(g\cdot B)_{z^{\prime}_{i},z^{\prime}_{j}}\bigr)^{1-x_{ij}}=\prod_{i<j}B_{z_{i},z_{j}}^{x_{ij}}(1-B_{z_{i},z_{j}})^{1-x_{ij}}.

Multiplying (a) and (b) gives the transport identity

(347)

p_{g\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{g}(z)\bigr)\ =\ p_{\theta}(x,z)\qquad\forall(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}.

Together with Steps 1–2, this is precisely Assumption
C.1
.

Step 4 (Observed invariance by marginalization and reindexing).

Let
P_{\theta}^{X,Z}
be the joint law with density
p_{\theta}
w.r.t.
\mu\otimes\nu
and
P_{\theta}^{X}
its marginal.
For any measurable
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\sum_{x\in A}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\sum_{x\in A}p_{g\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{g}(z)\bigr)\qquad\text{(reindex using the bijection $\tau_{g}$)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\sum_{x\in A}p_{\theta}(x,z)=P_{\theta}^{X}(A),

where we used (
347
). Hence
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
, proving (i).

Step 5 (Posterior transport).

Fix
B\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
. Since
\mathcal{Z}
is finite, Bayes’ rule yields, on the set where the marginal likelihood

p_{\theta}^{X}(x):=\sum_{z}p_{\theta}(x,z)
is positive,

\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)=\frac{\sum_{z\in B}p_{\theta}(x,z)}{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)}.

Using (
347
) and the bijection
\tau_{g}
,

\sum_{z\in B}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)=\sum_{z\in B}p_{g\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{g}(\tau_{g}^{-1}(z))\bigr)=\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)}p_{g\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{g}(z^{\prime})\bigr)=\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)}p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime}).

Similarly,
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p_{\theta}(x,z)
.
Thus, whenever the denominators are nonzero,

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\kappa_{\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)\bigr).

Since
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
, this holds
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e., proving (ii).

Step 6 (
Q
-equivariance and M-step equivariance).

Assume
Q(\cdot\mid\cdot)
is well-defined (integrability).
By definition,

Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}\big[\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)\big]\Big].

Apply this with
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
replaced by
(g\cdot\theta,g\cdot\theta^{\prime})
and use Step 5:
if
Z\sim\kappa_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)
then
Z^{\prime}:=\tau_{g}^{-1}(Z)\sim\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)
.
Then (
347
) gives
\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X,Z)=\log p_{\theta}(X,Z^{\prime})
.
Hence, by a change of variables over the finite latent space,

\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\kappa_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}[\log p_{g\cdot\theta}(X,Z)]=\mathbb{E}_{Z^{\prime}\sim\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,\cdot)}[\log p_{\theta}(X,Z^{\prime})],

and taking
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}
yields
Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
.
The usual maximizer-transfer argument then implies equivariance of the M-step correspondence, proving (iii).
∎

F.4.
Signed-permutation symmetries (hyperoctahedral groups)

Finite sign and permutation symmetries arise whenever the latent prior is invariant under coordinate relabeling and sign flips.
This includes many ICA/dictionary-learning style latent models (where identifiability is only up to signed permutation).

Signed permutation group.

Let
r\geq 1
and define the finite group

\mathsf{SP}(r):=\Big\{R\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}:\ R\text{ has exactly one nonzero entry in each row/column, equal to }\pm 1\Big\}.

Equivalently,

\mathsf{SP}(r)=\{D\Pi:\ D\in\{\pm 1\}^{r\times r}\text{ diagonal},\ \Pi\in S_{r}\text{ permutation matrix}\}.

Every
R\in\mathsf{SP}(r)
is orthogonal:
R^{\top}R=I_{r}
and
R^{-1}=R^{\top}
.

ICA / linear latent-factor template with i.i.d. symmetric priors

Let
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{r}
with its Borel
\sigma
-field and let
\nu=\nu_{0}^{\otimes r}
, where
\nu_{0}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}

that is symmetric about
0
, i.e.
\nu_{0}(B)=\nu_{0}(-B)
for all Borel
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}
(equivalently,
U\sim\nu_{0}\Rightarrow-U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}U
).
Then
\nu
is invariant under signed permutations in the following strong (measure-pushforward) sense.

Lemma F.11

(Invariance of the product prior under
\mathsf{SP}(r)
)
.

Let
\nu=\nu_{0}^{\otimes r}
with
\nu_{0}
symmetric. For each
R\in\mathsf{SP}(r)
define
\tau_{R}:\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
by
\tau_{R}(z)=Rz
.
Then
\tau_{R}
is a Borel bijection and

\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu\qquad\text{for all }R\in\mathsf{SP}(r).

Proof.

Step 1 (Borel bijection).

Since
\tau_{R}
is linear and invertible, it is continuous (hence Borel measurable) and bijective with inverse
\tau_{R^{-1}}
.

Step 2 (Reduction to generators).

It suffices to verify
\nu(\tau_{R}^{-1}(C))=\nu(C)
for all cylinder rectangles

C=\prod_{j=1}^{r}C_{j}
with Borel
C_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, because such rectangles form a
\pi
-system generating the Borel
\sigma
-field on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
and both
\nu
and
\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}
are probability measures.

Step 3 (Explicit form of
\tau_{R}
).

Write
R=D\Pi
with
\Pi
a permutation matrix and
D=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{r})
with
\sigma_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Then
(\tau_{R}(z))_{j}=\sigma_{j}z_{\pi^{-1}(j)}
, i.e.
\tau_{R}
permutes coordinates and flips signs of selected coordinates.

Step 4 (Compute
\nu(\tau_{R}^{-1}(C))
).

We have

\tau_{R}^{-1}(C)=\Big\{z:\ \sigma_{j}z_{\pi^{-1}(j)}\in C_{j}\ \forall j\Big\}=\Big\{z:\ z_{i}\in\sigma_{\pi(i)}^{-1}C_{\pi(i)}\ \forall i\Big\},

where
\sigma^{-1}C:=\{\sigma^{-1}x:x\in C\}
equals
C
if
\sigma=1
and
-C
if
\sigma=-1
.
By product structure of
\nu
,

\nu(\tau_{R}^{-1}(C))=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\nu_{0}\big(\sigma_{\pi(i)}^{-1}C_{\pi(i)}\big).

By symmetry of
\nu_{0}
,
\nu_{0}(-B)=\nu_{0}(B)
for all
B
, hence
\nu_{0}(\sigma^{-1}B)=\nu_{0}(B)
for
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Therefore

\nu(\tau_{R}^{-1}(C))=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\nu_{0}(C_{\pi(i)})=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\nu_{0}(C_{j})=\nu(C),

since
\pi
is a permutation. This proves
\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu
.
∎

Model.

Let
\Theta
contain a mixing matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
and possibly nuisance parameters
\eta
(e.g. noise scale/covariance).
Assume the conditional law of
X
given
Z=z
depends on
A
only through
Az
; concretely,

X\mid Z=z\sim p_{\mathrm{noise}}(\,\cdot\,;Az,\eta),

where for each
(m,\eta)
the map
x\mapsto p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;m,\eta)
is a
\mu
-density on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
,
and
(x,z,A,\eta)\mapsto p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;Az,\eta)
is jointly measurable.
With
\nu
as above, define the complete-data density w.r.t.
\mu\otimes\nu
by

p_{(A,\eta)}(x,z)=p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;Az,\eta).

Action and transport.

Let
G=\mathsf{SP}(r)
act on
\Theta
by
right
transformation on
A
:

R\cdot(A,\eta):=(AR^{-1},\eta),\qquad R\in\mathsf{SP}(r),

and define latent transport
\tau_{R}(z):=Rz
on
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{r}
.

Lemma F.12

(Signed-permutation transport and EM equivariance)
.

In the ICA-style template above, Assumption
C.1
holds with
G=\mathsf{SP}(r)
,
\tau_{R}(z)=Rz
, and prior
\nu=\nu_{0}^{\otimes r}
.
Consequently:

(i)

(
Observed invariance
)
P_{R\cdot(A,\eta)}^{X}=P_{(A,\eta)}^{X}
for all
R\in\mathsf{SP}(r)
;

(ii)

(
Posterior transport
) for all Borel
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
P_{(A,\eta)}^{X}
-a.e.
x
,

\kappa_{R\cdot(A,\eta)}(x,B)=\kappa_{(A,\eta)}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)\bigr);

(iii)

(
Q
-equivariance and M-step equivariance
) whenever
Q
is well-defined,

Q(R\cdot\theta\mid R\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\qquad\forall\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta,\ \forall R\in\mathsf{SP}(r),

and therefore the M-step correspondence
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
\mathcal{T}(R\cdot\theta^{\prime})=R\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Write
\theta=(A,\eta)
.

Step 1 (Group action and measurability).

\mathsf{SP}(r)
is a finite group under matrix multiplication. The action
\theta\mapsto R\cdot\theta=(AR^{-1},\eta)
is continuous in
A
for fixed
R
,
hence Borel measurable on any Borel parameter space
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\times\mathsf{H}
(with
\mathsf{H}
Euclidean for
\eta
).
The latent transport
\tau_{R}(z)=Rz
is continuous and bijective, hence Borel measurable with measurable inverse.

Step 2 (Base-measure invariance on the latent space).

By Lemma
F.11
,
\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu
for all
R\in\mathsf{SP}(r)
.

Step 3 (Complete-data equivariance identity).

For every
(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
,

\displaystyle p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=p_{(AR^{-1},\eta)}(x,Rz)=p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;AR^{-1}(Rz),\eta)=p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;Az,\eta)=p_{\theta}(x,z).

This is exactly the density-transport identity required in Assumption
C.1
.

Step 4 (Observed invariance by marginalization and change of variables).

Let
p_{\theta}^{X}
be the
\mu
-density of
P_{\theta}^{X}
, given by integrating out
z
against
\nu
:

p_{\theta}^{X}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;Az,\eta)\,\nu(dz).

Then, using Step 3 and Step 2,

\displaystyle p_{R\cdot\theta}^{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{R\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\nu(d(\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})\qquad(\text{since }\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime})\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=p_{\theta}^{X}(x),

so
P_{R\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
, proving (i).

Step 5 (Posterior transport).

Whenever
p_{\theta}^{X}(x)>0
, Bayes’ formula yields the posterior as a probability kernel

\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)=\frac{\int_{B}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)}.

Using Step 3 and the change of variables
z=\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})
plus Step 2,

\displaystyle\int_{B}p_{R\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)

\displaystyle=\int_{\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)}p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=\int_{\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)}p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime})\,\nu(dz^{\prime}),

and similarly
\int p_{R\cdot\theta}(x,z)\nu(dz)=\int p_{\theta}(x,z)\nu(dz)
.
Therefore
\kappa_{R\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\kappa_{\theta}(x,\tau_{R}^{-1}(B))
for all
B
and for all
x
with
p_{\theta}^{X}(x)>0
.
Since
P_{R\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
(Step 4), this holds
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e., proving (ii).

Step 6 (
Q
-equivariance and M-step equivariance).

Assume
Q(\cdot\mid\cdot)
is well-defined (integrability of
\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)
under the relevant posteriors).
By definition,

Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\log p_{\theta}(X,z)\,\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,dz)\Big].

Apply this with
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
replaced by
(R\cdot\theta,R\cdot\theta^{\prime})
and use Steps 3 and 5 with the change of variables
z=\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\int\log p_{R\cdot\theta}(X,z)\,\kappa_{R\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(X,dz)

\displaystyle=\int\log p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(X,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,dz^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\log p_{\theta}(X,z^{\prime})\,\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,dz^{\prime}).

Taking
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}
yields
Q(R\cdot\theta\mid R\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
.
Equivariance of the M-step correspondence
\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\in\arg\max_{\theta}Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
follows immediately:
if
\theta\in\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
, then for all
\vartheta
,

Q(R\cdot\theta\mid R\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\geq Q(\vartheta\mid\theta^{\prime})=Q(R\cdot\vartheta\mid R\cdot\theta^{\prime}),

so
R\cdot\theta\in\mathcal{T}(R\cdot\theta^{\prime})
.
∎

Measurable section.

Since
\mathsf{SP}(r)
is finite, Lemma
F.5
yields a global Borel section on
\Theta/\mathsf{SP}(r)
whenever
Assumption
F.4
holds.

F.5.
Rotational symmetries in linear-Gaussian factor models (
G=O(r)
)

This is the continuous-group analogue of label switching. It is canonical in factor analysis and probabilistic PCA,
where
A
is only identified up to right orthogonal transformations.

Model and domination.

Let
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{r}
with its Borel
\sigma
-field and let
\nu=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{r})
be the standard Gaussian
measure
on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
.
Let
\mu
be Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, fix
d\geq r
, and let
\Psi\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be positive definite.
Under
P_{A}
, sample
Z\sim\nu
and then

X\mid Z=z\sim\mathcal{N}(Az,\Psi).

Write
\varphi_{d}(\cdot;m,\Psi)
for the
\mu
-density of
\mathcal{N}(m,\Psi)
.
Since we take
\mu\otimes\nu
as the dominating measure for
(X,Z)
, the joint density is simply the conditional density:

p_{A}(x,z)\ :=\ \frac{dP_{A}^{X,Z}}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)}(x,z)\ =\ \varphi_{d}(x;Az,\Psi).

Action and transport.

Let
G=O(r)
act by right multiplication
R\cdot A:=AR
and define
\tau_{R}(z):=R^{\top}z
.

Lemma F.13

(Latent transport and observed invariance in the factor model)
.

In the factor model above, Assumption
C.1
holds with
G=O(r)
,
\tau_{R}(z)=R^{\top}z
, and
\nu=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{r})
.
Consequently,
P_{AR}^{X}=P_{A}^{X}
for all
R\in O(r)
; posteriors transport by
\tau_{R}
; and
Q
(hence the M-step) is
O(r)
-equivariant whenever defined.

Proof.

Step 1 (Group action, transport map, and measurability).

The map
(A,R)\mapsto AR
is continuous, hence the action
A\mapsto AR
is Borel measurable on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
for each fixed
R
.
For each
R\in O(r)
,
\tau_{R}(z)=R^{\top}z
is linear and invertible, hence a Borel bijection with inverse
\tau_{R^{\top}}
.

Step 2 (Gaussian base-measure invariance).

Let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{r})
. For any
R\in O(r)
,
R^{\top}Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}Z
.
Equivalently,
\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu
.
A quick verification: the characteristic function satisfies

\mathbb{E}[e^{i\langle t,R^{\top}Z\rangle}]=\mathbb{E}[e^{i\langle Rt,Z\rangle}]=\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\|Rt\|^{2}\Big)=\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\|t\|^{2}\Big),

using
R
orthogonal.

Step 3 (Complete-data equivariance identity).

For all
(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
R\in O(r)
,

p_{AR}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z)\bigr)=\varphi_{d}\bigl(x;AR(R^{\top}z),\Psi\bigr)=\varphi_{d}(x;Az,\Psi)=p_{A}(x,z).

Thus the density-transport identity holds.

Step 4 (Observed invariance by marginalization).

The observed density is

p_{A}^{X}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\varphi_{d}(x;Az,\Psi)\,\nu(dz).

Using Step 3 with
z=\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})
and Step 2,

p_{AR}^{X}(x)=\int\varphi_{d}(x;ARz,\Psi)\,\nu(dz)=\int\varphi_{d}\bigl(x;AR(\tau_{R^{\top}}(z^{\prime})),\Psi\bigr)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=\int\varphi_{d}(x;Az^{\prime},\Psi)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=p_{A}^{X}(x),

so
P_{AR}^{X}=P_{A}^{X}
.

Step 5 (Posterior transport).

For
p_{A}^{X}(x)>0
,

\kappa_{A}(x,B)=\frac{\int_{B}p_{A}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)}{\int p_{A}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)}.

Using Step 3 and change of variables
z=\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})
with Step 2,

\int_{B}p_{AR}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)}p_{AR}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=\int_{\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)}p_{A}(x,z^{\prime})\,\nu(dz^{\prime}).

The denominator is invariant by the same argument, so
\kappa_{AR}(x,B)=\kappa_{A}(x,\tau_{R}^{-1}(B))
.

Step 6 (
Q
-equivariance).

As in Lemma
F.12
, combine Step 3 (transport for
\log p
) and Step 5 (posterior transport) inside the defining conditional expectation for
Q
.
This yields
Q(AR_{1}\mid AR_{2})=Q(A\mid A)
with the appropriate pairing (more generally
Q(R\cdot A\mid R\cdot A^{\prime})=Q(A\mid A^{\prime})
),
whenever both sides are finite. Equivariance of the M-step correspondence follows.
∎

Local measurable sections (minor charts and polar normalization)

Global measurable sections for
A\mapsto AR
generally do not exist on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
, but local sections on an open cover of
the full-column-rank set suffice for slice-based arguments.

Minor charts.

Fix
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}
with
|I|=r
and let
A_{I}
be the
r\times r
submatrix formed by rows indexed by
I
.
Define the open set

\Omega_{I}:=\{A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}:\ \det(A_{I})\neq 0\}.

Every full-column-rank
A
lies in at least one
\Omega_{I}
.

Polar factors and normalization.

For invertible
B\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
define its polar decomposition
B=U(B)H(B)
with

H(B):=(B^{\top}B)^{1/2}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r},\qquad U(B):=B\,H(B)^{-1}\in O(r).

On
\Omega_{I}
define

R_{I}(A):=U(A_{I})^{\top}\in O(r),\qquad\tilde{s}_{I}(A):=A\,R_{I}(A)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}.

Lemma F.14

(Local section for the right
O(r)
action)
.

Fix
I
and work on
\Omega_{I}
. Then:

(i)

R_{I}
and
\tilde{s}_{I}
are continuous (hence Borel) on
\Omega_{I}
;

(ii)

\tilde{s}_{I}
is constant on right-orbits in
\Omega_{I}
:
\tilde{s}_{I}(AR)=\tilde{s}_{I}(A)
for all
R\in O(r)
;

(iii)

(\tilde{s}_{I}(A))_{I}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
(slice normalization);

(iv)

consequently,
\tilde{s}_{I}
factors through the local quotient
\Omega_{I}/O(r)
to a measurable section.

Proof.

Step 1 (Continuity of the polar maps on
\GL(r)
).

On
\GL(r)
, the map
B\mapsto B^{\top}B
is polynomial and hence continuous, and its image lies in
\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
.
The principal square-root map
M\mapsto M^{1/2}
is continuous on
\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
(indeed smooth), and so is inversion
M\mapsto M^{-1}
.
Therefore
H(B)=(B^{\top}B)^{1/2}
and
H(B)^{-1}
depend continuously on
B\in\GL(r)
.
Consequently
U(B)=B\,H(B)^{-1}
is continuous on
\GL(r)
.

Step 2 (Proof of (i)).

On
\Omega_{I}
, the map
A\mapsto A_{I}
is linear (hence continuous).
Since
A_{I}\in\GL(r)
on
\Omega_{I}
, Step 1 implies
A\mapsto U(A_{I})
is continuous on
\Omega_{I}
.
Thus
R_{I}(A)=U(A_{I})^{\top}
is continuous on
\Omega_{I}
, and
\tilde{s}_{I}(A)=AR_{I}(A)
is continuous as a product of continuous maps.

Step 3 (A conjugacy identity for square-roots).

For
M\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
and
R\in O(r)
,

(348)

(R^{\top}MR)^{1/2}=R^{\top}M^{1/2}R.

Indeed,
R^{\top}M^{1/2}R
is symmetric positive definite and

\bigl(R^{\top}M^{1/2}R\bigr)^{2}=R^{\top}M^{1/2}(RR^{\top})M^{1/2}R=R^{\top}MR,

so by uniqueness of the principal square root on
\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
, (
348
) holds.

Step 4 (Equivariance of the polar orthogonal factor).

Let
B\in\GL(r)
and
R\in O(r)
. Then

(BR)^{\top}(BR)=R^{\top}(B^{\top}B)R.

Applying (
348
) with
M=B^{\top}B
gives

H(BR)=\bigl((BR)^{\top}(BR)\bigr)^{1/2}=(R^{\top}(B^{\top}B)R)^{1/2}=R^{\top}H(B)R.

Therefore,

U(BR)=(BR)\,H(BR)^{-1}=BR\,(R^{\top}H(B)R)^{-1}=BR\,(R^{\top}H(B)^{-1}R)=B\,H(B)^{-1}\,R=U(B)\,R,

where we used
(R^{\top}HR)^{-1}=R^{\top}H^{-1}R
for orthogonal
R
.

Step 5 (Proof of (ii): orbit constancy).

Fix
A\in\Omega_{I}
and
R\in O(r)
. Then
(AR)_{I}=A_{I}R
.
By Step 4,

U((AR)_{I})=U(A_{I}R)=U(A_{I})\,R.

Hence

R_{I}(AR)=U((AR)_{I})^{\top}=(U(A_{I})R)^{\top}=R^{\top}\,U(A_{I})^{\top}=R^{-1}R_{I}(A).

Therefore,

\tilde{s}_{I}(AR)=AR\,R_{I}(AR)=AR\,(R^{-1}R_{I}(A))=AR_{I}(A)=\tilde{s}_{I}(A),

proving (ii).

Step 6 (Proof of (iii): slice normalization).

Write the polar decomposition
A_{I}=U(A_{I})H(A_{I})
with
H(A_{I})\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
.
Then

(\tilde{s}_{I}(A))_{I}=A_{I}R_{I}(A)=A_{I}U(A_{I})^{\top}=U(A_{I})H(A_{I})U(A_{I})^{\top}.

This matrix is symmetric positive definite because it is an orthogonal conjugate of
H(A_{I})\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
.
Thus
(\tilde{s}_{I}(A))_{I}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
, proving (iii).

Step 7 (Proof of (iv): factorization through the local quotient).

Let
q_{I}:\Omega_{I}\to\Omega_{I}/O(r)
be the orbit map and equip
\Omega_{I}/O(r)
with the quotient
\sigma
-field

\mathcal{B}(\Omega_{I}/O(r)):=\{B\subseteq\Omega_{I}/O(r):\ q_{I}^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega_{I})\}.

By (i),
\tilde{s}_{I}:\Omega_{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
is Borel measurable; by (ii), it is constant on orbits.
Define
s_{I}:\Omega_{I}/O(r)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
by
s_{I}(\bar{A}):=\tilde{s}_{I}(A)
for any
A
with
q_{I}(A)=\bar{A}
.
This is well-defined by orbit constancy and satisfies
\tilde{s}_{I}=s_{I}\circ q_{I}
.
To see
s_{I}
is measurable, let
C
be a Borel set in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
. Then

q_{I}^{-1}\bigl(s_{I}^{-1}(C)\bigr)=\{A\in\Omega_{I}:\ s_{I}(q_{I}(A))\in C\}=\{A\in\Omega_{I}:\ \tilde{s}_{I}(A)\in C\}=\tilde{s}_{I}^{-1}(C),

which is Borel since
\tilde{s}_{I}
is Borel. By definition of the quotient
\sigma
-field,
s_{I}^{-1}(C)\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega_{I}/O(r))
.
Thus
s_{I}
is measurable and provides a local measurable section.
∎

Remark F.15

(Uniqueness of orbit intersection with the slice)
.

Let
S_{I}:=\{B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}:\ B_{I}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}\}
.
Each orbit in
\Omega_{I}
intersects
S_{I}
in exactly one point, namely
\tilde{s}_{I}(A)
.

Existence.
For any
A\in\Omega_{I}
, Lemma
F.14
(iii) shows
\tilde{s}_{I}(A)\in S_{I}
and

\tilde{s}_{I}(A)=AR_{I}(A)
lies on the right orbit of
A
.

Uniqueness.
Suppose
B_{1},B_{2}\in S_{I}\cap\Omega_{I}
lie on the same orbit, so
B_{2}=B_{1}R
for some
R\in O(r)
.
Restricting to the
I
-minor gives
B_{2,I}=B_{1,I}R
with
B_{1,I},B_{2,I}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
.
Then
R=B_{1,I}^{-1}B_{2,I}
is the product of SPD matrices, hence has positive real spectrum and is itself SPD.
But an orthogonal SPD matrix must be the identity: if
R\in O(r)
and
R\succ 0
, then all eigenvalues of
R
lie in
\{1\}
, so
R=I_{r}
.
Thus
B_{1}=B_{2}
. Therefore the orbit intersection with
S_{I}
is unique.

F.6.
Signed-permutation symmetries (hyperoctahedral groups)

Finite sign and permutation symmetries arise whenever the latent prior is invariant under coordinate relabeling and sign flips.
This includes many ICA/dictionary-learning style latent models (where identifiability is only up to signed permutation).

Signed permutation group.

Let
r\geq 1
and define the finite group

\mathsf{SP}(r):=\Big\{R\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}:\ R\text{ has exactly one nonzero entry in each row/column, equal to }\pm 1\Big\}.

Equivalently,

\mathsf{SP}(r)=\{D\Pi:\ D\in\{\pm 1\}^{r\times r}\text{ diagonal},\ \Pi\in S_{r}\text{ permutation matrix}\}.

Every
R\in\mathsf{SP}(r)
is orthogonal:
R^{\top}R=I_{r}
and
R^{-1}=R^{\top}
.

ICA / linear latent-factor template with i.i.d. symmetric priors

Let
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{r}
with its Borel
\sigma
-field and let
\nu=\nu_{0}^{\otimes r}
, where
\nu_{0}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}

that is symmetric about
0
, i.e.
\nu_{0}(B)=\nu_{0}(-B)
for all Borel
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}
(equivalently,
U\sim\nu_{0}\Rightarrow-U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}U
).
Then
\nu
is invariant under signed permutations in the following strong (measure-pushforward) sense.

Lemma F.16

(Invariance of the product prior under
\mathsf{SP}(r)
)
.

Let
\nu=\nu_{0}^{\otimes r}
with
\nu_{0}
symmetric. For each
R\in\mathsf{SP}(r)
define
\tau_{R}:\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
by
\tau_{R}(z)=Rz
.
Then
\tau_{R}
is a Borel bijection and

\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu\qquad\text{for all }R\in\mathsf{SP}(r).

Proof.

Step 1 (Borel bijection).

Since
\tau_{R}
is linear and invertible, it is continuous (hence Borel measurable) and bijective with inverse
\tau_{R^{-1}}
.

Step 2 (Reduction to generators).

It suffices to verify
\nu(\tau_{R}^{-1}(C))=\nu(C)
for all cylinder rectangles

C=\prod_{j=1}^{r}C_{j}
with Borel
C_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, because such rectangles form a
\pi
-system generating the Borel
\sigma
-field on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
and both
\nu
and
\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}
are probability measures.

Step 3 (Explicit form of
\tau_{R}
).

Write
R=D\Pi
with
\Pi
a permutation matrix and
D=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{r})
with
\sigma_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Then
(\tau_{R}(z))_{j}=\sigma_{j}z_{\pi^{-1}(j)}
, i.e.
\tau_{R}
permutes coordinates and flips signs of selected coordinates.

Step 4 (Compute
\nu(\tau_{R}^{-1}(C))
).

We have

\tau_{R}^{-1}(C)=\Big\{z:\ \sigma_{j}z_{\pi^{-1}(j)}\in C_{j}\ \forall j\Big\}=\Big\{z:\ z_{i}\in\sigma_{\pi(i)}^{-1}C_{\pi(i)}\ \forall i\Big\},

where
\sigma^{-1}C:=\{\sigma^{-1}x:x\in C\}
equals
C
if
\sigma=1
and
-C
if
\sigma=-1
.
By product structure of
\nu
,

\nu(\tau_{R}^{-1}(C))=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\nu_{0}\big(\sigma_{\pi(i)}^{-1}C_{\pi(i)}\big).

By symmetry of
\nu_{0}
,
\nu_{0}(-B)=\nu_{0}(B)
for all
B
, hence
\nu_{0}(\sigma^{-1}B)=\nu_{0}(B)
for
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Therefore

\nu(\tau_{R}^{-1}(C))=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\nu_{0}(C_{\pi(i)})=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\nu_{0}(C_{j})=\nu(C),

since
\pi
is a permutation. This proves
\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu
.
∎

Model.

Let
\Theta
contain a mixing matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
and possibly nuisance parameters
\eta
(e.g. noise scale/covariance).
Assume the conditional law of
X
given
Z=z
depends on
A
only through
Az
; concretely,

X\mid Z=z\sim p_{\mathrm{noise}}(\,\cdot\,;Az,\eta),

where for each
(m,\eta)
the map
x\mapsto p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;m,\eta)
is a
\mu
-density on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
,
and
(x,z,A,\eta)\mapsto p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;Az,\eta)
is jointly measurable.
With
\nu
as above, define the complete-data density w.r.t.
\mu\otimes\nu
by

p_{(A,\eta)}(x,z)=p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;Az,\eta).

Action and transport.

Let
G=\mathsf{SP}(r)
act on
\Theta
by
right
transformation on
A
:

R\cdot(A,\eta):=(AR^{-1},\eta),\qquad R\in\mathsf{SP}(r),

and define latent transport
\tau_{R}(z):=Rz
on
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{r}
.

Lemma F.17

(Signed-permutation transport and EM equivariance)
.

In the ICA-style template above, Assumption
C.1
holds with
G=\mathsf{SP}(r)
,
\tau_{R}(z)=Rz
, and prior
\nu=\nu_{0}^{\otimes r}
.
Consequently:

(i)

(
Observed invariance
)
P_{R\cdot(A,\eta)}^{X}=P_{(A,\eta)}^{X}
for all
R\in\mathsf{SP}(r)
;

(ii)

(
Posterior transport
) for all Borel
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
P_{(A,\eta)}^{X}
-a.e.
x
,

\kappa_{R\cdot(A,\eta)}(x,B)=\kappa_{(A,\eta)}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)\bigr);

(iii)

(
Q
-equivariance and M-step equivariance
) whenever
Q
is well-defined,

Q(R\cdot\theta\mid R\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\qquad\forall\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta,\ \forall R\in\mathsf{SP}(r),

and therefore the M-step correspondence
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
\mathcal{T}(R\cdot\theta^{\prime})=R\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Write
\theta=(A,\eta)
.

Step 1 (Group action and measurability).

\mathsf{SP}(r)
is a finite group under matrix multiplication. The action
\theta\mapsto R\cdot\theta=(AR^{-1},\eta)
is continuous in
A
for fixed
R
,
hence Borel measurable on any Borel parameter space
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\times\mathsf{H}
(with
\mathsf{H}
Euclidean for
\eta
).
The latent transport
\tau_{R}(z)=Rz
is continuous and bijective, hence Borel measurable with measurable inverse.

Step 2 (Base-measure invariance on the latent space).

By Lemma
F.11
,
\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu
for all
R\in\mathsf{SP}(r)
.

Step 3 (Complete-data equivariance identity).

For every
(x,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
,

\displaystyle p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=p_{(AR^{-1},\eta)}(x,Rz)=p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;AR^{-1}(Rz),\eta)=p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;Az,\eta)=p_{\theta}(x,z).

This is exactly the density-transport identity required in Assumption
C.1
.

Step 4 (Observed invariance by marginalization and change of variables).

Let
p_{\theta}^{X}
be the
\mu
-density of
P_{\theta}^{X}
, given by integrating out
z
against
\nu
:

p_{\theta}^{X}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{\mathrm{noise}}(x;Az,\eta)\,\nu(dz).

Then, using Step 3 and Step 2,

\displaystyle p_{R\cdot\theta}^{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{R\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\nu(d(\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})\qquad(\text{since }\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime})\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=p_{\theta}^{X}(x),

so
P_{R\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
, proving (i).

Step 5 (Posterior transport).

Whenever
p_{\theta}^{X}(x)>0
, Bayes’ formula yields the posterior as a probability kernel

\kappa_{\theta}(x,B)=\frac{\int_{B}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)}.

Using Step 3 and the change of variables
z=\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})
plus Step 2,

\displaystyle\int_{B}p_{R\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)

\displaystyle=\int_{\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)}p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=\int_{\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)}p_{\theta}(x,z^{\prime})\,\nu(dz^{\prime}),

and similarly
\int p_{R\cdot\theta}(x,z)\nu(dz)=\int p_{\theta}(x,z)\nu(dz)
.
Therefore
\kappa_{R\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\kappa_{\theta}(x,\tau_{R}^{-1}(B))
for all
B
and for all
x
with
p_{\theta}^{X}(x)>0
.
Since
P_{R\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
(Step 4), this holds
P_{\theta}^{X}
-a.e., proving (ii).

Step 6 (
Q
-equivariance and M-step equivariance).

Assume
Q(\cdot\mid\cdot)
is well-defined (integrability of
\log p_{\theta}(X,Z)
under the relevant posteriors).
By definition,

Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\log p_{\theta}(X,z)\,\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,dz)\Big].

Apply this with
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
replaced by
(R\cdot\theta,R\cdot\theta^{\prime})
and use Steps 3 and 5 with the change of variables
z=\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\int\log p_{R\cdot\theta}(X,z)\,\kappa_{R\cdot\theta^{\prime}}(X,dz)

\displaystyle=\int\log p_{R\cdot\theta}\bigl(X,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,dz^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\log p_{\theta}(X,z^{\prime})\,\kappa_{\theta^{\prime}}(X,dz^{\prime}).

Taking
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\star}}
yields
Q(R\cdot\theta\mid R\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
.
Equivariance of the M-step correspondence
\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})\in\arg\max_{\theta}Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
follows immediately:
if
\theta\in\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
, then for all
\vartheta
,

Q(R\cdot\theta\mid R\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})\geq Q(\vartheta\mid\theta^{\prime})=Q(R\cdot\vartheta\mid R\cdot\theta^{\prime}),

so
R\cdot\theta\in\mathcal{T}(R\cdot\theta^{\prime})
.
∎

Measurable section.

Since
\mathsf{SP}(r)
is finite, Lemma
F.5
yields a global Borel section on
\Theta/\mathsf{SP}(r)
whenever
Assumption
F.4
holds.

F.7.
Rotational symmetries in linear-Gaussian factor models (
G=O(r)
)

This is the continuous-group analogue of label switching. It is canonical in factor analysis and probabilistic PCA,
where
A
is only identified up to right orthogonal transformations.

Model and domination.

Let
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{r}
with its Borel
\sigma
-field and let
\nu=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{r})
be the standard Gaussian
measure
on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
.
Let
\mu
be Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, fix
d\geq r
, and let
\Psi\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be positive definite.
Under
P_{A}
, sample
Z\sim\nu
and then

X\mid Z=z\sim\mathcal{N}(Az,\Psi).

Write
\varphi_{d}(\cdot;m,\Psi)
for the
\mu
-density of
\mathcal{N}(m,\Psi)
.
Since we take
\mu\otimes\nu
as the dominating measure for
(X,Z)
, the joint density is simply the conditional density:

p_{A}(x,z)\ :=\ \frac{dP_{A}^{X,Z}}{d(\mu\otimes\nu)}(x,z)\ =\ \varphi_{d}(x;Az,\Psi).

Action and transport.

Let
G=O(r)
act by right multiplication
R\cdot A:=AR
and define
\tau_{R}(z):=R^{\top}z
.

Lemma F.18
