(Latent transport and observed invariance in the factor model)
.

In the factor model above, Assumption
C.1
holds with
G=O(r)
,
\tau_{R}(z)=R^{\top}z
, and
\nu=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{r})
.
Consequently,
P_{AR}^{X}=P_{A}^{X}
for all
R\in O(r)
; posteriors transport by
\tau_{R}
; and
Q
(hence the M-step) is
O(r)
-equivariant whenever defined.

Proof.

Step 1 (Group action, transport map, and measurability).

The map
(A,R)\mapsto AR
is continuous, hence the action
A\mapsto AR
is Borel measurable on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
for each fixed
R
.
For each
R\in O(r)
,
\tau_{R}(z)=R^{\top}z
is linear and invertible, hence a Borel bijection with inverse
\tau_{R^{\top}}
.

Step 2 (Gaussian base-measure invariance).

Let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{r})
. For any
R\in O(r)
,
R^{\top}Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}Z
.
Equivalently,
\nu\circ\tau_{R}^{-1}=\nu
.
A quick verification: the characteristic function satisfies

\mathbb{E}[e^{i\langle t,R^{\top}Z\rangle}]=\mathbb{E}[e^{i\langle Rt,Z\rangle}]=\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\|Rt\|^{2}\Big)=\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\|t\|^{2}\Big),

using
R
orthogonal.

Step 3 (Complete-data equivariance identity).

For all
(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
R\in O(r)
,

p_{AR}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z)\bigr)=\varphi_{d}\bigl(x;AR(R^{\top}z),\Psi\bigr)=\varphi_{d}(x;Az,\Psi)=p_{A}(x,z).

Thus the density-transport identity holds.

Step 4 (Observed invariance by marginalization).

The observed density is

p_{A}^{X}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\varphi_{d}(x;Az,\Psi)\,\nu(dz).

Using Step 3 with
z=\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})
and Step 2,

p_{AR}^{X}(x)=\int\varphi_{d}(x;ARz,\Psi)\,\nu(dz)=\int\varphi_{d}\bigl(x;AR(\tau_{R^{\top}}(z^{\prime})),\Psi\bigr)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=\int\varphi_{d}(x;Az^{\prime},\Psi)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=p_{A}^{X}(x),

so
P_{AR}^{X}=P_{A}^{X}
.

Step 5 (Posterior transport).

For
p_{A}^{X}(x)>0
,

\kappa_{A}(x,B)=\frac{\int_{B}p_{A}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)}{\int p_{A}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)}.

Using Step 3 and change of variables
z=\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})
with Step 2,

\int_{B}p_{AR}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int_{\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)}p_{AR}\bigl(x,\tau_{R}(z^{\prime})\bigr)\,\nu(dz^{\prime})=\int_{\tau_{R}^{-1}(B)}p_{A}(x,z^{\prime})\,\nu(dz^{\prime}).

The denominator is invariant by the same argument, so
\kappa_{AR}(x,B)=\kappa_{A}(x,\tau_{R}^{-1}(B))
.

Step 6 (
Q
-equivariance).

As in Lemma
F.12
, combine Step 3 (transport for
\log p
) and Step 5 (posterior transport) inside the defining conditional expectation for
Q
.
This yields
Q(AR_{1}\mid AR_{2})=Q(A\mid A)
with the appropriate pairing (more generally
Q(R\cdot A\mid R\cdot A^{\prime})=Q(A\mid A^{\prime})
),
whenever both sides are finite. Equivariance of the M-step correspondence follows.
∎

Local measurable sections (minor charts and polar normalization)

Global measurable sections for
A\mapsto AR
generally do not exist on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
, but local sections on an open cover of
the full-column-rank set suffice for slice-based arguments.

Minor charts.

Fix
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}
with
|I|=r
and let
A_{I}
be the
r\times r
submatrix formed by rows indexed by
I
.
Define the open set

\Omega_{I}:=\{A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}:\ \det(A_{I})\neq 0\}.

Every full-column-rank
A
lies in at least one
\Omega_{I}
.

Polar factors and normalization.

For invertible
B\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
define its polar decomposition
B=U(B)H(B)
with

H(B):=(B^{\top}B)^{1/2}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r},\qquad U(B):=B\,H(B)^{-1}\in O(r).

On
\Omega_{I}
define

R_{I}(A):=U(A_{I})^{\top}\in O(r),\qquad\tilde{s}_{I}(A):=A\,R_{I}(A)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}.

Lemma F.19

(Local section for the right
O(r)
action)
.

Fix
I
and work on
\Omega_{I}
. Then:

(i)

R_{I}
and
\tilde{s}_{I}
are continuous (hence Borel) on
\Omega_{I}
;

(ii)

\tilde{s}_{I}
is constant on right-orbits in
\Omega_{I}
:
\tilde{s}_{I}(AR)=\tilde{s}_{I}(A)
for all
R\in O(r)
;

(iii)

(\tilde{s}_{I}(A))_{I}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
(slice normalization);

(iv)

consequently,
\tilde{s}_{I}
factors through the local quotient
\Omega_{I}/O(r)
to a measurable section.

Proof.

Step 1 (Continuity of the polar maps on
\GL(r)
).

On
\GL(r)
, the map
B\mapsto B^{\top}B
is polynomial and hence continuous, and its image lies in
\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
.
The principal square-root map
M\mapsto M^{1/2}
is continuous on
\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
(indeed smooth), and so is inversion
M\mapsto M^{-1}
.
Therefore
H(B)=(B^{\top}B)^{1/2}
and
H(B)^{-1}
depend continuously on
B\in\GL(r)
.
Consequently
U(B)=B\,H(B)^{-1}
is continuous on
\GL(r)
.

Step 2 (Proof of (i)).

On
\Omega_{I}
, the map
A\mapsto A_{I}
is linear (hence continuous).
Since
A_{I}\in\GL(r)
on
\Omega_{I}
, Step 1 implies
A\mapsto U(A_{I})
is continuous on
\Omega_{I}
.
Thus
R_{I}(A)=U(A_{I})^{\top}
is continuous on
\Omega_{I}
, and
\tilde{s}_{I}(A)=AR_{I}(A)
is continuous as a product of continuous maps.

Step 3 (A conjugacy identity for square-roots).

For
M\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
and
R\in O(r)
,

(349)

(R^{\top}MR)^{1/2}=R^{\top}M^{1/2}R.

Indeed,
R^{\top}M^{1/2}R
is symmetric positive definite and

\bigl(R^{\top}M^{1/2}R\bigr)^{2}=R^{\top}M^{1/2}(RR^{\top})M^{1/2}R=R^{\top}MR,

so by uniqueness of the principal square root on
\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
, (
348
) holds.

Step 4 (Equivariance of the polar orthogonal factor).

Let
B\in\GL(r)
and
R\in O(r)
. Then

(BR)^{\top}(BR)=R^{\top}(B^{\top}B)R.

Applying (
348
) with
M=B^{\top}B
gives

H(BR)=\bigl((BR)^{\top}(BR)\bigr)^{1/2}=(R^{\top}(B^{\top}B)R)^{1/2}=R^{\top}H(B)R.

Therefore,

U(BR)=(BR)\,H(BR)^{-1}=BR\,(R^{\top}H(B)R)^{-1}=BR\,(R^{\top}H(B)^{-1}R)=B\,H(B)^{-1}\,R=U(B)\,R,

where we used
(R^{\top}HR)^{-1}=R^{\top}H^{-1}R
for orthogonal
R
.

Step 5 (Proof of (ii): orbit constancy).

Fix
A\in\Omega_{I}
and
R\in O(r)
. Then
(AR)_{I}=A_{I}R
.
By Step 4,

U((AR)_{I})=U(A_{I}R)=U(A_{I})\,R.

Hence

R_{I}(AR)=U((AR)_{I})^{\top}=(U(A_{I})R)^{\top}=R^{\top}\,U(A_{I})^{\top}=R^{-1}R_{I}(A).

Therefore,

\tilde{s}_{I}(AR)=AR\,R_{I}(AR)=AR\,(R^{-1}R_{I}(A))=AR_{I}(A)=\tilde{s}_{I}(A),

proving (ii).

Step 6 (Proof of (iii): slice normalization).

Write the polar decomposition
A_{I}=U(A_{I})H(A_{I})
with
H(A_{I})\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
.
Then

(\tilde{s}_{I}(A))_{I}=A_{I}R_{I}(A)=A_{I}U(A_{I})^{\top}=U(A_{I})H(A_{I})U(A_{I})^{\top}.

This matrix is symmetric positive definite because it is an orthogonal conjugate of
H(A_{I})\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
.
Thus
(\tilde{s}_{I}(A))_{I}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
, proving (iii).

Step 7 (Proof of (iv): factorization through the local quotient).

Let
q_{I}:\Omega_{I}\to\Omega_{I}/O(r)
be the orbit map and equip
\Omega_{I}/O(r)
with the quotient
\sigma
-field

\mathcal{B}(\Omega_{I}/O(r)):=\{B\subseteq\Omega_{I}/O(r):\ q_{I}^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega_{I})\}.

By (i),
\tilde{s}_{I}:\Omega_{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
is Borel measurable; by (ii), it is constant on orbits.
Define
s_{I}:\Omega_{I}/O(r)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
by
s_{I}(\bar{A}):=\tilde{s}_{I}(A)
for any
A
with
q_{I}(A)=\bar{A}
.
This is well-defined by orbit constancy and satisfies
\tilde{s}_{I}=s_{I}\circ q_{I}
.
To see
s_{I}
is measurable, let
C
be a Borel set in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
. Then

q_{I}^{-1}\bigl(s_{I}^{-1}(C)\bigr)=\{A\in\Omega_{I}:\ s_{I}(q_{I}(A))\in C\}=\{A\in\Omega_{I}:\ \tilde{s}_{I}(A)\in C\}=\tilde{s}_{I}^{-1}(C),

which is Borel since
\tilde{s}_{I}
is Borel. By definition of the quotient
\sigma
-field,
s_{I}^{-1}(C)\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega_{I}/O(r))
.
Thus
s_{I}
is measurable and provides a local measurable section.
∎

Remark F.20

(Uniqueness of orbit intersection with the slice)
.

Let
S_{I}:=\{B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}:\ B_{I}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}\}
.
Each orbit in
\Omega_{I}
intersects
S_{I}
in exactly one point, namely
\tilde{s}_{I}(A)
.

Existence.
For any
A\in\Omega_{I}
, Lemma
F.14
(iii) shows
\tilde{s}_{I}(A)\in S_{I}
and

\tilde{s}_{I}(A)=AR_{I}(A)
lies on the right orbit of
A
.

Uniqueness.
Suppose
B_{1},B_{2}\in S_{I}\cap\Omega_{I}
lie on the same orbit, so
B_{2}=B_{1}R
for some
R\in O(r)
.
Restricting to the
I
-minor gives
B_{2,I}=B_{1,I}R
with
B_{1,I},B_{2,I}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{r}
.
Then
R=B_{1,I}^{-1}B_{2,I}
is the product of SPD matrices, hence has positive real spectrum and is itself SPD.
But an orthogonal SPD matrix must be the identity: if
R\in O(r)
and
R\succ 0
, then all eigenvalues of
R
lie in
\{1\}
, so
R=I_{r}
.
Thus
B_{1}=B_{2}
. Therefore the orbit intersection with
S_{I}
is unique.

F.8.
Pure sign symmetries (
G=\{\pm 1\}
)

This is the simplest nontrivial symmetry and is often combined with permutations (Appendix
F.4
).

Setup.

Let
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
be Borel and assume it is stable under the sign-flip map

\iota:\Theta\to\Theta,\qquad\iota(u,v)=(-u,-v).

Let
G=\{\pm 1\}
act on
\Theta
by

g\cdot(u,v):=(gu,gv).

Let
q:\Theta\to\Theta/G
be the orbit map, and equip
\Theta/G
with the quotient
\sigma
-field

\mathcal{B}(\Theta/G):=\{A\subseteq\Theta/G:\ q^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}(\Theta)\}.

Assumption F.21

(Sign-invariant parametrization)
.

There exists a measurable map
\Psi:\Theta\to\mathcal{W}
into a measurable space
\mathcal{W}
such that:

(i)

(
Sign invariance
)
\Psi(u,v)=\Psi(-u,-v)
for all
(u,v)\in\Theta
;

(ii)

(
Model depends only on
\Psi
) if
\Psi(u,v)=\Psi(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
, then
P_{(u,v)}^{X}=P_{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})}^{X}
.

Lemma F.22

(Observed invariance and projection-set invariance under sign flips)
.

Under Assumption
F.21
,
P_{-1\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Consequently,
\Phi(-\theta)=\Phi(\theta)
and
\bar{\Theta}=\arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta}\Phi(\theta)
is
G
-invariant.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in\Theta
. By Assumption
F.21
(i),
\Psi(\theta)=\Psi(-\theta)
.
By Assumption
F.21
(ii), equality of
\Psi
implies equality of the observed laws, hence

P_{-\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
, i.e. observed invariance holds for the
G=\{\pm 1\}
action.

Now apply Lemma
F.7
:
observed invariance implies
\Phi(-\theta)=\Phi(\theta)
(as extended real numbers), and the maximizer set

\bar{\Theta}
is invariant under the group action. (Equivalently:
\theta\in\bar{\Theta}\Rightarrow-\theta\in\bar{\Theta}
.)
∎

A canonical Borel representative.

A convenient global representative for each orbit is obtained by fixing a measurable tie-breaking rule.
We use the “first nonzero coordinate is positive” convention.

To make this explicit, identify
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d=d_{1}+d_{2}
by concatenation:
for
\theta=(u,v)
write
\theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d})
where
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d_{1}})=u
and

(\theta_{d_{1}+1},\dots,\theta_{d})=v
.

Define the
first-nonzero index
map
J:\Theta\to\{1,\dots,d\}\cup\{\infty\}
by

J(\theta):=\begin{cases}\min\{j\in\{1,\dots,d\}:\ \theta_{j}\neq 0\},&\theta\neq 0,\\
\infty,&\theta=0,\end{cases}

and define the
canonical sign

\sigma:\Theta\to\{\pm 1\}
by

\sigma(\theta):=\begin{cases}+1,&J(\theta)=\infty,\\
+1,&J(\theta)<\infty\text{ and }\theta_{J(\theta)}>0,\\
-1,&J(\theta)<\infty\text{ and }\theta_{J(\theta)}<0.\end{cases}

Finally define the
canonical relabeling map

\tilde{s}:\Theta\to\Theta
by

(350)

\tilde{s}(\theta):=\sigma(\theta)\cdot\theta.

(When
\theta=0
, this fixes the point:
\tilde{s}(0)=0
.)

Lemma F.23

(A Borel section for the sign symmetry)
.

Assume
\Theta
is Borel and stable under
\theta\mapsto-\theta
.
Let
\tilde{s}
be defined by (
350
).
Then:

(i)

\tilde{s}
is Borel measurable;

(ii)

\tilde{s}
is constant on
G
-orbits:
\tilde{s}(-\theta)=\tilde{s}(\theta)
for all
\theta\in\Theta
;

(iii)

\tilde{s}
factors through the quotient: there exists a unique measurable map
s:\Theta/G\to\Theta
such that

\tilde{s}=s\circ q;

(iv)

s
is a measurable section:
q(s(\bar{\theta}))=\bar{\theta}
for all
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G
.

Proof.

Step 1 (Measurability of the index map
J
).

For each
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
,

\{J=j\}=\Big(\bigcap_{i<j}\{\theta_{i}=0\}\Big)\cap\{\theta_{j}\neq 0\},

which is Borel since each coordinate projection
\theta\mapsto\theta_{i}
is continuous and
\{0\}
is closed.
Also,

\{J=\infty\}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{d}\{\theta_{i}=0\},

which is Borel. Therefore
J
is measurable as a map into the countable measurable space

\{1,\dots,d\}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Step 2 (Measurability of the sign map
\sigma
).

For
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
define the Borel sets

A_{j}^{+}:=\{J=j\}\cap\{\theta_{j}>0\},\qquad A_{j}^{-}:=\{J=j\}\cap\{\theta_{j}<0\}.

Then
\{J=\infty\}\cup(\bigcup_{j}A_{j}^{+})=\{\sigma=+1\}
and
\bigcup_{j}A_{j}^{-}=\{\sigma=-1\}
are Borel.
Hence
\sigma
is measurable.

Step 3 (Measurability of
\tilde{s}
).

The map
\theta\mapsto-\theta
is continuous, hence Borel.
Since

\tilde{s}(\theta)=\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma(\theta)=+1\}}\theta+\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma(\theta)=-1\}}(-\theta),

and indicator functions of Borel sets are measurable,
\tilde{s}
is Borel measurable.

Step 4 (Orbit constancy).

Fix
\theta\in\Theta
. If
\theta=0
then
\tilde{s}(-\theta)=\tilde{s}(\theta)=0
.
Otherwise,
J(-\theta)=J(\theta)
and
(-\theta)_{J(\theta)}=-\theta_{J(\theta)}
, so
\sigma(-\theta)=-\sigma(\theta)
.
Therefore

\tilde{s}(-\theta)=\sigma(-\theta)\cdot(-\theta)=(-\sigma(\theta))\cdot(-\theta)=\sigma(\theta)\cdot\theta=\tilde{s}(\theta).

Thus
\tilde{s}
is constant on orbits.

Step 5 (Factorization through the quotient and measurability of
s
).

By Step 4,
\tilde{s}
is
G
-invariant. By Lemma
F.1
(invariant functions factor through the quotient),
there exists a unique measurable map
s:\Theta/G\to\Theta
such that
\tilde{s}=s\circ q
.

Step 6 (Section property).

Fix
\theta\in\Theta
. By construction,
\tilde{s}(\theta)\in\{\theta,-\theta\}
.
Therefore
\tilde{s}(\theta)
lies in the same orbit as
\theta
, i.e.
q(\tilde{s}(\theta))=q(\theta)
.
Using
\tilde{s}=s\circ q
and surjectivity of
q
, for every
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta/G
choose
\theta
with
q(\theta)=\bar{\theta}
and compute

q(s(\bar{\theta}))=q(s(q(\theta)))=q(\tilde{s}(\theta))=q(\theta)=\bar{\theta}.

This proves that
s
is a section.
∎

F.9.
How to read these instantiations in the main text

The preceding examples cover common nonidentifiabilities in EM analyses:

•

Finite label-switching
(
S_{k}
): mixtures, HMMs, latent class and SBM-type models (Appendix
F.1
–
F.3
).

•

Signed permutations
(finite
\mathsf{SP}(r)
): ICA/dictionary-learning style latent models with i.i.d. symmetric priors (Appendix
F.4
).

•

Orthogonal rotations
(
O(r)
): factor analysis and probabilistic PCA with standard Gaussian priors (Appendix
F.5
).

•

Pure sign flips
(
\{\pm 1\}
): the simplest symmetry, often combined with permutations (Appendix
F.8
).

Across these cases, the verification steps used in the main text always follow the same template.

Step A: verify latent transport.

One explicitly constructs a measurable transport
\tau_{g}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}
such that:

(i)

\tau_{gh}=\tau_{g}\circ\tau_{h}
and
\tau_{e}=\mathrm{id}
(compatibility with the group);

(ii)

\nu\circ\tau_{g}^{-1}=\nu
(base latent measure is preserved);

(iii)

the complete-data likelihood is equivariant:

p_{g\cdot\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{g}(z)\bigr)=p_{\theta}(x,z)\qquad\text{for all }(x,z)\ \text{(or for a.e.\ }(x,z)\text{ w.r.t.\ }\mu\otimes\nu\text{)}.

These items are exactly what Assumption
C.1
encodes.

Step B: deduce observed invariance by marginalization.

From
\nu
-preservation and the complete-data equivariance, one obtains

p_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}(x)=\int p_{g\cdot\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=\int p_{\theta}(x,z)\,\nu(dz)=p_{\theta}^{X}(x),

hence
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
. This yields invariance of the population objective
\Phi
and
G
-invariance of

\bar{\Theta}=\arg\max\Phi
(Lemma
F.7
).

Step C: transport posteriors and obtain
Q
-equivariance.

Bayes’ rule plus the same change-of-variables argument implies posterior transport

\kappa_{g\cdot\theta}(x,B)=\kappa_{\theta}\bigl(x,\tau_{g}^{-1}(B)\bigr),

and inserting this into the definition of
Q
yields
Q(g\cdot\theta\mid g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=Q(\theta\mid\theta^{\prime})
whenever defined.
Thus the M-step correspondence is equivariant:
\mathcal{T}(g\cdot\theta^{\prime})=g\cdot\mathcal{T}(\theta^{\prime})
.

Step D: choose representatives (sections/slices) to enforce transverse geometry.

To turn orbit-level statements into concrete Euclidean estimates on parameters, one chooses orbit representatives:

•

for
finite groups
, a global measurable section exists by orbit minimization (Lemma
F.5
) or by explicit
tie-breaking rules (Lemma
F.23
);

•

for
continuous groups
like
O(r)
, one typically works on an open cover and uses local slices (Lemma
F.14
).

These representatives are what make it meaningful to impose strong concavity/contractivity
transversely
to orbits, while still
respecting the intrinsic nonidentifiability.

Summary of what the main results need.

The main text uses only the following outputs of the appendices:

(i)

orbit-level targets under misspecification are well-defined via
\bar{\Phi}
on
\Theta/G
;

(ii)

EM dynamics are compatible with the symmetry (equivariance), so orbit-level convergence statements are canonical;

(iii)

slice/section representatives allow Euclidean curvature and contraction estimates in directions orthogonal to orbits.

Appendix G
IPM choices: separation, moduli, and estimability

Throughout this appendix, let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
be the observation space, and let
\mathcal{P}
denote a class of probability measures on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
that contains the model range

\mathcal{P}_{\Theta}:=\{P_{\theta}^{X}:\ \theta\in\Theta\}.

For a class of measurable test functions
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\{f:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}\}
for which all expectations exist (possibly as extended reals when explicitly stated), the associated integral probability metric (IPM) is

(351)

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q)\ :=\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{Q}[f(X)]\big|.

We use
\mathsf{D}(\cdot,\cdot)
as a generic IPM notation when the underlying class is clear.

Role in the EM theory.

The main text uses IPMs in two logically distinct ways:

(1)

Statistical deviation control
: bounding
\mathsf{D}(\widehat{P}_{n},P^{\star})
(one-sample) or
\mathsf{D}(\widehat{P}_{n},\widehat{Q}_{m})
(two-sample) as a function of
n
and the complexity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
;

(2)

Geometric translation
: relating
\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})
to orbit/quotient distances (Appendix
B.4
) via moduli of continuity and separation properties on
\Theta/G
.

Accordingly, this appendix organizes IPM choices around (i) separation on the relevant range, (ii) modulus comparisons, and (iii) estimability.

G.1.
When an IPM is determining vs. pseudometric

Basic separation notions.

Fix a class
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
and a measurable class
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\{f:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}\}
for which

\mathbb{E}_{P}f
is well-defined for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
and all
f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
(allowing extended values only when explicitly intended).
Recall the induced integral probability metric (IPM)

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q)\ :=\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\Big|\mathbb{E}_{P}f-\mathbb{E}_{Q}f\Big|,\qquad P,Q\in\mathcal{P}.

We say:

•

\mathcal{F}_{0}
is
measure-determining on
\mathcal{P}
(or
\mathcal{P}
-determining
) if

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q)=0\ \Longrightarrow\ P=Q\qquad\forall P,Q\in\mathcal{P}.

•

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
is a
pseudometric on
\mathcal{P}
if it is symmetric, satisfies the triangle inequality, and
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,P)=0
,
but it may fail to separate points.

By construction,
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
is always a pseudometric on any subcollection of measures for which the defining expectations are finite and
the class
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is closed under negation (or if we replace
\mathcal{F}_{0}
by
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}:=\mathcal{F}_{0}\cup(-\mathcal{F}_{0})
).

Elementary sufficient conditions for being determining.

The following templates are standard.

Lemma G.1

(Determining classes via generating
\pi
-systems)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
be a
\pi
-system generating
\mathcal{F}
(i.e.
\sigma(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{F}
), and suppose

\{\mathbf{1}_{C}:\ C\in\mathcal{C}\}\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{F}_{0}.

Then
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q)=0
implies
P=Q
for all probability measures
P,Q
on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
.

Proof.

Assume
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q)=0
. Since
\mathbf{1}_{C}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
,

0=d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q)\ \geq\ \big|\mathbb{E}_{P}\mathbf{1}_{C}-\mathbb{E}_{Q}\mathbf{1}_{C}\big|\ =\ |P(C)-Q(C)|\qquad\forall C\in\mathcal{C}.

Hence
P(C)=Q(C)
for all
C\in\mathcal{C}
.

Define

\Lambda:=\{A\in\mathcal{F}:\ P(A)=Q(A)\}.

We verify
\Lambda
is a
\lambda
-system:

•

\mathsf{X}\in\Lambda
because
P(\mathsf{X})=Q(\mathsf{X})=1
.

•

If
A\in\Lambda
, then
P(A^{c})=1-P(A)=1-Q(A)=Q(A^{c})
, so
A^{c}\in\Lambda
.

•

If
A_{1},A_{2},\dots
are disjoint with each
A_{i}\in\Lambda
, then by countable additivity,

P(\cup_{i}A_{i})=\sum_{i}P(A_{i})=\sum_{i}Q(A_{i})=Q(\cup_{i}A_{i})
, so
\cup_{i}A_{i}\in\Lambda
.

Thus
\Lambda
is a
\lambda
-system.

Since
P(C)=Q(C)
for all
C\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Lambda
.
By the
\pi
–
\lambda
theorem,
\sigma(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\Lambda
.
But
\sigma(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{F}
, hence
\Lambda=\mathcal{F}
, meaning
P(A)=Q(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
.
Therefore
P=Q
.
∎

Lemma G.2

(Determining classes via dense function algebras on compact spaces)
.

Let
(\mathsf{X},\rho)
be compact metric and let
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq C(\mathsf{X})
be a subalgebra containing constants and separating points.
Let
\mathrm{span}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
denote the linear span of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Then for all Borel probability measures
P,Q
on
\mathsf{X}
,

\big(\mathbb{E}_{P}f=\mathbb{E}_{Q}f\ \forall f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}\big)\ \Longrightarrow\ P=Q,

equivalently
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q)=0\Rightarrow P=Q
.

Proof.

Assume
\mathbb{E}_{P}f=\mathbb{E}_{Q}f
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
. By linearity of the integral, the same equality holds for all
g\in\mathrm{span}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
.

Step 1 (Uniform density).

Because
\mathsf{X}
is compact metric,
C(\mathsf{X})
is a Banach space under
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
.
By the Stone–Weierstrass theorem, the hypotheses (subalgebra, constants, point-separating) imply that

\mathrm{span}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
is uniformly dense in
C(\mathsf{X})
: for every
h\in C(\mathsf{X})
and every
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists
g\in\mathrm{span}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
with
\|h-g\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
.

Step 2 (Pass equality of integrals from the dense subspace to all of
C(\mathsf{X})
).

Fix
h\in C(\mathsf{X})
and choose
g_{\varepsilon}\in\mathrm{span}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
such that

\|h-g_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
. Then

\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}h-\mathbb{E}_{Q}h\big|\leq\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}(h-g_{\varepsilon})\big|+\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}g_{\varepsilon}-\mathbb{E}_{Q}g_{\varepsilon}\big|+\big|\mathbb{E}_{Q}(g_{\varepsilon}-h)\big|.

The middle term is
0
by the assumption on
\mathrm{span}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
.
The outer terms are bounded by
\|h-g_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
since
P,Q
are probability measures:

\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}(h-g_{\varepsilon})\big|\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}|h-g_{\varepsilon}|\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\big|\mathbb{E}_{Q}(g_{\varepsilon}-h)\big|\leq\varepsilon.

Thus
|\mathbb{E}_{P}h-\mathbb{E}_{Q}h|\leq 2\varepsilon
. Letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
yields
\mathbb{E}_{P}h=\mathbb{E}_{Q}h
for all
h\in C(\mathsf{X})
.

Step 3 (Continuous functions determine Borel measures on a compact metric space).

On a compact metric space, equality of integrals against all continuous bounded functions implies equality of measures
(equivalently:
C(\mathsf{X})
determines the weak topology and separates probability measures).
Hence
P=Q
.
∎

Canonical examples.

•

Total variation (TV).
Taking
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\{f:\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}
yields

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q)=\sup_{\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}f-\mathbb{E}_{Q}f\big|=2\,\|P-Q\|_{\mathrm{TV}},

which is determining on all probability measures.

•

Bounded-Lipschitz (BL) metric.

On a metric space
(\mathsf{X},\rho)
, let

\|f\|_{\mathrm{BL}}:=\|f\|_{\infty}+\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(f),\qquad\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\{f:\|f\|_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq 1\}.

On Polish
(\mathsf{X},\rho)
,
d_{\mathrm{BL}}
is determining and metrizes weak convergence.

•

Wasserstein-1.

On
(\mathsf{X},\rho)
, define
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\{f:\mathrm{Lip}_{\rho}(f)\leq 1\}
.
Then
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=W_{1}
by Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality, but it is finite only on measures with finite first moment,
so
W_{1}
is determining on

\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\Big\{P:\int\rho(x,x_{0})\,P(dx)<\infty\ \text{for some (hence all) }x_{0}\Big\}.

•

MMD (kernel IPM).

Let
k
be a measurable positive definite kernel with RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, and take

\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\{f\in\mathcal{H}_{k}:\ \|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq 1\}.

Then
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q)=\|\mu_{P}-\mu_{Q}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
where
\mu_{P}:=\mathbb{E}_{P}[k(X,\cdot)]
is the kernel mean embedding (when
k
is bounded).
This is determining on a class
\mathcal{P}
iff
k
is
characteristic on
\mathcal{P}
; otherwise it is only a pseudometric.

Determining
on the model range
(the relevant notion here).

In applications one rarely needs
\mathcal{F}_{0}
to separate
all
probability measures on
\mathsf{X}
.
What matters is separation on the
image of the model
, possibly modulo symmetries.
Write

\mathcal{P}_{\Theta}\ :=\ \{P_{\theta}^{X}:\ \theta\in\Theta\}\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X}).

Even if
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is not determining on
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{X})
, it can be determining on
\mathcal{P}_{\Theta}

(or determining on
\mathcal{P}_{\Theta}

up to the group action
).
This is precisely the notion needed to translate distributional convergence into parameter/orbit convergence.

Definition G.3

(Orbit-determining IPMs)
.

Assume a group
G
acts on
\Theta
and
P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
for all
g,\theta
.
We say
\mathcal{F}_{0}
(equivalently
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
) is
orbit-determining on the model range
if

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \theta^{\prime}\in\left[(\right]\theta).

Equivalently, the induced pseudometric on
\Theta/G
(Lemma
G.14
below) is a genuine metric.

Remark G.4

(Why this is the right notion)
.

If the model is nonidentifiable, then even an “ideal” metric such as total variation cannot separate parameters on the same orbit.
Orbit-determiningness is the sharp replacement:
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
should separate
orbits
(equivalence classes), not raw parameters.

A practical orbit-separation template: finite feature embeddings.

A common situation is that an IPM controls finitely many moments/features.

Lemma G.5

(Feature IPMs are norms of mean-feature differences)
.

Let
\phi:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be measurable and define the function class

\mathcal{F}_{\phi}:=\{f_{u}(x):=\langle u,\phi(x)\rangle:\ u\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\ \|u\|\leq 1\}.

Assume
\mathbb{E}_{P}\|\phi(X)\|<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}_{Q}\|\phi(X)\|<\infty
.
Then

d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P,Q)=\big\|\mathbb{E}_{P}[\phi(X)]-\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\phi(X)]\big\|.

Consequently,
d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}
is orbit-determining on
\mathcal{P}_{\Theta}
whenever the mean-feature map

\Theta/G\ \ni\ \bar{\theta}\ \longmapsto\ \mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{X}}\phi(X)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

is injective (well-definedness follows from observed invariance).

Proof.

Step 1 (Well-definedness and finiteness).

The integrability assumptions ensure the vector means
\mathbb{E}_{P}\phi
and
\mathbb{E}_{Q}\phi
exist in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Moreover for each
\|u\|\leq 1
,
|\mathbb{E}_{P}\langle u,\phi\rangle|\leq\mathbb{E}_{P}\|\phi\|<\infty
, so
d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P,Q)
is finite.

Step 2 (Compute the supremum).

By linearity of expectation,

\mathbb{E}_{P}f_{u}-\mathbb{E}_{Q}f_{u}=\mathbb{E}_{P}\langle u,\phi(X)\rangle-\mathbb{E}_{Q}\langle u,\phi(X)\rangle=\Big\langle u,\mathbb{E}_{P}\phi-\mathbb{E}_{Q}\phi\Big\rangle.

Hence

d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P,Q)=\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\Big|\Big\langle u,\mathbb{E}_{P}\phi-\mathbb{E}_{Q}\phi\Big\rangle\Big|.

By Cauchy–Schwarz,
|\langle u,\Delta\rangle|\leq\|u\|\,\|\Delta\|\leq\|\Delta\|
where
\Delta:=\mathbb{E}_{P}\phi-\mathbb{E}_{Q}\phi
,
so the supremum is at most
\|\Delta\|
.

To see equality, if
\Delta\neq 0
choose
u^{\star}:=\Delta/\|\Delta\|
, which satisfies
\|u^{\star}\|=1
and yields

|\langle u^{\star},\Delta\rangle|=\|\Delta\|
. If
\Delta=0
the supremum is
0
.
Therefore
d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P,Q)=\|\mathbb{E}_{P}\phi-\mathbb{E}_{Q}\phi\|
.

Step 3 (Orbit-determiningness criterion).

If the mean-feature map is injective on
\Theta/G
, then

d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=0\iff\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{X}}\phi=\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}}\phi\iff q(\theta)=q(\theta^{\prime}),

i.e.
\theta^{\prime}
lies in the orbit of
\theta
. This is exactly Definition
G.3
.
∎

Remark G.6

(Feature IPMs as dual norms)
.

The argument above is simply duality between the Euclidean norm and its dual:

\|\Delta\|=\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\langle u,\Delta\rangle
.
One can replace
\|\cdot\|
by any norm
\|\cdot\|_{\ast}
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and take
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}
indexed by the corresponding dual unit ball,
obtaining
d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P,Q)=\|\mathbb{E}_{P}\phi-\mathbb{E}_{Q}\phi\|_{\ast}
.

Local orbit-separation via transverse Jacobians.

Orbit-determiningness is frequently required only locally on the stability region where EM is analyzed.
The next lemma gives a standard differential criterion on a slice/section.

Lemma G.7

(Local orbit separation from a feature Jacobian on a slice)
.

Assume there is a
C^{1}
slice map (local section)
s:U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\Theta
such that

q\circ s
is a homeomorphism onto its image in
\Theta/G
.
Let
\phi:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be measurable with
\mathbb{E}_{P_{s(u)}^{X}}\|\phi(X)\|<\infty
for all
u\in U
, and define

m(u):=\mathbb{E}_{P_{s(u)}^{X}}[\phi(X)]\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Assume
m
is differentiable on a neighborhood of
u_{0}\in U
and that its Jacobian at
u_{0}
has full column rank, with

\sigma_{\min}(\nabla m(u_{0}))\geq\alpha>0.

Then there exists a neighborhood
U_{0}\subseteq U
of
u_{0}
such that for all
u,v\in U_{0}
,

\|m(u)-m(v)\|\ \geq\ \frac{\alpha}{2}\,\|u-v\|.

Equivalently, with
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}
as in Lemma
G.5
,

d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{s(u)}^{X},P_{s(v)}^{X})\ \geq\ \frac{\alpha}{2}\,\|u-v\|,\qquad u,v\in U_{0}.

In particular,
d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}
separates points on the slice locally, hence is locally orbit-determining in the chart neighborhood.

Proof.

Step 1 (Quantitative lower bound for the linearization at
u_{0}
).

Let
A:=\nabla m(u_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times p}
.
The condition
\sigma_{\min}(A)\geq\alpha
means

(352)

\|Ah\|\ \geq\ \alpha\|h\|\qquad\forall h\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.

Step 2 (Control the Jacobian uniformly near
u_{0}
).

Since
m
is differentiable in a neighborhood of
u_{0}
,
\nabla m
exists there.
Assume (as stated) that
\nabla m
is continuous at
u_{0}
; this is automatic if
m
is
C^{1}
, and it also holds under standard
assumptions used to justify differentiation under the expectation defining
m
.
By continuity, choose a neighborhood
U_{0}
of
u_{0}
such that

(353)

\|\nabla m(w)-A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \alpha/2\qquad\forall w\in U_{0}.

Step 3 (Mean value theorem in integral form).

Fix
u,v\in U_{0}
and consider the segment
w(t):=v+t(u-v)
for
t\in[0,1]
.
For vector-valued differentiable maps, the fundamental theorem of calculus gives

(354)

m(u)-m(v)=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla m(w(t))\,(u-v)\,dt.

Step 4 (Lower bound by the linear part minus a perturbation).

Add and subtract
A(u-v)
inside the integral:

m(u)-m(v)=A(u-v)+\int_{0}^{1}\big(\nabla m(w(t))-A\big)(u-v)\,dt.

Take norms and use the triangle inequality in the form
\|a+b\|\geq\|a\|-\|b\|
:

\displaystyle\|m(u)-m(v)\|

\displaystyle\geq\|A(u-v)\|-\left\|\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla m(w(t))-A)(u-v)\,dt\right\|

\displaystyle\geq\|A(u-v)\|-\int_{0}^{1}\|(\nabla m(w(t))-A)(u-v)\|\,dt

\displaystyle\geq\|A(u-v)\|-\int_{0}^{1}\|\nabla m(w(t))-A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|u-v\|\,dt.

By (
352
),
\|A(u-v)\|\geq\alpha\|u-v\|
.
By (
353
),
\|\nabla m(w(t))-A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\alpha/2
for all
t
.
Therefore

\|m(u)-m(v)\|\geq\alpha\|u-v\|-\int_{0}^{1}(\alpha/2)\|u-v\|\,dt=\frac{\alpha}{2}\|u-v\|.

Step 5 (Translate to an IPM lower bound).

By Lemma
G.5
,

d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{s(u)}^{X},P_{s(v)}^{X})=\|m(u)-m(v)\|,

so the same lower bound holds for the IPM.

Step 6 (Local orbit-determiningness on the slice).

If
d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{s(u)}^{X},P_{s(v)}^{X})=0
for
u,v\in U_{0}
, then the inequality forces
\|u-v\|=0
,
hence
u=v
and
s(u)=s(v)
. Since
q\circ s
is injective on
U_{0}
(homeomorphism onto its image),
this implies the corresponding orbits are distinct unless
u=v
.
Thus the IPM separates orbits locally in the chart neighborhood.
∎

Remark G.8

(Where continuity of
\nabla m
comes from in EM applications)
.

In EM-style settings
m(u)=\mathbb{E}_{P_{s(u)}^{X}}\phi(X)
is a parameterized expectation.
One typically justifies differentiability and continuity of
\nabla m
by dominated convergence:
if
u\mapsto p_{s(u)}(x)
is smooth and
\phi(x)
has an integrable envelope uniform over
u
in a neighborhood,
then
m
is
C^{1}
with
\nabla m(u)=\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{u}(\phi(X)\ \text{under}\ P_{s(u)}^{X})]
.
Lemma
D.6
records the same dominated-convergence mechanism for second derivatives.

Remark G.9

(Interpretation: a local bi-Lipschitz embedding of the quotient)
.

The lemma says that near
u_{0}
the map
u\mapsto m(u)
is locally injective with a quantitative inverse Lipschitz constant
\alpha/2
.
Composed with the local section
s
, this yields a locally injective map on
\Theta/G
.
This is the precise sense in which finitely many moments/features can identify orbits
locally
:
the feature mean embedding is transverse to the orbits in the neighborhood where EM contracts.

G.2.
Statistical estimation of IPMs and induced rates

Empirical IPMs.

Given i.i.d.
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P
, write
P_{n}:=\widehat{P}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
and define the plug-in IPM

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)\ :=\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{P}f(X)\Big|\ =\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\big|(P_{n}-P)f\big|.

Its statistical behavior is governed by:
(i) the “size” of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
(Rademacher complexity, VC/entropy, chaining),
and (ii) envelope/tail conditions ensuring concentration.

Throughout, whenever we take
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}
inside expectations or probabilities, we tacitly impose a standard

measurability/separability hypothesis
(e.g. Assumption
E.1
) so that the supremum is measurable;
otherwise, one may interpret all expectations/probabilities as
outer
expectation/probability, which yields identical inequalities.

A generic complexity–concentration template

The next lemma packages the two standard steps:

symmetrization
(to control expectations via Rademacher averages) and

Talagrand–Bousquet concentration
(to upgrade to high probability).
We state it in a form tailored to empirical IPMs.

Lemma G.10

(Symmetrization and Bousquet concentration for bounded IPMs)
.

Assume
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\{f:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}\}
has bounded envelope

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\|f\|_{\infty}\ \leq\ b<\infty.

Let
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n}
be i.i.d. Rademacher signs independent of
X_{1:n}
and define the conditional empirical Rademacher complexity

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}(\mathcal{F}_{0};X_{1:n}):=\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(X_{i})\ \Big|\ X_{1:n}\Big].

Let the
variance proxy
be

v\ :=\ \sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\Var\!\big(f(X)\big)\ \leq\ b^{2}.

Then:

(1)

(
Expectation bound
)

\mathbb{E}\big[d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)\big]\ \leq\ 2\,\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}(\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm};X_{1:n})\big],\qquad\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}:=\mathcal{F}_{0}\cup(-\mathcal{F}_{0}).

In particular, if
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is symmetric (
\mathcal{F}_{0}=-\mathcal{F}_{0}
), then

\mathbb{E}[d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)]\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}(\mathcal{F}_{0};X_{1:n})]
.

(2)

(
High-probability bound
) for all
t>0
, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

(355)

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\big[d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)\big]\ +\ \sqrt{\frac{2vt}{n}}\ +\ \frac{bt}{3n}.

In particular, using
v\leq b^{2}
,

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\big[d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)\big]\ +\ b\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}\ +\ \frac{bt}{3n}.

Combining with (i) yields the common “complexity
+
concentration” form

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)\ \leq\ 2\,\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}(\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm};X_{1:n})\big]\ +\ \sqrt{\frac{2vt}{n}}\ +\ \frac{bt}{3n}.

Proof.

Step 0 (Absolute supremum as a one-sided supremum over
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}
).

By definition,

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\big|(P_{n}-P)f\big|=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}(P_{n}-P)g.

Thus it suffices to work with the one-sided supremum functional over
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}
.

Step 1 (Expectation: symmetrization).

Let
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}P
be an independent ghost sample, with empirical measure
P_{n}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}^{\prime}}
.
For each
g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}
,

(P_{n}-P)g\ =\ (P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})g\ +\ (P_{n}^{\prime}-P)g.

Taking
\mathbb{E}[\cdot\mid X_{1:n}]
and using
\mathbb{E}[P_{n}^{\prime}g\mid X_{1:n}]=Pg
gives

\mathbb{E}\big[(P_{n}-P)g\mid X_{1:n}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})g\mid X_{1:n}\big].

Now apply Jensen to the convex functional
u(\cdot)\mapsto\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}u(g)
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}(P_{n}-P)g\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}\mathbb{E}\big[(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})g\mid X_{1:n}\big]\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})g\Big].

Conditioning on
(X_{1:n},X_{1:n}^{\prime})
, the random variables

\{g(X_{i})-g(X_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{n}
are symmetrized by Rademachers:

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})g\ \Big|\ X_{1:n},X_{1:n}^{\prime}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\big(g(X_{i})-g(X_{i}^{\prime})\big)\Big].

Use
\sup(a+b)\leq\sup a+\sup b
:

\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{g}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\epsilon_{i}(g(X_{i})-g(X_{i}^{\prime}))\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{g}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\epsilon_{i}g(X_{i})\Big]+\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{g}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}(-\epsilon_{i})g(X_{i}^{\prime})\Big].

The two terms are equal after taking expectation over
(X_{1:n},X_{1:n}^{\prime})
(symmetry of
\epsilon
and i.i.d. samples),
so

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}(P_{n}-P)g\Big]\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}g(X_{i})\Big]=2\,\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}(\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm};X_{1:n})\big],

which proves (i).

Step 2 (High probability: apply Bousquet to the absolute supremum).

Define the random variable

Z\ :=\ \sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}}(P_{n}-P)g\ =\ d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P).

Since
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq b
for all
g\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}
and
\Var(g(X))\leq v
, Lemma
E.15
applies and yields:
for all
t>0
, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

Z\leq\mathbb{E}[Z]+\sqrt{\frac{2vt}{n}}+\frac{bt}{3n}.

This is exactly (
355
). The simplification
v\leq b^{2}
is immediate.
∎

Remark G.11

(A common tightening and a data-dependent alternative)
.

Lemma
G.10
cleanly separates: (a) complexity (through
\mathbb{E}Z
) and (b) concentration.
In many analyses one further bounds
\mathbb{E}Z
by deterministic entropy integrals (Appendix
E.3
).
If one prefers
data-dependent
bounds, there are also standard results of the form

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)\ \lesssim\ 2\,\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}(\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm};X_{1:n})\ +\ b\sqrt{\frac{t}{n}}

with probability
\geq 1-e^{-t}
(obtained by combining symmetrization with concentration around the empirical Rademacher complexity).
We keep the present form because it is modular and pairs directly with Bousquet/Talagrand.

Vector-valued test statistics.

Many applications (e.g. gradient/Hessian control) produce vector classes
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\{h:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
and consider

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\|(P_{n}-P)h\|
. Appendix
E.1
reduces this to finitely many scalar IPMs by sphere nets:

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\|(P_{n}-P)h\|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{1-\eta}\max_{v\in\mathcal{N}_{\eta}}\ \sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}(P_{n}-P)\langle v,h\rangle.

One then applies Lemma
G.10
to each induced scalar class and union-bounds over the net.

Two canonical estimable IPMs with explicit bounds

Finite-dimensional feature IPMs.

Lemma
G.5
identifies
d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{n},P)
with a Euclidean mean deviation.

Lemma G.12

(Estimation of feature IPMs)
.

Let
\phi:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be measurable with
\|\phi(x)\|\leq B
for all
x
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{\phi}:=\{x\mapsto\langle u,\phi(x)\rangle:\ \|u\|\leq 1\}
.
Then for i.i.d.
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P
,

d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{n},P)=\big\|(P_{n}-P)\phi\big\|.

Moreover:

(1)

(
Expectation
)
\mathbb{E}[d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{n},P)]\leq 2B/\sqrt{n}
.

(2)

(
Concentration
) for all
t>0
, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{n},P)\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}[d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{n},P)]\ +\ B\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}\ \leq\ \frac{2B}{\sqrt{n}}\ +\ B\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}.

Proof.

Step 1 (Identity with a Euclidean mean deviation).

By Lemma
G.5
,

d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{n},P)=\big\|\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}\phi-\mathbb{E}_{P}\phi\big\|=\|(P_{n}-P)\phi\|.

Step 2 (Expectation bound via symmetrization and a Khintchine–Jensen step).

Apply symmetrization (Lemma
E.6
) to the
vector
mean deviation via scalarization by duality:

\mathbb{E}\|(P_{n}-P)\phi\|=\mathbb{E}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\big\langle u,(P_{n}-P)\phi\big\rangle=\mathbb{E}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}(P_{n}-P)\langle u,\phi\rangle\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\langle u,\phi(X_{i})\rangle.

The inner supremum is again a dual norm:

\sup_{\|u\|\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\langle u,\phi(X_{i})\rangle=\Big\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})\Big\|.

Thus

\mathbb{E}\|(P_{n}-P)\phi\|\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}\Big\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})\Big\|.

Now condition on
X_{1:n}
and use Jensen in the form
\mathbb{E}\|S\|\leq(\mathbb{E}\|S\|^{2})^{1/2}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})\Big\|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Compute the second moment (cross-terms vanish because
\mathbb{E}[\epsilon_{i}\epsilon_{j}]=0
for
i\neq j
):

\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}a_{i}\Big\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\sum_{i,j}\epsilon_{i}\epsilon_{j}\langle a_{i},a_{j}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|a_{i}\|^{2}.

With
a_{i}:=\phi(X_{i})
this gives

\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})\Big\|\leq\frac{1}{n}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi(X_{i})\|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\leq\frac{B}{\sqrt{n}}.

Taking expectation over
X_{1:n}
yields
\mathbb{E}[d_{\mathcal{F}_{\phi}}(P_{n},P)]\leq 2B/\sqrt{n}
.

Step 3 (Concentration via bounded differences / McDiarmid).

Consider the function of the sample

F(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}):=\|(P_{n}-P)\phi\|=\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}\phi(X)\right\|.

If we replace
X_{i}
by
X_{i}^{\prime}
, then
P_{n}\phi
changes by
\frac{1}{n}(\phi(X_{i})-\phi(X_{i}^{\prime}))
, so

|F(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-F(X_{1},\dots,X_{i}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n})|\leq\frac{1}{n}\|\phi(X_{i})-\phi(X_{i}^{\prime})\|\leq\frac{2B}{n}.

Thus McDiarmid’s inequality gives: for all
t>0
, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

F\leq\mathbb{E}[F]+B\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}.

Insert the expectation bound from Step 2.
∎

Kernel IPMs (MMD).

For bounded kernels, MMD is naturally Hilbert-valued mean estimation and admits dimension-free root-
n
bounds.

Lemma G.13

(MMD estimation under a bounded kernel)
.

Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
be a measurable space and let
k:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable positive definite kernel
with RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
Assume the diagonal is uniformly bounded:

\sup_{x\in\mathsf{X}}k(x,x)\ \leq\ \kappa^{2}<\infty.

Let

\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\{f\in\mathcal{H}_{k}:\ \|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq 1\},\qquad d_{k}:=d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}.

Assume (as is standard in learning theory, and automatic e.g. when
\mathsf{X}
is standard Borel and
k
is measurable) that
the canonical feature map
\Phi:\mathsf{X}\to\mathcal{H}_{k}
,
\Phi(x):=k(x,\cdot)
is strongly measurable, so Bochner expectations below are well-defined.
Then for i.i.d.
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim P
and
P_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
:

(i)

(Mean embedding identity).
The mean element
\mu_{P}:=\mathbb{E}[\Phi(X)]\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
exists and

d_{k}(P,Q)=\|\mu_{P}-\mu_{Q}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\qquad\text{whenever }\mu_{P},\mu_{Q}\text{ exist}.

In particular,
\mu_{P_{n}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi(X_{i})
and
d_{k}(P_{n},P)=\|\mu_{P_{n}}-\mu_{P}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
.

(ii)

(Expectation).

\mathbb{E}[d_{k}(P_{n},P)]\leq 2\kappa/\sqrt{n}
.
(Indeed, one can sharpen this to
\mathbb{E}[d_{k}(P_{n},P)]\leq\kappa/\sqrt{n}
.)

(iii)

(Concentration).
For all
t>0
, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

d_{k}(P_{n},P)\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}[d_{k}(P_{n},P)]+\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}\ \leq\ \frac{2\kappa}{\sqrt{n}}+\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}.

Proof.

Step 0 (Basic RKHS facts and integrability).

For each
x\in\mathsf{X}
,
\Phi(x)=k(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
and the reproducing property gives

f(x)=\langle f,\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\qquad\forall f\in\mathcal{H}_{k}.

Moreover,

\|\Phi(x)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\langle\Phi(x),\Phi(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=k(x,x)\leq\kappa^{2},

so
\|\Phi(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq\kappa
almost surely. Hence
\mathbb{E}\|\Phi(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}<\infty
, and the Bochner mean element

\mu_{P}:=\mathbb{E}[\Phi(X)]\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
exists. Likewise
\mu_{P_{n}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi(X_{i})
exists almost surely.

Step 1 (Identity
d_{k}(P,Q)=\|\mu_{P}-\mu_{Q}\|
).

Fix probability measures
P,Q
such that
\mu_{P},\mu_{Q}
exist.
For any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
,

\mathbb{E}_{P}f-\mathbb{E}_{Q}f=\mathbb{E}_{P}\langle f,\Phi(X)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}-\mathbb{E}_{Q}\langle f,\Phi(X)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\Big\langle f,\mathbb{E}_{P}\Phi(X)-\mathbb{E}_{Q}\Phi(X)\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\langle f,\mu_{P}-\mu_{Q}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}},

where we used linearity and continuity of the inner product to exchange
\mathbb{E}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
(justified by the bound

|\langle f,\Phi(X)\rangle|\leq\|f\|\,\|\Phi(X)\|\leq\kappa\|f\|
).
Therefore,

d_{k}(P,Q)=\sup_{\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq 1}\big|\langle f,\mu_{P}-\mu_{Q}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\big|=\|\mu_{P}-\mu_{Q}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}},

by Hilbert-space duality:
\sup_{\|f\|\leq 1}|\langle f,h\rangle|=\|h\|
, achieved at
f=h/\|h\|
when
h\neq 0
.
Taking
Q=P_{n}
yields the claimed identity for
d_{k}(P_{n},P)
.

Step 2 (Expectation bound: symmetrization route).

Let
\Phi_{i}:=\Phi(X_{i})
and
\mu:=\mu_{P}=\mathbb{E}[\Phi(X)]
.
Then
\mu_{P_{n}}-\mu=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Phi_{i}-\mu)
, so by Step 1,

\mathbb{E}[d_{k}(P_{n},P)]=\mathbb{E}\|\mu_{P_{n}}-\mu\|.

Introduce i.i.d. Rademacher signs
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n}
, independent of
X_{1:n}
.
A standard symmetrization inequality for Banach-valued means (apply the usual ghost-sample symmetrization to the scalar class

\{x\mapsto\langle h,\Phi(x)\rangle:\ \|h\|\leq 1\}
and then take the supremum over
\|h\|\leq 1
) yields

\mathbb{E}\|\mu_{P_{n}}-\mu\|\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\Phi_{i}\right\|.

Condition on
X_{1:n}
. In a Hilbert space,

\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\Phi_{i}\right\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\Phi_{i},\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon_{j}\Phi_{j}\right\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\Phi_{i}\|^{2}\quad\text{(since }\mathbb{E}[\epsilon_{i}\epsilon_{j}]=\mathbf{1}\{i=j\}\text{)}.

By Jensen,

\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\Phi_{i}\right\|\leq\left(\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\Phi_{i}\right\|^{2}\right)^{1/2}=\frac{1}{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\Phi_{i}\|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa^{2}\right)^{1/2}=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{n}}.

Taking expectation over
X_{1:n}
gives
\mathbb{E}[d_{k}(P_{n},P)]\leq 2\kappa/\sqrt{n}
.

(Sharper constant, optional).
One may avoid symmetrization and compute the second moment directly:

\mathbb{E}\|\mu_{P_{n}}-\mu\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Phi_{i}-\mu)\right\|^{2}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\|\Phi_{i}-\mu\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot n\,\mathbb{E}\|\Phi(X)\|^{2}\leq\frac{\kappa^{2}}{n},

hence
\mathbb{E}\|\mu_{P_{n}}-\mu\|\leq(\mathbb{E}\|\mu_{P_{n}}-\mu\|^{2})^{1/2}\leq\kappa/\sqrt{n}
.

Step 3 (Concentration via bounded differences / McDiarmid).

Define the functional of the sample

F(X_{1:n})\ :=\ \|\mu_{P_{n}}-\mu\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi(X_{i})-\mu\right\|.

Let
X_{i}^{\prime}
be an independent copy of
X_{i}
and write
X^{(i)}:=(X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1},X_{i}^{\prime},X_{i+1},\dots,X_{n})
.
Then

\mu_{P_{n}}(X_{1:n})-\mu_{P_{n}}(X^{(i)})=\frac{1}{n}\big(\Phi(X_{i})-\Phi(X_{i}^{\prime})\big),

so by the reverse triangle inequality,

|F(X_{1:n})-F(X^{(i)})|\leq\left\|\mu_{P_{n}}(X_{1:n})-\mu_{P_{n}}(X^{(i)})\right\|=\frac{1}{n}\|\Phi(X_{i})-\Phi(X_{i}^{\prime})\|\leq\frac{1}{n}\big(\|\Phi(X_{i})\|+\|\Phi(X_{i}^{\prime})\|\big)\leq\frac{2\kappa}{n}.

Thus
F
satisfies bounded differences with constants
c_{i}=2\kappa/n
.
McDiarmid’s inequality gives, for all
t>0
, with probability at least
1-e^{-t}
,

F\leq\mathbb{E}[F]+\sqrt{\frac{t}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}}=\mathbb{E}[F]+\sqrt{\frac{t}{2}\cdot n\cdot\frac{4\kappa^{2}}{n^{2}}}=\mathbb{E}[F]+\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2t}{n}}.

Substitute
\mathbb{E}[F]=\mathbb{E}[d_{k}(P_{n},P)]
and apply Step 2.
∎

Qualitative comparison of estimability across IPMs.

The lemmas above illustrate the standard geometry–statistics tradeoff:

•

Smaller/more regular test classes
\mathcal{F}_{0}
yield smaller empirical-process complexity and hence tighter estimation error
for
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{n},P)
.

•

But smaller
\mathcal{F}_{0}
typically weakens separation on the model range (or the modulus linking
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})

to orbit/parameter distances).

In EM analyses, one typically chooses
\mathcal{F}_{0}
to be just strong enough to be (locally) orbit-determining on the stability region,
while remaining estimable at the sample size and dimension of interest.

G.3.
Compatibility with quotient geometry and induced moduli

Descending an IPM to the quotient

Assume the observed marginal laws are
G
-invariant:

(356)

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}\qquad\forall g\in G,\ \forall\theta\in\Theta.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be any pseudometric on probability laws on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F})
(e.g. an IPM
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
),
possibly taking values in
[0,\infty]
.
Throughout this subsection we work on a region where
\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})<\infty
for the pairs of parameters considered,
so that the triangle inequality is meaningful.

Orbit-invariance of
\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}^{X}
implies descent.

Define the induced parameter-level pseudometric

\mathsf{D}_{\Theta}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\ :=\ \mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}).

By (
356
),
\mathsf{D}_{\Theta}
is constant on orbits in each argument:
for all
g,h\in G
,

\mathsf{D}_{\Theta}(g\cdot\theta,h\cdot\theta^{\prime})=\mathsf{D}(P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X},P_{h\cdot\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=\mathsf{D}_{\Theta}(\theta,\theta^{\prime}).

Hence
\mathsf{D}_{\Theta}
canonically defines a pseudometric on the orbit space
\Theta/G
.

Lemma G.14

(Well-defined quotient distance induced by an IPM)
.

Let
q:\Theta\to\Theta/G
be the orbit map. Under (
356
), the function

\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})\ :=\ \mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}),\qquad\bar{\theta}=q(\theta),\ \bar{\theta}^{\prime}=q(\theta^{\prime}),

is well-defined on
\Theta/G
(i.e. independent of the chosen representatives) and is a pseudometric there.

Proof.

Step 1 (Well-definedness).

Let
\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in\Theta
with
q(\theta)=q(\tilde{\theta})
. Then
\tilde{\theta}=g\cdot\theta
for some
g\in G
.
Using (
356
),

\mathsf{D}(P_{\tilde{\theta}}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=\mathsf{D}(P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}).

The same argument applies in the second argument. Thus
\overline{\mathsf{D}}
does not depend on representatives.

Step 2 (Pseudometric properties).

Nonnegativity and the identity
\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta})=0
follow from the corresponding properties of
\mathsf{D}
.
Symmetry follows from symmetry of
\mathsf{D}
.
For the triangle inequality, pick representatives
\theta,\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}
of
\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime},\bar{\theta}^{\prime\prime}
and use

\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime\prime})=\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime\prime}}^{X})\leq\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})+\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime\prime}}^{X})=\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})+\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta}^{\prime},\bar{\theta}^{\prime\prime}).

∎

Equivalent “inf-over-representatives” form.

Even though representative-independence makes the following trivial under (
356
), it is sometimes conceptually useful to note:

\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})=\inf\big\{\mathsf{D}(P_{\vartheta}^{X},P_{\vartheta^{\prime}}^{X}):\ q(\vartheta)=\bar{\theta},\ q(\vartheta^{\prime})=\bar{\theta}^{\prime}\big\}.

Under (
356
), the infimum is attained by
any
representatives and has the same value.

G.4.
Compatibility with quotient geometry and induced moduli

Descending an IPM to the quotient

Assume the model has a
G
-symmetry on the observed marginal laws:

(357)

P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}\qquad\forall g\in G,\ \forall\theta\in\Theta.

Let
q:\Theta\to\Theta/G
denote the orbit map,
q(\theta)=:\bar{\theta}
.

Lemma G.15

(Well-defined quotient distance induced by a law-level pseudometric)
.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be any pseudometric on probability measures such that
\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})
is well-defined (possibly
+\infty
)
for the pairs
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
under consideration.
Under (
356
), the function

\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})\ :=\ \mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}),\qquad\bar{\theta}=q(\theta),\ \bar{\theta}^{\prime}=q(\theta^{\prime}),

is well-defined on
\Theta/G
and is a pseudometric there.

Proof.

Well-definedness.

Suppose
q(\theta)=q(\tilde{\theta})
. Then
\tilde{\theta}=g\cdot\theta
for some
g\in G
, hence by (
356
),

P_{\tilde{\theta}}^{X}=P_{g\cdot\theta}^{X}=P_{\theta}^{X}
. Therefore

\mathsf{D}(P_{\tilde{\theta}}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}).

The same argument applies to the second argument, proving that the right-hand side depends only on the orbits.

Pseudometric properties.

Symmetry and the triangle inequality transfer directly from
\mathsf{D}
because
\overline{\mathsf{D}}
is defined by composing
\mathsf{D}

with the model map
\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}^{X}
.
∎

Lemma G.16

(Orbit separation
\Longleftrightarrow
metric on
\Theta/G
)
.

Assume (
356
) and let
\overline{\mathsf{D}}
be defined as in Lemma
G.14
.
Then
\overline{\mathsf{D}}
is a genuine metric on
\Theta/G
(i.e. it separates points) if and only if

(358)

\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \theta^{\prime}\in\left[(\right]\theta).

Proof.

By Lemma
G.14
,
\overline{\mathsf{D}}
is a pseudometric on
\Theta/G
, so the only issue is separation.

(
\Rightarrow
).

Assume
\overline{\mathsf{D}}
is a metric. If
\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=0
, then by definition of
\overline{\mathsf{D}}
,

\overline{\mathsf{D}}\big(q(\theta),q(\theta^{\prime})\big)=0.

Since
\overline{\mathsf{D}}
separates points on
\Theta/G
, it follows that
q(\theta)=q(\theta^{\prime})
, i.e.
\theta^{\prime}\in\left[(\right]\theta)
.

(
\Leftarrow
).

Assume (
358
). If
\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})=0
, choose representatives
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
with

q(\theta)=\bar{\theta}
and
q(\theta^{\prime})=\bar{\theta}^{\prime}
. Then

0=\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})=\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}),

so by (
358
) we have
\theta^{\prime}\in\left[(\right]\theta)
, hence
q(\theta^{\prime})=q(\theta)
and therefore
\bar{\theta}^{\prime}=\bar{\theta}
.
Thus
\overline{\mathsf{D}}
separates points, i.e. it is a metric.
∎

Remark G.17

(What the quotient pseudometric measures)
.

Under (
356
), the model map
\theta\mapsto P_{\theta}^{X}
is constant on orbits and therefore factors through
\Theta/G
.
The quantity
\overline{\mathsf{D}}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})
is simply the law-level distance
\mathsf{D}
evaluated on the corresponding observed laws,
hence it is the intrinsic statistical distance between orbit-parameters.

Upper moduli: continuity of the moment map

Let
\mathsf{D}=d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
be an IPM induced by a scalar class
\mathcal{F}_{0}
:

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P,Q):=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\big|\mathbb{E}_{P}f-\mathbb{E}_{Q}f\big|.

Lemma G.18

(Upper modulus from uniform Lipschitz dependence of expectations)
.

Fix any pseudometric
d_{\Theta}
on
\Theta
and assume there exists
L<\infty
such that

(359)

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\big|\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta}^{X}}f-\mathbb{E}_{P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}}f\big|\ \leq\ L\,d_{\Theta}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\qquad\forall\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta.

Then for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
,

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})\ \leq\ L\,d_{\Theta}(\theta,\theta^{\prime}).

If, moreover, the observed invariance (
356
) holds and
d_{G}
is the orbit pseudometric induced by
d_{\Theta}
,

d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime}):=\inf_{g\in G}d_{\Theta}(\theta,g\cdot\theta^{\prime}),

then

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})\ \leq\ L\,d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime}).

Proof.

The first claim is immediate from the definition of
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
as the supremum of the left-hand side of (
359
).

For the orbit bound, fix
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
and any
g\in G
. By (
356
),

P_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}^{X}=P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}
, hence

d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})=d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{g\cdot\theta^{\prime}}^{X})\leq L\,d_{\Theta}(\theta,g\cdot\theta^{\prime}).

Taking the infimum over
g\in G
yields
d_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X})\leq L\,d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
.
∎

Remark G.19

(Upper moduli on the quotient)
.

If
\bar{d}(\bar{\theta},\bar{\theta}^{\prime}):=d_{G}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
denotes the induced pseudometric on
\Theta/G
, then Lemma
G.18

implies
\overline{\mathsf{D}}\leq L\,\bar{d}
on
\Theta/G
.

Lower moduli: local injectivity and transverse identifiability

Definition G.20

(Local lower modulus around an orbit)
.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be any pseudometric on observed laws and define the induced pseudometric on parameters by

\mathsf{D}_{\Theta}(\theta,\theta^{\prime}):=\mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\theta^{\prime}}^{X}).

Fix
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
and an orbit pseudometric
d_{G}
on
\Theta
(e.g. induced from a base pseudometric
d_{\Theta}
).
We say that
\mathsf{D}
admits a
local lower modulus

\underline{\omega}
around
\left[(\right]\theta_{0})
if there exist
r>0
and a nondecreasing function

\underline{\omega}:[0,r)\to[0,\infty)
with
\underline{\omega}(0)=0
such that

(360)

\underline{\omega}\!\big(d_{G}(\theta,\theta_{0})\big)\ \leq\ \mathsf{D}_{\Theta}(\theta,\theta_{0})\qquad\text{whenever }d_{G}(\theta,\theta_{0})<r.

If
\underline{\omega}
is strictly increasing on
[0,r)
, define its right-continuous generalized inverse by

\underline{\omega}^{-1}(y)\ :=\ \inf\{u\in[0,r):\ \underline{\omega}(u)\geq y\}.

Remark G.21

(Interpretation)
.

The inequality (
360
) states that motion
transverse to the orbit
(measured by
d_{G}
) forces a nontrivial movement of the observed law
(measured by
\mathsf{D}
). When
\underline{\omega}(u)\asymp u
locally, this is a local bi-Lipschitz identifiability condition on the quotient.

Rate propagation through moduli

Lemma G.22

(From
\mathsf{D}
-estimation to an orbit rate via the inverse modulus)
.

Assume the model is well-specified:
P^{\star}=P_{\theta_{0}}^{X}
for some
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
.
Let
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
be any (possibly randomized) estimator such that

\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P_{\theta_{0}}^{X})\ =\ O_{\mathbb{P}}(r_{n})\qquad\text{for some }r_{n}\downarrow 0.

If
\mathsf{D}
admits a strictly increasing local lower modulus
\underline{\omega}
around
\left[(\right]\theta_{0})
as in
Definition
G.20
, then

d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\theta_{0})\ =\ O_{\mathbb{P}}\big(\underline{\omega}^{-1}(r_{n})\big).

Proof.

Let
r
be the radius in Definition
G.20
.
By (
360
), on the event
\{d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\theta_{0})<r\}
we have

\underline{\omega}\!\big(d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\theta_{0})\big)\leq\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P_{\theta_{0}}^{X}).

Applying the (monotone) generalized inverse
\underline{\omega}^{-1}
gives

d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\theta_{0})\leq\underline{\omega}^{-1}\!\big(\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P_{\theta_{0}}^{X})\big)\qquad\text{on }\{d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\theta_{0})<r\}.

Since
\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P_{\theta_{0}}^{X})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(r_{n})
and
r_{n}\downarrow 0
, the right-hand side converges to
0
in probability,
hence
\Pr(d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\theta_{0})<r)\to 1
; thus the inequality above holds with probability tending to one.
Finally, monotonicity of
\underline{\omega}^{-1}
and
O_{\mathbb{P}}(r_{n})
yield the claimed rate.
∎

Misspecification (distance to an orbit-invariant target set).

Under misspecification, one often targets an orbit-invariant set
\bar{\Theta}\subseteq\Theta
(e.g.
\arg\max\Phi
).
Define the orbit-distance-to-set

d_{G}(\theta,\bar{\Theta}):=\inf_{\vartheta\in\bar{\Theta}}d_{G}(\theta,\vartheta),

and the approximation error (in
\mathsf{D}
) to that set:

\varepsilon_{\star}(\bar{\Theta})\ :=\ \inf_{\vartheta\in\bar{\Theta}}\mathsf{D}(P^{\star},P_{\vartheta}^{X}).

(If
\bar{\Theta}=\Theta
this reduces to the model-mismatch radius
\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\mathsf{D}(P^{\star},P_{\vartheta}^{X})
.)

Lemma G.23

(Misspecified rate propagation to distance-to-set)
.

Fix an orbit-invariant set
\bar{\Theta}\subseteq\Theta
and an orbit pseudometric
d_{G}
.
Assume there exist
r>0
and a strictly increasing
\underline{\omega}:[0,r)\to[0,\infty)
such that for every
\vartheta\in\bar{\Theta}
,

(361)

\underline{\omega}\!\big(d_{G}(\theta,\vartheta)\big)\ \leq\ \mathsf{D}(P_{\theta}^{X},P_{\vartheta}^{X})\qquad\text{whenever }d_{G}(\theta,\vartheta)<r.

Let
\widehat{\theta}_{n}
satisfy
\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P^{\star})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(r_{n})
with
r_{n}\downarrow 0
.
Then

d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\bar{\Theta})\ =\ O_{\mathbb{P}}\!\Big(\underline{\omega}^{-1}\!\big(r_{n}+\varepsilon_{\star}(\bar{\Theta})\big)\Big),

provided
r_{n}+\varepsilon_{\star}(\bar{\Theta})<\underline{\omega}(r)
eventually (so that the inverse is evaluated within its domain).

Proof.

Fix
\delta>0
and choose
\vartheta_{\delta}\in\bar{\Theta}
such that

\mathsf{D}(P^{\star},P_{\vartheta_{\delta}}^{X})\leq\varepsilon_{\star}(\bar{\Theta})+\delta.

By the triangle inequality,

\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P_{\vartheta_{\delta}}^{X})\leq\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P^{\star})+\mathsf{D}(P^{\star},P_{\vartheta_{\delta}}^{X})\leq\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P^{\star})+\varepsilon_{\star}(\bar{\Theta})+\delta.

On the event
\{d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\vartheta_{\delta})<r\}
, the uniform lower modulus (
361
) gives

\underline{\omega}\!\big(d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\vartheta_{\delta})\big)\leq\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P_{\vartheta_{\delta}}^{X}).

Apply
\underline{\omega}^{-1}
and use
d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\bar{\Theta})\leq d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\vartheta_{\delta})
to obtain

d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\bar{\Theta})\leq\underline{\omega}^{-1}\!\Big(\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P^{\star})+\varepsilon_{\star}(\bar{\Theta})+\delta\Big)\qquad\text{on }\{d_{G}(\widehat{\theta}_{n},\vartheta_{\delta})<r\}.

Since
\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P^{\star})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(r_{n})
, the right-hand side is

O_{\mathbb{P}}(\underline{\omega}^{-1}(r_{n}+\varepsilon_{\star}(\bar{\Theta})+\delta))
.
Letting
\delta\downarrow 0
yields the claim.
∎

Remark G.24

(What must be checked in concrete EM problems)
.

To use Lemmas
G.22
–
G.23
effectively, one typically verifies:
(i) an estimable control
\mathsf{D}(P_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}}^{X},P^{\star})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(r_{n})
(Appendix
E
),
and (ii) a lower modulus on a slice (transverse identifiability), e.g. via a Jacobian nonsingularity condition for a finite feature map
(Lemma
G.7
) or via curvature of a likelihood-type criterion restricted to a slice.
